# Third Part: Concerning the Methodology and Problematic of Pure Phenomenology

## **First Chapter: Methodological Preliminary Considerations**

### **§ 63\. The Particular Significance of Methodological Considerations for Phenomenology**

Hussserl opens the Third Part by surveying the opened field and the problem of the beginning: if we observe the norms prescribed by the phenomenological reductions, switch off all transcendencies exactly as required, and take the experiences purely according to their own essence, then — after all that has been laid out — there opens to us a field of eidetic cognitions. It presents itself, once the difficulties of the beginnings are overcome, as an all-sidedly infinite one. The manifold of experience-kinds and \-forms, with their reell and intentional essence-stocks, is inexhaustible; correspondingly inexhaustible is the manifold of essence-connections grounded in them and of apodictically-necessary truths. So this infinite field of the consciousness-a priori — which in its own-ness never came to its right, indeed was properly never even seen — it is now a matter of making fruitful (urbar) and drawing full-valued fruits from it. But how to find the right beginning? In fact, the beginning is here the most difficult, and the situation an unusual one. The new field does not lie so spread-out before our gaze, with fullnesses of distinct givennesses, that we could simply reach in and be sure of the possibility of making them objects of a science — let alone sure of the method by which to proceed here. If we observe the norms that the phenomenological reductions prescribe to us, switch off all transcendencies exactly as they demand, take the experiences thus purely according to their own essence, then there opens to us, according to all that has been laid out, a field of eidetic cognitions. It presents itself, once one has overcome the difficulties of the beginnings, as an all-sidedly infinite one. The manifold of experience-kinds and \-forms, with their reell and intentional essence-stocks, is precisely an inexhaustible one; accordingly inexhaustible is also the manifold of essence-connections grounding in them and of apodictically-necessary truths. Thus this infinite field of the consciousness-a priori — which in its own-ness never came to its right, indeed was properly never even seen — it is now a matter of making fruitful and drawing full-valued fruits from it. But how to find the right beginning? In fact, the beginning is here the most difficult, and the situation an unusual one. The new field does not lie so spread-out before our gaze, with fullnesses of distinct givennesses, that we could simply reach in and be sure of the possibility of making them objects of a science, let alone sure of the method by which to proceed here. The opening of the Third Part: the infinite field and the problem of method/beginning. The reductions have opened the field of pure consciousness — and it proves infinite and inexhaustible: an unlimited manifold of experience-kinds (perception, memory, judgment, feeling, will...), each with its reell and intentional essence-stock (its hyletic and noetic-noematic structure), grounding inexhaustible essence-connections and apodictic truths. This is the "consciousness-a priori" (*Bewußtseinsapriori*) — the total essential structure of consciousness — which, Hussserl claims, no prior philosophy ever properly saw. The task now is to cultivate (urbar machen — "make arable") this field scientifically. But the beginning is hardest: unlike the natural sciences, whose field (the world) lies ready and familiar before us, the phenomenological field is unfamiliar, not spread out in distinct givennesses — one cannot "simply reach in" and start; both the possibility of a science of it and its method are initially unclear. This is why the Third Part must begin with methodological preliminary considerations (§§63–75): the phenomenological method itself must be worked out before the substantive analyses (the noetic-noematic, §§76ff.) can proceed. The "particular significance of methodological considerations for phenomenology" (the section title) lies precisely in this: phenomenology, uniquely among sciences, must create its own method from scratch, having no inherited, familiar field or procedure.

Hussserl contrasts the natural sciences' situation: it is *not* as with the givennesses of the natural attitude — especially the objects of *nature* — which are *well-familiar* to us through *constant experience* and through the *thought-exercise of millennia*, \[familiar\] as to manifold properties, elements, and laws, when we *self-actively, researching, try to further their cognition*. *All the unknown is thereby horizon of a known.* *All methodical effort links up with the given, all further-formation of method with already-available method*; in general it is a matter of *mere development of special methods* that *fit into the pre-given and fixed style of a proven scientific methodology in general* and, in their invention, *have their guidance from this style*. *It is not as with the givennesses of the natural attitude — especially with the objects of nature — which are well-familiar to us through constant experience and through the thought-exercise of millennia, as to manifold properties, elements, and laws, when we self-actively, researching, try to further their cognition.* *All the unknown is thereby horizon of a known.* *All methodical effort links up with the given, all further-formation of method with already-available method; in general it is a matter of mere development of special methods that fit into the pre-given and fixed style of a proven scientific methodology in general and, in their invention, have their guidance from this style.* The contrast with the *natural* sciences' methodological situation. In natural science, the field (nature) is *long familiar* (through everyday experience and millennia of inquiry); research *extends* known cognition into its *horizon* (the unknown is always the *horizon of a known*); and *method* always *links up with given method* — new (special) methods *develop within* a *pre-given, proven methodological style* that *guides* their invention. So natural-scientific method is *continuous, cumulative, traditioned*: one *inherits* a working method and *refines* it. The phenomenologist enjoys *none* of this (next paragraph): no familiar field, no inherited method, no guiding style. This contrast sharpens the *difficulty* of phenomenology's beginning — it must *originate* its method without the support of tradition, familiarity, or a pre-given methodological style. (The point is not that phenomenology is harder *in degree* but that it faces a *categorially different* methodological situation — the creation of a method for an *unprecedented* field.)

Hussserl details phenomenology's unique methodological burden: *how otherwise in phenomenology*. *Not only* that *before all matter-determining method, a method is already needed* — namely, to bring the matter-field of transcendentally-pure consciousness into the grasping gaze at all; *not only* that this requires a *laborious turning-away of the gaze* from the *constantly-conscious* natural givennesses (which are, as it were, *interwoven* with the newly-intended ones), so that the *danger* always threatens of *confusing* the one with the other: *there is also lacking all that benefits us for the natural object-sphere* — the *familiarity through practiced intuition*, the *favor of inherited theorizings and professional methods*. Self-evidently, there is also lacking, for the already-developed methodology, the *accommodating trust* that could draw *nourishment from manifold successful and proven applications* in the recognized sciences and the practice of life. *How otherwise in phenomenology.* *Not only that, before all matter-determining method, a method is already needed — namely, to bring the matter-field of transcendentally-pure consciousness into the grasping gaze at all; not only that this requires a laborious turning-away of the gaze from the constantly-conscious — thus, as it were, with the newly-intended interwoven — natural givennesses, so that always the danger threatens of confusing the one and the other: there is also lacking all that benefits us for the natural object-sphere, the familiarity through practiced intuition, the favor of inherited theorizings and professional methods.* *Self-evidently there is also lacking, for the already-developed methodology, the accommodating trust that could draw nourishment from manifold successful and proven applications in the recognized sciences and the practice of life.* The full statement of phenomenology's *unique methodological burden*. Phenomenology faces *layered* difficulties absent in the natural sciences: (i) it needs a *method even to access its field* — the reduction is a *method-before-method*, required just to bring pure consciousness into view (whereas natural science's field is simply *there*); (ii) accessing the field requires a *laborious turning-away* from the *natural givennesses*, which are *constantly co-conscious* and *interwoven* with the pure ones, breeding the *constant danger of confusion* (the *metabasis* of §61 — mistaking the mundane for the pure); (iii) it *lacks all the inherited supports* of natural science — *familiarity* (practiced intuition of the field), *inherited theories* and *professional methods*, and the *trust* born of *proven successful applications*. So the phenomenologist works *without* tradition, familiarity, or track-record — in an *unfamiliar* field, with a *novel* method, under *constant threat of confusion*, and *without* the confidence-building support of past success. This explains both the *difficulty* and the *special importance of methodological care* in phenomenology (the section's thesis).

Hussserl states the two reasons for phenomenology's exceptional need of methodological clarity: the *newly-appearing phenomenology* must therefore *reckon with a fundamental mood of skepsis*; it has not only to *develop the method* of winning novel cognitions from novel matters, but to *create the most perfect clarity over the sense and validity of the method*, in which it can *withstand all serious objections*. To this is added — *and that is, because related to the principial, much more important* — that *phenomenology by its essence must raise the claim to be "first" philosophy and to offer the means for all the critique of reason to be accomplished*; that it *therefore demands the most perfect presuppositionlessness, and, in relation to itself, absolute reflective insight*. *Its own essence is to realize the most perfect clarity over its own essence, and thus also over the principles of its method.* *The newly-appearing phenomenology must therefore reckon with a fundamental mood of skepsis.* *It has not only to develop the method of winning novel cognitions from novel matters; it has to create the most perfect clarity over the sense and validity of the method, in which it can withstand all serious objections.* *To this is added — and that is, because related to the principial, much more important — that phenomenology by its essence must raise the claim to be "first" philosophy and to offer the means for all the critique of reason to be accomplished; that it therefore demands the most perfect presuppositionlessness and, in relation to itself, absolute reflective insight.* *Its own essence is to realize the most perfect clarity over its own essence, and thus also over the principles of its method.* The two grounds of phenomenology's exceptional methodological demand. (i) *External*: phenomenology faces a *climate of skepsis* (the novelty and unfamiliarity breed distrust), so it must not only *develop* its method but *justify* it with perfect clarity, able to *withstand objections*. (ii) *Internal and more important*: phenomenology *claims to be first philosophy* (*prima philosophia*) — the *ultimate foundation* offering the means for *all critique of reason*. This claim *demands*: (a) *perfect presuppositionlessness* (it can presuppose *nothing*, else it would not be *first* — §§59–61), and (b) *absolute reflective insight into itself* (it must be *fully self-transparent*, clear about its own essence and method). Indeed, it is phenomenology's *very essence* to *realize perfect clarity about its own essence* — phenomenology is the *self-clarifying* science, the science that *grounds itself*. This is the deepest reason for the methodological preliminary considerations: as *first philosophy*, phenomenology must be *absolutely self-grounding and self-transparent* — its method cannot be borrowed or assumed but must be *clarified from within*, with the same rigor it brings to all else. (This *self-referential* demand — phenomenology clarifying its own possibility and method phenomenologically — is taken up in §§65, 76, and is a recurring theme of Hussserl's reflections on phenomenology as *first philosophy*.)

Hussserl closes §63: for these reasons, the *careful efforts toward insight into the foundational pieces of the method* — into what is *methodically determining* for the new science *from the very beginning and constantly in its progress* — have for phenomenology a *wholly other significance* than analogous efforts could ever have for other sciences. *For these reasons, the careful efforts toward insight into the foundational pieces of the method — thus into what is methodically determining for the new science from the very beginning and constantly in its progress — have for phenomenology a wholly other significance than analogous efforts could ever have for other sciences.* The conclusion of §63, justifying the *whole* methodological First Chapter. Because of the two grounds (the climate of skepsis and, above all, the claim to be self-grounding first philosophy), *methodological self-clarification* has a *categorially greater significance* for phenomenology than for any other science. Other sciences can *take their method largely for granted* (inheriting and refining it); phenomenology must *constantly* secure and clarify its method, *from the beginning and throughout*. This justifies the substantial methodological reflections that follow (§§64–75) before the substantive analyses. (For an ordinary science, an extended methodological prologue would be dispensable; for *first philosophy*, it is *essential*.) The "foundational pieces of the method" (*Grundstücke der Methode*) — the reduction, the eidetic method, the methods of clarity, the noetic-noematic guiding-thread — are what the First Chapter will secure.

### **§ 64\. The Self-Switching-Off of the Phenomenologist**

Hussserl raises a *self-referential objection*: first let a *methodical reservation* be mentioned that could *hamper the very first steps*. We switch off the *whole natural world and all transcendent-eidetic spheres* and are thereby to win a "*pure*" consciousness. *But did we not just say "we" switch off — can we phenomenologists set ourselves out of play, we who are after all also members of the natural world?* *First let a methodical reservation be mentioned that could hamper the very first steps.* *We switch off the whole natural world and all transcendent-eidetic spheres and are thereby to win a "pure" consciousness.* *But did we not just say "we" switch off — can we phenomenologists set ourselves out of play, we who are after all also members of the natural world?* The *self-referential* objection. The reduction brackets the *whole natural world* — but the *phenomenologist* (the "we" who *performs* the reduction) is *himself* a member of that world (a human being, an empirical psyche). So: does the reduction *bracket the phenomenologist too*? And if so, *who* is left to *do* the phenomenology? This is a version of the *reflexivity* problem: the bracketing seems to *undermine its own performer*. (It parallels the ancient self-refutation worry about skepticism — "if you doubt everything, do you doubt your doubting?") Hussserl resolves it (next paragraph) by distinguishing the *empirical* phenomenologist (a worldly human, bracketed *qua* worldly) from the *phenomenologizing subject* (the transcendental ego, which *performs* the reduction and is *not* bracketed — indeed is the *residue*, §57). The "we" who brackets is the *transcendental* subject, not the *empirical* human.

Hussserl resolves the objection: one soon convinces oneself that *this has no difficulty at all*, provided only we have *not shifted the sense of "switching-off."* We can even *calmly continue to speak as we, as natural humans, have to speak*; for *as phenomenologists we are not to cease being natural humans* and positing ourselves as such *in speech*. But *as a piece of the method* — for the *establishments to be entered into the new fundamental book of phenomenology* — we *give ourselves the norm of phenomenological reduction, which co-relates to our empirical existence (Dasein)* and *forbids us to enter any proposition that explicitly or implicitly contains such natural positings*. As far as *individual existence* is concerned, the phenomenologist *proceeds no differently than every eidetic researcher*, e.g. the *geometer*: in their scientific treatises the geometers not seldom *speak of themselves and their researching*; but the *mathematizing subject does not belong in the eidetic content of the mathematical propositions themselves*. *One soon convinces oneself that this has no difficulty at all, provided only we have not shifted the sense of "switching-off."* *We can even calmly continue to speak as we, as natural humans, have to speak; for as phenomenologists we are not to cease being natural humans, and positing ourselves as such also in speech.* *But as a piece of the method, for the establishments to be entered into the newly-to-be-laid-out fundamental book of phenomenology, we give ourselves the norm of phenomenological reduction, which co-relates to our empirical existence and forbids us to enter any proposition that explicitly or implicitly contains such natural positings.* *As far as individual existence is concerned, the phenomenologist proceeds no differently than every eidetic researcher, e.g. the geometer.* *In their scientific treatises the geometers not seldom speak of themselves and their researching; but the mathematizing subject does not belong in the eidetic content of the mathematical propositions themselves.* The resolution of the self-referential objection, via the *eidetic-researcher* analogy. There is *no difficulty*, provided "switching-off" is *rightly understood*: the reduction does *not* require the phenomenologist to *stop being a natural human* (he continues to live, speak, posit himself as a human — *outside* the strict phenomenological work). The reduction is a *methodical norm* governing *what may be entered into phenomenology's "fundamental book"* (its scientific results): *no proposition* containing *natural positings* (about the phenomenologist's empirical existence) may be admitted *as a phenomenological result*. The illuminating analogy: the *geometer* may *speak of himself* (his discovery, his labor) in his treatise — but his *empirical existence does not enter the eidetic content of the geometrical propositions* (the theorems are about *space*, not about the geometer). Likewise, the *phenomenologist* may speak of himself as a human, but his *empirical existence does not enter the eidetic content of the phenomenological propositions* (which are about *pure consciousness as such*, not about *this* human's psyche). So the *empirical* phenomenologist is "bracketed" *from the eidetic content* of phenomenology — while remaining, *qua* living human, the *performer* of the science. The "self-switching-off" is thus the *exclusion of the phenomenologist's empirical existence from the eidetic content* of his results, *not* a paradoxical self-annihilation. (The deeper point — that the *performing* subject is the *transcendental* ego, §57 — underlies this, but Hussserl here gives the *methodological* resolution via the eidetic-researcher parallel.)

### **§ 65\. The Back-Reference of Phenomenology to Itself**

Hussserl raises a *second self-referential difficulty*: again one could take offense that, *in the phenomenological attitude*, we *direct the gaze upon some pure experiences to research them* — but that *the experiences of this researching itself belong to the stock of the phenomenological sphere* — so that the *phenomenological experiences* (the researching acts) would themselves fall under phenomenological study, raising a question of *circularity or regress*. *Again one could take offense that, in the phenomenological attitude, we direct the gaze upon some pure experiences to research them — but that the experiences of this researching itself belong to the stock of the phenomenological sphere*; \[so that phenomenology's own acts of research are themselves phenomena to be studied\]. The *second* self-referential difficulty: phenomenology's *back-reference to itself* (*Rückbeziehung auf sich selbst*). When the phenomenologist *researches* pure experiences, his *researching acts* (the perceivings, reflectings, judgings *of the phenomenologist*) are *themselves* pure experiences — hence *themselves* belong to the phenomenological field and *could be studied phenomenologically*. This raises the worry of *vicious circularity* or *infinite regress*. The following paragraphs resolve it.

Hussserl resolves the difficulty: *this too is no difficulty*. It stands *exactly so* in *psychology* and likewise in *logical noetics*: the *thinking of the psychologist* is itself something *psychological*, the *thinking of the logician* a *logical* one (itself belonging within the compass of the logical norms). This *back-relatedness to itself* would be *worrying only if* the cognition of *all the other matters* in the relevant research-fields *depended on* the phenomenological, psychological, or logical cognition of the *respective thinking of the respective thinker* — *which is a visibly countersensical presupposition*. *This too is no difficulty.* *It stands exactly so in psychology and likewise in logical noetics: the thinking of the psychologist is itself something psychological, the thinking of the logician a logical one — namely, itself belonging within the compass of the logical norms.* *This back-relatedness to itself would be worrying only if the cognition of all the other matters in the relevant research-fields depended on the phenomenological, psychological, or logical cognition of the respective thinking of the respective thinker — which is a visibly countersensical presupposition.* The resolution of the self-reference worry by *analogy* with psychology and logic. The phenomenologist's researching acts being *themselves* phenomena is *no more problematic* than the parallel facts that the *psychologist's* thinking is itself *psychological*, or the *logician's* thinking is itself *logical* (subject to logical norms). These are *unproblematic* self-inclusions. The self-reference would be *vicious* (circular) *only if* the cognition of *all other* matters in the field *depended on* first cognizing *one's own* thinking — i.e. if phenomenology had to *first* analyze the phenomenologist's own acts *before* it could study anything else. But that is *not* the case (it is a "*countersensical presupposition*"): the phenomenologist can study *any* pure experience *directly*, without *first* studying his *own researching*. The self-inclusion is *not* a *foundational dependence* (which would be circular) but a *harmless* fact that phenomenology's acts *also* fall within its domain. So the back-reference is *non-vicious*: phenomenology *can* study its own acts, but does *not* have to do so *first*, and the study of other phenomena does *not* depend on it. No circularity.

Hussserl concedes a *real* (but surmountable) difficulty of self-related disciplines: a *certain difficulty* does lie, in all *self-related disciplines*, in that the *first introduction*, as also the *first researching penetration* into them, must *operate with methodical aids that they have only afterward to form scientifically and definitively*. *Without preliminary and preparatory matter-related and methodical consideration, no design of a new science comes about.* But the *concepts and other methodical elements* with which beginning psychology, phenomenology, etc., *operate in such preparatory works* are *themselves psychological, phenomenological, etc.*, and *win their scientific stamp only in the system of the already-grounded science*. *A certain difficulty does lie, in all self-related disciplines, in that the first introduction, as also the first researching penetration into them, must operate with methodical aids that they have only afterward to form scientifically and definitively.* *Without preliminary and preparatory matter-related and methodical consideration, no design of a new science comes about.* *But the concepts and other methodical elements with which beginning psychology, phenomenology, etc., operate in such preparatory works are themselves psychological, phenomenological, etc., and win their scientific stamp only in the system of the already-grounded science.* The *real* (but benign) difficulty of *self-grounding* disciplines: the *bootstrapping* problem. To *begin* a self-related science, one must *already use* methodical means (concepts, procedures) that the science will only *later* refine and ground. There is *no* presuppositionless *first step* in the sense of starting with *nothing* — every "design of a new science" needs *preliminary* (provisional) considerations. *But* — the resolution — the *provisional* concepts and methods used at the start are *themselves* phenomenological (psychological, etc.), and they receive their *final scientific form only later*, *within* the developed science. So the science *retroactively grounds its own beginnings*: it starts with provisional, naively-used means and *later* clarifies and secures them by its own developed method. This is the *hermeneutic circle* of self-grounding sciences — benign, not vicious, because the circle *closes* (the science eventually grounds the very means it began with). (This is exactly what *Ideas I* itself does: it uses provisional notions early and clarifies them as it proceeds — and §65.¶6 will note that the *whole book* is itself phenomenology.)

Hussserl states the *general procedure* of descriptive phenomenology: in this direction there lie *obviously no serious reservations* that could hinder the actual execution of such sciences, especially phenomenology. If it wants to be a *science within the frame of mere immediate intuition*, a purely "*descriptive*" *essence-science*, then *the general of its procedure is pre-given as something quite self-evident*. It has to *set pure consciousness-occurrences exemplarily before its eyes*, to *bring them to perfect clarity*, on them within this clarity to *practice analysis and essence-grasping*, to *follow the insightful essence-connections*, to *grasp what is in each case beheld in faithful conceptual expressions* that let their *sense be prescribed purely by the beheld* (or generally insightfully-seen), and so forth. *In this direction there lie obviously no serious reservations that could be hindering to the actual execution of such sciences, and especially of phenomenology.* *If it wants to be a science within the frame of mere immediate intuition, a purely "descriptive" essence-science, then the general of its procedure is pre-given as something quite self-evident.* *It has to set pure consciousness-occurrences exemplarily before its eyes, to bring them to perfect clarity, on them within this clarity to practice analysis and essence-grasping, to follow the insightful essence-connections, to grasp what is in each case beheld in faithful conceptual expressions that let their sense be prescribed purely by the beheld (or generally insightfully-seen), and so forth.* The statement of phenomenology's *basic descriptive procedure* — the core method. As a *purely descriptive eidetic science within immediate intuition*, phenomenology's general procedure is "*self-evident*" (it follows from its nature). The steps: (i) *exemplify* — set pure consciousness-occurrences (e.g. a perception, a judgment) *before one's eyes* as *examples*; (ii) *clarify* — bring them to *perfect clarity* (full intuitive givenness — the method of clarification, §67); (iii) *analyze and grasp essence* — within that clarity, *analyze* the example and *grasp its essence* (via eidetic seeing / free variation, §§3–4, §70); (iv) *follow essence-connections* — trace the *insightful* eidetic relations; (v) *express faithfully* — capture the *beheld* in *faithful concepts* whose *sense is prescribed purely by the beheld* (faithful expression, §66). This five-fold procedure (exemplify → clarify → analyze/grasp-essence → follow-connections → express-faithfully) is the *basic descriptive method* of phenomenology. Crucially, it is *grounded in intuition throughout*: every concept must *answer to the beheld*, drawing its sense *purely* from what is *seen* (the *principle of all principles*, §24). This is the operational core of phenomenological method.

Hussserl distinguishes the *naive* practice of the method from its *reflective grounding*: if this procedure, *naively practiced*, serves at first only to *move about in the new field*, to *practice in general the seeing, grasping, analyzing* in it and to become *a little acquainted* with its givennesses, then *scientific reflection on the essence of the procedure itself* — on the essence of the *givenness-kinds* playing in it, on *essence, accomplishment, conditions of perfect clarity and insight*, as well as of *perfectly faithful and fixed conceptual expression*, and the like — *takes over the function of a general and logically-rigorous grounding of the method*. *Consciously followed*, it now *takes on the character and rank of scientific method*, which in the given case, *in application of the strictly-formulated methodical norms, permits exercising delimiting and improving critique*. *If this procedure, naively practiced, serves at first only to move about in the new field, to practice in general the seeing, grasping, analyzing in it, and to become a little acquainted with its givennesses, then scientific reflection on the essence of the procedure itself — on the essence of the givenness-kinds playing in it, on essence, accomplishment, conditions of perfect clarity and insight, as well as of perfectly faithful and fixed conceptual expression, and the like — takes over the function of a general and logically-rigorous grounding of the method.* *Consciously followed, it now takes on the character and rank of scientific method, which in the given case, in application of the strictly-formulated methodical norms, permits exercising delimiting and improving critique.* The *two levels* of phenomenological method: *naive practice* and *reflective grounding*. (i) *Naively practiced*, the descriptive procedure (¶4) lets one *find one's way* in the new field, *practicing* seeing/grasping/analyzing and getting *acquainted* with the givennesses — this is the *first*, *pre-reflective* exercise of method. (ii) But then *scientific reflection on the procedure itself* — on the *essence* of the givenness-kinds, of *clarity, insight, faithful expression* — provides the *rigorous grounding* of the method. *Consciously followed*, the method attains *scientific rank*, with *strictly-formulated norms* that permit *critique* (delimiting and improving the work). So phenomenological method *develops* from *naive practice* to *reflectively-grounded science* — and the *grounding* is *itself* a *phenomenological* investigation (of clarity, insight, expression). This is the *self-grounding* structure: phenomenology *grounds its own method phenomenologically* (by reflecting phenomenologically on its own procedure). The method is not *imported* but *won from within* — confirming phenomenology's self-referential, self-clarifying character (§63.¶4).

Hussserl draws the striking conclusion about the book itself: the *essential relatedness of phenomenology to itself* shows itself here in that *what is considered and established in the methodical reflection* (under the titles *clarity, insight, expression*, etc.) *itself belongs to the phenomenological domain* — that *all these reflexive analyses are phenomenological essence-analyses*, and the won methodological insights *themselves stand, as regards their establishment, under the norms they formulate*. In *new reflections* one must therefore be able *at any time* to convince oneself that the *states-of-affairs asserted in the methodological statements are to be given in perfect clarity*, that the *concepts used really faithfully adapt themselves to the given*, etc. The said *holds evidently for all methodological investigations relating to phenomenology*, however widely we span their frame; *and so it is understandable that this whole writing, which wants to prepare the way for phenomenology, is according to its content itself through and through phenomenology*. *The essential relatedness of phenomenology to itself shows itself here in that what is considered and established in the methodical reflection (under the titles clarity, insight, expression, and the like) itself belongs to the phenomenological domain — that all these reflexive analyses are phenomenological essence-analyses, and the won methodological insights themselves stand, as regards their establishment, under the norms they formulate.* *In new reflections one must therefore be able at any time to convince oneself that the states-of-affairs asserted in the methodological statements are to be given in perfect clarity, that the concepts used really faithfully adapt themselves to the given, etc.* *The said holds evidently for all methodological investigations relating to phenomenology, however widely we span their frame; and so it is understandable that this whole writing, which wants to prepare the way for phenomenology, is according to its content itself through and through phenomenology.* The remarkable *self-application* conclusion. Phenomenology's *self-relatedness* shows in that its *methodological reflections* (on clarity, insight, expression) are *themselves phenomenological essence-analyses* — and the methodological insights *stand under the very norms they formulate* (they must *themselves* be given in clarity, faithfully expressed, etc.). So the method is *self-applying*: the norms of phenomenological method *govern* the very statements *that formulate* those norms. One can *always check* (in new reflection) that the methodological claims *meet their own standards* (are clearly given, faithfully expressed). This is *full reflexive self-consistency* — phenomenology *practices on itself* what it preaches. The striking final claim: *this entire book*, which *prepares the way* for phenomenology, *is itself, in its content, through and through phenomenology*. So *Ideas I* is *not* a mere *external introduction* to phenomenology but is *itself phenomenological work* — the introduction *is* phenomenology (analyzing the reduction, the attitudes, the method, all *phenomenologically*). This is the deepest expression of phenomenology's *self-grounding* character: even its *propaedeutic* is *internal* to it. (This vindicates the book's procedure: it does not stand *outside* phenomenology looking in, but *enacts* phenomenology in clarifying it.)

### **§ 66\. Faithful Expression of Clear Givennesses. Unambiguous Terms**

Hussserl turns to the problem of *faithful expression*: let us pursue the *most general methodological thoughts* of the previous section *a stretch further*. In phenomenology — which wants to be *nothing other than essence-doctrine within pure intuition* — we therefore perform, *on exemplary givennesses of transcendentally-pure consciousness, immediate essence-beholdings* (*Wesenserschauungen*) and *fix them* \[conceptually\]... \[the sentence and section continue\]. *Let us pursue the most general methodological thoughts that emerged in the previous paragraph a stretch further.* *In phenomenology — which wants to be nothing other than essence-doctrine within pure intuition — we therefore perform, on exemplary givennesses of transcendentally-pure consciousness, immediate essence-beholdings and fix them* \[in faithful concepts and terms\]. The opening of §66, on *faithful conceptual-terminological expression* — the problem of *language* in phenomenology. Phenomenology *performs immediate essence-beholdings* (*Wesenserschauungen*) on *exemplary* pure-consciousness givennesses and must *fix* (*fixieren*) them *conceptually and terminologically* — i.e. *express* the beheld essences in *words*. The challenge (developed in the rest of §66, into File 16): the expressions must be *faithful* (drawing their sense *purely from the beheld*, §65.¶4) and the *terms unambiguous* (*eindeutig*) — a serious difficulty, since ordinary language is *vague, equivocal, and laden with natural-attitude meanings*, ill-suited to the *novel* essences of pure consciousness. Phenomenology must therefore *discipline its language* — forming *faithful, univocal terms* answering to *clearly-given* essences. This problem of *faithful expression of clear givennesses* (the section title) is the bridge from the *seeing* (essence-beholding) to the *saying* (conceptual-terminological fixing) — both essential to phenomenology as a *science* (which must *communicate* its intuitions in *fixed concepts*). \[The body of §66 — the requirements of clear givenness and unambiguous terminology, and the warnings against importing pre-formed meanings — continues on the following pages and will be rendered at the head of File 16, so that §66 is presented complete.\]

Hussserl describes how ordinary words acquire fixed phenomenological sense: we fix the beheld essences *conceptually and terminologically*. The words used may *stem from ordinary language*, be *many-meaninged*, *vague* according to their changing sense. *As soon as they "cover" themselves, in the manner of actual expression, with the intuitively-given*, they take on a *determinate sense as their hic et nunc actual and clear sense*; and *from here they can be scientifically fixed*. But *with the mere performance of applying the word in faithful adaptation to the intuitively-grasped essence, not everything is done* — even when, on the side of this intuitive grasping, the necessary is fully accomplished. *We fix them conceptually and terminologically.* *The words used may stem from ordinary language, be many-meaninged, vague according to their changing sense.* *As soon as they "cover" themselves, in the manner of actual expression, with the intuitively-given, they take on a determinate sense as their hic et nunc actual and clear sense; and from here they can be scientifically fixed.* *But with the mere performance of applying the word in faithful adaptation to the intuitively-grasped essence, not everything is done — even when, on the side of this intuitive grasping, the necessary is fully accomplished.* The mechanism of *faithful terminological fixing*. Phenomenology must *use ordinary words* (it avoids artificial coinages where possible — ¶3), and these are *vague and equivocal* in ordinary use. But when a word is *applied to an intuitively-given essence* — when it "*covers*" (*sich decken*) the beheld — it takes on, *here and now*, a *determinate, clear* sense (the sense *of just this beheld essence*). From this *act of covering* the word can be *scientifically fixed* (given a stable phenomenological meaning). So phenomenological terminology is won by *applying ordinary words to clearly-beheld essences* and *fixing* the resulting determinate sense. *But* — Hussserl immediately qualifies — *this faithful application is not yet enough* for *science*: more is required (the *preservation* and *univocal fixing* of the sense for *systematic*, *intersubjective* use — next paragraphs). The *intuitive grasping* (the seeing) and its *faithful expression* (the covering) are *necessary* but not *sufficient* for *scientific* knowledge, which needs *stable, communicable, systematically-usable* concepts.

Hussserl explains *why* faithful intuitive expression is not yet sufficient for science: *science is only possible where the thought-results are preservable in the form of knowledge and usable for further thinking* in the form of a *system of assertion-propositions* that are, *according to their logical sense, distinct* (*deutlich*), but *can be understood, or judgingly actualized, without clarity of the presentational underlayers, hence without insight*. Of course, science *demands at the same time subjective and objective precautions for the arbitrary (and indeed intersubjective) production of the pertinent groundings and actual insights*. *Science is only possible where the thought-results are preservable in the form of knowledge and usable for further thinking — in the form of a system of assertion-propositions that are, according to their logical sense, distinct, but can be understood, or judgingly actualized, without clarity of the presentational underlayers, hence without insight.* *Of course, science demands at the same time subjective and objective precautions for the arbitrary (and indeed intersubjective) production of the pertinent groundings and actual insights.* The crucial point about *science* requiring *detachable, communicable* propositions. A *science* requires that its results be *preserved* (as *knowledge*) and *usable for further thinking* — in a *system of propositions* that are *logically distinct* (*deutlich* — clear in their *logical articulation*, their meaning-structure) *even without* the *intuitive clarity* (the *insight*) that originally grounded them. I.e. one must be able to *understand* and *use* a phenomenological proposition (work with it logically, infer from it, build on it) *without* having, *at that moment*, the *full intuitive insight* into the essence it expresses. (Just as one can use a geometrical theorem without re-intuiting its proof each time.) This is the distinction between *distinctness* (*Deutlichkeit* — logical-articulative clarity of the proposition) and *clarity* (*Klarheit* — intuitive fullness of the underlying presentation). Science operates *largely* with *merely distinct* (not currently intuited) propositions — *but* it *demands* that the *insight* (the intuitive grounding) be *reproducible at will* (the "*subjective and objective precautions*" for *re-producing* the groundings, *intersubjectively*). So phenomenological propositions must be *detachable* from their original intuition (usable in merely-distinct form) *yet* always *re-groundable* in intuition (the insight re-producible at will, by anyone). This is what makes phenomenology a *science* (a system of communicable, reusable, re-verifiable propositions) and not merely a series of private intuitions. (The *Deutlichkeit/Klarheit* distinction — distinct vs. clear, articulated vs. intuitively-filled — becomes central in the analysis of judgment and reason, §§133ff.)

Hussserl states the requirement of *unambiguous terms*: to all this belongs also that *the same words and propositions receive unambiguous coordination to certain intuitively-graspable essences that make up their "fulfilling sense.*" On the basis of intuition and *well-practiced exemplary singular intuitions*, they are thus *equipped with distinct and unique meanings* (as it were *under "crossing-out" of the other meanings that habitually, under circumstances, force themselves forward*), in such a way that they *hold fast their thought-concepts in all possible connections of actual thinking* and *lose the adaptability to other intuitive givennesses with other fulfilling essences*. *To all this belongs now also that the same words and propositions receive unambiguous coordination to certain intuitively-graspable essences that make up their "fulfilling sense."* *On the basis of intuition and well-practiced exemplary singular intuitions, they are thus equipped with distinct and unique meanings (as it were under "crossing-out" of the other meanings that habitually, under circumstances, force themselves forward), in such a way that they hold fast their thought-concepts in all possible connections of actual thinking and lose the adaptability to other intuitive givennesses with other fulfilling essences.* The requirement of *univocity* (*Eindeutigkeit*) — the disciplining of terms. Phenomenological terms must be *unambiguously coordinated* to *determinate intuitable essences* (their "*fulfilling sense*" — *erfüllender Sinn*, the essence that *fulfills* the word-meaning in intuition). On the basis of *repeated, well-practiced exemplary intuitions*, the term is *equipped with a single, distinct meaning* — and the *other* (ordinary) meanings that "habitually force themselves forward" are, as it were, "*crossed out*" (*durchgestrichen*) — *suppressed* and *excluded*. The term thereby *holds fast* its *one* phenomenological concept *across all* contexts of thinking and *loses* its ordinary *adaptability* to *other* meanings/essences. So phenomenological terminology is *disciplined ordinary language*: ordinary words, *fixed* to *single* intuited essences, *stripped* of their equivocity. This is the *terminological labor* of phenomenology — forging *univocal* terms from *vague* words by *anchoring* them to *clearly-beheld* essences. (E.g. "perception," "reflection," "noema," "horizon" — each fixed to a determinate beheld essence, its ordinary equivocations crossed out.)

Hussserl closes §66 with the practical caution: *continually* — since, for *good reasons*, *artificial words foreign to the generally-valid languages are avoided as much as possible* — there is need, *over against the existing equivocities of common word-use*, for *caution and frequent re-examination* whether what was fixed in an earlier connection is *really applied in the same sense in a new one*. But *this is not the place to enter more closely into these and similar rules* (e.g. also into those that have reference to *science as a structure of intersubjective cooperation*). *Continually — since, for good reasons, artificial words foreign to the generally-valid languages are avoided as much as possible — there is need, over against the existing equivocities of common word-use, for caution and frequent re-examination whether what was fixed in an earlier connection is really applied in the same sense in a new one.* *But this is not the place to enter more closely into these and similar rules (e.g. also into those that have reference to science as a structure of intersubjective cooperation).* The closing practical caution of §66. Because phenomenology *avoids artificial jargon* (preferring *ordinary words*, for "*good reasons*" — accessibility, connection to the natural understanding), it must *constantly guard* against the *equivocity* of those ordinary words *creeping back in* — *re-examining* whether a term fixed earlier is *still used in the same sense* later. (The ever-present danger: an ordinary word, fixed to one essence, *slides back* toward its ordinary meaning in a new context.) Hussserl flags but *defers* the detailed *rules* of phenomenological terminology and of *science as intersubjective cooperation* (the social-communicative dimension of science — that results must be *communicable and re-verifiable by others*). So §66 establishes the *requirement* of faithful, univocal, intuition-anchored, re-verifiable terminology, while leaving its detailed rules aside. This completes the treatment of *expression*; §67 turns to the *clarity of the givennesses themselves* (the prior condition of faithful expression).

### **§ 67\. Method of Clarification. "Nearness to Givenness" and "Farness from Givenness"**

Hussserl turns from expression to the more fundamental issue of *clarity of the givennesses*: of *greater interest* to us are methodical considerations that relate, *instead of to the expression, to the essences and essence-connections to be expressed by it and beforehand to be grasped*. *If the researching gaze directs itself upon experiences, they will in general offer themselves in an emptiness and vague farness that makes them usable neither for a singular nor for an eidetic establishment.* It would stand *otherwise* if we, *interested instead of in them themselves rather in their mode of givenness*, wanted to *research the essence of the emptiness and vagueness itself* — which, for its part, *comes to givenness here not vaguely but in fullest clarity*. *Of greater interest to us are methodical considerations that relate, instead of to the expression, to the essences and essence-connections to be expressed by it and beforehand to be grasped.* *If the researching gaze directs itself upon experiences, they will in general offer themselves in an emptiness and vague farness that makes them usable neither for a singular nor for an eidetic establishment.* *It would stand otherwise if we, interested instead of in them themselves rather in their mode of givenness, wanted to research the essence of the emptiness and vagueness itself — which, for its part, comes to givenness here not vaguely but in fullest clarity.* The turn to the *method of clarification* (*Methode der Klärung*) — the prior condition of faithful expression. Before one can *faithfully express* an essence (§66), one must *clearly grasp* it — and *that* is often the difficulty: experiences, when one first turns to them reflectively, typically offer themselves in *emptiness and vague farness* (*Leerheit und vage Ferne*) — *unclear*, *indistinct*, *not fully intuitively given* — and so *unusable* for *either* singular *or* eidetic determination (one cannot reliably analyze or grasp the essence of a *vaguely*\-given experience). Hussserl notes a subtle exception: if one is interested *not* in the vague experience *itself* but in its *mode of givenness* (the *vagueness as such*), then the *vagueness* can be studied *clearly* (the *essence of vagueness* is itself a *clear* phenomenological theme — vagueness given *clearly as vagueness*). But for studying the *vague experience itself* (e.g. an unclear memory), the vagueness is a *defect* to be *overcome* — by *clarification* (bringing the vague to clarity). So the *method of clarification* is needed: a procedure for *bringing the vaguely-given to full intuitive clarity*, so that *valuable essence-intuition* can be performed on it. This is a *fundamental* methodological problem, since *most* experiences (especially in memory and phantasy, the materials of eidetic variation) are *initially unclear*.

Hussserl states the *correlation* of clarity between intuition, essence-grasping, and the grasped: but if the *vague conscious itself* — e.g. the *unclearly-hovering of memory or phantasy* — is to *yield up its own essences*, then *what it yields can only be an imperfect one*. That is: *where the singular intuitions underlying the essence-grasping are of lower clarity-level, there the essence-graspings are too*, and *correlatively the grasped is, in its sense, "unclear,"* it has its *blurrings, its outer and inner undividednesses*. It becomes *impossible, or only "in the rough" possible, to decide whether what is grasped here and there is the same (or the same essence) or a different one*; *it cannot be established what really lies in it as components*, and what the components — possibly already showing themselves in vague relief, waveringly indicating themselves — "*really are*." *But if the vague conscious itself — e.g. the unclearly-hovering of memory or phantasy — is to yield up its own essences, then what it yields can only be an imperfect one.* *That is: where the singular intuitions underlying the essence-grasping are of lower clarity-level, there the essence-graspings are too, and correlatively the grasped is, in its sense, "unclear," it has its blurrings, its outer and inner undividednesses.* *It becomes impossible, or only "in the rough" possible, to decide whether what is grasped here and there is the same (or the same essence) or a different one; it cannot be established what really lies in it as components, and what the components — possibly already showing themselves in vague relief, waveringly indicating themselves — "really are."* The *correlation-law of clarity*: the clarity of the *result* tracks the clarity of the *underlying intuition*. If the *singular intuitions* (the *examples* — the remembered or phantasied experiences) underlying the *essence-grasping* are *unclear* (low clarity-level), then the *essence-grasping* is *also* unclear, and *correlatively* the *grasped essence* is *unclear* in its *sense* — blurred, with *undivided* (indistinct) outer and inner moments. The *consequences* of such unclarity are severe for science: (i) one *cannot reliably decide identity/difference* (whether two grasped essences are *the same* or *different*); (ii) one *cannot establish the components* (what *really* belongs to the essence); (iii) one cannot determine what the *vaguely-indicated* components "*really are*." So *unclear* intuition yields *unscientific* (undecidable, indeterminate) results. This is *why* clarification is *essential*: *eidetic science* requires *clear* intuitions (to decide identity, analyze components, determine essences). The whole rigor of phenomenology depends on *first* bringing the examples to *clarity*. (This is especially pressing because the eidetic method works largely with *phantasy* examples — §70 — which are *notoriously unclear* and must be *clarified* before variation can yield determinate essences.)

Hussserl states the *task* of clarification: it is therefore a matter of *bringing what in each case hovers in flowing unclarity, in greater or lesser intuition-farness, to the normal nearness, to perfect clarity*, in order to practice on it correspondingly *valuable essence-intuitions* in which the *intended essences and essence-relations attain to perfect givenness*. *It is therefore a matter of bringing what in each case hovers in flowing unclarity, in greater or lesser intuition-farness, to the normal nearness, to perfect clarity, in order to practice on it correspondingly valuable essence-intuitions in which the intended essences and essence-relations attain to perfect givenness.* The statement of the *clarificatory task*. The work of clarification is to *bring* the unclear, intuition-*far* given (the vaguely-hovering memory/phantasy) to "*normal nearness*" and "*perfect clarity*" (*vollkommene Klarheit*) — so that, on the *clarified* example, *valuable essence-intuitions* can be performed, in which the *essences and essence-relations* come to *perfect givenness*. The spatial metaphor — "*nearness*" (*Nähe*) and "*farness*" (*Ferne*) of givenness — structures the whole section: the unclear is "far," the clear is "near"; clarification *brings the far near*. This is the *positive operation* of the method of clarification: a *deliberate bringing-to-clarity* of the example, prior to and enabling the essence-intuition. (Concretely: dwelling on a faint memory until it *fills out* and becomes vivid and determinate; or constructing a phantasy-example with full intuitive clarity rather than a vague schematic one.) The "nearness/farness" terminology (the section's subtitle) names the *clarity-dimension* of givenness — orthogonal to adumbration (§44.¶14) — along which clarification moves the given from far to near.

Hussserl establishes the *gradation* of clarity and its *upper limit* (absolute nearness): the essence-grasping accordingly has *itself its clarity-levels*, just as the *hovering singular* does. But there is, *for every essence* — as for the corresponding moment at the individual — so to speak an *absolute nearness*, in which its givenness is, *in respect of this step-series, an absolute one*, i.e. *pure self-givenness*. *The essence-grasping accordingly has itself its clarity-levels, just as the hovering singular does.* *But there is, for every essence — as for the corresponding moment at the individual — so to speak an absolute nearness, in which its givenness is, in respect of this step-series, an absolute one, i.e. pure self-givenness.* The *gradation* of clarity and its *limit*. *Clarity* comes in *degrees* (*Klarheitsstufen*) — both for the *singular* example *and* for the *essence-grasping* (and the grasped essence). And there is an *upper limit* of this gradation: an "*absolute nearness*" in which the essence reaches *pure self-givenness* (*reine Selbstgegebenheit*) — *absolute* clarity, the *fullest* intuitive presence (relative to the clarity-series). So clarification *moves toward* this limit of *absolute clarity / pure self-givenness*, where the essence is given *in person*, with *no* remaining vagueness. This *absolute nearness* is the *ideal terminus* of clarification — the state of *perfect intuitive givenness* in which *valuable* essence-intuition is possible (the essence given *itself*, completely clear). (Note: this *clarity*\-limit is *different* from the *adequacy*\-limit of thing-perception — a *thing* can never reach *absolute self-givenness* because of adumbration, §44; but an *essence* or an *immanent* moment *can* reach absolute clarity, since the eidetic and the immanent are not adumbrated. So *absolute self-givenness* is attainable in the *eidetic* and *immanent* spheres — which is precisely what makes *rigorous eidetic phenomenology* possible.)

**\[¶5 — "Das Gegenständliche ist nicht nur überhaupt als selbst 'vor dem Blicke stehend und als gegeben' bewußt…"\]** *(the characterization of pure self-givenness continues across the page-break)*

Hussserl begins to characterize *pure self-givenness* more closely: the objective is then *not only* in general conscious *as itself "standing before the gaze and as given"* \[but in a distinctive maximal mode of intuitive fullness — the characterization continuing on the next page\]. *The objective is then not only in general conscious as itself "standing before the gaze and as given"* \[but given in the maximal mode of clarity, with no admixture of emptiness or vagueness — pure self-givenness\]. The beginning of the closer characterization of *pure self-givenness* (*reine Selbstgegebenheit*) — the limit-state of clarification. In *pure self-givenness*, the object (essence or immanent moment) is given *not merely* as "*there before the gaze*" (which holds for *any* givenness, however unclear) but in the *maximal* mode (continued below).

Hussserl completes the characterization of pure self-givenness and its opposite pole: the objective is conscious *as purely-given self, wholly and entirely, as it is in itself*. *So far as a remainder of unclarity still remains, so far it shadows, in the self-"given," moments that thus do not reach into the light-circle of the purely-given.* In the case of *full unclarity*, the *counter-pole of full clarity*, *nothing at all has come to a givenness*; the consciousness is a *"dark" one, no longer at all intuiting, in the proper sense no longer "giving" at all*. *The objective is conscious as purely-given self, wholly and entirely, as it is in itself.* *So far as a remainder of unclarity still remains, so far it shadows, in the self-"given," moments that thus do not reach into the light-circle of the purely-given.* *In the case of full unclarity, the counter-pole of full clarity, nothing at all has come to a givenness; the consciousness is a "dark" one, no longer at all intuiting, in the proper sense no longer "giving" at all.* The completion of the clarity-spectrum with its *two poles*. *Pure self-givenness* (the upper pole): the object given *wholly, as it is in itself*, in the full "*light-circle*" (*Lichtkreis*) of clarity — *no* remaining unclarity. Any *residual unclarity* "*shadows*" certain moments (leaves them *outside* the light-circle, not purely given). The *opposite pole* — *full unclarity* (*volle Unklarheit*): here *nothing comes to givenness at all*; consciousness is *"dark,"* *no longer intuiting*, *no longer "giving"* (in the proper sense). So the clarity-spectrum runs from *full darkness* (nothing given) to *full clarity / pure self-givenness* (object wholly given as it is in itself). The light/shadow metaphor is precise: clarity is the "*light*" in which the object stands forth; unclarity is *shadow*; full unclarity is *darkness* (no light, nothing seen). Clarification *illuminates* — moves the object from shadow toward the full light-circle of pure self-givenness. This establishes the *clarity-dimension* as a continuum between two poles, along which the method of clarification operates.

Hussserl fixes the terminology: we have accordingly to say: *giving consciousness in the pregnant sense* and *intuitive* (over against *non-intuitive*), *clear* (over against *dark*) — *that coincides*. Likewise: *levels of givenness, of intuitiveness, of clarity*. The *zero-limit is darkness*, the *one-limit is full clarity, intuitiveness, givenness*. *We have accordingly to say: giving consciousness in the pregnant sense, and intuitive (over against non-intuitive), clear (over against dark) — that coincides.* *Likewise: levels of givenness, of intuitiveness, of clarity.* *The zero-limit is darkness, the one-limit is full clarity, intuitiveness, givenness.* The terminological *identification* of three coincident notions. *Giving consciousness* (in the pregnant sense), *intuitive* consciousness, and *clear* consciousness *coincide* (are the same) — as do their opposites (*non-giving \= non-intuitive \= dark*). And they admit *the same levels* (degrees of givenness \= degrees of intuitiveness \= degrees of clarity), running from the *zero-limit* (*darkness* — nothing given) to the *one-limit* (*full clarity \= full intuitiveness \= full givenness*). So "*clarity*," "*intuitiveness*," and "*givenness*" (in the pregnant sense) are *one* dimension, scaled 0 to 1\. This *fixes* the terminology of the clarity-analysis: to *clarify* is to *raise* the level of *intuitive givenness* toward the *one-limit* (pure self-givenness). The precise *coincidence* of clarity/intuitiveness/givenness is methodologically important: it means *clarification* (raising clarity) *is* the *raising of intuitive givenness* — the bringing of the object to *fuller intuitive presence*.

Hussserl carefully distinguishes *self-givenness* (clarity) from *originary* givenness (perception): *but givenness here is not to be understood as originary givenness, hence not as perceptual*. The "*self-given*" we do *not* identify with the "*originarily-given*," the "*bodily*." In the determinately-designated sense, "*given*" and "*self-given*" are *one and the same*, and the use of the *over-full expression* shall serve us *only* to *exclude* the *givenness in the wider sense* — in which, finally, of *every* presentational it is said that it is given in the presentation (but perhaps "*in empty manner*"). *But givenness here is not to be understood as originary givenness, hence not as perceptual.* *The "self-given" we do not identify with the "originarily-given," the "bodily."* *In the determinately-designated sense, "given" and "self-given" are one and the same, and the use of the over-full expression shall serve us only to exclude the givenness in the wider sense — in which, finally, of every presentational it is said that it is given in the presentation (but perhaps "in empty manner").* A crucial *disambiguation*: *self-givenness* (= *clarity*) ≠ *originary* givenness (= *bodily*, *perceptual* presence). This is subtle but important. *Clarity* (self-givenness) is a *separate dimension* from *originarity* (bodily presence, §43). A *phantasy* or *memory* can be *perfectly clear* (high self-givenness) *without* being *originary* (it does *not* give its object *bodily* — phantasy/memory *re-present*, do not *present*, §43). So "*self-given*" (clear) is *not* the same as "*originarily-given*" (bodily-perceived). Hussserl uses "*self-given*" (an "*over-full*" — pleonastic — expression) *only* to *exclude* the *empty* mode of givenness (the *merely-meant*, the *signitively-intended* — where an object is "given" in the presentation but *emptily*, with *no* intuitive filling). So the contrast is: *clear/intuitive (self-given)* vs. *empty (merely-meant)* — *not* *bodily (originary)* vs. *re-presented*. This means *clarification* applies to *phantasy* and *memory* (raising *their* clarity) just as to perception — which is *essential*, since the eidetic method works largely with *phantasy* (§70), which is non-originary but *can be made perfectly clear*. The independence of the *clarity*\-dimension from the *originarity*\-dimension is what allows *clear eidetic intuition* on *phantasy* examples.

Hussserl notes the *generality* of the clarity-determinations: our determinations hold further, as is *readily evident*, *for arbitrary intuitions, or empty-presentations* — hence also *without restriction in regard to the objectivities* — although here we are interested *only* in the *givenness-modes of experiences and their phenomenological (reell and intentional) stocks*. *Our determinations hold further, as is readily evident, for arbitrary intuitions, or empty-presentations — hence also without restriction in regard to the objectivities — although here we are interested only in the givenness-modes of experiences and their phenomenological (reell and intentional) stocks.* The *generality* of the clarity-analysis. The clarity/darkness gradation holds for *all* intuitions and presentations *whatever* their objects (things, essences, experiences) — it is a *universal* structure of consciousness. *But* in the present (phenomenological) context, Hussserl is interested *specifically* in the clarity-modes of *experiences* and their *phenomenological stocks* (the *reell* — hyletic/noetic — and *intentional* — noematic — components). I.e. the *method of clarification* is to be applied to bringing *experiences* (and their reell and intentional moments) to clarity, for the sake of *eidetic phenomenological analysis*. So the universal clarity-structure is here *applied* to the *phenomenological* field. (The reminder of the *reell/intentional* distinction — the hyletic-noetic vs. the noematic — anticipates the central analyses of §§85ff. and the noesis-noema doctrine.)

Hussserl closes §67 by distinguishing *givenness* from *being-grasped* (the attentional ray): with regard to *future analyses* it is also to be noted that the *essential of the state-of-affairs remains preserved* whether the *gaze of the pure I goes through the relevant consciousness-experience* — more distinctly, whether the *pure I turns to a "given" and possibly "grasps" it* — *or not*. Thus, e.g., "*perceptually given*" can — *instead of* meaning as much as "*perceived*" in the proper and normal sense of the *being-grasping* of this given — also *merely say* "*perception-ready*"; likewise "*phantasy-given*" need *not yet* say "*phantasizingly grasped*," and so in general, *also in regard to all clarity- or darkness-levels*. Let *reference be made in advance to the "readiness" to be discussed more closely later*, but at the same time it be *remarked that under the title givenness*, where nothing contrary is added or self-evident in the connection, *we co-understand the being-grasped, and in the case of essence-givenness the originary being-grasped*. *With regard to future analyses it is also to be noted that the essential of the state-of-affairs remains preserved whether the gaze of the pure I goes through the relevant consciousness-experience — more distinctly, whether the pure I turns to a "given" and possibly "grasps" it — or not.* *Thus, e.g., "perceptually given" can — instead of meaning as much as "perceived" in the proper and normal sense of the being-grasping of this given — also merely say "perception-ready"; likewise "phantasy-given" need not yet say "phantasizingly grasped," and so in general, also in regard to all clarity- or darkness-levels.* *Let reference be made in advance to the "readiness" to be discussed more closely later, but at the same time it be remarked that under the title givenness, where nothing contrary is added or self-evident in the connection, we co-understand the being-grasped, and in the case of essence-givenness the originary being-grasped.* The closing distinction of §67: *givenness* (clarity) vs. *being-grasped* (the *attentional ray*, §§35, 37). The clarity-structure is *independent* of whether the *pure I actually attends* (grasps) the given: an experience can be *clearly given* (in the background, "ready") *without* being *grasped* (attended). So "*perceptually given*" can mean *either* "*perceived*" (grasped) *or* merely "*perception-ready*" (clearly there but unattended — the *Bereitschaft*, "readiness," of §45). Likewise "*phantasy-given*" need not mean "*phantasizingly grasped*." The *clarity*\-level is *orthogonal* to the *grasped/ungrasped* distinction (one can have a clear-but-unattended or unclear-but-attended given). Hussserl flags "*readiness*" (*Bereitschaft*) for *later* discussion (the potentiality of attention, §§35, 84, 115). And he fixes a *terminological convention*: henceforth "*givenness*," *unless* otherwise indicated, *co-understands being-grasped* (attended) — and, for *essence*\-givenness, *originary* being-grasped (the essence *itself* intuited, not merely emptily meant). This convention matters for reading the subsequent analyses: "given" will normally mean "*grasped* and (for essences) *originarily* intuited." This closes §67's analysis of the clarity-dimension and its careful disambiguations (clarity ≠ originarity; givenness ≠ being-grasped), preparing §68's distinction of *genuine* from *non-genuine* clarity-levels.

### **§ 68\. Genuine and Non-Genuine Degrees of Clarity. The Essence of Normal Clarification**

Hussserl opens §68 by distinguishing *two kinds* of clarity-gradation: but our descriptions *still need continuation*. If we speak of *givenness- or clarity-levels*, we must *distinguish* between *genuine gradual levels of clarity* — to which one may also adjoin *gradual levels within darkness* — and *non-genuine clarity-levels*, namely *extensive enlargements of the compass of clarity*, possibly under simultaneous *intensive clarity-increase*. *But our descriptions still need continuation.* *If we speak of givenness- or clarity-levels, we must distinguish between genuine gradual levels of clarity — to which one may also adjoin gradual levels within darkness — and non-genuine clarity-levels, namely extensive enlargements of the compass of clarity, possibly under simultaneous intensive clarity-increase.* The crucial distinction of *two dimensions* of "clarity-increase." (i) *Genuine* (intensive) clarity-levels: the *degree of clarity* of an *already-given* moment (how *intensively* clear a given color or tone is) — a continuous *intensive* scale (from dark to fully clear), with *gradations even within darkness*. (ii) *Non-genuine* clarity-levels: *extensive enlargements of the compass* of clarity — i.e. *more* of the object *coming into* clarity (the *extent* of the clear field growing), possibly *together with* intensive increase. So "becoming clearer" is *ambiguous*: it can mean *either* (a) an *already-clear* moment becoming *more intensively* clear (genuine), *or* (b) *more moments* coming *into* the clear field (extensive — the clear *region* enlarging). These are *different operations*. The distinction matters for the *method of clarification* (§68's theme): clarification involves *both* raising the *intensity* of clarity (genuine) *and* enlarging the *extent* of what is clearly given (non-genuine/extensive) — bringing *more* of the object *into* clarity *and* making it *more intensively* clear. (The next paragraphs, into File 17, develop this via the example of a color given in varying clarity, and analyze *normal clarification* as the coordinated raising of intensity and extent toward pure self-givenness.) \[The body of §68 continues on the following pages and will be rendered at the head of File 17, so that §68 is presented complete.\]

Husserl finishes characterizing the genuine clarity-levels. A lower clarity-level "points toward" the fully clear given, but one must not read this pointing as a sign-relation, and not as a presentation through adumbration either. The lower level does not adumbrate the clear thing the way a sensation-moment adumbrates a thing-property. The graduated clarity-differences are differences of the givenness-mode itself, a kind all their own. He had just warned (with a back-reference to §44) against two misreadings. First misreading: that the unclear color is a sign for the clear color. Second: that the unclear color "presents" the clear one by adumbrating it, as a sensed color-datum adumbrates the objective color of a thing. Both are wrong. Clarity is not a second object pointing to a first, and it is not the adumbration-relation. Clarity is its own dimension of how something is given. The same color, the same essence, can be given more clearly or less clearly, and this more-or-less is not adumbration and not signification. The point matters because the whole method of clarification rests on clarity being an independent axis: one raises the clarity of a given without changing what is given and without invoking signs or profiles.

Husserl now describes the non-genuine (extensive) kind of clarity-increase. Here an apprehension that reaches beyond the intuitively given is woven together with the genuinely intuitive apprehension. Empty apprehensions are mixed in with intuitive ones. As clarification proceeds, more and more of what was merely emptily meant becomes intuitive, and conversely some of what was intuitive can lapse back into empty meaning. This is a different operation from raising intensity. In any actual experience, part of the object is genuinely intuited and part is only emptily co-meant (the horizon, §44). The non-genuine clarity-increase is the conversion of empty meaning into intuition: bringing the merely co-meant into actual intuitive presence. This enlarges the extent of what is intuitively there. It is "quasi-graduated" because it looks like a gradual increase, but it is really a matter of more of the object entering intuition, not of an already-intuited moment becoming more vivid.

Husserl identifies the two component processes of clarification. Making something clear to oneself involves two processes that combine. First, processes of intuitionalization (Veranschaulichung): bringing the merely emptily meant into intuition. Second, processes of raising the clarity of what is already intuitive. So clarification has both an extensive and an intensive side. The extensive side fills in the empty, co-meant parts, so that more of the object becomes intuitively present. The intensive side sharpens the parts already present, raising them toward full clarity. Real clarification almost always combines the two: one brings more of the object into view and at the same time makes what is in view more vivid.

Husserl states that this describes the essence of normal clarification. The rule is that pure intuitions are not simply on hand, and that pure empty presentations do not pass over all at once into pure intuitions. Instead, impure intuitions play the leading role, often as intermediate stages. An impure intuition brings its object to intuition on some sides or moments and merely empties-meaning it on others. This is the realistic picture of how clarification works. We rarely start from a wholly empty presentation or arrive at a wholly intuitive one in a single step. We start from a mixed (impure) intuition: some of the object is intuited, some only co-meant. Clarification then works on this mixture, gradually converting the empty parts into intuition and sharpening the intuitive parts, often passing through a series of intermediate impure intuitions. So "normal clarification" is a step-by-step transformation of impure intuitions toward the limit of pure, full self-givenness. This closes §68 and sets up §69, which applies the result to the grasping of essences.

### **§ 69\. The Method of Perfectly Clear Essence-Grasping**

Husserl states why perfect clarity matters. A perfectly clear grasp has the advantage that, by its nature, it permits absolutely indubitable identification and distinction, explication, relating, and so on. That is, it permits the insightful performance of all logical acts. This connects clarity (§§67–68) to the logical work of science (§66). Only on a perfectly clear given can one decide identity and difference with certainty, lay out the object's components (explication), and relate it to other objects. These are the basic "logical acts" on which all science rests. If the underlying intuition is unclear, these acts are uncertain (§67.¶2: one cannot decide whether two grasped essences are the same). So perfect clarity is the precondition of rigorous logical work. The acts of essence-grasping belong here too: the clarity-differences carry over to their objective correlates (the essences), and the methodological results about clarification carry over to achieving perfect essence-givenness.

Husserl notes that the method requires a step-by-step procedure, and that this method is a foundational piece of the method of eidetic science in general. He describes a typical case. The singular intuitions serving the essence-grasping may be clear enough to win some essential universal with full clarity, but not clear enough to reach as far as the guiding intention aims. Clarity is lacking on the side of the closer determinations of the essences that are interwoven with the one grasped. So one needs either to bring the exemplary singular cases nearer (clarify them further) or to procure new, better-fitting cases, ones on which the features intended only in confusion and darkness can stand out and then be brought to clearest givenness. The point is practical. Often one grasps the general essence (say, "perception") clearly enough, but the finer essential features one is after (some specific structure of perception) remain unclear, because the examples are not clear in that respect. The remedy is to clarify the examples further, or to find better examples in which the wanted feature shows up sharply. Then one can bring that feature to full clarity and grasp it. This is the working method of eidetic phenomenology: choose and clarify examples until the essential feature you are tracking stands out clearly enough to be grasped with certainty.

Husserl observes that a bringing-nearer already takes place within the sphere of darkness itself. What is darkly presented can come nearer to us in its own way. It finally "knocks at the gate of intuition," though it need not cross over into intuition, and perhaps cannot, because of psychological inhibitions. This is a fine descriptive point. Even before something becomes intuitive, there are degrees of nearness within the dark (non-intuitive) sphere. A dark presentation can grow more determinate, more "almost there," approaching the threshold of intuition. Husserl's image: it knocks at the gate of intuition. Sometimes it does not pass through, and sometimes it cannot, for psychological reasons (an inhibition blocks the memory or phantasy from becoming vivid).
