So clarification has work to do even below the threshold of intuition, bringing the dark nearer to that threshold.

Husserl notes the surrounding halo. What is given at any moment is mostly surrounded by a halo (Hof) of indeterminate determinability. This halo has its own manner of "unfolding" bringing-nearer, by spreading out into series of presentations. At first this may again be in the dark, then anew in the sphere of givenness, until what is intended steps into the sharply illuminated circle of perfect givenness. This applies the horizon-structure (§44) to the clarification of essences. Around any clearly given core there is a halo of further features that are determinable but not yet determined. Clarification can "unfold" this halo by running through series of presentations (varying and developing the example), bringing halo-features first out of darkness, then into givenness, and finally into the sharp circle of full clarity. So clarification is not only deepening a single given but unfolding its horizon through a series of connected presentations.

Husserl adds an important qualification. It would be saying too much to claim that all evidence of essence-grasping requires full clarity of the underlying singular cases in their full concreteness. To grasp the most general essential differences, such as the difference between color and tone, or between perception and will, it is enough to have the examples given at a low clarity-level. It is as though the most general feature, the genus (color in general, tone in general), were already fully given in them, but not yet the difference. Husserl calls this an "offensive" (jarring) way of speaking, but says he cannot avoid it, and asks the reader to make the situation present in living intuition. The qualification keeps the clarity-requirement from being overstated. Full clarity of the concrete example is needed for grasping fine essential features. But for the broadest distinctions (color versus tone, perception versus will), even unclear examples suffice. A dim memory of some color and some tone is enough to grasp that color and tone differ in genus. It is as if the genus is fully present even in the dim example, while only the specific difference would need clarity. Husserl admits the phrasing is awkward (the genus is not literally "given" while the difference is withheld), but the phenomenon is real: the level of clarity required depends on how fine the essential distinction is. Broad distinctions need little clarity; fine ones need much. This completes §69.

### **§ 70\. The Role of Perception in the Method of Essence-Clarification. The Privileged Position of Free Phantasy**

Husserl turns to some especially important features of the method of essence-grasping, beginning with a point already stressed at §4. It belongs to the general nature of immediately intuitive essence-grasping that it can be carried out on the basis of either perception or mere phantasy (re-presentation). This is the setup for the central methodological claim of §70: that for grasping essences, phantasy is not merely an acceptable substitute for perception but actually holds a privileged position. The footnote points back to §4, where Husserl first noted that essence-seeing does not require an actually existing instance, since an imagined instance serves just as well. He now develops the consequences.

Husserl completes the point and then states the general advantage of perception. Essence-grasping can be carried out on the basis of mere re-presentation of exemplary cases. And re-presentation, for instance phantasy, can be perfectly clear, so that it makes perfect essence-graspings and essence-insights possible (this was the result of §§67–68). In general, though, originary-giving perception has its advantages over all kinds of re-presentation, especially outer perception. This advantage is not as an experiencing act for existence-claims (those are not in question here, since they are bracketed), but as a basis for phenomenological essence-claims. Husserl is being fair to perception before arguing for phantasy. Perception's advantage is not that it certifies existence (irrelevant after the reduction), but that it offers especially clear and stable examples for essence-analysis.

Husserl details perception's advantage, using the example of anger. Outer perception has perfect clarity for all the objective moments that have actually come to givenness in it in the mode of originarity. With the help of reflection turned back on it, it also offers clear and stable singular cases for general essence-analyses, even for act-analyses. But now the contrast: anger may evaporate through reflection and quickly modify its content. Anger is not always ready the way perception is, and cannot be produced at any time by convenient experimental arrangements. To study anger reflectively in its originarity means studying an evaporating anger, which is not meaningless, but perhaps not what should be studied. Outer perception, by contrast, is much more accessible and does not evaporate through reflection. We can study its general essence, and the essence of its components and correlates, within originarity, without special efforts to produce clarity. This is the honest difficulty with using originary perception for inner (act) analyses. When you reflect on your own anger to study it, the reflection changes and dissipates the anger, so you end up studying a fading anger rather than full anger. Inner states are not reliably reproducible on demand. Outer perception is different: it is always available, easily repeated, and does not dissolve when you reflect on it. So for the phenomenology of outer perception itself, perception offers good, stable examples. But this advantage is limited to such cases.

Husserl sets aside a complication. One might say perceptions also have their clarity-differences, in cases of perceiving in the dark, in fog, and so on. He will not go into whether these differences are quite the same as the ones discussed before. It is enough that perception is not normally clouded, and that clear perception, of the kind needed, is always at our command. The point is simply that perception is normally clear and available, so it can serve as a ready source of clear examples where it applies.

This is the central claim of §70. If the advantages of originarity were methodologically very important, Husserl says, we would now have to consider where and how and to what extent originarity is realizable in the various kinds of experience. But we can set all that aside. There are reasons why, in phenomenology as in all eidetic sciences, re-presentations, and more precisely free phantasies, win a privileged position over perceptions. This holds even in the phenomenology of perception itself, though not for the phenomenology of sensation-data (the hyletic data), which are excluded from this privilege. So despite perception's local advantages, free phantasy is privileged overall, for reasons given in the following paragraphs (the geometer analogy and the freedom of variation). The one exception Husserl flags: the sensation-data (hyle). For studying the hyletic data themselves, actual perception is needed, since phantasied data are not the genuine impressional data. But for the rest, including the act-structures of perception, free phantasy is the better instrument.

Husserl gives the geometer analogy, which carries the argument. The geometer, in his researching thought, operates incomparably more in phantasy than in perception of the figure or the model. This holds even for the "pure" geometer, the one who renounces the algebraic method. In phantasy he must, admittedly, work to get clear intuitions, which the drawing and the model spare him. But in actual drawing and modeling he is bound, whereas in phantasy he has incomparable freedom in arbitrarily reshaping the invented figures, in running through continuously modified possible shapes, in generating an endless number of new formations. This freedom first opens up access to the vast reaches of essential possibilities, with their infinite horizons of essential knowledge. The analogy is decisive. A geometer thinking about triangles does not stare at a drawn triangle. He imagines triangles and freely varies them: stretching, tilting, deforming, running through endless possible shapes. A drawn figure is fixed and limits him to one case. Phantasy lets him survey the whole range of possible figures, and only by surveying that range can he grasp what holds for triangles as such. The cost is that imagined figures need effort to keep clear (a drawing holds itself clear for you). But the gain in freedom outweighs this, because freedom of variation is exactly what opens access to the realm of essential possibilities.

Husserl notes how drawings actually function in geometry. The drawings normally follow after the phantasy-constructions and the eidetically pure thinking carried out on their basis. Drawings serve mainly to fix stages of the process already carried out in phantasy, and thereby to make it easier to re-present that process again. Even where one "thinks further" with an eye on the figure, the new thought-processes, as to their sensuous basis, are phantasy-processes, whose results the new lines on the figure fix. So the drawing is secondary. The real work happens in phantasy. The drawing records results, so they can be recalled and shared. This confirms that even in geometry, the science usually taken as the model of working with figures, phantasy does the productive work and the drawing only records it.

Husserl transfers the lesson to phenomenology. For the phenomenologist, who deals with reduced experiences and their essentially belonging correlates, the matter is in the main no different. The phenomenological essence-formations are also infinitely many. He too can make only limited use of the aid of originary givenness. He does have at his free command, in originary givenness, all the main types of perceptions and re-presentations, namely as perceptual exemplifications for a phenomenology of perception, of phantasy, and so on. So like the geometer, the phenomenologist faces an infinite range of possible essential formations, and perception can supply only a limited stock of actual examples. Perception gives him good examples of the main act-types (an actual perceiving for the phenomenology of perception, an actual remembering for the phenomenology of memory, and so on). But to survey the full range of possible forms of consciousness, he must, like the geometer, turn to free phantasy and its freedom of variation. The remainder of §70 — including the famous statement that fiction is the vital element of phenomenology and the source from which the knowledge of "eternal truths" draws its nourishment — continues on the next page and will be presented at the head of File 18, so that §70 is given whole.

He then balances the claim. It is of course important (again as in geometry, which lately, not for nothing, places great value on collections of models) to exercise phantasy richly in the perfect clarification required here, and in the free reshaping of phantasy-data. But it is also important to fertilize phantasy beforehand through observation in originary intuition, as rich and good as possible. This fertilizing does not mean that experience as such has any validity-grounding function. Husserl guards against a misreading. Phantasy is the working medium, but it should be fed by good observation. Watching real cases enriches and sharpens the phantasy one will then vary freely. Crucially, this feeding role does not give experience any grounding force: the essential insight is still grounded in the eidetic seeing, not in the existence of the observed cases. Observation nourishes phantasy; it does not certify the result.

He adds a striking source of nourishment. Extraordinarily much profit is to be drawn from what history offers, and in still richer measure from what art offers, especially poetry. These are imaginings, but in the originality of their new formations, the fullness of their detail, and the seamlessness of their motivation, they tower high above the products of our own phantasy. Moreover, through the suggestive power of artistic means of presentation, they convert themselves, for one who grasps them understandingly, with special ease into perfectly clear phantasies. So art and poetry are practical aids to phenomenology. A great writer constructs richer, more coherent, more vivid imaginative cases than we could invent on our own, and these cases pass readily into the clear phantasies that eidetic analysis needs. Literature is a reservoir of well-made examples of consciousness.

This leads to the famous statement. One can really say, if one likes paradoxical talk, and can say it in strict truth if one understands the ambiguous sense rightly, that "fiction" makes up the vital element of phenomenology as of all eidetic science, that fiction is the source from which the knowledge of "eternal truths" draws its nourishment. A footnote remarks, with dry self-awareness, that this is a sentence especially suited to be quoted in order to mock the eidetic mode of knowledge in naturalistic fashion. The claim, rightly understood, is not provocative but exact. Eidetic science studies what is essentially possible, not what actually exists. Its examples therefore need not exist; freely invented (fictional) examples serve as well or better. Since the freedom to invent and vary cases is what opens the field of essential possibilities, fiction is the working medium of all essential knowledge. The "eternal truths" (the necessary essential truths) are drawn out by varying fictional cases. Husserl notes, with a touch of irony, that the sentence is easy to misquote: a naturalist can wave it about as if Husserl admitted phenomenology is mere fantasy. Understood correctly, it says the opposite. Fiction is the medium precisely because essential truth is independent of actual existence.

### § 71\. The Problem of the Possibility of a Descriptive Eidetics of Experiences

Husserl raises a basic methodological worry. He has repeatedly called phenomenology a descriptive science. Now a fundamental methodical question arises, a reservation that holds us back just as we are eager to enter the new field. Is it correct to set phenomenology the goals of mere description? Is a descriptive eidetics not something perverse, something wrong-headed in principle? The worry is sharp. The model eidetic sciences (geometry, arithmetic) are not descriptive but deductive and exact. So calling phenomenology a merely descriptive eidetic science can look like a contradiction, as if one wanted an essential science that renounces the very rigor that makes the known essential sciences rigorous.

He locates the source of the worry. The motives for such questions lie near for all of us. Anyone who feels his way into a new eidetics, asking what researches are possible here, what starting points to take, what methods to follow, involuntarily looks to the old, highly developed eidetic disciplines, the mathematical ones, especially geometry and arithmetic. But we notice at once that these disciplines cannot be called to guide us in our case, that in them the situation must lie essentially otherwise. For someone who has not yet come to know a single piece of genuine phenomenological essence-analysis, there is some danger here of becoming doubtful about the very possibility of a phenomenology. The natural move, when founding a new essential science, is to imitate the existing ones, which are mathematical. But mathematics is the wrong model for phenomenology, and Husserl will argue that the two differ in basic type. Someone who has never seen actual phenomenological analysis, and who assumes all eidetic science must be mathematical, may conclude that phenomenology is impossible.

He explains why the mathematical model misleads. Since the mathematical disciplines are at present the only ones that can effectively represent the idea of a scientific eidetics, the thought stays remote that there could be eidetic disciplines of another kind, non-mathematical ones, fundamentally different in their whole theoretical type from the familiar ones. So if one has been won over, by general considerations, to the postulate of a phenomenological eidetics, then the attempt to establish something like a "mathematics of phenomena," which immediately goes wrong, may tempt one to give up the idea of a phenomenology altogether. But that would be all the more wrong. The diagnosis: people assume "eidetic science" means "mathematical science," because mathematics is the only developed example. So they try to build phenomenology as a "mathematics of phenomena," it fails (because phenomena do not admit that treatment), and they wrongly conclude that phenomenology is impossible. The error is in the assumption, not in phenomenology.

He states the task that resolves the worry. Let us make clear to ourselves, in the most general terms, what is peculiar to mathematical disciplines in contrast to an essence-doctrine of experiences, and thereby make clear what sorts of goals and methods are supposed to be in principle inappropriate in the sphere of experiences. The resolution is to distinguish two types of eidetic science. Mathematics is one type (exact, deductive, "mathematical"). Phenomenology is another type (descriptive, intuitive, "morphological"). Once the difference in type is clear, the worry dissolves: phenomenology is not a failed mathematics but a different and legitimate kind of eidetic science, for which description, not deduction, is the appropriate method. The detailed distinction is carried out in §§72–74.

### § 72\. Concrete, Abstract, and "Mathematical" Essence-Sciences

Husserl begins the distinction from the division of essences and essence-sciences into material and formal. He starts from the distinction already established (§§9–10): material versus formal essences and the sciences answering to them. The formal essences and the whole body of the formal mathematical disciplines can be set aside, since phenomenology clearly belongs among the material eidetic sciences. If the analogy with mathematics is to guide us methodically at all, it will work most strongly if we restrict ourselves to material mathematical disciplines, such as geometry, and so ask more specifically: must a phenomenology be constituted as a "geometry" of experiences, or can it be? So Husserl sharpens the question of §71. The relevant comparison is not with formal mathematics (algebra, formal logic) but with a material mathematical science, geometry, which is about a particular region (space). The question becomes precise: is phenomenology a "geometry of lived-experiences"?

To answer, Husserl recalls some points from the general theory of science, with a back-reference to §§12, 15, 16\. Every theoretical science binds an ideally closed totality through its relation to a domain of knowledge, and that domain is determined by a highest genus. A radical unity is gained only by going back to the simply highest genus, that is, to the region and the regional generic components: the highest genera that unite, and possibly found one another, within the regional genus. The structure of the highest concrete genus (the region) out of partly disjoint, partly mutually founded highest genera corresponds to the structure of the concreta belonging to it out of partly disjoint, partly mutually founded lowest differences. In the thing, for example, these are temporal, spatial, and material determinateness. The framework is that of §§15–16: a region is the highest concrete genus; under it stand highest genera (some independent, some founded on others), and these answer to the moments of the region's concreta. A material thing, for instance, has temporal, spatial, and material structure, each tracing to a highest genus within the region "thing."

He continues with the architecture of the sciences. To each region corresponds a regional ontology, with a series of self-contained, possibly mutually resting regional sciences, answering to the highest genera that have their unity in the region. To subordinate genera correspond mere disciplines or so-called theories: for example, to the genus "conic section" the discipline of conic sections. Such a discipline has, understandably, no full independence, since in its knowledge and its groundings it must draw on the whole foundation of essential knowledge that has its unity in the highest genus. So the hierarchy of genera generates a hierarchy of sciences. A region grounds a regional ontology, which contains several regional sciences (answering to the highest genera in the region). Lower genera ground mere "disciplines" or "theories" within a science (the theory of conic sections within geometry), and these are not independent, since they rest on the whole science above them.

He then introduces the concrete/abstract distinction among sciences. Depending on whether the highest genera are regional (concrete) or merely components of such genera, the sciences are concrete or abstract. This division corresponds to that between concrete and abstract genera in general (back-reference to §15). To the domain belong, in the one case, concrete objects, as in the eidetics of nature; in the other case, abstract objects, such as spatial shapes, time-shapes, and motion-shapes. The essential relation of all abstract genera to concrete ones, and ultimately to regional ones, gives all abstract disciplines and full sciences an essential relation to the concrete, the regional. So sciences divide into concrete (about whole concreta, like the eidetics of nature) and abstract (about a single abstract moment, like geometry, which is about spatial shape alone). Geometry is abstract in this sense: it isolates the spatial moment of things and studies it on its own. And every abstract science ultimately refers back to the concrete region from which its abstract moment is drawn.

He notes a parallel on the side of the empirical sciences. A division of the eidetic sciences runs exactly parallel to a division of the experiential sciences, which again separate according to regions. There is, for example, one physical natural science, and the individual natural sciences are really mere disciplines: the powerful stock of laws, eidetic and empirical, that belong to physical nature in general, before all separation into special spheres, gives them their unity. Moreover, different regions can prove to be linked by empirical regularities, as the region of the physical and that of the psychical are. The architecture of regions, genera, and disciplines holds for empirical sciences too. The point about the physical and psychical being empirically linked recalls the psychophysical conditioning discussed earlier (§§53–54).

Now comes the decisive observation about the known eidetic sciences. Looking at them, it strikes us that they do not proceed descriptively. Geometry, for example, does not grasp the lowest eidetic differences (the countless spatial shapes that can be drawn in space) in singular intuitions, describe them, and order them by classification, the way the descriptive natural sciences do with empirical natural formations. Instead, geometry fixes a few kinds of basic formations, the ideas body, surface, point, angle, and the like, the same ones that play the determining role in the axioms. With the help of the axioms, that is, the primitive essential laws, geometry is then in a position to derive purely deductively all the shapes that "exist" in space (that is, are ideally possible) and all the essential relations belonging to them, in the form of exactly determining concepts, which stand in for essences that in general remain foreign to our intuition. This is the heart of the contrast. A descriptive science would survey its endless particular cases one by one, describe them, and sort them into classes. Geometry does not do this. It fixes a few basic ideas and a few axioms, and from these it deduces everything about all possible spatial shapes. It never needs to intuit or describe the endless individual figures, because the axioms already control them all in advance. And its concepts are "exact": they determine essences that we cannot, in general, intuit at all (no one can intuit a thousand-sided figure, but geometry determines it exactly through concepts).

He explains why geometry can work this way. Such is the generic essence of the geometrical domain, such the pure essence of space, that geometry can be wholly certain of really mastering, by its method, all possibilities, and mastering them exactly. In other words, the manifold of spatial shapes in general has a remarkable logical fundamental property, for which Husserl introduces a name on the following page: that of a "definite" manifold, a mathematical manifold in the strict sense. The key is that space has a special property: its whole range of possible shapes is exhaustively governed by a finite set of axioms. Because of this, a few axioms suffice to determine everything, deductively and exactly, with no remainder left to intuition or description. The name for this property — the "definite" or mathematical manifold — and its precise meaning are developed at the start of §72's continuation, which falls on the next page and will be presented at the head of File 19, so that the account of the "definite manifold" is given whole.

Husserl now names the special property of space that makes geometry possible. The manifold of spatial shapes in general has a remarkable logical fundamental property, for which he introduces the name "definite manifold" or "mathematical manifold in the pregnant sense." It is characterized by this: that a finite number of concepts and propositions, drawn as the case may be from the essence of the domain in question, completely and unambiguously determines the totality of all possible formations of the domain, in the manner of pure analytic necessity, so that in principle nothing more remains open. So a definite manifold is one whose entire range of possibilities is fixed in advance by finitely many axioms. From the axioms, everything about the domain follows by purely analytic (logical) deduction. Nothing is left undetermined; every question about the domain has, in principle, an answer derivable from the axioms. Space is such a manifold, which is why geometry can master all spatial possibilities deductively and exactly.

He restates the property in terms of definability. One can also say that such a manifold has the distinguished property of being "mathematically exhaustively definable." The "definition" lies in the system of axiomatic concepts and axioms, and the "mathematically exhaustive" character lies in this: that the defining assertions imply, with respect to the manifold, the greatest conceivable prejudgment — nothing remains undetermined. The system of axioms does not merely describe the domain partially; it "prejudges" everything about it. Every possible fact about the domain is already settled by the axioms. This is what "exhaustive definition" means: the axioms leave no open questions.

He gives an equivalent formulation in terms of truth and consequence. An equivalent of the concept of a definite manifold lies in the following propositions. Every proposition that can be formed from the distinguished axiomatic concepts, in whatever logical forms, is either a pure formal-logical consequence of the axioms, or a formal-logical counter-consequence, that is, it formally contradicts the axioms, so that its contradictory opposite is then a formal-logical consequence of the axioms. In a mathematically definite manifold, the concepts "true" and "formal-logical consequence of the axioms" are equivalent, and likewise the concepts "false" and "formal-logical counter-consequence of the axioms." This is a completeness condition. In a definite manifold, every well-formed proposition about the domain is decided by the axioms: it either follows from them (true) or contradicts them (false). There is no proposition that is neither provable nor refutable from the axioms. So "true" coincides with "derivable from the axioms." This is a strong logical property, and Husserl notes (in the footnote) its closeness to Hilbert's completeness axiom.

He fixes the terminology and notes the footnote. An axiom-system that "exhaustively defines" a manifold in this purely analytic way he also calls a definite axiom-system. Every deductive discipline resting on such a system is a definite discipline, or a discipline mathematical in the pregnant sense. The footnote is substantial and historically important. Husserl notes that these concepts served him already in the early 1890s, in investigations meant as a continuation of his Philosophy of Arithmetic, mainly to find a principled solution to the problem of the imaginary (how operations with "impossible" numbers, like the imaginary numbers, can be justified). He developed the relevant concepts and theories in lectures, treated them in a double lecture to the Göttingen Mathematical Society in winter 1901/02, and notes that some of these ideas had entered the literature without his being named as the source. He remarks that the close relation of his concept of definiteness to Hilbert's completeness axiom (introduced for the foundation of arithmetic) will be evident to any mathematician. The historical note shows that the notion of a definite manifold is not borrowed from Hilbert but developed independently and earlier by Husserl, in the context of justifying the imaginary numbers, and that it is mathematically akin to Hilbert's completeness axiom.

He closes §72 by noting the move to pure forms. The definitions remain entirely in force if we let the material particularity of the manifold become wholly undetermined, that is, if we carry out the formalizing generalization. The axiom-system then turns into a system of axiom-forms, the manifold into a manifold-form, and the discipline relating to the manifold into a discipline-form. So the whole apparatus can be lifted from any particular (material) manifold to the purely formal level. At that level one has the formal theory of manifolds (Mannigfaltigkeitslehre), the highest part of formal mathematics, which studies manifold-forms in general. This is the theory of deductive systems as such, the instrument of all exact deductive science. With this, §72 has fully specified what a "mathematical" (definite, deductive, exact) discipline is. §73 now asks whether phenomenology is such a discipline.

### § 73\. Application to the Problem of Phenomenology. Description and Exact Determination

Husserl applies the result to phenomenology. How does phenomenology stand in comparison with geometry, as the representative of a material mathematics in general? It is clear that phenomenology belongs among the concrete eidetic disciplines. Its extension is made up of experience-essences, and these are not abstracta but concreta. So phenomenology differs from geometry already in being concrete, not abstract. Geometry studies an abstract moment (spatial shape) in isolation. Phenomenology studies whole experiences, which are concreta. An experience (a perception, a judgment) is a concrete whole, not an isolated abstract moment.

He poses the precise question. These concreta have, as such, various abstract moments, and the question now is: do the highest genera belonging to these abstract moments here too form domains for definite disciplines, for "mathematical" disciplines on the model of geometry? Must we here too search for a definite axiom-system and build deductive theories on it? Must we here too look for "basic formations" and derive from them all the other essential formations of the domain and their essential determinations constructively, that is, deductively, by consistent application of the axioms? An experience has abstract moments too. The question is whether the highest genera of those moments form definite (mathematical) manifolds, so that phenomenology could be built like geometry: a few basic ideas, a few axioms, and everything else deduced. This is the question of whether there can be a "mathematics of phenomena" (raised at §71).

He notes an important feature of deductive derivation. To the essence of such deriving it belongs, and this too is to be noted, that it is a mediate logical determining, whose results, even when they are "drawn" on the figure, cannot in principle be grasped in immediate intuition. So deductive science works with results that are not intuitable. The geometer's exactly determined thousand-sided figure cannot be intuited; it is reached only through mediate logical construction. Deduction reaches its objects through chains of inference, not through direct seeing. This is a defining feature of the exact, mathematical type, and it is exactly what will turn out to be inappropriate for phenomenology, which is bound to immediate intuition (§§65, 67).

He restates the question in correlative terms, beginning a sentence that continues on the next page. The question can also be put in words, at the same time in a correlative turn: is the stream of consciousness... \[a definite manifold, governed by a definite axiom-system, so that a "mathematics of consciousness" would be possible?\]. The correlative formulation asks about the stream of consciousness itself: is it a definite manifold, like space? Husserl completes the question and points toward the answer.

Husserl finishes the correlative question and compares the stream with physical nature. Is the stream of consciousness a genuine mathematical manifold? Does it resemble, taken in its facticity, physical nature, which (if the ideal ultimately guiding the physicists is valid and grasped in a strict concept) would have to be called a concrete definite manifold? So the question is sharpened by comparison with physics. Physics aims at an ideal in which nature would be a definite manifold, fully governed by exact laws. Husserl asks whether consciousness is like that. The answer, developed in §74, is that it is not: consciousness is not exhaustively governed by exact, axiomatizable laws, because its essences are not exact but morphological.

He flags the deep science-theoretic problem behind this. It is a highly significant problem of the theory of science to become wholly clear about all the principial questions here, that is, after fixing the concept of the definite manifold, to weigh the necessary conditions a materially determined domain must satisfy if it is to correspond to this idea. One condition is the exactness of concept-formation, which is by no means a matter of our free choice and logical art, but, with respect to the pretended axiomatic concepts (which must after all be exhibitable in immediate intuition), presupposes exactness in the grasped essences themselves. But how far "exact" essences are to be found in an essence-domain, and whether exact essences can be substructed under all essences graspable in actual intuition (and so under all essence-components), depends entirely on the peculiarity of the domain. This is the crucial point. A domain can be treated mathematically (axiomatically, exactly) only if its essences are themselves exact. Exact concepts are not something we can impose by logical skill; they require that the essences they capture be exact. And whether a domain has exact essences depends on the domain itself. Space has exact essences (the ideal limit-shapes). Whether consciousness does is the open question, answered negatively in §74.

He locates the problem in a wider context and defers full treatment. The problem just touched is intimately interwoven with the fundamental and still unsolved problems of a principial clarification of the relation between "description" with its "descriptive concepts," and "unambiguous," "exact determination" with its "ideal concepts"; and, parallel to this, of a clarification of the still so little understood relation between "describing sciences" and "explaining" ones. An attempt bearing on this is to be communicated in the continuation of these investigations. Here we may not hold up the main line of our considerations too long, and we are also not prepared enough to treat such questions exhaustively now. Let it suffice to indicate a few points in a general way. So Husserl flags two large, unsolved problems: the relation between descriptive and exact concepts, and the relation between describing and explaining sciences. He defers full treatment (to the projected continuation, Book Two and Three) and gives only the points needed here. The contrast of describing and explaining sciences (later central to the human-sciences debate and to the Krisis) is noted but not developed.

### § 74\. Descriptive and Exact Sciences

Husserl draws the contrast from geometry versus descriptive natural science. The geometer is not interested in the factual, sensuously intuitive shapes, as the descriptive natural scientist is. He does not, like the latter, form morphological concepts of vague shape-types, which are grasped directly on the basis of sensuous intuition and fixed conceptually and terminologically, vague as they are. This is the key distinction. The descriptive natural scientist (the botanist, the zoologist) forms morphological concepts: concepts of vague but recognizable shape-types (leaf-shapes, body-types), grasped directly in intuition and named while still vague. The geometer does not. He works with exact ideal concepts, not vague morphological ones.

He defends the vagueness of morphological concepts. The vagueness of these concepts, the circumstance that they have fluid spheres of application, is no defect to be charged against them. For the sphere of knowledge they serve, they are simply indispensable, indeed they are the only legitimate ones there. If the task is to bring the intuitive thing-givennesses, in their intuitively given essential characters, to adequate conceptual expression, then it means precisely taking them as they give themselves. And they give themselves precisely as fluid, and typical essences can be grasped in them only in immediately analyzing essence-intuition. This is a strong and important claim. Vagueness is not a flaw in morphological concepts; it is exactly what fits them to their objects. Intuitively given shapes are themselves fluid and typical (not sharply bounded). To describe them faithfully, one must use concepts that are themselves fluid and typical. A sharp, exact concept would falsify them. So for the descriptive sphere, vague (morphological) concepts are not just acceptable but the only correct ones.

He drives the point home with the botanist's vocabulary. The most perfect geometry and the most perfect practical mastery of it cannot help the descriptive natural scientist to express precisely what he expresses in such simple, understandable, wholly adequate ways with the words notched, indented, lens-shaped, umbel-shaped, and the like — all of them concepts that are essentially and not accidentally inexact, and therefore also unmathematical. The example is decisive. "Lens-shaped" is a perfectly good, fully adequate descriptive concept. No amount of geometry can replace it with an exact concept, because the thing it describes is itself a vague type, not an exact figure. And the inexactness is essential, not a temporary failing: these concepts are inexact by their very nature, because their objects are. So they are unmathematical in principle, not just undeveloped.

He distinguishes ideal concepts from descriptive concepts, and introduces the Kantian Idea. The geometrical concepts are ideal concepts; they express something one cannot "see." Their origin, and so also their content, is essentially other than that of the descriptive concepts, as concepts that express essences drawn immediately from simple intuition, and not "ideals." Exact concepts have their correlates in essences that have the character of "Ideas in the Kantian sense." Over against these Ideas or ideal essences stand the morphological essences, as correlates of the descriptive concepts. This fixes the two kinds of essence. Exact (ideal) essences are "Ideas in the Kantian sense": ideal limits one can never intuit, only approach (the exact circle, the exact straight line). Morphological essences are the vague, intuitable types ("lens-shaped"). The exact concepts express the former, the descriptive concepts the latter. The phrase "Idea in the Kantian sense" recurs at §143 (the thing as a Kantian Idea), and names an ideal that regulates an endless approximation without ever being given.

He distinguishes the two operations that yield these two kinds of essence, beginning a sentence completed on the next page. The ideation that yields the ideal essences as "ideal limits," which in principle cannot be found in any sensuous intuition, and which morphological essences more or less "approach" without ever reaching, is something fundamentally other than essence-grasping through simple "abstraction," in which a salient "moment" is raised into the region of essences as something in principle vague, as something typical. So there are two different acts. One act ("idealization," producing Ideas in the Kantian sense) yields exact ideal limits that no intuition contains, only approximates. The other act (simple abstraction) yields the vague, typical morphological essences directly from intuition. Geometry rests on the first; descriptive science on the second. Phenomenology, as §75 will state, rests on the second: it grasps morphological essences of experiences by simple essence-intuition, not ideal limits by idealization. The completion of this paragraph (on the firmness and clean distinguishability proper to morphological grasping) and the close of §74 fall on the next page and will be presented at the head of File 20\.

Husserl finishes the paragraph distinguishing the two kinds of grasping and draws the closing conclusion of the section. The firmness and clean distinguishability of the generic concepts, or generic essences, that have their extension in the fluid, must not be confused with the exactness of the ideal concepts and the genera that have something purely ideal in their extension. The point guards against a likely error. Morphological (descriptive) concepts can be firm and clearly distinguished from one another even though they are vague. "Lens-shaped" and "kidney-shaped" are clearly different concepts, each securely applicable, yet each has a fluid range. This firmness is not the same as the exactness of geometrical concepts. A descriptive concept can be both stably usable and inexact. So one must not assume that, because a concept is well-defined and reliably applied, it must be exact. There are two kinds of well-functioning concepts, the exact and the descriptive, and only the first is mathematical.

He states the relation between the two kinds of science. It is then further to be seen that exact and purely descriptive sciences do indeed combine, but that they can never stand in for one another, and that no development of exact science, however far it reaches, that is, of a science operating with ideal substructions, can solve the original and legitimate tasks of pure description. This is the decisive conclusion. Exact and descriptive sciences can cooperate (a descriptive science may use exact results, and an exact science rests on descriptions of its starting points). But neither can replace the other. No matter how far one develops an exact, idealizing science, it cannot do the work of description. Describing the intuitively given, in its own fluid character, is a task that idealization can never take over. This secures a permanent and independent place for descriptive science, and so for phenomenology.

### § 75\. Phenomenology as a Descriptive Eidetic Doctrine of the Pure Experiences

Husserl now applies the result directly. As for phenomenology, it wants to be a descriptive eidetic doctrine of the transcendentally pure experiences in the phenomenological attitude, and like every descriptive, non-substructing and non-idealizing discipline, it has its right in itself. Whatever can be grasped eidetically in pure intuition on reduced experiences, whether as a reell component or as an intentional correlate, is its own, and this is for it a great source of absolute knowledge. So phenomenology is settled to be a descriptive, not a mathematical, eidetic science. It grasps, in pure intuition, the essences of reduced experiences, both their reell (hyletic-noetic) components and their intentional (noematic) correlates. The mention of "reell component or intentional correlate" again signals the two great headings of the analyses to come (the noesis and the noema, §§85ff.).

He turns to how scientific description is possible in this fluid field. He looks more closely at how, on the phenomenological field with its countless eidetic concreta, genuinely scientific descriptions can be established, and what they can accomplish. The worry is that the field is so fluid that nothing fixed can be said. Husserl answers by distinguishing levels of generality.

He states the source of the difficulty. It lies in the peculiarity of consciousness in general to be a fluctuating that runs along in various dimensions, so that there can be no question of a conceptually exact fixing of any eidetic concreta and of all the moments immediately constituting them. He gives an example. Take an experience of the genus "thing-phantasy," as it is given in phenomenologically immanent perception or some other (always reduced) intuition. Then the phenomenologically singular (the eidetic singularity) is this thing-phantasy, in the whole fullness of its concretion, exactly as it flows along in the experience-stream, exactly in the determinateness and indeterminateness with which it brings its thing to appearance now from these, now from those sides, exactly in the distinctness or blurredness, in the fluctuating clarity and intermittent darkness, that is just its own. This is the point about the lowest-level concreta. A particular phantasy, taken in its full concrete detail, is utterly fluid: it flows, shifts its sides, wavers in clarity. There is no hope of fixing it in exact concepts. It is too individual and too fluctuating.

He notes what phenomenology does with such a concretum. Only the individuation does phenomenology let fall; the whole essential content, in the fullness of its concretion, it raises into eidetic consciousness and takes as an ideal-identical essence, which, like every essence, could be singularized not only here and now but in countless instances. One sees at once that a conceptual and terminological fixing of this and every such fluid concretum is not to be thought of, and that the same holds for each of its immediate, no less fluid parts and abstract moments. So even when one moves from the individual phantasy to its essence (dropping the individuation), the lowest-level essence is still too fluid to fix exactly. The eidetic singularity (the fully specific essence of just this kind of fluid phantasy) is as unfixable as the individual was. Exact determination fails at the lowest level.

He locates where firm description becomes possible. Although there is no question of an unambiguous determination of the eidetic singularities in our descriptive sphere, it stands quite otherwise with the essences of a higher level of speciality. These are accessible to firm distinction, identifying maintenance, and strict conceptual grasp, likewise to analysis into component essences; and accordingly the tasks of a comprehensive scientific description can be meaningfully set for them. This is the resolution. Firm, scientific description is possible not at the lowest (fully specific) level, but at higher levels of generality. The lowest eidetic singularities are too fluid; but the more general essences above them are firm, distinguishable, and analyzable. So phenomenology describes at the level of these higher essences, not at the level of the fully specific fluid singularities.

He gives examples of the firmly describable essences. So we describe, and thereby determine in strict concepts, the generic essence of perception in general, or of subordinate kinds, such as perception of physical thinghood, of animal beings, and the like; likewise of memory in general, empathy in general, willing in general, and so on. Before these stand the highest generalities: experience in general, cogitatio in general, which already make comprehensive essential descriptions possible. So the firmly describable themes are the act-genera and their main species: perception as such, memory as such, willing as such, and above them the highest essences, experience as such and cogitatio as such. These admit strict description. This is exactly where the analyses of the following chapters operate: the general structures of experience as such, of the cogitatio as such, and of the main act-types.

He notes a feature of generic essence-grasping. In the nature of generic essence-grasping, analysis, and description it lies that no such dependence of the achievements at higher levels on those at lower levels obtains, that, say, a systematic inductive procedure would be methodically required, a step-by-step ascent on the ladder of generality. The point is that phenomenology need not build up from the lowest level. Unlike an inductive empirical science, which must gather particulars and generalize, phenomenological description can grasp the higher essences directly, without first determining all the lower ones. One can describe the essence of perception in general without having fixed every particular kind of perception. This frees phenomenology from a hopeless bottom-up procedure.

He draws a consequence about deduction and inference. Deductive theorizings are, according to what has been carried out, excluded from phenomenology. Mediate inferences are not flatly denied to it; but since all its knowledge is to be descriptive, purely fitted to the immanent sphere, inferences, non-intuitive procedures of every kind, have only the methodical significance of leading us toward the matters that a subsequent direct essence-intuition has to bring to givenness. Analogies that force themselves on us may, before actual intuition, suggest surmises about essential connections, and from these further inferences may be drawn; but in the end actual seeing of the essential connections must redeem the surmises. As long as that is not the case, we have no phenomenological result. This fixes the role of inference in phenomenology. Phenomenology is not deductive: it does not prove its results by inference. But it may use inference heuristically, to lead the gaze toward what must then be directly seen. Analogy and inference can suggest where to look, but only direct essence-intuition can establish a phenomenological result. An unredeemed inference is not yet phenomenology. This keeps phenomenology bound throughout to intuitive fulfillment (the principle of all principles, §24).

He leaves one question open. The question that forces itself, whether in the eidetic domain of the reduced phenomena (whether as a whole or in some subdomain) there might be, alongside the describing procedure, also an idealizing one, which substitutes pure and strict ideals for the intuitive givennesses, ideals that could then even serve as basic means for a mathesis of experiences, as a counterpart of descriptive phenomenology, is thereby not yet answered. So Husserl does not rule out that some idealizing, mathematical treatment of consciousness might be possible alongside description, in some subdomain. He leaves this open. (Such a possibility is realized in part later, for instance in the mathematics of time-consciousness, but it does not displace description as phenomenology's basic method.)

He sums up the gain of the methodological chapter. However much the investigations just carried out had to leave open, they have advanced us considerably, and not only in bringing a series of important problems into our field of view. With analogizing, this is now wholly clear to us, nothing is to be gained for the grounding of phenomenology. It is only a misleading prejudice to suppose that the methodology of the historically given a priori sciences, which are throughout exact ideal sciences, must without further ado be a model for every new science, and especially for our transcendental phenomenology, as if there could be eidetic sciences of only a single methodical type, that of "exactness." But transcendental phenomenology, as a descriptive essence-science, belongs to a totally other fundamental class of eidetic sciences than the mathematical sciences. This is the chapter's main result. Phenomenology must not be modeled on mathematics. Trying to build it as a "mathematics of phenomena" is a mistake born of the prejudice that all eidetic science must be exact. There are two fundamental classes of eidetic science: the exact (mathematical) and the descriptive (morphological). Phenomenology belongs firmly to the second. With this, the methodological worry of §71 is fully answered, and the First Chapter of the Third Part closes.

## Second Chapter: General Structures of Pure Consciousness

### § 76\. The Theme of the Following Investigations

Husserl opens the new chapter by recalling the standing of pure consciousness. Through the phenomenological reduction, the realm of transcendental consciousness had resulted for us as the realm of "absolute" being in a determinate sense. It is the primal category of being in general (or, in our way of speaking, the primal region), in which all other regions of being are rooted, to which they are by their essence related, and on which they are therefore all essentially dependent. This restates the result of the Second Part. Pure consciousness is the absolute, the primal region, on which all other regions of being depend. The doctrine of categories (the systematic account of the kinds of being) must therefore start from this most radical of all distinctions of being: being as consciousness, and being as the "transcendent" being that "announces itself" in consciousness. And this distinction, Husserl notes, can be won and appreciated in its purity only through the method of the phenomenological reduction.

He states where the relations between phenomenology and the other sciences are grounded. In the essential relation between transcendental and transcendent being are grounded the relations, already repeatedly touched on but to be explored more deeply later, between phenomenology and all other sciences, relations whose sense entails that the domain of dominion of phenomenology extends, in a certain remarkable way, over all the other sciences that it nevertheless switches off. He explains how. The switching-off has at the same time the character of a revaluing change of sign, and with this the revalued thing orders itself again into the phenomenological sphere. Speaking figuratively: what is bracketed is not wiped from the phenomenological slate, but only bracketed, and thereby provided with an index. But with this index it is in the main theme of the research. This is an important clarification of what bracketing accomplishes. Bracketing does not delete the world. It changes its "sign," its value: the world is no longer posited as absolute being, but taken as constituted correlate. With this changed sign, the bracketed world re-enters phenomenology, now as the theme of constitution. So phenomenology studies the very things it brackets, not as posited realities but as correlates of consciousness. This is why phenomenology's reach extends over all the sciences it suspends: it studies their objects as constituted phenomena.

He spells out the consequence with examples. It is necessary to understand this state of affairs thoroughly, from the ground up. For example, physical nature is subject to the switching-off; yet there is not only a phenomenology of natural-scientific consciousness, on the side of natural-scientific experiencing and thinking, but also a phenomenology of nature itself, as the correlate of natural-scientific consciousness. Likewise, although psychology and the human sciences are affected by the switching-off, there is a phenomenology of the human being, of his personality, his personal properties, and his (human) course of consciousness; further, a phenomenology of the social spirit, of social formations, of cultural formations, and so on. Everything transcendent, insofar as it comes to givenness in consciousness, is an object of phenomenological investigation not only on the side of the consciousness of it (for example, the various modes of consciousness in which it comes to givenness as the same), but also, though essentially interwoven with this, as the given and as what is taken up in the givennesses. This is the doctrine of the two-sidedness of phenomenological study. Everything bracketed (nature, the human being, society, culture) returns as a phenomenological theme, studied in two interwoven respects: on the side of the consciousness of it (the acts in which it appears), and on the side of the given as such (the object exactly as it is meant and given, the noema). So there is a phenomenology of nature, of the person, of society, of culture, not as realities but as constituted correlates. This anticipates the noesis/noema two-sidedness (the act-side and the object-side) that organizes the following analyses.

He notes how unexpected these domains are. There are in this way powerful domains of phenomenological research that one is not at all prepared for, starting from the idea of experience, especially if one begins, as we all do, with the psychologistic attitude and has at first let the concept of experience be given in advance by the psychology of our time. One will at first, under the influence of inner inhibitions, be little inclined to recognize these domains as phenomenological at all. The point is that the object-side (the noema, the given as such) is easily overlooked if one thinks of phenomenology as merely the study of mental acts. Coming from psychology, one expects phenomenology to study "experiences" in the narrow sense (mental events). One is not prepared for a phenomenology of nature or culture as given correlates. But the correlate-side is a genuine and large part of the field.

He notes the resulting complications for psychology and the human sciences. Through this inclusion of the bracketed, quite peculiar and at first confusing situations result for psychology and the human sciences. To indicate this only for psychology: consciousness, as the given of psychological experience, that is, as human or animal consciousness, is the object of psychology, in experiential-scientific research of empirical psychology, in essence-scientific research of eidetic psychology. On the other hand, in the modification of bracketing, the whole world, with its psychic individuals and their psychic experiences, belongs in phenomenology, all of it as correlate of absolute consciousness. So consciousness appears in different modes of apprehension and connections, and in different ones within phenomenology itself: in it once as the absolute consciousness, the other time in the correlate as psychological consciousness, which is now ordered into the natural world, in a certain way revalued and yet not deprived of its own content as consciousness. This is the difficult relation between the transcendental and the psychological treatment of consciousness. The same consciousness figures twice: as the absolute, constituting consciousness (phenomenology proper), and as the psychological consciousness that is part of the constituted world (a correlate studied within phenomenology). The psychological consciousness is the transcendental consciousness "revalued," set into the world, yet still consciousness in its content. This is the basis of the close parallel, and the sharp difference, between transcendental phenomenology and eidetic psychology (§§53–54, and developed at length in Husserl's later Phenomenological Psychology).

He states the resulting task and its importance. These are difficult connections and extraordinarily important ones. On them it depends that every phenomenological establishment about absolute consciousness can be reinterpreted into an eidetic-psychological one (which, strictly considered, is by no means itself a phenomenological one), where, however, the phenomenological way of consideration is the more comprehensive and, as absolute, the more radical. To see all this, and further to bring the essential relations between pure phenomenology, eidetic and empirical psychology, or the human sciences, to fully illuminated clarity, is a great matter for the disciplines involved here and for philosophy. So every transcendental-phenomenological result has a psychological "double," reachable by re-interpreting it as a truth about the mundane psyche. But the transcendental version is more comprehensive and more radical (it concerns the absolute, not the mundane). Sorting out the exact relations among transcendental phenomenology, eidetic psychology, and empirical psychology is a major task. Husserl adds a pointed remark: especially psychology, so vigorously rising in our time, can gain the radical grounding it still lacks only if it commands far-reaching insights into these essential connections. (This is the program of grounding psychology phenomenologically, central to his later work and to the Krisis.)

He measures how far we still are from understanding phenomenology, and states the chapter's task. The indications just given make us feel how far we still are from an understanding of phenomenology. We have learned to practice the phenomenological attitude, we have removed a series of confusing methodical reservations, we have defended the right of a pure description: the field of research lies open. But we do not yet know what the great themes in it are, more precisely, which basic directions of description are prescribed by the most general essential kind of the experiences. To create clarity in these respects, we try in the following chapters to characterize just this most general essential kind, at least in some especially important features. So the chapter turns from method to content. The field is open, but its main structures are not yet known. The task now is to lay out the most general structures of pure consciousness, the structures that prescribe the basic directions any description must take. These are the structures developed in the rest of the book: the act and its content, attention and the act-ray, the reell and the intentional, hyle and morphe, noesis and noema.

He closes by noting that this is still, in a sense, methodology. With these new considerations we do not really leave the problems of method. Already the previous methodical discussions were determined by the most general insights into the essence of the phenomenological sphere. It is self-evident that a deeper-penetrating knowledge of it, not in its details but in its pervasive generalities, must also put richer methodical norms into our hands, to which all special methods will have to bind themselves. Method is, after all, nothing brought to a domain from outside. Formal logic, or noetics, gives not method but the form of possible method; and however useful knowledge of form can be in methodological respects, a determinate method, not in mere technical particularity but in general methodical type, is a norm that springs from the regional basic kind of the domain and its general structures, and so, in its cognitive grasp, is essentially dependent on knowledge of these structures. This is an important methodological principle. Real method is not imposed from outside (formal logic gives only the empty form of method). The actual method of a science springs from the basic nature of its domain. So to know phenomenology's method fully, one must know the general structures of pure consciousness. The structural analyses that follow are therefore also the deeper grounding of phenomenological method. Method and subject matter are not separable: the structures of the field dictate how it must be studied.

With §76 the program of the Second Chapter is set: to lay out the general structures of pure consciousness. §77 turns to the first of these, reflection and the way consciousness is given to itself.

### § 77\. Reflection as the Fundamental Peculiarity of the Experience-Sphere. Studies in Reflection

Husserl names the first general structure to be treated and why. Among the most general essential peculiarities of the pure experience-sphere, he treats reflection in the first place. He does so for the sake of its universal methodological function: the phenomenological method moves entirely in acts of reflection. But skeptical reservations bear on the capacity of reflection, and so on the possibility of a phenomenology at all, which he wants first of all to settle thoroughly. The reason for starting here is plain. Phenomenology works by reflecting: it studies consciousness by turning the gaze back on consciousness. So the whole method stands or falls with reflection. And reflection has been doubted (the "self-observation" worry, taken up in §79). Husserl must therefore secure reflection before building on it.

He notes that the earlier results about reflection can now be taken over. Already in the preliminary considerations he had to speak of reflection (back-references to §38 and §45). What emerged there, before phenomenological ground was entered, he can now take over while strictly carrying out the reduction, since those establishments concerned merely what is essentially own to the experiences, that is, the kind of thing that, only transcendentally purified as to its apprehension, remains a secure possession. He will first recapitulate the known, then press deeper. The point is that the earlier observations about reflection (in the natural attitude) concerned the experiences in their own essence, which survives the reduction unchanged in content (only its apprehension is purified). So those results carry over.

He gives the basic description of reflection. Every ego lives through its experiences, and in these much is reell and intentionally enclosed. To live through them does not mean to have them, and what is enclosed in them, "in view," and to grasp them in the manner of immanent experience or some other immanent intuition. Every experience that is not in view can, by ideal possibility, become a "viewed" one: a reflection of the ego directs itself upon it, and it now becomes an object for the ego. The same holds for the possible ego-glances at the components of the experience and at its intentionalities (at what they are possibly consciousness of). The reflections are again experiences, and as such can become substrates of new reflections, and so on to infinity, in principial generality. This is the basic structure. I live through my experiences without normally attending to them; they are conscious but not "in view." Any experience can, in principle, be made an object by a reflective glance turned on it. So can its components and its intentional correlates. And the reflective acts are themselves experiences, open to further reflection, without limit. This is the reflexive openness of consciousness: every experience is reflectible, and every reflection is itself reflectible.

He turns to how the reflected experience is given, with the time-structure. The experience actually lived through at a given moment gives itself, newly stepping into the reflecting glance, as actually lived through, as being "now"; but not only that, it gives itself also as having just been, and insofar as it was unviewed, precisely as such, as having been unreflected. This is a decisive observation (already at §45.¶1). Reflection finds the experience not as just beginning, but as already underway, as having been lived before the glance turned to it. So reflection discloses the pre-reflective being of the experience: it was, and was lived by me, before I attended to it. Reflection does not create its object but finds it as already there. Husserl notes that in the natural attitude we take this for granted: experiences are, even when we are not turned to them; and they really were, and were really lived by us, when in immanent reflection within retention (primary memory) they are still conscious as "just" having been.

He extends this to recollection and to expectation, laying out the time-structure. We are further convinced that reflection on the ground of, and "in," recollection gives us tidings of our earlier experiences, which "then" were present, then immanently perceivable though not immanently perceived. The same holds, on the naive-natural view, for fore-memory, for forward-looking expectation. Here come into question first the immediate "protention" (as we might say), the exact counterpart of immediate retention, and then the quite otherwise re-presenting, in the more proper sense reproducing, fore-memory, which is the counterpart of recollection. The intuitively expected, consciousness of as "coming in the future," has, thanks to the reflection possible "in" the fore-memory, at the same time the significance of something that will have been perceived, just as the recollected has the significance of something perceived-having-been. So in fore-memory too we can reflect and make conscious our own experiences, which we were not set on in it, as belonging to the fore-remembered as such: as we always do where we say that we shall see the coming thing, the reflecting glance having turned to the "future" perceptual experience. This lays out the full temporal structure of consciousness (the basis of the time-consciousness analyses). Around the living now there is retention (the just-past, still held) and protention (the immediately anticipated). Beyond these stand the reproductive modes: recollection (re-presenting the past) and fore-memory (re-presenting the future). And reflection can operate "in" each: in recollection I can reflect on the past perceiving; in fore-memory on the future perceiving. This is why "I remember A" entails "I have perceived A," and "I anticipate A" entails "I shall perceive A."

He notes the transition to the eidetic. All this we make clear to ourselves in the natural attitude, say as psychologists, and follow it into its further connections. If we now carry out the phenomenological reduction, the establishments (in their bracketing) turn into exemplary cases of essential universalities, which we can make our own within the frame of pure intuition and study systematically. So the natural-attitude observations about reflection become, after the reduction, examples for grasping the essential laws of reflection. The same descriptive facts now serve as eidetic examples.

He gives the worked example: joy. We place ourselves, in living intuition (it may even be imagined), into some act-performance, say into a joy over a freely and fruitfully running theoretical train of thought. We carry out all reductions and see what lies in the pure essence of the phenomenological matters. First, then, a being-turned-toward the running thoughts. We develop the exemplary phenomenon further: during the pleasing course, a reflecting glance turns to the joy. The joy becomes a viewed and immanently perceived experience, fluctuating and fading in the glance of reflection in such and such ways. The freedom of the thought-course suffers thereby; it is now consciousness in a modified way, and the pleasingness belonging to its progress is essentially affected too. This can be established, whereby we again have to carry out new glance-turnings. This is a careful, realistic description. When I turn to observe my joy, the observing changes the joy: the free flow of thought is disturbed, and the joy itself is altered by being observed. This is the kernel of the "self-observation" difficulty (§79): reflection seems to modify what it observes. Husserl notes it honestly here, and will resolve it.

He draws the key result from the example: the reflected experience is found as already underway. The first reflection on the joy finds it as actually present, but not as just beginning. It stands there as continuing, already lived through before and only not envisaged. That is, there is evidently the possibility of going back to the past duration and givenness-mode of the pleasing thing, of attending to the earlier stretch of the theoretical thought-course, but also to the glance that was earlier turned to it; on the other hand, of attending to the joy-turning toward it, and, in the contrast, of grasping the lack of a glance turned to it in the elapsed phenomenon. This confirms the central point. Even though observing the joy modifies it, reflection still finds the joy as having been there, unobserved, before. By reflecting on the retained past stretch, I can grasp the joy as it was while unreflected, and contrast it with the observed joy. So reflection has access to the unreflected, through retention, and can register the very modification it introduces.

He notes the further reflective resources. We also have the possibility, with respect to the joy that has subsequently become an object, of carrying out reflection on the reflection that objectifies it, and so of bringing to clarity, still more effectively, the difference between lived but unviewed joy and viewed joy; likewise the modifications that come in through the acts of grasping, explicating, and so on, that set in with the glance-turning. So reflection can turn on reflection, making the difference between the unobserved and the observed experience fully clear, and registering the changes that grasping introduces. The reflexive depth of consciousness is itself a resource for clarifying how reflection works.

He states the scope of this study. All this we can consider in phenomenological attitude and eidetically, whether in higher generality or according to what may turn out essentially for particular kinds of experience. The whole experience-stream, with its experiences lived through in the mode of unreflected consciousness, can thus be subjected to a scientific essence-study aiming at systematic completeness, and indeed also with respect to all possibilities of intentionally enclosed experience-moments, so also especially with respect to the experiences possibly modified-conscious in them and their intentional correlates. So the entire stream, including its unreflected experiences and their intentional contents, can be studied eidetically through reflection. Examples of the modified-conscious experiences are the perceived-having-been enclosed in every recollection and the will-be-perceived enclosed in every expectation.

He closes §77 by noting the reflexive layering and warning against vague talk. The study of the experience-stream is itself carried out in various peculiarly built reflective acts, which themselves again belong in the experience-stream and can, and must, be made objects of phenomenological analyses in corresponding reflections of higher level. For a general phenomenology, and for the methodological insight quite indispensable to it, such analyses are fundamental. Something similar evidently holds for psychology. With the vague talk of studying experiences in reflection, or, what is usually identified with it, in memory, nothing is yet done, apart from much that is false which tends to get woven in here (precisely because serious essence-analysis is lacking), such as that there could be no such thing as immanent perception and observation at all. This sets up §78 and §79. The study of consciousness is itself a layered reflective enterprise, requiring careful analysis. Loose talk of "introspection" (studying experiences "in reflection" or "in memory") settles nothing and breeds errors, the chief being the denial that immanent perception is possible at all. §78 analyzes the kinds of reflection; §79 answers the denial.

### § 78\. The Phenomenological Study of Experience-Reflections

Husserl fixes what "reflection" names. Reflection, according to what has just been carried out, is a title for acts in which the experience-stream, with all its manifold occurrences (experience-moments, intentional correlates), becomes evidently graspable and analyzable. It is, we can also express it, the title of the consciousness-method for the knowledge of consciousness in general. But in just this method it itself becomes an object of possible studies: reflection is also a title for essentially belonging-together kinds of experience, and so the theme of a main chapter of phenomenology. To distinguish the various "reflections" and to analyze them completely in systematic order is the task therein. So "reflection" is both the method of phenomenology and one of its objects. The same reflective acts by which phenomenology studies consciousness are themselves a kind of consciousness to be studied. Sorting out and analyzing the kinds of reflection is therefore a main task.

He states the first essential point: reflection is a modification. One must first make clear that every kind of reflection has the character of a consciousness-modification, and indeed of one that in principle every consciousness can undergo. There is talk of modification here insofar as every reflection arises essentially from changes of attitude, whereby a pre-given experience, or experience-datum (the unreflected), undergoes a certain transformation, precisely into the mode of reflected consciousness (or of the reflected). The pre-given experience can itself already have the character of a reflected consciousness of something, so that the modification is of a higher level; but in the end we come back to absolutely unreflected experiences and their reell or intentional data. By essential law, every experience can now be transferred into reflective modifications, and that in various directions, which we shall come to know more exactly. This is the structural claim. Reflection transforms an experience from the unreflected mode into the reflected mode. This presupposes the unreflected: every chain of reflections traces back to absolutely unreflected experiences. So unreflected experience is the bedrock on which reflection works, and every experience is in principle reflectible, in various directions.

He notes the methodological importance and lists the modes of reflection. The fundamental methodological significance of the essence-study of the reflections for phenomenology, and no less for psychology, shows itself in that under the concept of reflection fall all modes of immanent essence-grasping and, on the other hand, of immanent experience. So, for example, immanent perception, which is indeed a reflection, insofar as it presupposes a glance-turning from something otherwise conscious to the consciousness of it. Likewise, as touched on in the previous section in discussing the self-evidences of the natural attitude, every memory permits not only a reflective glance-turning on itself, but also the peculiar reflection "in" the memory. This is an important point. Immanent perception is itself a species of reflection (the glance turned from the object to the consciousness of it). And memory admits two reflections: on the remembering itself, and "in" the remembering (on the past perceiving).
