So reflection is a broad genus covering immanent perception, immanent memory, and the reflections "in" the re-presentations.

He gives the example of the music and the equivalences. First, in the memory, say, the running of a piece of music is unreflectedly conscious in the mode of "past." But to the essence of something so conscious belongs the possibility of reflecting on its having-been-perceived. Likewise, for expectation, for the consciousness looking toward the "coming," there is the essential possibility of deflecting the glance from this coming thing to its will-have-been-perceived. On these essential connections it depends that the propositions "I remember A" and "I have perceived A," "I look toward A" and "I shall perceive A," are a priori and immediately equivalent; but only equivalent, for the sense is a different one. This formalizes the earlier point (§77). Remembering A entails having perceived A, and anticipating A entails will-perceive A, by an a priori essential law. But the propositions are only equivalent, not identical in sense: "I remember A" and "I have perceived A" say different things, though each follows from the other. The reflection "in" memory or expectation is what makes the past or future perceiving available.

He states the systematic task. The phenomenological task here is to investigate systematically all the experience-modifications falling under the title reflection, in connection with all the modifications with which they stand in essential relation and which they presuppose. The latter concerns the totality of essence-modifications that every experience must undergo during its originary course, and besides this the various kinds of variations that can ideally be thought as carried out on every experience in the manner of "operations." So studying reflection requires studying two further sets of modifications it presupposes: the modifications every experience undergoes in its own temporal course, and the ideal "operations" that can be performed on any experience. The next paragraphs analyze these.

He describes the first: the temporal flux of every experience. Every experience is in itself a flux of becoming; it is what it is in an originary production of an unchangeable essence-type: a constant flux of retentions and protentions mediated by a self-flowing phase of originarity, in which the living now of the experience becomes conscious over against its "before" and "after." This is the time-consciousness structure. Every experience flows: it is continually produced, with a living now-phase flanked by retention (just-past) and protention (just-coming). The essence-type stays fixed while the experience flows through this temporal form. This constant inner time-structure is the first set of modifications every experience undergoes.

He describes the second: the reproductive parallels. On the other hand, every experience has its parallels in various forms of reproduction, which can be regarded as ideal "operative" reshapings of the original experience: every one has its "exactly corresponding" and yet thoroughly modified counterpart in a recollection, likewise in a possible fore-memory, in a possible mere phantasy, and again in the iterations of such reshapings. So besides its inner temporal flux, every experience has reproductive "doubles": its counterpart in recollection, in fore-memory, in phantasy, and in iterations of these. These are the second set of modifications. Husserl notes that the parallelized experiences are thought as sharing a common essential content (the same intentional objectivities, in identical givenness-modes), so that the variation is only in the reproductive form, not in the content.

He notes that these operations are infinitely iterable and lead back to impressions. Since the modifications in view belong to every experience as ideally possible reshapings, designating in a way ideal operations that can be thought as carried out on each, they are repeatable to infinity; they are also to be carried out on the modified experiences. Conversely, from every experience that is already characterized as such a modification, and then always characterized in itself as such, we are led back to certain primal experiences, to "impressions," which present the absolutely originary experiences in the phenomenological sense. So thing-perceptions are originary experiences in relation to all memories, phantasy-re-presentations, and so on. They are as originary as concrete experiences can be at all. For, exactly considered, they have in their concretion only one, but continually one continuously flowing absolutely originary phase, the moment of the living now. This is a key result. The reproductive operations can be iterated without end, but they always trace back to impressions: the absolutely originary experiences (paradigmatically thing-perceptions), which are not reproductions of anything. An impression carries the absolutely originary phase, the living now. (Memory and phantasy are modifications of such impressions; impression is the primal, unmodified mode.) Note that even the impression has only one absolutely originary phase, the now; the rest of its concretion is already retentional or protentional.

He relates these modifications to reflection. We can primarily relate these modifications to the unreflectedly conscious actual experiences, since it is at once to be seen that in these primary modifications all the reflectedly conscious ones must eo ipso share, by the fact that they, as reflections on experiences and taken in full concretion, are themselves unreflectedly conscious experiences and as such take on all modifications. Now reflection itself is certainly a new kind of general modification: this directing of the ego upon its experiences, and in one with it the carrying out of acts of the cogito (especially of acts of the lowest, fundamental layer, that of simple presentations), "in" which the ego directs itself upon its experiences; but precisely the interweaving of reflection with intuitive or empty apprehensions, or graspings, conditions, for the study of the reflective modification, the necessary interweaving with that of the modifications indicated above. The point is that reflection is one modification among others, and it interweaves with the temporal and reproductive modifications. A reflective act is itself an experience that flows in time and can be reproduced. So the study of reflection cannot be separated from the study of the temporal and reproductive modifications: they are interwoven.

He states what reflection alone gives us, securing the field. Through reflectively experiencing acts alone do we know anything of the experience-stream and of its necessary relatedness to the pure ego: so of the fact that it is a field of free performance of cogitationes of the one and same pure ego; that all the experiences of the stream are its own precisely insofar as it can look upon them or "through them" look upon other ego-foreign things. We convince ourselves that these experiences keep sense and right also as reduced, and in general essential universality we grasp the right of experiences so constituted in general, just as, parallel to this, we grasp the right of essence-intuitions related to experiences in general. This is fundamental. Reflection is the sole access to the experience-stream and to its belonging to the pure ego. Without reflection we would know nothing of the stream as a field of the ego's acts. And reflection, even reduced, keeps its right (its evidential validity): we grasp, eidetically, that reflective experience and essence-intuition of experiences are valid in principle. This secures the very possibility of phenomenology, which is reflective throughout.

He spells out the grades of evidential right that reflection carries. So we grasp, for example, the absolute right of immanently perceiving reflection, that is, of immanent perception simply, with respect to what it brings to actually originary givenness in its close; likewise the absolute right of immanent retention with respect to what is conscious in it in the character of the "still" living and "just" having-been, but of course only so far as the content of what is so characterized reaches. So, for example, in the respect that it was a perceiving of a tone and not of a color. Likewise we grasp the relative right of immanent recollection, which reaches so far as the content of this recollection, considered singly, shows the genuine recollection-character (which by no means every moment of the recollected does), a right that holds quite so for every recollection. But of course it is a merely "relative" one, one that can be outweighed, however much it is for that reason still right. And so on. This grades the evidence of the reflective modes. Immanent perception has absolute right (for what it actually brings to originary givenness). Immanent retention has absolute right (for what is given as still-living and just-past, within the reach of that character: that it was a tone, not a color). Recollection has only relative right (it can be deceived and outweighed by further experience), though within its genuine recollection-character it is still right. So the reflective modes are not all equally certain: perception and retention are absolute, recollection is relative.

He closes §78 by drawing the anti-skeptical conclusion, invoking the principle of all principles. In the most perfect clarity, then, we see, and in the consciousness of unconditional validity, that it would be countersense to suppose that experiences are epistemically secured only insofar as they are given in the reflecting consciousness of immanent perception; or even that they are secured only in the respective actual now; that it would be wrong to doubt the having-been of what is found, in turning the glance back, as "still" conscious (immediate retention), and again to doubt whether at the end the experiences that step into the glance do not, precisely thereby, transform themselves into something completely different, and so on. It is only a matter here of not letting oneself be confused by arguments that, for all their formal precision, lack any measuring against the primal sources of validity, those of pure intuition; it is a matter of remaining true to the "principle of all principles," that perfect clarity is the measure of all truth, and that assertions that give faithful expression to their givennesses need not trouble themselves about any arguments, however fine. This is the decisive anti-skeptical stand. The skeptic says: experiences are secured only in the present moment of immanent perception, and even there reflection may distort them. Husserl answers that this is countersense. Retention gives the just-past with absolute right; reflection finds the unreflected as already there. The skeptical arguments, however formally precise, fail because they do not measure themselves against intuition, the sole source of validity. The principle of all principles (§24) settles the matter: what is given in perfect clarity (here, the retained just-past, the reflected unreflected experience) is true, and no clever argument can override a faithful description of it. §79 takes up the specific psychological form of this skepticism, the "self-observation" objection.

§ 79\. Critical Excursus. Phenomenology and the Difficulties of "Self-Observation"

Husserl states the upshot of §§77–78 and identifies the objector. From the last presentations one sees that phenomenology is not affected by the methodological skepsis that, in parallel, in empirical psychology has so often led to the denial or inappropriate restriction of the value of inner experience. Recently H. J. Watt has nevertheless believed he could maintain this skepsis against phenomenology, though he did not grasp the peculiar sense of pure phenomenology that the Logical Investigations tried to introduce, and did not see the difference between the pure-phenomenological situation and the empirical-psychological one. The footnote gives the reference (Watt's 1907 survey-report on recent research in memory- and association-psychology, in the Archiv für die gesamte Psychologie, vol. IX) and notes that although Watt deals explicitly only with Th. Lipps and does not name Husserl, Husserl believes he may regard the critique as also directed against him, since much of Watt's reporting could apply equally to the Logical Investigations as to Lipps's later writings. So §79 is a polemical excursus answering a specific psychological objection to the possibility of phenomenology, the "self-observation" (introspection) skepsis.

He draws the crucial distinction between the psychological and the phenomenological question. However related the difficulties on both sides are, it is still a difference whether the question is raised about the scope and the principial cognitive value of the existence-establishments that bring to expression givennesses of our (human) inner experiences, that is, the question of psychological method; or, on the other hand, that of phenomenological method, the question of the principial possibility and scope of essence-establishments that, on the ground of pure reflection, are to concern experiences as such, according to their own essence, free from nature-apperception. This is the key move. The psychological question is about the reliability of introspective reports on the existence of one's actual mental states (a question of fact-establishing). The phenomenological question is about the possibility of grasping the essence of experiences, on the basis of pure reflection, independently of any claim about their existence. These are different questions. Watt confuses them. Still, Husserl grants, there are inner relations and even congruences between the two, which justify taking Watt's objections into account, especially some remarkable sentences.

He quotes Watt's skeptical claims. Watt writes that one can hardly even make conjectures about how one comes to knowledge of immediate experiencing, since it is neither knowledge nor an object of knowledge, but something else, and that it is not to be seen how a report on the experiencing of experiencing, even when it is there, gets onto paper; that this is, after all, the ultimate question of the fundamental problem of self-observation; and that nowadays one calls this absolute description "phenomenology." So Watt's challenge is that immediate experiencing (the living-through of an experience) is not itself knowledge and not an object of knowledge, so it is mysterious how any report on it is possible, and that "phenomenology" is the name for this allegedly impossible absolute description.

He quotes Watt's report of Lipps and Watt's own additions. Reporting on Lipps, Watt says that over against the known reality of the objects of self-observation stands the reality of the present ego and the present consciousness-experiences; this reality is lived (namely merely lived, not "known," that is, reflectively grasped), and is thereby absolute reality. Watt adds that one may hold very different opinions about what one can do with this absolute reality; that it is probably only a matter of results of self-observation; and that if this always retrospective consideration is always a knowing of just-had experiences as objects, how is one to posit states of which one can have no knowledge, which are only conscious? The whole importance of the discussion turns on this, namely on the derivation of the concept of immediate experiencing, which is no knowing. One must be able to observe; everyone, finally, experiences; only he does not know this. And if he knew it, how could he know that his experiencing really is absolutely as he thinks it? Out of whose head may phenomenology spring ready-made into life? Is a phenomenology possible, and in what sense? Watt suggests that perhaps a discussion of the question of self-observation from experimental psychology will throw new light on the field, since the problem of phenomenology necessarily arises also for experimental psychology; perhaps its answer will be more cautious, since it lacks the zeal of the discoverer of phenomenology, and is in any case more dependent, out of itself, on an inductive method. So Watt's worry, sharpened, is this: all self-observation is retrospective (a knowing of just-had experiences as objects); but then how can one establish anything about the merely-lived, unreflected experiences, which are "only conscious" and not known? And even if one could observe them, how would one know the observation does not distort them? He suggests experimental psychology, with its inductive method, may handle this more cautiously than phenomenology does.

He notes a revealing inconsistency in Watt. Given the pious faith in the omnipotence of the inductive method that speaks from these last lines (a faith Watt would hardly hold to if he reflected on the conditions of the possibility of this method), the admission that "a functionally analyzing psychology will never be able to explain the fact of knowing" is then surprising. The jab is sharp. Watt trusts induction to settle the problem, yet himself admits that a functional (inductive, explanatory) psychology can never explain knowing. Husserl notes that Watt would not trust induction so if he asked after the conditions of possibility of induction itself (which are precisely what phenomenology, not induction, must clarify). So Watt's own admission undercuts his appeal to inductive psychology.

He makes the first reply: the psychological/phenomenological distinction. Over against these utterances, characteristic of present-day psychology, insofar as they are meant psychologically, he would first assert the above distinction between the psychological and the phenomenological question, and emphasize that a phenomenological essence-doctrine has as little to interest itself in the methods by which the phenomenologist could assure himself of the existence of the experiences that serve him as substrates in his phenomenological establishments, as geometry has to interest itself in how the existence of the figures on the board or of the models in the cabinet is to be methodically secured. Geometry and phenomenology, as sciences of pure essence, know no establishments about real existence. This is exactly why clear fictions offer them not only equally good but in large measure better substrates than givennesses of actual perception and experience (back-reference to §70). This is the decisive first answer. Watt's worry is about securing the existence of inner states (a fact-question). But phenomenology, like geometry, makes no existence-claims. The geometer does not worry whether the drawn triangle really exists; he studies the essence "triangle." The phenomenologist does not worry whether the example experience really existed; he studies its essence. So the introspective reliability worry, which is about existence, does not touch phenomenology, which is essential. And, as §70 showed, fictions serve essence-study as well as or better than actual cases.

He concedes that the worry can be reformulated as an essence-worry, and restates it sharply. If now phenomenology also has to make no existence-establishments about experiences, hence no "experiences" and "observations" in the natural sense in which a fact-science must rest on such, it nevertheless makes, as a principial condition of its possibility, essence-establishments about unreflected experiences. But these it owes to reflection, more closely to reflective essence-intuition. Consequently the skeptical reservations regarding self-observation come into consideration also for phenomenology, and indeed insofar as these reservations can, in an obvious way, be extended from immanently experiencing reflection to every reflection whatever. So the worry can be re-pitched at the essential level: phenomenology must make essence-claims about unreflected experiences, but it reaches them only by reflection, and reflection is exactly what is doubted. So the skepsis returns, now extended to every kind of reflection.

He develops the reformulated objection fully. Indeed, what should become of phenomenology if it were not to be seen how a report on the experiencing of experiencing, even when it is there, gets onto paper? What should become of it if it might make assertions about the essences of "known," reflected experiences, but not about essences of experiences simply? What should become of it if one could "hardly even make conjectures about how one comes to knowledge of immediate experiencing," or of its essence? It may be that the phenomenologist has to carry out no existence-establishments regarding the experiences that hover before him as exemplary for his ideations. Yet, one might object, in these ideations he beholds only Ideas of such as he just has before his eyes in the example. As soon as his glance turns to the experience, it first becomes that as which it now presents itself to him; as soon as he turns the glance away, it becomes another. The grasped essence is only essence of the reflected experience, and the opinion of being able, through reflection, to win absolutely valid knowledge that holds for experiences in general, whether reflected or unreflected, is wholly unfounded. How is one to posit states, be it even as essential possibilities, of which one can have no knowledge? This is the objection at full strength. The worry is that reflection alters its object, so that the essence one grasps is only the essence of the reflected (altered) experience, never of the unreflected experience. Then phenomenology could say nothing about unreflected experiences as such, and its claim to absolutely valid knowledge of experiences in general would collapse.

He shows the objection's reach with the centaur example. This evidently concerns every kind of reflection, though in phenomenology each is supposed to count as a source of absolute knowledge. In phantasy a thing hovers before me, say even a centaur. I think I know that it presents itself in certain "modes of appearance," in certain "sensation-adumbrations," apprehensions, and the like. I think I have the essential insight that such an object can be intuited at all only in such modes of appearance, only by means of such adumbration-functions, and whatever else may play a role here. But having the centaur in view, I do not have its modes of appearance, adumbrating data, apprehensions in view, and grasping its essence, I do not grasp them and their essence. For that, certain reflective glance-turnings are needed, which, however, set the whole experience in flux and modify it; and so in the new ideation I have something new before my eyes, and may not assert that I have won essential components of the unreflected experience. I may not assert that it belongs to the essence of a thing as such to present itself in "appearances," adumbrating itself in the indicated way through sensation-data that for their part undergo apprehensions, and so on. The example makes the worry concrete and shows it threatens the core analyses. When I imagine a centaur, I am directed at the centaur, not at the appearances and adumbrations through which it is given. To study those, I must reflect, and the reflection modifies the experience. So (the objection runs) I cannot claim that the adumbration-structure I find by reflecting belongs to the unreflected imagining. This would undermine the whole doctrine of adumbration (§§41–44) and, more generally, every claim about the structure of unreflected consciousness.

He extends the worry to the analyses of sense, citing Watt again. The difficulty evidently concerns also the consciousness-analyses with respect to the "sense" of the intentional experiences, with respect to all that belongs to the meant, to the intentional-objective as such, to the sense of an assertion, and the like. For these too are analyses within peculiarly directed reflections. Watt himself even goes so far as to say that psychology must make clear to itself that with self-observation the objective relation of the experiences to be described is changed, and that perhaps this change has a much greater significance than one is inclined to believe. If Watt is right, then we, establishing in self-observation that we were just now attentive to this book here and still are, would thereby have asserted too much. That held at most before the reflection. But reflection changes the experience of attention here "to be described," and indeed (according to Watt) with respect to the objective relation. So the worry threatens the noematic analyses too (the analyses of sense, of the intentional object as such, the center of the book to come). If reflection changes the very objective-relatedness of the experience, then no reflective analysis of intentional sense could be trusted. Husserl now turns to the refutation.

He states the general anti-skeptical principle. All genuine skepticism, of whatever kind and direction, shows itself by the principial countersense that, in its argumentations, it implicitly presupposes, as conditions of the possibility of their validity, precisely what it denies in its theses. One convinces oneself without difficulty that this mark applies also to the argumentations in question. This is the classic self-refutation argument, which Husserl wielded against psychologism in the Logical Investigations and now turns against the self-observation skepsis. A skepticism refutes itself when its own assertion presupposes what it denies. Husserl will show that the self-observation skepsis does exactly this.

He applies it: doubting reflection presupposes reflection. Whoever even says, "I doubt the cognitive significance of reflection," asserts a countersense. For in asserting about his doubting, he reflects, and to put this assertion forward as valid presupposes that reflection really and indubitably has (namely for the cases at hand) the doubted cognitive value, that it does not change the objective relation, that the unreflected experience in passing over into reflection does not forfeit its essence. The argument is tight. To doubt reflection, I must reflect on my doubt (to state it). To assert my doubt as a valid claim, I must trust that this reflection on my doubt is accurate, that it does not distort my doubt. So the very act of doubting reflection presupposes that reflection is reliable. The skepsis refutes itself.

He gives a second self-refutation. Further: in the argumentations there is constant talk of reflection as a fact, and of what it causes or could cause; with this, of course, also talk of the "unknown," unreflected experiences, again as facts, namely as those from which the reflected ones arise. So a knowledge of unreflected experiences, among them of unreflected reflections, is constantly presupposed, while at the same time the possibility of such knowledge is put in question. This happens insofar as the possibility is doubted of establishing anything about the content of the unreflected experience and about the achievement of reflection: how far it changes the original experience, and whether it does not falsify it into a totally other one. The point is that the skeptic talks confidently about unreflected experiences (claiming reflection alters them, that they "arise" into reflected ones), which presupposes knowledge of unreflected experiences, the very knowledge he says is impossible. To claim "reflection distorts the unreflected experience," one must know the unreflected experience (to compare). So the skeptic helps himself to exactly what he denies.

He draws out the contradiction fully. It is clear that, if this doubt and the possibility posited in it held good, not the slightest ground of right would remain for the certainty that there is and can be an unreflected experience and a reflection at all. It is further clear that the latter, which was after all the constant presupposition, can be known only through reflection, and that as immediate knowledge it can be grounded only through reflective giving intuition. Likewise with respect to the assertion of the reality or possibility of the modifications that come about through reflection. But if such a thing is given through intuition, it is given in an intuitional content, so it is countersense to assert that here there is nothing knowable at all, nothing with respect to the content of the unreflected experience and the kind of modifications it undergoes. The contradiction is complete. The skeptic's whole position (that there are unreflected experiences which reflection modifies) is itself knowable only by reflection. If reflection were worthless, the skeptic could not even state his own position. And since the modifications he talks about are given in reflective intuition, it is countersense to say nothing about them is knowable. The skepsis destroys its own premises.

He states the resolution by return to intuition. This suffices to make the countersense clear. Here as everywhere, skepsis loses its force by going back from the verbal argumentations to essence-intuition, to the originarily giving intuition and its own primal right. Everything depends, of course, on one actually carrying this out and being able to raise the matter in question into the light of genuine essential clarity, or to take up presentations, such as those attempted in the previous section, as intuitively as they are carried out and presented. This is the methodological heart of the answer. The skeptical arguments are merely verbal; they collapse once one returns to actual intuition. One must actually perform the reflection and see, with essential clarity, that reflection does give the unreflected experience (through retention, §78), and see what modifications it introduces. The arguments cannot override what is directly seen. (This applies the principle of all principles, §24, exactly as §78 concluded.)

He affirms that reflection is a sphere of clear givenness, listing the basic reflective insight. The phenomena of reflection are in fact a sphere of pure and possibly perfectly clear givennesses. It is an essential insight, attainable at any time because immediate, that from the objectively given as such a reflection is possible upon the giving consciousness and its subject: from the perceived, the bodily "there," upon the perceiving; from the remembered, as it hovers as such, as "having been," upon the remembering; from the assertion, in its running givenness, upon the asserting, and so on; whereby the perceiving comes to givenness as perceiving of just this perceived, the respective consciousness as consciousness of this respective conscious thing. So the basic structure of reflection is itself intuitively given and clear: from any object-as-given one can reflect on the consciousness of it and on its subject, and in this reflection the consciousness is given precisely as consciousness of that object. This is not a doubtful inference but an immediate essential insight, available at any time.

He states the strong conclusion: reflection is the only possible access to consciousness, binding even God. It is evident that essentially, hence not from merely accidental grounds, not merely "for us" and our accidental "psychophysical constitution," only through reflections of this kind is something like consciousness and consciousness-content (in the reell or intentional sense) knowable at all. To this absolute and insightful necessity, then, even God is bound, just as to the insight that 2 \+ 1 \= 1 \+ 2\. He too could win knowledge of his consciousness and consciousness-content only reflectively. A footnote clarifies the use of "God": we are not here playing the dispute over into the domain of theology; the Idea God is a necessary limit-concept in epistemological considerations, or an indispensable index for the construction of certain limit-concepts, which even the philosophizing atheist could not do without. This is the powerful closing point. Reflection is not a contingent human peculiarity but the essentially necessary, and only possible, mode of access to consciousness. Just as even God cannot make 2 \+ 1 ≠ 1 \+ 2 (an essential necessity, not a human limitation, recalling §§43, 51), so even God could know his own consciousness only by reflecting on it. So the dependence of self-knowledge on reflection is an absolute essential law, not a defect of human cognition. The footnote carefully marks that "God" here is an epistemological limit-concept (the idea of a perfect knower), used to mark essential necessity, not a theological claim, available even to the atheist as a conceptual device. With this, the self-observation skepsis is fully refuted, and the right of reflection, hence the possibility of phenomenology, is secured.

He draws the consequence that reflection cannot conflict with the ideal of perfect knowledge. With this it is at the same time said that reflection cannot be entangled in any antinomic conflict with the ideal of perfect knowledge. Every kind of being, as we have had to emphasize several times, has essentially its modes of givenness and thereby its modes of cognitive method. To treat essential peculiarities of these as defects, even to charge them as accidental, factual defects of "our human" cognition, is countersense. This restates a principle used before (§§43, 44, 51, 138): each kind of being has its own essentially necessary mode of givenness. Consciousness is given by reflection; that is its essential mode, not a deficiency. To complain that we know consciousness "only" by reflection is like complaining that we know spatial things "only" by adumbration: it mistakes an essential necessity for a contingent human limitation. So reflection's character cannot conflict with any ideal of perfect knowledge, because perfect knowledge of consciousness would still be reflective.

He distinguishes three separate questions, to keep them from being confused. Another question, but likewise to be weighed in essential insight, is that of the possible "scope" of the cognition in question, that is, the question how we have to guard ourselves against assertions that go beyond what is in each case actually given and eidetically to be grasped. And again another is the question of empirical methodology: how we human beings, say as psychologists, have to proceed under the given psychophysical circumstances in order to lend our human cognitions as high a dignity as possible. So three questions must be kept apart. First, whether reflection is valid at all (answered: yes, by essential necessity). Second, how far its valid reach extends (a question of not over-claiming beyond the given, as graded in §78, perception and retention absolute, recollection relative). Third, the empirical-psychological question of how to make actual human introspective reports reliable under real conditions. Watt's worry belongs to the third (empirical) question and was wrongly raised against the first (the principial possibility of phenomenology).

He defends the appeal to insight against a charge of "bluff," in a long footnote answering Ziehen. He emphasizes that the repeated recourse to insight (evidence, intuition) is not a phrase but, in the sense of the introductory part, means the return to the ultimate in all cognition, exactly as in talk of insight in the case of the most primitive logical and arithmetical axioms. A substantial footnote responds to a fresh attack: Th. Ziehen, in his just-published Epistemology on a Psychophysiological and Physical Basis, had written of "that suspicious so-called intuition or evidence" which, he claimed, changes from philosopher to philosopher and from school to school, and which conveniently shows up precisely when an author presents a very doubtful point of his doctrine, so that we are to be preserved from doubts "by a bluff." Ziehen aims this at the Logical Investigations' doctrine of "general objects" or "essences" and of essence-intuition, dismissing the ascribed special generality and absolute exactness of such concepts as "human presumptions," and demanding, for any primary intuition, the agreement of all sensing and thinking individuals as its only credential. Husserl's reply is pointed: of course mischief has often been done by appealing to "intuition," but the only way to expose a merely alleged intuition is through actual intuition; the same mischief is done by appealing to "experience," and it would go badly if one therefore branded all experience a "bluff" and made its credentials depend on the agreement of all individuals. So the appeal to intuition is not a rhetorical dodge but the return to the ultimate ground of all knowledge, the same ground on which the simplest logical and arithmetical axioms rest. A false appeal to intuition is corrected only by genuine intuition, not by abolishing intuition, just as a false appeal to experience is corrected by better experience, not by rejecting experience.

He turns the result toward psychology, drawing the strong methodological conclusion. Whoever has learned to grasp insightfully what is given in the sphere of consciousness will be able to read only with astonishment sentences like the one cited above, that one can make no conjectures about how one comes to knowledge of immediate experiencing; from this one can gather only how foreign immanent essence-analysis still is to modern psychology, although it forms the only possible method for fixing the concepts that have to function as determining in all immanent psychological description. (A footnote points to his Logos essay, and another remarks that recent essays by Messer and Cohn show how hard it is even for thorough researchers to free themselves from the reigning prejudices and grasp the peculiarity of phenomenology as an essence-doctrine, having so misunderstood his sense that the doctrines combated as his are not his at all, a misunderstanding he hopes the present fuller presentations will remove.) So the self-observation skepsis betrays that psychology still lacks immanent essence-analysis, which is precisely the method needed to fix the very concepts (perception, memory, attention, and so on) that all psychological description must use. Psychology cannot describe inner experience without essential concepts of the kinds of experience, and those concepts can be fixed only phenomenologically.

He closes §79 by stating the relation of phenomenology to psychology as that of norm to fact. In these problems of reflection, the intimate connection between phenomenology and psychology becomes especially palpable. Every essence-description relating to kinds of experience expresses an unconditionally valid norm for possible empirical existence. In particular, this concerns all the kinds of experience that are themselves constitutive for the psychological method, as holds for all modes of inner experience. So phenomenology is the instance for the methodological fundamental questions of psychology. What it has generally established, the psychologist must recognize as a condition of the possibility of all his further methodology, and draw upon as needed. What conflicts with it characterizes principial psychological countersense, exactly as, in the physical sphere, every conflict with the geometrical truths and the truths of the ontology of nature in general is the characteristic of principial natural-scientific countersense. This is the concluding thesis. Phenomenological essence-truths about kinds of experience are norms for any possible empirical (psychological) fact: every actual experience must conform to the essence of its kind. So phenomenology grounds psychology as geometry and the ontology of nature ground physics. To violate a phenomenological essence-truth in psychology is principial countersense, just as to violate geometry in physics is. This fixes the foundational relation of phenomenology to psychology (developed at length in Husserl's later Phenomenological Psychology and the Krisis).

He exposes the deepest inconsistency in Watt's hope. Such a principial countersense expresses itself in the hope of overcoming the skeptical reservations against the possibility of self-observation through psychological induction on the paths of experimental psychology. It is again just as if, in the domain of physical nature-knowledge, one wanted to overcome the parallel skepsis, whether at the end every outer perception deceives (since indeed every one, taken singly, could deceive), through experimental physics, which after all presupposes the right of outer perception at every step. The parallel is exact and devastating. Experimental psychology presupposes inner experience at every step (the subjects report their experiences). So it cannot ground inner experience; it already relies on it. To hope that experimental psychology will settle whether inner experience is valid is like hoping experimental physics will settle whether outer perception is valid, when physics itself rests on outer perception throughout. The skeptic's proposed cure presupposes what he doubts.

He closes §79 by deferring the deeper problems and stating an old ontological maxim. What is here said in general is to gain force through all that follows, especially through the clarifications about the scope of the reflective essence-insights. The relations touched on here, between phenomenology (or the eidetic psychology not yet provisionally separated from it here, and in any case intimately bound up with it) and experiential-scientific psychology, with all the deep problems belonging to them, are to be clarified in the second book of this work. Husserl declares his certainty that before long it will be common conviction that phenomenology (or eidetic psychology) is for empirical psychology the methodologically grounding science in the same sense in which the material mathematical disciplines (geometry, phoronomy) are grounding for physics. And he closes with an old ontological maxim: the old ontological doctrine, that the knowledge of the "possibilities" must precede that of the actualities, is, in his view, when rightly understood and rightly made usable, a great truth. So §79 ends by placing eidetic phenomenology as the a priori foundation of empirical psychology, just as geometry founds physics, and by endorsing the principle that knowledge of essential possibilities precedes and grounds knowledge of actualities. This is the deep sense of phenomenology as the science of essences serving as the norm for all fact-science. The critical excursus is thereby complete.

§ 80\. The Relation of the Experiences to the Pure Ego

Husserl states the priority of the ego-relation among the general structures. Among the general essential peculiarities of the transcendentally purified experience-domain, the first place really belongs to the relation of every experience to the "pure" ego. Every cogito, every act in a distinguished sense, is characterized as act of the ego; it "proceeds from the ego," the ego "lives" in it "actually." He recalls the earlier treatment (§57) in a few sentences. So the ego-relation is, strictly, the most basic structure of all, though Husserl treated reflection first for methodological reasons. Every act is an act of an ego: it issues from the ego, and the ego "lives" actively in it.

He surveys the range of acts in which the ego is actively engaged. Observing, I perceive something; likewise in memory I am often "occupied" with something; quasi-observing, I follow, in inventing phantasy, the goings-on in the phantasied world. Or I reflect, I draw inferences; I take back a judgment, possibly refraining from judgment altogether. I carry out a liking or disliking, I rejoice or am saddened, I wish, or I will and act; or also, I "refrain" from the joy, the wish, the willing, and acting. In all such acts I am there, actually there. So in the whole range of acts (perceiving, remembering, phantasying, thinking, inferring, judging or refraining, valuing, wishing, willing, acting, or holding back), the ego is actually engaged, "there" in the act. This breadth (cognitive, affective, volitional) shows that the ego-relation is a structure of every kind of act, not just theoretical ones.

He applies the reduction to the ego, recovering the result of §57. Reflecting, I grasp myself in this as the human being. But if I carry out the phenomenological epoché, then "I, the human being" falls to the switching-off, like the whole world of the natural thesis; the pure act-experience with its own essence remains behind. I see also that the apprehension of it as human experience, apart from the existence-thesis, brings in all sorts of things that need not necessarily be there, and that, on the other hand, no switching-off can cancel the form of the cogito and strike out the "pure" subject of the act. The "being-directed-upon," "being-occupied-with," "taking-a-position-toward," "experiencing, suffering from," necessarily harbors in its essence this, that it is precisely a "from the ego thither" or, in the reverse ray of direction, "toward the ego," and this ego is the pure one; no reduction can touch it. This recovers §57's result. The reduction brackets the human ego (the mundane person), but cannot bracket the pure ego, because the very form of the cogito (directedness from or toward an ego) essentially requires it. The act's directedness is a ray running from the ego to the object (or, reversed, from the object to the ego). The pure ego is the necessary source-point of this ray (§§37, 57), untouched by any reduction.

He extends the ego-relation to the non-act (background) experiences. So far we have spoken of experiences of the special type cogito. The remaining experiences, which form the general milieu for the ego-actuality, lack, of course, the distinguished ego-relatedness just discussed. And yet they too have their share in the pure ego, and it in them. They "belong" to it as "its own"; they are its consciousness-background, its field of freedom. This is an important extension. Not only the actual acts (the cogitationes the ego actively performs) but also the background experiences (the inactual, unattended experiences forming the surrounding milieu) belong to the pure ego, as its own. They are the ego's "consciousness-background" and its "field of freedom" (the field from which it can, at any time, turn to attend something, §§35, 92). So the whole stream, actual and inactual, is the ego's.

He states the emptiness of the pure ego taken in itself. In these peculiar interweavings with all "its" experiences, the experiencing ego is nevertheless nothing that could be taken for itself and made into a proper object of investigation. Apart from its "modes of relation" or "modes of comportment," it is completely empty of essence-components, it has no explicable content at all, it is in and for itself indescribable: pure ego and nothing further. This is a key thesis (in tension with the rich egology Husserl later develops). The pure ego, taken in abstraction from its acts, has no describable content of its own. It is not a bundle of properties, not a substance with an inner nature; it is the bare subject-pole, "pure ego and nothing further." Everything describable about it concerns only its modes of relating to objects (its ways of being directed, occupied, taking positions). In itself, apart from these, it is empty and indescribable. (Husserl will later, in Book Two and the Cartesian Meditations, add the ego's habitualities and its constitution of itself as a personal ego; here, in Book One, the pure ego is kept minimal.)

He notes that, even so, the ego-relation grounds many descriptions, and introduces the crucial two-sidedness. For that reason there is nevertheless occasion for a manifold of important descriptions, precisely with respect to the particular ways how it is experiencing ego in the respective kinds or modes of experience. In this there is continually distinguished, despite the necessary mutual relatedness, the experience itself and the pure ego of the experiencing. And again: the purely subjective of the experience-mode and the rest, the so to speak ego-averted content of the experience. So there is a certain, extraordinarily important two-sidedness in the essence of the experience-sphere, of which we can also say that, on the experiences, a subjectively-oriented side and an objectively-oriented side are to be distinguished. This is one of the most important structural results, the seed of the noesis/noema distinction. Within every experience two sides are distinguished: the subjectively-oriented side (the experience as the ego's act, its mode of being-directed, the "purely subjective" of the experience-mode) and the objectively-oriented side (the "ego-averted" content, what the experience is of). Husserl flags this two-sidedness as extraordinarily important; it organizes all the analyses to come.

He guards the two-sidedness against a misunderstanding and notes the corresponding division of inquiries. This way of expressing it must not be misunderstood, as if we taught that the possible "object" of the experience is, in the experience, something analogous to the pure ego. Nevertheless the expression will justify itself. And we add at once that to this two-sidedness, in considerable stretches at least, there corresponds a division of the investigations (though no real separation), the one oriented toward the pure subjectivity, the others toward what belongs to the "constitution" of objectivity for subjectivity. We shall have much to say of the "intentional relation" of experiences (or of the pure experiencing ego) to objects, and of various experience-components and "intentional correlates" connected with it. The warning is careful. The objectively-oriented side (the object-as-meant) is not a second ego-like pole inside the experience; the two-sidedness is not a symmetry of two subjects. Still, the distinction is real and will be justified (in the noesis/noema analyses). It grounds a division of phenomenological inquiry: some investigations oriented toward pure subjectivity (the act-side), others toward the constitution of objectivity for subjectivity (the object-side). The terms "intentional relation" and "intentional correlate" name this object-side, the theme of the central analyses to come.

He notes that much can be studied without going deeply into the pure ego, and announces the focus of the rest of the Part. Such things can be investigated and described analytically or synthetically in comprehensive investigations, without occupying oneself any more deeply with the pure ego and its modes of participation. One must, of course, touch on it often, insofar as it is precisely a necessary co-present. The meditations we intend to carry out further in this Part are to apply preferentially to the objectively-oriented side, as the one first presenting itself in starting out from the natural attitude. To it already point the problems indicated in the introductory section of this Part. So the rest of the Part will focus on the objectively-oriented side (the intentional object, the noema, the constitution of objectivity), because that is what first presents itself and what the announced problems (§76) concern. The pure ego will be touched on as needed, but the detailed egology is deferred (to Book Two). This sets the agenda: the analyses of intentionality, hyle and morphe, and noesis and noema, with emphasis on the noematic (object) side.

### § 81\. Phenomenological Time and Time-Consciousness

Husserl introduces time as a closed and exceptionally difficult problem-sphere, and makes a striking admission. Phenomenological time, as will emerge from the following investigations, is a title for a completely self-contained problem-sphere, and one of exceptional difficulty. It will show that our presentation hitherto has, in a certain way, kept silent about a whole dimension, and necessarily had to keep silent, in order to keep unconfused what is at first alone in view in the phenomenological attitude, and what, regardless of the new dimension, makes up a closed domain of investigation. The transcendental "Absolute" that we have prepared out for ourselves through the reductions is in truth not the last thing; it is something that constitutes itself in a certain deep-lying and wholly peculiar sense, and has its primal source in a last and truly Absolute. This is a remarkable and important admission. The whole analysis so far has deliberately kept silent about time-consciousness, to keep the field manageable. But this means the "absolute" consciousness reached by the reductions is not the ultimate level. Beneath the stream of experiences lies a still deeper dimension, the absolute time-constituting flow, in which the stream itself is constituted. So the experience-stream, which seemed absolute, is itself constituted in a "last and truly Absolute": the absolute flow of inner time-consciousness. (This is the deepest stratum of Husserl's phenomenology, analyzed in the time-consciousness lectures; here it is only pointed to.) The "absolute" of the reductions is penultimate; the time-constituting flow is ultimate.

He sets the time-riddles aside for the preparatory analyses, then touches on them. Fortunately, he says, we can leave the riddles of time-consciousness out of play in our preparatory analyses, without endangering their rigor. (A footnote notes that his long and long-vain efforts on this were essentially completed in 1905 and communicated in Göttingen university lectures, the time-consciousness lectures.) He then touches on time in a few sentences. So the time-analyses are bracketed for now, but a few essential points are stated. The footnote dates the breakthrough to 1905\.

He states the first point: temporality is a form binding experiences with experiences. The essential property that the title temporality expresses for experiences in general designates not only something generally belonging to every single experience, but a necessary form binding experiences with experiences. Every actual experience (we carry out this evidence on the ground of the clear intuition of an experience-actuality) is necessarily an enduring one; and with this duration it orders itself into an endless continuum of durations, a filled continuum. It has necessarily an all-sidedly infinite filled time-horizon. This says at the same time: it belongs to one infinite "experience-stream." So temporality is not just a feature of each experience but the form that binds all experiences into one stream. Every experience endures, and its duration fits into an endless, filled continuum of durations, an all-sided infinite time-horizon. This is why every experience belongs to one infinite stream: the time-form unifies them all.

He distinguishes the single experience from the stream, and ties time to the ego. Every single experience can, as it can begin, so end, and thereby close off its duration, for example an experience of joy. But the experience-stream cannot begin and end. Every experience, as a temporal being, is experience of its pure ego. Necessarily there belongs to this the possibility (which, as we know, is no empty logical possibility) that the ego directs its pure ego-glance upon this experience and grasps it as actually being, or as enduring in phenomenological time. So individual experiences begin and end, but the stream itself does not (it is endless in both directions). And every experience, as temporal, is the pure ego's, available to the ego's reflective glance as enduring in phenomenological time. This connects time (§81) to the ego-relation (§80) and to reflection (§§77–78).

He states the further point: the time-givenness is itself an experience, and describes the now and the just-past. Again there belongs to the essence of the situation the possibility that the ego directs the glance upon the temporal mode of givenness and recognizes with evidence (as we all in fact gain this evidence, re-living the described in intuition) that no enduring experience is possible unless it constitutes itself in a continuous flux of givenness-modes as the unitary thing of the process, or of the duration; further, that this mode of givenness of the temporal experience is itself again an experience, though of a new kind and dimension. For example, the joy that begins and ends and endures in between, I can first have in the pure glance itself, going along with its temporal phases. But I can also attend to its mode of givenness: to the respective mode of the "now," and to the fact that to this now, and in principle to every now, in necessary continuity, a new and continually new one attaches, that in one with this every actual now changes into a just-now, the just-now again and continuously into ever new just-nows of just-nows, and so on, for every newly attached now. This describes the running-off of time-consciousness. An enduring experience (the joy) is given through a continuous flux of givenness-modes: each now-phase becomes a just-past, which sinks into ever-deeper just-pasts, while ever-new nows attach. And this flux of givenness is itself an experience, of a new dimension (the time-constituting dimension pointed to above). So one can attend either to the joy (going along with it) or to its temporal givenness (the now sinking into the just-past).

He states the form/matter structure of the now and the retentional continuum. The actual now is necessarily, and remains, a punctual thing, an abiding form for ever new matter. Likewise with the continuity of the "just-nows"; it is a continuity of forms of ever new content. This says at the same time: the enduring experience of joy is "consciousness-wise" given in a consciousness-continuum of constant form: one phase, impression, as limit-phase of a continuity of retentions, which are, however, not co-ordinate, but continuously-intentionally to be referred to one another, a continuous one-within-another of retentions of retentions. The form receives ever new content; so continuously to every impression, in which the experience-now is given, a new one attaches, corresponding to a continuously new point of the duration; continuously the impression changes into retention, this continuously into modified retention, and so on. This is the core structure of time-consciousness. The now is an abiding form filled by ever-new content (impression). Around it is a continuity of retentions, not side by side, but nested: retentions of retentions, each holding the just-past as it sinks. The impression continuously becomes retention, the retention a modified (deeper) retention. So consciousness of the enduring joy has a constant form (impression flanked by a retentional continuum) filled with continuously changing content. This is the impression-retention structure (the heart of the time-consciousness analyses, §§77–78 already invoked the terms).

He completes the structure with protention. To this comes, however, the counter-direction of the continuous changes: to the before corresponds the after, to the continuum of retentions one of protentions. So the time-structure is symmetric: as the now sinks into retentions (the just-past), so it is anticipated by protentions (the just-coming). The full living present is impression flanked on both sides by a retentional continuum (backward) and a protentional continuum (forward). This completes the basic form of inner time-consciousness, as far as §81 develops it.

### § 82\. Continuation. The Threefold Experience-Horizon, at the Same Time as the Horizon of Experience-Reflection

Husserl draws out a further point: every experience-now has a horizon of the before and of the after. We recognize thereby also something more. Every experience-now, even that of the onset-phase of a newly appearing experience, has necessarily its horizon of the before. But this can in principle be no empty before, an empty form without content, a nonsense. Necessarily it has the significance of a past now, which in this form grasps a past something, a past experience. Necessarily, to every newly beginning experience, experiences have temporally preceded; the experience-past is continuously filled. But every experience-now has also its necessary horizon of the after, and that too is no empty horizon; necessarily every experience-now, even that of the end-phase of the duration of a ceasing experience, changes into a new now, and that is necessarily a filled one. This adds the horizon-character to the time-structure. Every now carries a horizon of the before (a continuously filled past: experiences always precede) and a horizon of the after (a continuously filled future: a new filled now always follows). These horizons are never empty: there is always a filled past and a filled coming. So even the first phase of a new experience has experiences before it, and even the last phase of a ceasing experience has a new now after it. The stream is gapless.

He states the consequence: no experience can cease without consciousness of its ceasing. One can also say: necessarily there attaches to the consciousness of the now the consciousness of the just-past, which consciousness is itself again a now. No experience can cease without consciousness of the ceasing and the having-ceased, and that is a new filled now. The experience-stream is an infinite unity, and the stream-form is a form necessarily encompassing all experiences of a pure ego, with various systems of forms. This is a striking structural law. An experience cannot simply vanish; its ceasing is itself registered (retained) in a new now. So the stream is necessarily continuous and infinite: every ending is held in consciousness, every now has a filled before and after. The stream-form encompasses all the experiences of one pure ego, organized by various form-systems (the time-form being the most fundamental). Husserl reserves the fuller development and the great metaphysical consequences of these insights for the announced future presentations (the time-consciousness analyses and Book Two).

He generalizes from temporal succession to simultaneity, introducing the threefold horizon. The general peculiarity of the experiences just treated, as possible givennesses of reflecting (immanent) perception, is a component of a still more comprehensive one, which expresses itself in the essential law that every experience stands in an essentially self-contained experience-connection not only under the point of view of temporal succession, but also under the point of view of simultaneity. This says that every experience now has a horizon of experiences that likewise have the originarity-form of the "now," and as such make up the one originarity-horizon of the pure ego, its whole originary consciousness-now. This adds the dimension of simultaneity. Besides the before/after (succession) horizons, every experience has a horizon of simultaneous experiences, those sharing its now-phase. These together make up the pure ego's total originary now: the whole field of what is now actually conscious (the many experiences co-present at this moment). So the "threefold horizon" of the title is the horizon of the before, the after, and the simultaneous. Every experience is embedded in all three: preceded by a filled past, followed by a filled future, and accompanied by a field of simultaneous experiences. And, as the title notes, this is at the same time the horizon of experience-reflection: reflection can turn to any of these (the just-past via retention, the simultaneous via the originary now), which is why reflection has access to the whole stream (§78).

He completes the structure by showing the simultaneity-horizon sinks, as a whole, into the past-modes. This horizon enters as a unity into the past-modes. Every before, as a modified now, implies, for every experience in view whose before it is, an endless horizon, encompassing all that belongs to the same modified now, in short its horizon of the "simultaneously having-been." So not only does the present now have its field of simultaneous experiences; every past now did too, and that whole simultaneity-field sinks together into the past. When I retain a past phase, I retain with it the whole field that was simultaneous with it. So the past is not a thin line of single past nows but a continuum of past total-fields, each a whole simultaneity-horizon sunk into pastness.

He states the full result: the threefold time-field and the correlation of one ego and one stream. The descriptions given before are thus to be supplemented by a new dimension, and only when we do this do we have the whole phenomenological time-field of the pure ego, which it can traverse from any one of "its" experiences according to the three dimensions of the before, after, and simultaneous; or we have the whole stream of temporal experience-unities, unitary by its essence and strictly self-contained. One pure ego, one experience-stream filled in all three dimensions, essentially connected in this filling, demanding itself in its content-continuity: these are necessary correlates. This is the culminating result of §§81–82. The full phenomenological time-field has three dimensions (before, after, simultaneous), traversable by the ego's reflective glance from any of its experiences. The stream is one, strictly self-contained, essentially connected, and continuously filled in all three dimensions. And one pure ego and one such stream are necessary correlates: there cannot be the one without the other. The ego is the identical pole of one stream; the stream is the one stream of one ego. (This correlation, ego and stream, grounds the individuation of each transcendental ego with its own stream, §57, and bears on the later problem of intersubjectivity, the plurality of egos each with its own stream.) With this, the time-structure of consciousness is laid out as far as the preparatory analyses require, and §83 turns to how this one infinite stream can be grasped as a unity, namely as an "Idea."

### § 83\. Grasping the Unitary Experience-Stream as an "Idea"

Husserl first completes the horizon-analysis begun in §82, then turns to how the whole stream is grasped. With this primal form of consciousness stands the following in essential-lawful relation. If the pure ego-glance, reflecting and perceptively grasping, strikes some experience, there is the a priori possibility of turning the glance to other experiences, as far as this connection reaches. But in principle this whole connection is never something given, or to be given, through a single pure glance. So one can always pass from any grasped experience to its neighbors in the stream, but the whole stream can never be caught in one glance. The grasping is always partial, always extendable, never complete in a single act.

He notes that the experience-horizon involves new kinds of givenness-mode, not just the time-horizon. Despite this, the whole connection too is in a certain way, though in a principially different one, intuitively graspable, namely in the manner of the "limitlessness in the progress" of the immanent intuitions, from the fixed experience to new experiences of its experience-horizon, from their fixation to that of their horizons, and so on. But the talk of the experience-horizon here means not only the horizon of phenomenological temporality in its described dimensions, but differences of new kinds of givenness-modes. (A footnote notes that "horizon" here means the same as the "halo" and "background" of §35.) So besides the time-horizon (before, after, simultaneous), there is a further horizon-structure of attention. An experience that has become the object of an ego-glance (the mode of the viewed) has its horizon of unviewed experiences; what is grasped in a mode of "attention," possibly in rising clarity, has its horizon of background inattention, with relative differences of clarity and darkness, of standing-out and not-standing-out. This is the attentional horizon (§§35, 92), distinct from the time-horizon. Around the attended experience lies a field of unattended ones, in various degrees of clarity and prominence.

He notes the eidetic possibilities rooted in this. In this are rooted eidetic possibilities: to bring the unviewed into the pure glance, the noticed-in-passing into the primarily noticed, the not-standing-out into the standing-out, the dark into the clear and ever clearer. So the attentional structure grounds essential possibilities of conversion: the ego can always turn an unattended experience into an attended one, raise a marginal experience to prominence, bring a dark one to clarity. This is the field of attentional modifications (developed in §92).

He states the key result: the stream is grasped as an Idea in the Kantian sense. In the continuous progress from grasping to grasping, we grasp, in a certain way, also the experience-stream as a unity. We do not grasp it like a singular experience, but in the manner of an Idea in the Kantian sense. It is nothing posited and asserted at random, but an absolutely indubitable given, in a correspondingly wide sense of the word givenness. This indubitability, though also grounded on intuition, has a wholly other source than the one that holds for the being of experiences that come to pure givenness in immanent perception. This is the central thesis of §83. The whole stream cannot be perceived (no single glance grasps it). But it can be grasped as an Idea in the Kantian sense: an ideal unity given not by perception but by the insight into the law of endless possible progression (the stream as the ideal limit of the limitless progress from experience to experience). This grasping is indubitable, but its indubitability has a different source from the indubitability of immanent perception. Immanent perception gives a single experience itself; the Idea gives the whole stream as the ideal pole of an endless progression. (The "Idea in the Kantian sense," introduced at §74 for exact essences, is here applied to the stream as a whole: an ideal unity regulating an endless approximation, never given in a single intuition.)

He notes that the Idea-grasping is insightful despite the inadequacy of its content. It is precisely the peculiarity of the ideation that beholds a Kantian "Idea," which does not for that reason lose its insightfulness, that the adequate determination of its content, here of the experience-stream, is unattainable. We see at the same time that to the experience-stream and its components as such belongs a series of distinguishable givenness-modes, whose systematic investigation must form a main task of general phenomenology. This is an important epistemological point. The Idea of the stream is insightfully (evidently) grasped, even though its content can never be adequately given (the whole stream is never fully present). So there is genuine insight into something whose full content is unattainable. This is the mark of the Kantian Idea: insight into an ideal limit, without adequate givenness of the limit itself. And the various givenness-modes (perception, the Idea, the horizon-modes) are themselves a main theme for phenomenology.

He draws the consequence about the non-self-sufficiency of every concrete experience. From our considerations we can also draw the eidetically valid and evident proposition that no concrete experience can count as self-sufficient in the full sense. Every one is "in need of completion" with respect to a connection that is not arbitrary in kind and form, but bound. This applies the part/whole doctrine (§§5, 16\) to experiences. No concrete experience stands alone; each requires, by essential necessity, a bound connection with others (in the stream). An experience torn from all connection with other experiences is impossible. So the experience is non-self-sufficient with respect to the stream-connection.

He illustrates with the house-perception and its experience-environment. For example, consider some outer perception, say this determinate house-perception, taken in concrete fullness; then there belongs to it, as a necessary determining-piece, the experience-environment. But it is a peculiar, necessary, and yet "extra-essential" determining-piece, namely one whose change changes nothing for the own essential content of the experience. According as the determinateness of the environment changes, the perception itself changes, while the lowest difference of the genus perception, its inner ownness, can be thought as identical. This is a subtle distinction. The house-perception necessarily has an experience-environment (the surrounding experiences in the stream). This environment is "extra-essential": its change does not change the perception's own inner essence (the house-perceiving as such stays the same). Yet the perception as a concrete individual does change with its environment, because it is this perception in this stream-position. So the perception has an inner essence (identical across environments) and an environment-relatedness (varying), and as a full concrete individual it includes both.

He draws the principle of individuation from this. That two perceptions essence-identical in this inner ownness are also identical with respect to the environment-determinateness is in principle impossible; they would be individually one perception. One can bring this to insight at any rate with respect to two perceptions, and so two experiences in general, that belong to one experience-stream. Every experience influences the (bright or dark) halo of the further experiences. This is the principle of individuation of experiences. Two experiences with the same inner essence cannot have the same environment (else they would be numerically one). So the environment (the stream-position, the halo of surrounding experiences) individuates the experience. The inner essence is repeatable; the concrete individual, with its unique environment, is not. And every experience affects the halo of the others, so each stream is a web of mutual influence.

He draws the consequence for the plurality of streams (bearing on intersubjectivity). A closer consideration would moreover show that two experience-streams (consciousness-spheres for two pure egos) of identical essential content are unthinkable, as also, as is already to be seen from the foregoing, that no fully determinate experience of the one could ever belong to the other; only experiences of identical inner kind can be common to them (though not individually identically common), never two experiences that moreover have an absolutely like "halo." This bears on the problem of intersubjectivity. Two distinct egos cannot have streams of identical content (they would be one stream, one ego). Two egos can share experiences of the same inner kind (both can perceive a house), but not individually-identical experiences, and never experiences with an absolutely identical halo (since each stream's web of surrounding experiences is unique to it). So each transcendental ego is individuated by its own stream with its own halo-structure. This separates the egos at the transcendental level and sets up the problem (deferred to Book Two and the Cartesian Meditations) of how one ego can nonetheless reach the other.

### § 84\. Intentionality as the Principal Phenomenological Theme

Husserl announces intentionality as the general theme of objectively-oriented phenomenology. We now pass to a peculiarity of the experiences that one can describe outright as the general theme of "objectively" oriented phenomenology: intentionality. It is an essential peculiarity of the experience-sphere in general insofar as all experiences in some way have a share in intentionality, even though we cannot say of every experience in the same sense that it has intentionality, as we can say of every experience that enters the glance of possible reflection as object, even an abstract experience-moment, that it is a temporal one. Intentionality is what characterizes consciousness in the pregnant sense, and what justifies designating the whole experience-stream at the same time as a consciousness-stream and as the unity of one consciousness. This sets intentionality as the central theme. Intentionality (being-consciousness-of-something) characterizes consciousness in the strict sense. Not every experience is intentional in the same way (some are non-intentional moments, like sensation-data, §85), but all share in intentionality, and it is intentionality that makes the stream a consciousness-stream. So intentionality is the principal theme of the objectively-oriented analyses (the noesis/noema analyses to come).

He explains why he recapitulates the earlier results on intentionality, now under the reduction. In the preparatory essence-analyses of the Second Part about consciousness in general, he had to work out (still before the entrance-gate of phenomenology, and specifically for the purpose of winning it through the method of reduction) a series of most general determinations about intentionality in general and about the distinction of the "act," the "cogitatio" (back-reference to §§36–38). He made use of these further on, and was entitled to, although the original analyses were not yet carried out under the express norm of the phenomenological reduction. For they concerned the pure own-essence of the experiences, and consequently could not be affected by switching off the psychological apperception and existence-positing. Since it is now a matter of discussing intentionality as a comprehensive title of pervasive phenomenological structures, and of sketching the problematic essentially related to these structures (insofar as such a thing is possible in a general introduction), we recapitulate what was said earlier, but in a development we need for our now essentially differently directed goals. So §84 takes up the intentionality-analyses of §§36–38, now explicitly within the reduction, and reorganizes them toward the structural analyses (hyle/morphe, noesis/noema) to come. The earlier analyses hold because they concerned the pure essence of experiences, untouched by the reduction.

He recalls the basic sense of intentionality and the explicit cogito. We understood by intentionality the ownness of experiences "to be consciousness of something." First this wonderful ownness, to which all reason-theoretical and metaphysical riddles lead back, met us in the explicit cogito: a perceiving is perceiving of something, say a thing; a judging is judging of a state-of-affairs; a valuing of a value-situation; a wishing of a wish-situation, and so on. Acting goes to action, doing to deed, loving to the loved, rejoicing to the joyful, and so on. In every actual cogito a "glance" radiating from the pure ego directs itself upon the "object" of the respective consciousness-correlate, upon the thing, the state-of-affairs, and so on, and carries out the very various consciousness of it. This restates intentionality (§§36–37). Every act is consciousness of an object; in the explicit cogito the ego's glance is directed upon the object. The range is universal: cognitive, affective, practical acts all have their intentional objects. Intentionality is the source of all the deepest philosophical problems (the "reason-theoretical and metaphysical riddles"), since the relation of consciousness to its object is the root of the problem of knowledge.

He notes that intentionality is present even where there is no actual ego-directedness, citing the objective background.
