But phenomenological reflection taught that not in every experience is this presenting, thinking, valuing ego-turning to be found, this actual occupying-oneself-with-the-correlate-object, being-directed-toward-it (or also away from it, and yet with the glance upon it), while it can nevertheless harbor intentionality in itself. So, for example, it is clear that the objective background, out of which the cogitatively perceived object stands out by receiving the distinguishing ego-turning, is really, experience-wise, an objective background. That is, while we are now turned to the pure object in the mode "cogito," nevertheless many objects "appear," they are intuitively "conscious," flowing together into the intuitive unity of one conscious object-field. This is the key extension (from §35). Intentionality is present even without actual ego-directedness. When I attend to one object, the background objects still "appear," are still intuitively conscious, forming a field. So the background-consciousness is intentional (it is consciousness of the background objects) even though the ego is not directed at them. Intentionality is therefore broader than the actual cogito: it includes the inactual, background consciousness. This distinction (actual / inactual intentionality) is the basis for distinguishing the act in the strict sense (the performed cogito) from the wider intentional consciousness.

He introduces the careful distinction of the act-concept, narrowing the terminology. (The text here distinguishes the experiences that have the distinguishing character as "act" from the broader intentional experiences.) The constant misinterpretations that this act-concept has undergone determine him (here as in his lectures for a number of years) to delimit the terminology somewhat more cautiously, and no longer to use the expressions act and intentional experience without precaution as equivalent. It will turn out further that his original act-concept is quite indispensable, but that it is necessary to take constant account of the modal difference between performed and unperformed acts. Where no addition stands and acts are spoken of simply, the proper, so to speak actual, performed acts are meant exclusively. This refines the terminology. "Act" in the strict sense is the performed cogito (the ego actually directed at the object). But there are also "unperformed" acts (the inactual, background intentional experiences, which are act-like but not actually performed by the ego). Husserl will reserve "act" for the performed cogito unless otherwise noted, while recognizing the modal difference between performed and unperformed. This guards against the misreading that intentionality is always actual directedness; much intentional life is inactual.

He gives a substantial methodological remark on the fluidity of terminology in a beginning science. Quite generally, in beginning phenomenology all concepts, or terms, must in a certain way remain in flux, always on the point of differentiating themselves according to the progress of consciousness-analysis and the knowledge of new phenomenological stratifications within what is at first beheld in undivided unity. All chosen terms have their connection-tendencies; they point to relation-directions of which it often turns out afterward that they have their source not only in one essence-layer, with which it results at the same time that the terminology is better restricted or otherwise modified. So only at a very far-advanced stage of the science can one reckon on definitive terminologies. It is misleading and fundamentally wrong to lay external-formal standards of a logic of terminology on scientific presentations just first working their way up, and to demand in the beginnings terminologies of a kind in which only the concluding results of great scientific developments fix themselves. For the beginning, every expression is good, and especially every suitably chosen pictorial expression, that is able to direct our glance upon a clearly graspable phenomenological occurrence. This is an important methodological defense. In a new science, terms must stay fluid, refining as analysis reveals new strata. Demanding fixed, final terminology at the start (as in the established exact sciences) is wrong: final terms come only at the end. At the start, any expression (including a well-chosen metaphor) is good if it directs the glance to a clearly graspable phenomenon. So Husserl defends his fluid, often pictorial terminology ("glance-ray," "halo," "background") against the demand for rigid definitions.

He adds that clarity does not require the exclusion of a halo of indeterminateness, and criticizes the demand for definitions. Clarity does not exclude a certain halo of indeterminateness. Its further determination, or clarification, is precisely the further task, as on the other hand the inner analysis to be carried out in comparisons or in the change of connections, the decomposition into components or layers. Those who, not satisfied with the offered intuitive exhibitions, demand "definitions" as in the "exact" sciences, or who believe they can operate freely, in a non-intuitive scientific thinking, with phenomenological concepts won as allegedly fixed on a few rough example-analyses, and thereby promote phenomenology, are still so much beginners that they have not grasped the essence of phenomenology and the methodology it in principle requires. This holds no less for the empirically directed psychological phenomenology, in the sense of a description of psychological phenomena binding itself to the immanent-essential. This is a sharp methodological warning. Phenomenological concepts are won by intuitive exhibition, not by definition, and they carry a halo of indeterminateness to be progressively clarified. One must not treat them as fixed definitions and then reason non-intuitively from them (as in deductive science). To do so is to misunderstand phenomenology's method (which is intuitive throughout, §§65, 75). The demand for exact definitions at the start betrays a beginner's misunderstanding.

He closes §84 by affirming intentionality as the indispensable basic concept, however vague at first. The concept of intentionality, grasped in the indeterminate width in which we have grasped it, is a quite indispensable starting- and fundamental concept at the beginning of phenomenology. The general that it designates may, before closer investigation, be ever so vague; it may appear in an ever so great multiplicity of essentially different formations; it may be ever so difficult to set out, in strict and clear analysis, what the pure essence of intentionality actually makes up, which components of the concrete formations actually carry it in themselves, and to which it is inwardly foreign. In any case, experiences are considered under a determinate and most important point of view when we recognize them as intentional and assert of them that they are consciousness of something. In such an assertion, by the way, it is indifferent to us whether it is a matter of concrete experiences or abstract experience-layers, for such too can show the peculiarity in question. This closes §84. Intentionality, however vague at the start, is the indispensable basic concept. The hard analytic work (what exactly intentionality is, which components carry it, which do not) lies ahead (the hyle/morphe and noesis/noema analyses). But recognizing experiences as "consciousness of something" already grasps them under the most important point of view. And intentionality belongs to both concrete experiences and abstract layers (some abstract moments are intentional, others, like the hyletic data, are not, §85). §85 takes up exactly this question, distinguishing the non-intentional sensuous stuff (hyle) from the intentional form (morphe).

### § 85\. Sensuous *hyle*, Intentional *morphe*

Husserl states the level of consideration and the basic distinction. He recalls that intentionality, apart from its puzzling forms and levels, also resembles a universal medium that finally carries in itself all experiences, even those not themselves characterized as intentional. At the level of consideration to which we are bound for now, which refrains from descending into the dark depths of the ultimate consciousness constituting all experience-temporality, and rather takes the experiences as they present themselves as unitary temporal processes in immanent reflection, we must, however, distinguish in principle: first, all the experiences designated in the Logical Investigations as "primary contents"; second, the experiences, or experience-moments, that carry in themselves the specific character of intentionality. This sets up the fundamental distinction of the chapter, at the level of constituted temporality (the deeper time-constituting flow, §81, stays bracketed). Within every experience, two kinds of moment are to be distinguished: the non-intentional "primary contents" (the sensuous stuff) and the intentional moments (the form that makes the experience consciousness-of). A footnote points to the Sixth Investigation, §58, and notes the concept of primary content goes back to the Philosophy of Arithmetic (1891).

He characterizes the first class, the sensuous data. To the former belong certain sensuous experiences, unitary as to their highest genus, "sensation-contents" like color-data, touch-data, tone-data, and the like, which we shall no longer confuse with appearing thingly moments, coloredness, roughness, and so on, which rather "present" themselves experience-wise by means of them. Likewise the sensuous pleasure-, pain-, tickle-sensations, and probably also sensuous moments of the sphere of the "drives." We find such concrete experience-data as components in more comprehensive concrete experiences that are intentional as wholes, and indeed in such a way that over those sensuous moments lies a so to speak "animating," sense-giving layer (or one essentially implying sense-giving), a layer through which, out of the sensuous, which in itself has nothing of intentionality, the concrete intentional experience comes about. This is the core of the doctrine. The sensuous data (color-data, tone-data, touch-data, and the sensuous feelings) are the raw stuff of experience. They must not be confused with the appearing thing-properties: the sensed color-datum is not the objective color of the thing; the color-datum "presents" or adumbrates the objective color (the adumbration doctrine, §41). These data have, in themselves, nothing of intentionality. They become part of an intentional experience only through an "animating," sense-giving layer that lies over them. So the concrete intentional experience is composed of two layers: the sensuous stuff (non-intentional) and the animating, sense-giving form (intentional) that "ensouls" the stuff and makes it a presentation of an object.

He leaves two questions open. Whether such sensuous experiences in the stream always and necessarily carry some "animating apprehension" (with all that this in turn requires and makes possible), or, as we also say, whether they always stand in intentional functions, is not to be decided here. On the other hand, we also leave open at first whether the characters essentially producing intentionality can have concretion without sensuous substrates. So two questions are bracketed: whether every sensuous datum is always animated (always part of an intentional experience), and whether there can be intentional form without sensuous stuff (a "stuffless form"). These are left open. (The second touches the question whether all consciousness has a hyletic basis, or whether some acts, e.g. certain categorial or "empty" acts, are formed without sensuous data.)

He states the dominant role of the stuff/form duality and introduces the terms. In any case, in the whole phenomenological domain (in the whole, within the constantly to be maintained level of constituted temporality) this remarkable duality and unity of sensuous *hyle* and intentional *morphe* plays a dominant role. Indeed, these concepts of stuff and form force themselves upon us outright when we make present to ourselves any clear intuitions or clearly performed valuings, acts of liking, willings, and the like. The intentional experiences stand there as unities through sense-giving (in a very widened sense). Sensuous data give themselves as stuffs for intentional formings or sense-givings of various levels, for simple and peculiarly founded ones, as we shall discuss more closely. How well these ways of speaking fit will be confirmed from another side by the doctrine of the "correlates." As for the possibilities left open above, they would thus be titled as form-less stuffs and stuff-less forms. This fixes the central terminology: *hyle* (the Greek for matter or stuff) and *morphe* (form). Every concrete intentional experience is a unity of sensuous *hyle* (the stuff) and intentional *morphe* (the animating, sense-giving form). The forming is a "sense-giving" in a widened sense (not yet the specific noematic Sinn, but the animating function generally). The two open questions become: are there form-less stuffs (sensuous data not animated) and stuff-less forms (intentional form without sensuous basis)? The Aristotelian *hyle/morphe* language is deliberately borrowed: consciousness is the in-forming of sensuous matter by intentional form.

He gives an extended terminological discussion, explaining why he abandons "primary content" and "sensuous" for a new term. The expression "primary content" no longer seems designating enough. The expression "sensuous experience" is unusable for the same concept, because the general talk of sensuous perceptions, sensuous intuitions, sensuous joy, and the like stands in the way, where not mere hyletic but intentional experiences are designated as sensuous. The talk of "mere" or "pure" sensuous experiences would not improve matters, given its new ambiguities. Besides, there are the ambiguities belonging to the word "sensuous" that remain in the phenomenological reduction. Apart from the double sense that emerges in the contrast of "sense-giving" (sinngebend) and "sensuous" (sinnlich), which, however disturbing it occasionally is, can hardly be avoided, Husserl notes the following. Sensuousness in a narrower sense designates the phenomenological residuum of what is mediated in normal outer perception through the "senses." After the reduction, an essential kinship of the relevant "sensuous" data of the outer intuitions shows itself, and to it corresponds a proper generic essence, a fundamental concept of phenomenology. In the wider and essentially unitary sense, sensuousness also comprises the sensuous feelings and drives, which have their own generic unity and on the other hand probably also an essential kinship of general kind with those sensuousnesses in the narrower sense. Both together forced the old transference of the originally narrower talk of sensuousness to the sphere of feeling and will, namely to the intentional experiences in which sensuous data of the designated spheres appear as functioning "stuffs." This is the careful terminological reasoning. "Primary content" is too vague; "sensuous" is too overloaded (it is used for whole intentional experiences, like "sensuous perception," not just the raw data). The data fall into two groups, sensation-data proper (color, tone, touch) and the sensuous feelings and drives, with a kinship between them. So a new term is needed for the whole group, marked by its common function (being stuff) and its contrast with the forming characters.

He introduces "hyletic data." We need in any case a new term that expresses the whole group through the unity of the function and the contrast to the forming characters, and choose for it the expression hyletic or stuff-like data, also simply stuffs. Where it is a matter of awakening the memory of the old, in their way unavoidable expressions, we say sensuous, also sensible stuffs. So the chosen term is hyletic data (or stuffs). They are defined by their function (being the stuff that is formed) and their contrast with the intentional form. "Sensuous stuffs" is retained as an alternative that recalls the old terminology.

He introduces the correlative term for the forming side, "noesis." What forms the stuffs into intentional experiences and brings in the specific character of intentionality is precisely the same as what gives talk of consciousness its specific sense, according to which consciousness eo ipso points to something of which it is consciousness. Since the talk of consciousness-moments, awarenesses, and all similar formations, and likewise the talk of intentional moments, is, through manifold and later clearly emerging equivocations, quite unusable, we introduce the term noetic moment or, more briefly, noesis. These noeses make up the specific character of the Nous in the widest sense of the word, which leads us back, according to all its actual life-forms, to cogitationes and then to intentional experiences in general, and so encompasses all that (and essentially only that) which is the eidetic presupposition of the idea of the norm. At the same time it is not unwelcome that the word Nous recalls one of its distinguished meanings, namely "sense" (Sinn), although the sense-giving carried out in the noetic moments comprises many things and only as a foundation a sense-giving in the pregnant sense of Sinn. This introduces the key term noesis. The intentional, forming moment is the noesis (from the Greek nous, mind or reason). The noeses are what carries intentionality, what makes the experience consciousness-of-something. They encompass the cogitationes and intentional experiences generally, all that is the eidetic presupposition of reason (the "idea of the norm"). The word's echo of "sense" (Sinn) is welcome, since the noeses are sense-giving, though sense-giving in the noetic moments is broader than the specific noematic Sinn (the noesis does many things, of which giving Sinn in the strict sense is one foundational layer). So noesis names the forming, animating, sense-giving side of the experience, correlative to the hyletic stuff.

He considers and rejects calling the noetic side "psychic," via the Brentano discussion. There would be good ground to designate this noetic side of the experiences as the psychic. For upon what intentionality brings in, the glance of the philosophical psychologists was directed with a certain preference in the talk of psyche and the psychic, while the sensuous moments were assigned to the body and its sense-activities. This old tendency finds its newest expression in Brentano's distinction of "psychic" and "physical phenomena." It is especially significant because it became path-breaking for the development of phenomenology, although Brentano himself remained far from the phenomenological ground, and although with his distinction he did not strike the one he actually sought, namely the distinction of the experience-domains of the physical natural sciences and of psychology. This places phenomenology in relation to Brentano. Brentano's distinction of psychic from physical phenomena (the psychic being the intentional, the "physical phenomena" being the sensory contents) was path-breaking: it first identified intentionality as the mark of the mental, and so opened the way to phenomenology. But Brentano did not reach the phenomenological ground (he remained in the natural attitude, treating the psychic empirically), and his distinction did not really mark off the domains of natural science and psychology as he intended. So Husserl honors Brentano as the source of the intentionality-concept while marking his limits.

He declines "psychic" as a term, for its ambiguities and empirical associations. (The intervening text notes that the prevailing concepts of the psychic are bound to the body and empirically regulated, with disposition-concepts as mere indices for these regulations.) But however that may be, the existing ambiguities, and above all the circumstance that the prevailing concepts of the psychic do not aim at the specifically intentional, make the word unusable for us. So "psychic" is rejected as a term for the noetic side, because in psychology it is tied to the empirical, body-bound psyche and does not specifically pick out intentionality. Husserl keeps "noetic" instead.

He states the result: the stream has a stuff-layer and a noetic layer. We therefore stay with the word noetic and say: the stream of phenomenological being has a stuff-like and a noetic layer. Phenomenological considerations and analyses that go specially to the stuff-like can be called hyletic-phenomenological, as on the other hand those relating to noetic moments noetic-phenomenological. The incomparably more important and richer analyses lie on the side of the noetic. This is the concluding statement of §85. The phenomenological stream has two layers, the hyletic (stuff) and the noetic (form). Analysis divides accordingly into hyletic phenomenology (of the sensuous data) and noetic phenomenology (of the intentional form). The noetic analyses are far the richer and more important, since they concern intentionality, the heart of consciousness. (The hyletic data are relatively few and simple; the noetic structures, the ways of being conscious-of, are inexhaustibly rich.) This sets the agenda: the analyses to come are primarily noetic (and, correlatively, noematic).

### § 86\. The Functional Problems

Husserl announces the greatest problems: the functional problems, the problems of constitution. But the very greatest problems are the functional problems, or those of the "constitution of the consciousness-objectivities." They concern the way in which, for example with respect to nature, noeses, animating the stuff-like and interweaving into manifold-unitary continua and syntheses, bring about consciousness of something, so that objective unity of the objectivity can therein unanimously "announce itself," "verify itself," and be "rationally" determined. This identifies the supreme theme. The functional problems are the problems of constitution: how the noeses, by animating the hyletic stuff and synthesizing into continua, bring about consciousness of an object, so that one and the same objective unity is given, confirmed, and rationally determined through the manifold of consciousness. This is the central problem of all phenomenology: how objectivity is constituted in and for consciousness.

He explains the special sense of "function" here. "Function" in this sense (totally different from that of mathematics) is something quite unique, grounded in the pure essence of the noeses. Consciousness is precisely consciousness "of" something; it is its essence to harbor in itself "sense," so to speak the quintessence of "soul," "spirit," "reason." Consciousness is not a title for "psychic complexes," for fused "contents," for "bundles" or streams of "sensations," which, senseless in themselves, also could yield no "sense" in any arbitrary blend, but it is through and through "consciousness," source of all reason and unreason, all right and wrong, all reality and fiction, all value and disvalue, all deed and misdeed. Consciousness is thus toto coelo different from what sensualism alone wants to see, from the in fact in itself senseless, irrational stuff, accessible, however, to rationalization. What this rationalization means we shall soon learn to understand better. This is a powerful anti-sensualist statement. "Function" means the constitutive accomplishment of the noeses (utterly different from the mathematical function-concept). Consciousness is not a bundle of senseless sensations (as sensualism, the empiricist tradition from Hume, holds); no blend of senseless data could ever yield sense. Consciousness is essentially sense-giving, the source of all reason, value, reality, and their opposites. The hyletic stuff is indeed in itself senseless and irrational, but it is accessible to rationalization, that is, to being formed and made sense of by the noeses. So sensualism sees only the senseless stuff and misses the sense-giving form, which is the essence of consciousness.

He states that the functional point of view is the central one of phenomenology. The point of view of function is the central one of phenomenology; the investigations radiating from it span pretty much the whole phenomenological sphere, and finally all phenomenological analyses enter somehow into its service as components or sub-levels. In place of the analysis and comparison, description and classification, sticking to the individual experiences, comes the consideration of the individualities under the "teleological" point of view of their function of making synthetic unity possible. This is a methodological turning-point. Phenomenology is not finally a matter of cataloguing individual experiences (analyzing, comparing, classifying them like specimens), but of understanding them functionally, under the "teleological" point of view of how they serve to constitute objective unity. The constitutive (functional) consideration is the central and organizing one; all other analyses serve it. So the goal is not a static inventory of consciousness but an understanding of its constitutive accomplishment.

He describes what the functional consideration studies. The consideration turns to the consciousness-manifolds, as it were prefigured in the experiences themselves, in their sense-givings, in their noeses in general, and as it were to be drawn out of them: so, for example, in the sphere of experience and experiential thinking, to the manifoldly-shaped consciousness-continua and set-off connections of consciousness-experiences that are connected in themselves through sense-belonging-together, through the unitarily spanning consciousness of the one and the same objective thing, appearing now in this, now in that way, giving itself intuitively, or determining itself thought-wise. It seeks to investigate how the selfsame, how objective, not reell immanent unities of every kind are "conscious," "meant," how to the identity of the meant belong consciousness-formations of very various and yet essentially required structure, and how these formations would be methodically strictly described. This describes the constitutive analysis concretely. It studies how one and the same object is constituted through a manifold of consciousness: the many appearances (the house seen from many sides), the many acts (perceiving, remembering, thinking the same object), all bound together by the unity of the object meant through them. The object is an identical unity (not a reell part of consciousness) meant through an essentially-structured manifold of consciousness-formations. The task is to describe these formations strictly. This is the constitution of the object as identical pole of a manifold (the analysis carried out for the thing in §§41–44, now generalized).

He extends it to the reason-problems (truth and verification). It further seeks to investigate how, corresponding to the double title "reason" and "unreason," unity of the objectivity of every objective region and category can and must "verify itself" and "reject itself" consciousness-wise, determine itself in the forms of the thinking-consciousness, "more closely" determine or "otherwise" determine, or be rejected entirely as "null" appearance. In connection with this stand all the distinctions under the trivial and yet so puzzling titles: "actuality" and "appearance," "true reality," "apparent reality," "true values," "apparent and disvalues," and so on, whose phenomenological clarification connects here. This extends constitution to the reason-problems (the theme of the Fourth Part, §§136ff.). Constitution is not only how an object is meant, but how it is verified or refuted: how consciousness confirms or rejects its object as real or illusory, true or false, valuable or worthless. So the constitution-problem includes the problem of reason (truth, verification, evidence). The distinctions actuality/appearance, true/apparent reality and value are to be clarified phenomenologically, by exhibiting the consciousness-structures in which an object verifies or annuls itself.

He states the universal task of constitution and the correlation-law. In the most comprehensive generality, then, it is a matter of investigating how objective unities of every region and category "constitute themselves consciousness-wise." It is a matter of showing systematically how, through their essence, all the connections of actual and possible consciousness of them are prefigured, precisely as essential possibilities: the simple or founded intuitions intentionally related to them, the thought-formations of lower and higher level, the confused or clear, the explicit or non-explicit, the pre-scientific and scientific, up to the highest formations of strict theoretical science. All fundamental kinds of possible consciousness, and the modifications, fusions, syntheses essentially belonging to them, are to be studied systematically in eidetic generality and phenomenological purity and made insightful: how through their own essence they prefigure all being-possibilities (and being-impossibilities); how, by absolutely fixed essential laws, an existing object is correlate for consciousness-connections of quite determinate essential content, just as, conversely, the being of such connections is equivalent to an existing object; and that always related to all being-regions and all levels of generality down to being-concretion. This is the full statement of the constitution-program and the central correlation-law. For every kind of object, the whole range of possible consciousness of it is prefigured by the object's essence (an essential law correlates each kind of object with the consciousness-structures in which it can be given). And conversely, the being of such consciousness-connections is equivalent to the being of the object: an existing object just is the correlate of a certain harmonious system of consciousness (recalling §§49, 55). So object and consciousness-system are strict correlates, by fixed essential law, for every region and at every level. This is the master-principle of constitutive phenomenology: the a priori correlation of object and consciousness.

He names the science transcendental phenomenology. In its purely eidetic attitude, "switching off" all transcendencies, phenomenology comes, on its own ground of pure consciousness, necessarily to this whole complex of the transcendental problems in the specific sense, and therefore it deserves the name transcendental phenomenology. On its own ground it must come to consider the experiences not like arbitrary dead things, like "content-complexes" that merely are but mean nothing, intend nothing, according to elements, complex-formations, classes, and sub-classes, but to take hold of the principially peculiar problematic that they present as intentional experiences, and present purely through their eidetic essence, as "consciousness-of." This justifies the name "transcendental phenomenology." Because phenomenology, on its pure ground, necessarily reaches the constitution-problems (the "transcendental problems" of how objectivity is constituted for consciousness), it is transcendental. It does not treat experiences as dead content-complexes (the sensualist error) but grasps them as intentional, as consciousness-of, and studies how they constitute their objects. The transcendental problematic (constitution) is what makes phenomenology transcendental.

He subordinates pure hyletics to the transcendental phenomenology of consciousness. Naturally, pure hyletics subordinates itself to the phenomenology of transcendental consciousness. It has, by the way, the character of a self-contained discipline, has as such its value in itself; on the other hand it has significance from the functional point of view, in that it furnishes possible insertions into the intentional weave, possible stuffs for intentional formings. Not only as regards the difficulty, but also as regards the rank of the problems from the standpoint of the idea of an absolute knowledge, it stands obviously far below the noetic and functional phenomenology (which two, by the way, are not really to be separated). So hyletics (the study of the sensuous data) is a self-contained discipline with its own value, but subordinate: it matters mainly because it supplies the stuffs for the intentional formings. The noetic and functional (constitutive) phenomenology ranks far higher, since it concerns intentionality and constitution. Husserl announces that he now proceeds to closer treatments in a series of chapters.

He adds an Annotation distinguishing his terms from Stumpf's. The word "function" in the combination "psychic function" is used by Stumpf, in his important Berlin Academy treatises, in contrast to what he calls "appearance." The distinction is meant as a psychological one and then coincides with our (only just psychologically-turned) contrast of "acts" and "primary contents." It is to be noted that the terms in question have in our presentations a wholly other meaning than in the revered researcher. Superficial readers of the writings on both sides have already several times thrown together Stumpf's concept of phenomenology (as doctrine of the "appearances") with ours. Stumpf's phenomenology would correspond to what was above determined as hyletics, only that our determination is essentially conditioned in its methodical sense by the comprehensive frame of transcendental phenomenology. On the other hand, the idea of hyletics transfers eo ipso from phenomenology to the ground of an eidetic psychology, into which, on our conception, Stumpf's "phenomenology" would order itself. This Annotation distinguishes Husserl's usage from Stumpf's. Stumpf's "phenomenology" (the doctrine of sensory "appearances") corresponds only to Husserl's hyletics, not to phenomenology as a whole. And Stumpf's discipline belongs really to eidetic psychology, not to transcendental phenomenology. The terms "function" and "phenomenology" mean something quite different for Husserl. (Stumpf was Husserl's teacher; the Annotation is respectful but clarifies the divergence.) With §86, the framework of the constitutive (functional) problematic is set, and the Third Chapter turns to the detailed analysis of the noesis–noema correlation, beginning at §87.

## Third Chapter: Noesis and Noema

### § 87\. Preliminary Remarks

Husserl opens the central chapter by contrasting the ease of naming intentionality with the difficulty of analyzing it. The peculiarity of the intentional experience is, in its generality, easily designated; we all understand the expression "consciousness of something," especially from arbitrary exemplifications. So much the harder are the phenomenological essence-peculiarities corresponding to it to grasp purely and correctly. That this title circumscribes a great field of laborious establishments, and indeed eidetic establishments, seems even today to be something foreign to the majority of philosophers and psychologists (if we may judge by the literature). This sets up the chapter's task and tone. Everyone understands "consciousness of something"; but to analyze the essential structures of intentionality is hard, laborious, eidetic work that most have not even recognized as a field. The chapter will carry out the most fundamental of these analyses, the noesis–noema distinction.

He warns against two cheap ways of seeming to do the work. For with this almost nothing is done, that one says and sees that every presenting relates to something presented, every judging to something judged, and so on; or that one moreover points to logic, epistemology, ethics with their many evidences, and now designates these as belonging to the essence of intentionality. That is at the same time a very simple way of claiming the phenomenological essence-doctrine as something age-old, as a new name for the old logic and the disciplines that might be set equal to it. For without having grasped the peculiarity of the transcendental attitude and really appropriated the pure phenomenological ground, one may indeed use the word phenomenology, but one does not have the thing. This guards against two errors. First, merely noting that every act has an object (presenting has the presented, and so on) does almost nothing; that is just the bare starting point. Second, pointing to the a priori truths of logic, epistemology, ethics, and calling these "phenomenology," wrongly reduces phenomenology to a new name for old logic. Phenomenology requires the transcendental attitude and the pure phenomenological ground; without these one has the word but not the thing.

He adds that even performing the reduction does not by itself turn logic into phenomenology. Moreover, the mere change of attitude, the mere carrying-out of the phenomenological reduction, does not suffice to make something like phenomenology out of pure logic. For in how far logical, and likewise purely ontological, purely ethical, and whatever other a priori propositions one may cite, really express something phenomenological, and to which phenomenological layers the same may in each case belong, that is by no means at hand. On the contrary, it harbors the most difficult problems, whose sense is naturally hidden to all those who have no inkling yet of the decisive fundamental distinctions. This is an important methodological point. The a priori truths of logic, ontology, ethics are not automatically phenomenological just because one performs the reduction. How and to which phenomenological layer each such truth belongs is itself a hard problem. (For instance, a logical law about propositions concerns the noematic layer; clarifying this is difficult work, not a given.) So phenomenology is not old logic relabeled, but a new investigation of how such truths are grounded in the structures of consciousness.

He speaks from his own experience of the long road. In fact it is (if I may allow myself a judgment from my own experience) a long and thorny way that leads from the purely logical insights, from meaning-theoretical, ontological, and noetic insights, likewise from the ordinary normative and psychological epistemology, to the grasping of immanent-psychological and then phenomenological givennesses in the genuine sense, and finally to all the essence-connections that make the transcendental relations a priori understandable to us. Similar holds wherever we may set in, to win, from objective insights, the way to the essentially belonging phenomenological ones. This is a candid autobiographical remark. The path from logic and ordinary epistemology to genuine phenomenological insight is long and thorny (Husserl's own path from the Logical Investigations onward). The general lesson: from any objective insight (a truth about objects), reaching the corresponding phenomenological insight (about the constituting consciousness) is hard work, not automatic.

He notes the skepticism that the difficulty breeds, and the obstacle of the wrong attitude. So "consciousness of something" is something very self-evident and yet at the same time highly unintelligible. The labyrinthine wrong paths into which the first reflections lead easily generate a skepsis that negates the whole uncomfortable problem-sphere. Not a few block their own entrance already by not being able to bring themselves to grasp the intentional experience, for example the perception-experience, with the essence proper to it as such. They do not manage, instead of living in the perception, turned contemplating and theorizing to the perceived, rather to direct the glance upon the perceiving, or upon the peculiarities of the givenness-mode of the perceived, and to take what presents itself in immanent essence-analysis as it gives itself. This identifies the basic obstacle. "Consciousness of something" is self-evident yet baffling. The first reflections lead into a labyrinth, breeding a skepticism that denies the whole problem. The root obstacle is the inability to shift the glance: from living in the perception (absorbed in the perceived object) to reflecting on the perceiving itself, or on the givenness-mode of the perceived. One must turn from the object to the act and its manner of giving the object, and take what shows itself as it shows itself. This reflective turn is exactly what §§77–79 secured.

He states the reward of the right attitude: firm, shareable, verifiable results. If one has won the right attitude and secured it through practice, but above all if one has won the courage, in radical freedom from prejudice, unconcerned with all current and learned theories, to follow the clear essence-givennesses, then firm results soon arise, and for all who are likewise attuned the same ones; there arise firm possibilities of communicating what one has oneself seen to others, of checking their descriptions, of bringing the unnoticed admixtures of empty word-meanings to prominence, of making errors recognizable and eradicating them, errors that here too, as in every validity-sphere, are possible, by re-measuring against intuition. But now to the matters. This is the methodological assurance that closes §87. With the right attitude and radical freedom from prejudice (following the clear essence-givennesses, ignoring inherited theories), one reaches firm results, the same for all who attain the attitude. These results are communicable, checkable, correctable: descriptions can be shared and verified, empty word-meanings exposed, errors caught by re-measuring against intuition. So phenomenology is a genuine science, intersubjectively valid and self-correcting, not private intuition. With this, Husserl turns to the matters themselves, beginning the noesis–noema analysis.

### § 88\. Reell and Intentional Experience-Components. The Noema

Husserl introduces the fundamental distinction between the reell components of the experience and its intentional correlate. Going, as in the present considerations generally, for the most general distinctions, graspable so to speak right at the threshold of phenomenology and determining for all further methodical procedure, we strike at once, with respect to intentionality, upon a quite fundamental one: the distinction between proper (reell) components of the intentional experiences and their intentional correlates, or the components of these. Already in the eidetic preliminary considerations of the Second Part we touched on this distinction (back-reference to §41). It served to make clear, in the transition from the natural to the phenomenological attitude, the own-being of the phenomenological sphere. But that it gains, within this sphere itself, within the frame of the transcendental reduction, a radical significance conditioning the whole problematic of phenomenology, of that there could be no talk there. This introduces the master-distinction of the chapter. Within intentionality, two sides must be distinguished: the reell components of the experience (the parts really, actually contained in the experience as a temporal mental process) and the intentional correlate (the object as meant, which is not a reell part of the experience). This distinction, touched on at §41 (the sensed color-datum is reell, the appearing color is not), now becomes radical and organizes the whole problematic. The reell is what is genuinely in the experience; the intentional correlate (the noema, introduced below) is the object-as-meant, which is in the experience only intentionally, not reell.

He characterizes the two sides: reell analysis and the "of something." On the one side, then, we have to distinguish the parts and moments that we find through a reell analysis of the experience, treating the experience as an object like any other, asking after its pieces or non-self-sufficient moments reell building it up. On the other side, however, the intentional experience is consciousness of something, and is so by its essence, for example as memory, as judgment, as will, and so on; and so we can ask what is to be essentially asserted on the side of this "of something." This distinguishes two kinds of analysis. Reell analysis treats the experience like any object, asking for its real parts and moments (the hyletic data, the noetic moments, all really contained in it). Intentional analysis asks after the "of something," the object-side: what the experience is consciousness of, and how. These are two directions of inquiry, the reell (what is in the experience) and the intentional (what the experience is of).

He describes the noetic moments, the sense-giving on the reell side. Every intentional experience is, thanks to its noetic moments, precisely noetic; it is its essence to harbor in itself something like a "sense," and possibly a manifold sense, and on the basis of these sense-givings, and in one with them, to carry out further accomplishments that thereby become precisely "sense-ful." Such noetic moments are, for example: glance-directions of the pure ego upon the object "meant" by it in virtue of the sense-giving, upon the one that "lies in its sense"; further, grasping of this object, holding-fast while the glance has turned to other objects that have entered the "meaning"; likewise accomplishments of explicating, relating, taking-together, the manifold position-takings of belief, surmise, valuing, and so on. All this is to be found in the relevant experiences, however differently built and in themselves changeable. This describes the noetic (reell, act) side. The noeses are the sense-giving moments and the accomplishments founded on them: the ego's glance-directing to the meant object, grasping it, holding it fast, explicating it, relating it, and the position-takings (belief, surmise, valuing). These are reell components of the experience, the acts of sense-giving and position-taking. They are richly structured and changeable.

He turns to the correlate, introducing the perception of a blossoming apple tree, and the natural-attitude relation. (The text develops the example.) Suppose we are in a garden and direct ourselves with pleasure to a blossoming apple tree. In the natural attitude, the apple tree is for us something existing in the transcendent space-reality, and the perception, as well as the pleasure-valuing, a psychic state of us real human beings. Between the one and the other real human and the real apple tree there exist real relations. In certain cases it is said, in such an experience-situation, that the perception is "mere hallucination," the perceived, this apple tree before us, does not exist in "actual" reality. Now the real relation, before meant as actually existing, is disturbed. The perception alone remains; there is nothing actual there to which it relates. This sets up the contrast between the natural and the phenomenological consideration. Naturally, the apple tree is a real thing in space, the perceiving and the pleasure are real psychic states, and there are real relations between the person and the tree. If it is a hallucination, the real relation breaks down: the tree is not there, only the perception remains. So the natural attitude makes the perception's relation to its object a real relation between two real things, which can fail (hallucination).

He performs the reduction and shows that a relation remains. Now we pass into the phenomenological attitude. The transcendent world receives its "bracket"; we practice, with respect to its actual being, the epoché. We now ask what is essentially to be found in the complex of noetic experiences of the perception and the pleasing valuation. With the whole physical and psychic world, the actual existence of the real relation between perception and perceived is switched off; and yet there has obviously remained a relation between perception and perceived (as likewise between pleasure and pleasing), a relation that comes to essence-givenness in "pure immanence," namely purely on the ground of the phenomenologically reduced perception- and pleasure-experience, as it orders itself into the transcendental experience-stream. This is the decisive move. After the reduction, the real relation (between the real person and the real tree) is bracketed. Yet a relation remains: the perception is still perception of its object, the pleasure still pleasure in its object. This remaining relation is not a real relation between two realities but an essential relation given in pure immanence, internal to the reduced experience itself. The experience still has its object, but now the object as meant, not the object as posited reality.

He notes that hallucination, in its natural role, is bracketed, and that everything stays as it was. It may be that phenomenology has something to say also about hallucinations, illusions, deceptive perceptions in general, and perhaps very much; but it is evident that these, here, in the role they played in the natural attitude, fall to the phenomenological switching-off. Here we have no question of the kind to put to the perception, or to an arbitrarily continuing perception-connection (as when we ambulando consider the blossoming tree), whether something in "the" actuality corresponds to it. This thetic actuality is, after all, judgmentally not there for us. And yet everything remains, so to speak, the old. Even the phenomenologically reduced perception-experience is perception of "this blossoming apple tree, in this garden, and so on," and likewise the reduced pleasure is pleasure in this same thing. The tree has not forfeited the slightest nuance of all the moments, qualities, characters with which it was appearing in this perception, "in" this pleasure "more beautiful," "more charming," and the like. This is a crucial clarification. After the reduction, the question whether the perceived tree really exists is not asked (the thetic actuality is bracketed). Whether it is veridical or hallucinatory does not matter for the phenomenological analysis. And yet nothing of the content is lost: the reduced perception is still perception of exactly this blossoming apple tree in this garden, with all its appearing qualities, and the pleasure still finds it beautiful and charming. So bracketing the tree's existence does not strip the perception of its object-as-meant; the full perceptual content (the tree as perceived, in all its nuances) remains as a theme for analysis.

He states the eidetic question and names the noema. In our phenomenological attitude we can and must put the essence-question: what the "perceived as such" is, which essence-moments it harbors in itself, as this perception-noema. We obtain the answer in pure devotion to the essentially given; we can describe the "appearing as such" faithfully, in perfect evidence. Only another expression for this is: "to describe the perception in noematic respect." This introduces the noema. The "perceived as such" (the tree exactly as it is perceived, with all its appearing characters) is the perception-noema: the intentional correlate, the object-as-meant. It is not the real tree (which is bracketed) but the tree-as-perceived, an essential component of the reduced experience, describable faithfully and evidently. To describe the perceived-as-such is to describe the perception in noematic respect (its object-side), as opposed to the noetic respect (its act-side). So every intentional experience has its noema, the object-as-meant, correlative to its noesis, the act of meaning. This is the central concept of the chapter and of Husserl's mature phenomenology: the noema, the objective sense, the intentional correlate given in pure immanence. The detailed doctrine of the noema, its relation to the real object, its core and characters, occupies the following sections, beginning with §89's distinction between noematic statements and reality-statements.

### § 89\. Noematic Statements and Reality-Statements. The Noema in the Psychological Sphere

Husserl draws out the radical sense-modification that noematic description undergoes. It is clear that all these describing statements, although they can sound the same as reality-statements, have undergone a radical sense-modification; just as the described itself, although it gives itself as "exactly the same," is yet a radically other, so to speak by a reversing change of sign. "In" the reduced perception (in the phenomenologically pure experience) we find, as belonging unremovably to its essence, the perceived as such, to be expressed as "material thing," "plant," "tree," "blossoming," and so on. The quotation marks are obviously significant; they express that change of sign, the corresponding radical meaning-modification of the words. This is the central point of §89. A noematic statement ("the perceived is a blossoming tree") can sound exactly like a reality-statement ("there is a blossoming tree"), but it means something radically different. The words are now in quotation marks: they describe the tree-as-perceived (the noema), not the tree as a real thing. The same words, the same described, but a reversed sign: object-as-meant versus object posited as real.

He gives the decisive contrast: the tree can burn, the sense cannot. The tree simpliciter, the thing in nature, is nothing less than this tree-perceived as such, which as perception-sense belongs to the perception, and inseparably. The tree simpliciter can burn up, dissolve into its chemical elements, and so on. But the sense, the sense of this perception, something necessarily belonging to its essence, cannot burn up; it has no chemical elements, no forces, no real properties. This is one of the most famous passages in the book. The real tree and the perception-sense (the noema, the tree-as-perceived) are toto caelo different. The real tree can burn; the sense cannot burn, has no chemical elements, no real properties. The noema is not a real thing; it is the objective sense, an irreal correlate belonging to the experience. So one must never confuse the noema (the perceived-as-such) with the real object (the tree simpliciter). They can be described with the same words, but they are radically different in being-status.

He states the abyss separating the immanent-reduced from all nature and psychology. Everything that is purely immanently and reductively proper to the experience, what cannot be thought away from it as it is in itself and in eidetic attitude eo ipso passes over into the Eidos, is separated by abysses from all nature and physics, and no less from all psychology, and even this image, as naturalistic, is not strong enough to indicate the difference. So the noematic sense, as a purely immanent component of the reduced experience, is separated by an abyss from everything real (natural, physical, psychological). The "abyss" image (recalling §49, the abyss of sense between consciousness and reality) is even too weak. The noema belongs to the absolute sphere of consciousness, not to the world.

He notes that the perception-sense belongs also to the unreduced perception, giving the reduction a methodical use for the psychologist. The perception-sense belongs of course also to the phenomenologically unreduced perception (the perception in the sense of psychology). One can here at the same time make clear how the phenomenological reduction can win, for the psychologist, the useful methodical function of fixing the noematic sense in sharp distinction from the object simpliciter, and of recognizing it as belonging inseparably to the psychological essence of the (then really apprehended) intentional experience. This is an important bridge to psychology. The noema belongs to the perception even in the natural (psychological) attitude; the reduction simply makes it visible as such, by bracketing the real object. So even the psychologist can use the reduction methodically, to distinguish the noematic sense (the object-as-meant) from the real object, and to recognize the sense as an inseparable component of the intentional experience. (This anticipates the role of the reduction in phenomenological psychology.)

He fixes the rule for noematic description: take only what really appears, exactly as it appears. On both sides, in psychological as in phenomenological attitude, it is to be kept sharply in view that the "perceived" as sense includes in itself nothing (so that nothing may be imputed to it on the basis of "indirect knowledge") except what in the perceptually appearing actually "appears" in the given case, and exactly in the mode, in the givenness-way, in which it is precisely conscious in the perception. Upon this sense, as it is immanent to the perception, a peculiar reflection can direct itself at any time, and only to what is grasped in it does the phenomenological judgment have to adapt itself in faithful expression. This fixes the descriptive rule. The noema (the perceived-as-such) contains only what actually appears, exactly as it appears, in the given mode of givenness. One may not add anything from "indirect knowledge" (what one knows about the tree but does not now perceive). The phenomenological description must adapt faithfully to what a peculiar reflection (the noematic reflection) grasps in the sense. So noematic description is strict: only the appearing, only as it appears, nothing imputed.

### § 90\. The "Noematic Sense" and the Distinction of "Immanent" and "Actual Objects"

Husserl generalizes: every intentional experience has its noematic sense. Similar to perception, every intentional experience has, and this precisely makes up the fundamental piece of intentionality, its "intentional object," that is, its objective sense. In other words: to have sense, or to have something "in one's sense," is the fundamental character of all consciousness, which is therefore not only experience in general, but sense-having, "noetic." This generalizes the result of §§88–89. Every intentional experience (not just perception) has its objective sense, its intentional object. Having sense (having something "in mind") is the fundamental character of consciousness; consciousness is essentially noetic, sense-having.

He notes that this "sense" does not yet exhaust the full noema. Of course, what in our example-analysis has stood out as "sense" does not exhaust the full noema; accordingly, the noetic side of the intentional experience does not consist merely of the moment of the proper "sense-giving," to which specially the "sense" belongs as correlate. It will soon show that the full noema consists in a complex of noematic moments, that in it the specific sense-moment forms only a kind of necessary core-layer, in which further moments are essentially founded, which we were only therefore allowed to designate likewise, but in an extended sense, as sense-moments. This is an important structural preview. The "sense" identified so far is only the core of the noema. The full noema is a complex: a core-layer (the objective sense, the "object in the how of its determinations") plus further founded moments (the characters, such as the givenness-modes perceptual/remembered/phantasied, and the position-characters certain/doubtful/etc.). These further moments are also called "sense-moments" in an extended sense. So the noema has a layered structure: core sense plus characters. (This structure is developed in §§99–117.)

He recalls the situation defining "sense" and connects it to the scholastic distinction. The intentional experience is, we showed, doubtless so constituted that, with a suitable glance-setting, a "sense" is to be drawn from it. The situation defining this sense for us, namely the circumstance that the non-existence (or the conviction of the non-existence) of the presented or thought object simpliciter cannot rob the relevant presentation (and so the respective intentional experience in general) of its presented as such, that therefore between the two a distinction must be made, could not remain hidden. The distinction, as so striking, had to express itself in the literature. In fact, the scholastic distinction between "mental," "intentional," or "immanent" object on the one hand and "actual" object on the other points back to it. But from a first grasping of a consciousness-distinction to its correct, phenomenologically pure fixing and correct evaluation is an enormous step, and precisely this step decisive for a unanimous, fruitful phenomenology has not been carried out. This connects to the scholastic tradition (Brentano's "intentional inexistence" derives from it). The scholastics noticed that the object's non-existence does not deprive the act of its "presented as such" (a hallucinated tree is still presented). So they distinguished the "intentional/immanent object" from the "actual object." But they did not take the decisive step of fixing this distinction phenomenologically and correctly. Husserl's noema is the correct working-out of what the scholastic distinction groped at.

He warns that the very names "immanent" and "mental object" mislead, and rejects the idea that the intentional object inheres reell in the experience. The decisive thing lies above all in the absolutely faithful description of what really lies present in phenomenological purity, and in keeping at a distance all interpretations transcending the given. Namings here already betray interpretations, and often very false ones. Such betray themselves in expressions like "mental," "immanent" object, and are at least promoted by the expression intentional "object." It lies all too near to say: given in the experience is the intention with its intentional object, which as such belongs inseparably to it, hence inheres reell in it itself. It is and remains, after all, its meant, presented, and the like, whether the corresponding "actual object" be in actuality or not, have meanwhile been annihilated, and so on. This identifies the tempting error. It is tempting to say the intentional object is a reell part of the experience (inheres in it), a kind of "immanent" or "mental" object inside the mind, which is there whether or not the real object exists. Husserl rejects this. The noema is not a reell part of the experience (an inner mental object). It is the object-as-meant, which is not a second real thing inside the mind.

He refutes the "two objects" / inner-image construction by the regress argument. If we try in this way to separate actual object (in the case of outer perception, the perceived thing of nature) and intentional object, the latter as "immanent" to the perception, inhering reell in the experience, we get into the difficulty that now two realities are supposed to stand opposed, while only one is findable and possible. The thing, the natural object, I perceive, the tree there in the garden; that and nothing else is the actual object of the perceiving "intention." A second immanent tree, or even an "inner image" of the actual tree standing before me out there, is in no way given, and to suppose such hypothetically leads only to countersense. The image as a reell piece in the psychologically real perception would again be a real thing, a real thing functioning as image for another. But that it could do only by virtue of an image-consciousness, in which something first appeared (with which we would have a first intentionality), and this in turn functioned consciousness-wise as "image-object" for another (for which a second intentionality, founded in the first, would be necessary). But it is no less evident that each single one of these consciousness-modes already requires the distinction between immanent and actual object, hence encloses in itself the same problem that was to be solved by the construction. This is the regress argument (the same that defeated the image-theory at §43, §52). Putting an "immanent tree" or "inner image" inside the experience creates two trees where there is only one. And the inner image could function as image only through an image-consciousness, which itself has an intentional object, requiring another image, and so on to infinity. So the construction is circular and generates an infinite regress. There is no inner image; there is one tree, perceived, and the noema is not a second thing but the tree-as-perceived.

He concludes that the actual object is to be bracketed, and describes what bracketing the position-takings yields. Against such aberrations we have to hold to what is given in the pure experience and to take it in the frame of clarity exactly as it gives itself. The "actual" object is then to be "bracketed." Beginning as naturally attuned humans, the actual object is the thing out there; we see it, stand before it, describe it, make our statements about it, take a position in valuing, handle it. If we now carry out the phenomenological reduction, every transcendent positing, above all the one lying in the perception itself, receives its switching-off bracket, and this passes over to all the founded acts, every perceptual judgment, the value-positing grounded in it, and so on. Therein lies: we allow ourselves only to consider all these perceptions, judgments, and so on, as the essences they are in themselves, to fix what is evidently given at or in them; but we permit no judgment that makes use of the thesis of the "actual" thing, of the whole "transcendent" nature, that "goes along with" it. As phenomenologists we abstain from all such positings. We do not therefore throw them away when we do "not place ourselves on their ground," "not go along with them." They are there, belong essentially to the phenomenon. Rather we look at them; instead of going along with them, we make them objects, we take them as components of the phenomenon, the thesis of the perception precisely as its component. This restates the reduction as applied within the noetic-noematic analysis. The reduction brackets the perception's own positing of the real thing (and all founded positings). The positings are not discarded but made objects: we study the perception's thesis (its positing of the object as real) as a component of the phenomenon, without going along with it. So the position-character (the believing, the positing-as-real) becomes itself a noematic theme (the "actuality-character" of the noema, §§103ff.), studied without being shared.

### § 91\. Transference to the Widest Sphere of Intentionality

Husserl extends the noema-doctrine to all kinds of acts. What has so far been carried out under preference for perception now holds really of all kinds of intentional experiences. In memory we find, after the reduction, the remembered as such; in expectation the expected as such; in inventing phantasy the phantasied as such. So every act has its noema: memory has the remembered-as-such, expectation the expected-as-such, phantasy the phantasied-as-such. The noema-structure is universal across all act-types.

He notes that the noematic sense differs in kind across act-types, even with a common core. To each of these experiences a noematic sense "dwells in." And however this sense may be related in different experiences, indeed possibly essence-alike as to a core-stock, it is in any case, in kind-different experiences, a different-kind one; the possibly common is at least otherwise characterized, and that in necessity. It may everywhere be a matter of a blossoming tree, and everywhere this tree may appear in such a way that the faithful description of the appearing as such necessarily proceeds with the same expressions. But the noematic correlates are therefore yet essentially different for perception, phantasy, pictorial re-presentation, memory, and so on. Once the appearing is characterized as "bodily actuality," another time as fictum, then again as memory-re-presentation, and so on. This is a crucial refinement. The same tree may be the core sense of a perceiving, a remembering, a phantasying (the "object in the how of its determinations" is the same). But the full noemata differ, because the tree is characterized differently: as bodily-actual (in perception), as fictum (in phantasy), as past (in memory). So the noema has a common core (the objective sense) plus a differentiating character (the givenness-mode). The core can be identical across acts; the character necessarily differs.

He names these characters and locates them in the full noema. These are characters that we find at the perceived, phantasied, remembered, and so on, as such, at the perception-sense, the phantasy-sense, the memory-sense, as something inseparable, and as necessarily belonging in correlation to the relevant kinds of noetic experiences. Where it is a matter of describing the intentional correlates faithfully and completely, we must co-grasp all such never accidental, but essence-lawfully regulated characters, and fix them in strict concepts. We notice hereby that within the full noema we must distinguish essentially different layers, which group around a central "core," around the pure "objective sense," around what in our examples could everywhere be a describable with purely identical objective expressions, because in the parallel kind-different experiences it could be an identical. This fixes the layered structure of the full noema. The full noema has a central core (the pure objective sense, identical across act-types) surrounded by layers of characters (the givenness-modes and, later, the position-characters), which differ by act-type and are essence-lawfully regulated. Faithful description must capture both the core and the characters.

He draws the parallel distinction among kinds of unmodified objectivity, and the one central object. We see at the same time that, parallel, if we remove again the bracketings carried out on the theses, corresponding to the different concepts of sense, different concepts of unmodified objectivities must be distinguishable, of which the "object simpliciter," namely the identical that is once perceived, another time directly re-presented, a third time pictorially presented in a painting, and the like, only indicates one central concept. This indicates the correlation between the noematic core and the object simpliciter. Corresponding to the core sense is the "object simpliciter" (the identical object meant through all the different acts). The various senses point back to various concepts of objectivity, of which the "object simpliciter" (the identical pole) is the central one. (This sets up the later distinction, §§129–131, between the "sense" and the "object in the how" and the pure object-pole, the "determinable X.")

He announces the program of surveying the main consciousness-modes to confirm the correlation. We look around somewhat further in the consciousness-sphere and try to get to know the noetic-noematic structures at the principal consciousness-modes. In the actual demonstration we assure ourselves at the same time, step by step, of the pervasive validity of the fundamental correlation between noesis and noema. So the following sections (§§92ff.) survey the main act-types (attention, the modes of belief, the doxic and other modalities) to exhibit the noetic-noematic structures and confirm, throughout, the master-correlation: to every noetic feature corresponds a noematic feature, and conversely. This is the program of the rest of the chapter.

### § 92\. The Attentional Changes in Noetic and Noematic Respect

Husserl takes attention as the first structure crossing all others, recalling the glance-ray. We have already spoken several times, in our preparatory chapters, of a kind of remarkable consciousness-changes that cross with all other kinds of intentional occurrences and so make up a quite general consciousness-structure of its own dimension: we speak in the image of the "mental glance" or "glance-ray" of the pure ego, of its turnings-to and turnings-away. The relevant phenomena came to unitary, perfectly clear and distinct prominence for us. They play, wherever "attention" is spoken of, the main role, without phenomenological separation from other phenomena, and mixed with these they are designated as modes of attention. We, for our part, want to hold to the word and moreover speak of attentional changes, but exclusively with reference to the occurrences distinctly separated by us. This introduces attention (the glance-ray, §§35, 37, 92\) as a structure crossing all act-types. Attention is the turning and directing of the ego's glance-ray. The ordinary notion of "attention" mixes several phenomena; Husserl isolates the attentional changes proper, the modifications of the glance-ray.

He characterizes the attentional changes: they presuppose a noetic core, do not change the noematic achievements from themselves, yet modify the whole experience on both sides. It is here a matter of a series of ideally possible changes that already presuppose a noetic core and characterizing moments of various genus necessarily belonging to it, that from themselves do not change the relevant noematic achievements, and that yet present modifications of the whole experience on its noetic as well as its noematic side. The glance-ray of the pure ego goes now through this, now through that noetic layer, or (as for example with memories in memories) through this or that nesting-level, now straight ahead, now reflecting. Within the given total field of potential noeses, or noetic objects, we look now at a whole, the tree say, that is perceptively present, now at this or that part and moment of it; then again at a neighboring thing, or at a many-shaped connection and process. Suddenly we turn the glance to a memory-object "occurring" to us: instead of through the perception-noese, which in continuously unitary way, though manifoldly articulated, constitutes for us the constantly appearing thing-world, the glance goes through a memory-noese into a memory-world, moves wandering in it, passes into memories of other levels, or into phantasy-worlds, and so on. This describes the wandering of the glance-ray. Attention is the ego's glance-ray traversing the field of potential noeses: it can rest on a whole (the tree) or its parts, move to a neighboring thing, or suddenly pass through a memory-noese into a memory-world. The attentional change is this redirecting of the glance, which presupposes the noetic content (the noeses, with their noemata) but redistributes the ego's attending across it.

He fixes the example and states the noematic constancy under mere attention-shift. Let us stay, for simplicity, in one intentional layer, in the perception-world, which stands there in simple certainty. Let us fix in the Idea a perceptually conscious thing or thing-process with respect to its noematic content, just as we fix the whole concrete consciousness of it in the corresponding section of the phenomenological duration, as to its full immanent essence. Then to this Idea belongs also the fixing of the attentional ray in its belonging determinate wandering. For it too is an experience-moment. It is then evident that change-modes of the fixed experience are possible, which we designate under the title "mere changes in the distribution of attention and its modes." It is clear that thereby the noematic content of the experience remains the same insofar as it can now everywhere be said: it is the same objectivity continually characterized as bodily existing, presenting itself in the same appearance-modes, the same orientations, appearing marks; of it the such-and-such content-stock is conscious in the same modes of indeterminate indication and non-intuitive co-presentation, and so on. This is the key descriptive result. Under a mere shift of attention, the noematic core stays the same: the same object, the same appearance-modes, the same orientations, the same content. What changes is only the distribution of attention over this constant content.

He describes what the attentional change consists in: preferring, primary/secondary noticing. The change consists, we say, lifting out and comparing parallel noematic states, merely in this, that in the one comparison-case this, in the other that objective moment is "preferred," or that one and the same is once "primarily attended to," the other time only secondarily, or only "just still co-noticed," where not even "completely unnoticed," although still appearing. There are precisely various modes specially belonging to attention as such. Thereby the group of actuality-modes separates from the mode of inactuality, from what we simply call inattention, the mode of so to speak dead being-conscious. This describes the attentional modes. Within the constant noematic content, attention varies by preferring some moments over others: a moment can be primarily attended, secondarily attended, just co-noticed, or unnoticed (though still appearing). So there is a scale from primary attention to complete inattention. The actuality-modes (the attended) separate from the inactuality-mode (the unattended, the "dead" being-conscious).

He shows the attentional changes also affect the noema, by the light-image. On the other hand, it is clear that these modifications are not only such of the experience itself in its noetic content, but that they also affect its noemata, that they present, on the noematic side, regardless of the identical noematic core, a proper genus of characterizations. One is accustomed to compare attention with an illuminating light. What is attended to in the specific sense is in the more or less bright light-cone, but it can also move into the half-shadow and into full darkness. As insufficient as the image is to mark off all the modes to be phenomenologically fixed, it is so far designating as it indicates changes at the appearing as such. This illumination-change does not change the appearing according to its own sense-content, but brightness and darkness modify its appearance-mode; in the glance-direction upon the noematic object they are findable and to be described. This is the key claim: attention modifies the noema too. The light-image: the attended is in the bright cone, the unattended in shadow or darkness. This illumination does not change the object's sense-content (the object stays the same), but it modifies its appearance-mode, its givenness. So the attentional characters are noematic characters (modes of givenness of the noema), not just noetic ones. There is an attentional modification on both sides, noetic (the glance-ray) and noematic (the illumination of the appearing).

He notes the modifications are not external annexes but pervade the concrete noema. Evidently the modifications in the noema are not such that to an identically remaining thing mere external annexes are added; rather the concrete noemata change through and through; it is a matter of necessary modes of the givenness-mode of the identical. So attention does not just add a label to an unchanged noema; it pervades the concrete noema. The identical core is given differently (in different attentional modes), and these are necessary modes of the givenness of the identical. (The core stays identical; its mode of givenness changes throughout.)

He qualifies: the attentional core is not strictly constant, since some noeses presuppose attention. More closely seen, the situation is nevertheless not that the entire noematic content attentionally characterized in the respective mode (so to speak the attentional core) is a constant to be maintained against arbitrary attentional modifications.
