Rather it shows, seen from the noetic side, that certain noeses, whether necessarily or as to their determinate possibility, are conditioned by modes of attention, and in particular by positive attention in the distinguishing sense. All act-performances, the actual position-takings, for example the performance of a doubt-decision, a rejection, a subject-positing and predicating positing-thereon, the performance of a valuing and a valuing for the sake of another, that of a choice, and so on, all that presupposes positive attention upon that to which the ego takes position. This is an important qualification. Some acts require attention: one cannot perform a judgment, a valuing, a choice, without positively attending to what one judges or values. So attention is not merely an external overlay; certain higher acts (the position-takings) presuppose it. This means the attentional modification is not wholly independent of the act-content; raising or lowering attention can enable or disable certain acts.

He states the result: attention is a peculiar dimension of correlative noetic and noematic modifications. But that changes nothing in the fact that this function of the wandering glance, expanding and narrowing with respect to its span, means a peculiar dimension of correlative noetic and noematic modifications, whose systematic essence-investigation belongs to the fundamental tasks of general phenomenology. So attention is a dimension of correlative noetic-noematic modifications: every change of attention is at once a noetic change (the glance-ray) and a noematic change (the illumination of the appearing). Its systematic study is a fundamental task. This confirms the noesis-noema correlation for attention: the two sides vary together.

He connects attention to subjectivity and the ego's living in acts. The attentional formations have, in their actuality-modes, in a distinguished way the character of subjectivity, and this same character then all the functions gain that are modalized precisely by these modes, or that presuppose them as to their kind. The attending ray gives itself as radiating from the pure ego and terminating in the objective, directed to it or deflecting from it. The ray does not separate from the ego, but is itself and remains ego-ray. The "object" is affected, target-point, only set in relation to the ego (and by it itself), but not itself "subjective." A position-taking that carries the ego-ray in itself is thereby an act of the ego itself; the ego does or suffers, is free or conditioned. The ego, we also expressed it, "lives" in such acts. This living does not mean the being of any "contents" in a content-stream, but a manifold of describable ways how the pure ego, in certain intentional experiences that have the general mode of the cogito, lives therein as the free "being" it is. The expression "as free being" says, however, nothing other than such life-modes of going-out-of-itself-freely or going-back-into-itself, of spontaneous doing, of experiencing, suffering something from the objects, and so on. What proceeds outside the ego-ray, or the cogito, in the experience-stream is essentially otherwise characterized; it lies outside the ego-actuality and yet has, as we have already indicated earlier, ego-belonging insofar as it is the field of potentiality for free acts of the ego. This connects attention to the ego (§§80, 84). The attentional ray is the ego-ray; it does not separate from the ego. A position-taking carrying the ego-ray is an act of the ego, in which the ego "lives" as a free being. This "living" is not contents in a stream but the ego's modes of spontaneous doing, experiencing, suffering. What lies outside the ego-ray (the background) is not ego-actual but is the field of potentiality for the ego's free acts. So attention is the ego's actual life in its acts, against the background of its potential field.

He closes §92 by deferring the full phenomenology of attention. So much for the general characteristic of the noetic-noematic themes that must be treated in the phenomenology of attention with systematic thoroughness. So the full phenomenology of attention (the systematic study of its noetic-noematic modes) is announced as a task and deferred. §93 turns from attention to the noetic-noematic structures of the higher consciousness-spheres (judgment, the modalities), continuing the survey that confirms the noesis-noema correlation throughout.

### § 93\. Transition to the Noetic-Noematic Structures of the Higher Sphere of Consciousness

Husserl moves from the simplest acts to the founded, higher acts, noting that the position-takings themselves become noematic objects. (The section opens by recalling that the position-takings, when made the theme of a further reflective investigation, themselves become something objective, of their own dignity.) The position-takings, themselves becoming, in the reflective investigation about them, again an objective, though of its own dignity, have their givenness-modes, their "characters," their manifold modes, with which they are conscious in the full noema of the relevant noetic experience, or of the relevant experience-kind. Naturally, here too, parallel to all the separations in the noema, ones in the unmodified objectivity must correspond. This sets up the transition. The position-takings (believing, doubting, valuing, willing) can themselves be made objects of reflection. When they are, they show their own characters and givenness-modes, and these appear in the full noema. So the higher acts have their own noetic-noematic structure, parallel to that of the simple acts, and to every noematic distinction there corresponds one in the unmodified objectivity.

He states the task of distinguishing what is bound by the kind and what by the special differences, and affirms the pervasive correlation. It is then further a matter of closer phenomenological study to establish what, for the noemata of the changing particularizations of a fixed kind (for example perception), is essence-lawfully bound precisely by the kind, and what by the differentiating particularizations. But the binding goes through; in the essence-sphere there are no accidents, everything is connected through essence-relations, so in particular noesis and noema. This is a methodological program and a key principle. For each act-kind, one must distinguish what its noema owes to the kind as such (perception in general) from what it owes to the particular case. And the master-principle: in the essence-sphere there are no accidents; everything is bound by essential relations, above all noesis and noema. So the noetic-noematic correlation is not a contingent pairing but an essential law: every noetic feature is essentially correlated with a noematic feature. This is the thesis the following analyses confirm.

### § 94\. Noesis and Noema in the Realm of Judgment

Husserl takes the predicative judgment as the example of a founded act. Consider, as an example from this sphere of founded essences, the predicative judgment. The noema of judging, that is, of the concrete judgment-experience, is the "judged as such," but that is nothing other, or at least as to its main core nothing other, than what we ordinarily simply call the judgment. So the judgment-noema is the "judged as such," which is essentially what we ordinarily call "the judgment" (the proposition judged). The concrete act of judging has its noema, the judged content.

He distinguishes the judged (noema) from the judged-about (the substrates). To grasp the full noema, it must really be taken in the full noematic concretion in which it is conscious in the concrete judging. The judged is not to be confused with the judged-about. When the judging builds itself on the basis of a perceiving or some other simply "positing" presenting, then the noema of the presenting enters into the full concretion of the judging (just as the presenting noesis becomes an essence-component of the concrete judgment-noesis) and takes on certain forms in it. The presented (as such) receives the form of the apophantic subject or object, and the like. For simplicity we abstract here from the higher layer of verbal "expression." These "objects-about-which," in particular the subject-objects, are the judged-about. This makes a crucial distinction. The judged (the noema, the whole proposition-as-meant) is not the same as the judged-about (the objects the judgment is about, the subject-objects). When I judge "this tree is blossoming," the tree is what the judgment is about (the substrate, the judged-about); the whole proposition "this-tree-is-blossoming" is what is judged (the noema). The underlying presentations (of the tree) enter the judgment-noema as its subject-members, taking the apophantic form (subject, object). So the judgment-noema is built on the presentation-noemata, which become its formed members.

He defines the full judgment-noema as the judged "what" in its givenness-mode. The whole formed out of them, the entire judged "what," and moreover taken exactly so, with the characterization, in the givenness-mode in which it is conscious in the experience, forms the full noematic correlate, the (most widely understood) "sense" of the judgment-experience. More precisely, it is the "sense in the how of its givenness-mode," so far as this is findable at it as character. This defines the full judgment-noema. It is the whole judged "what" (the proposition), taken exactly with its characterization and givenness-mode. Husserl's precise formula: the noema is "the sense in the how of its givenness-mode" (der Sinn im Wie seiner Gegebenheitsweise). This formula (the object/sense in the how of its being-given) becomes the standard characterization of the full noema: not just the bare sense, but the sense as given, in its mode of givenness and its characters.

He applies the reduction and opposes the concrete judgment-noesis to the judgment-noema as Eidos. But the phenomenological reduction is not to be overlooked, which demands of us, if we want to gain precisely the pure noema of our judgment-experience, to "bracket" the judging. If we do so, then there stand opposed in phenomenological purity the full concrete essence of the judgment-experience, or, as we now express it, the concretely-as-essence-grasped judgment-noesis, and the belonging and necessarily united-with-it judgment-noema, that is, the "passed judgment" as Eidos, again in phenomenological purity. So after bracketing the act of judging (its positing of the state-of-affairs as real), one has, purely, the judgment-noesis (the concrete judging-essence) and its judgment-noema (the passed judgment as Eidos). The judgment-noema is the proposition-as-meant, taken as essence, in pure immanence.

He addresses the psychologists and the need for several Ideas, recalling the Logical Investigations. The psychologists will everywhere take offense; they are already not inclined to distinguish between judging as empirical experience and judgment as "Idea," as essence. This distinction needs no further grounding for us. But even one who accepts it will be struck. For it is demanded of him to recognize that with this one distinction there is by no means a sufficiency, and that the fixing of several Ideas is needed, which lie in the essence of the judgment-intentionality on two different sides. It must above all be recognized that here, as with all intentional experiences, the two sides, noesis and noema, must be distinguished in principle. This presses the noesis-noema distinction in the judgment-sphere. It is not enough to distinguish the empirical judging from the judgment as Idea (essence). One must distinguish several Ideas, on two sides: the noetic Idea (judging as such, as essence) and the noematic Idea (the judgment as such, the proposition). So in the judgment-sphere there are at least two essences: the judging-essence (noetic) and the judgment-essence (noematic, the proposition).

He criticizes his own Logical Investigations for a one-sided noetic reading, and locates the pure-logical concept of the proposition. Critically it is to be noted here that the concepts of "intentional" and "epistemic essence" fixed in the Logical Investigations (Fifth Investigation, §21) are correct, but capable of a second interpretation, insofar as they can in principle be understood as expressions not only of noetic but also of noematic essences, and that the noetic apprehension, as it was there one-sidedly carried out, is precisely not the relevant one for the conception of the pure-logical judgment-concept (the concept that pure logic in the sense of pure mathesis requires, in contrast to the noetic judgment-concept of normative logical noetics). The distinction, already asserting itself in ordinary speech, between the passing of a judgment and the passed judgment can point to the right thing, namely that to the judgment-experience there belongs correlatively the judgment simpliciter as noema. This is an important self-correction. In the Logical Investigations, the "intentional essence" and "epistemic essence" of an act were read one-sidedly as noetic (act-essences). But the pure-logical concept of the proposition (the proposition that pure logic studies) is noematic, not noetic. The ordinary distinction between passing a judgment (the act) and the passed judgment (the proposition) points to this: the proposition (noema) is correlated to the judging (noesis). So pure logic studies the noematic proposition, while normative logical noetics studies the noetic judging.

He identifies the pure-logical proposition with the noematic core, and notes the difference from the full noema, with the Bolzano footnote. This would then be understood as the "judgment," or the proposition in the pure-logical sense, only that pure logic is not interested in the noema in its full stock, but in the same insofar as it is thought as exclusively determined by a narrower essence, to whose closer determination the above-mentioned separation-attempt of the Logical Investigations has shown the way. If we want, starting from a determinate judgment-experience, to gain the full noema, we must, as said above, take "the" judgment exactly as it is conscious in this experience, while in the formal-logical considerations the identity of "the" judgment reaches much further. An evident judgment S is P and the "same" blind judgment are noematically different, but identical as to a sense-core, which alone becomes determining for the formal-logical consideration. It is a similar difference as the one already touched, between the noema of a perception and that of a parallel re-presentation, which has the same object in exactly the same determination-content, in the same characterization (as "certainly being," "doubtfully being," and the like) present. The act-kinds are different, and there remains otherwise a large play-room for phenomenological differences, but the noematic What is identical. This is a crucial refinement. The pure-logical proposition is not the full noema but a narrower core: the sense-core that is identical across, say, an evident and a blind judging of "S is P." Formal logic abstracts to this identical sense-core, ignoring the further noematic differences (evidence, givenness-mode). So the full noema is richer than the pure-logical proposition; the latter is the identical core that formal logic alone considers. The Bolzano footnote notes that Bolzano's "proposition in itself" (Satz an sich) was a path-breaking but unclear conception: Bolzano never saw that there are two possible interpretations, the noetic Idea (the specific of the judgment-experience) and the correlative noematic Idea, and his descriptions waver between them. So Husserl credits Bolzano with the pioneering concept of the objective proposition while noting his failure to distinguish the noetic from the noematic.

He adds the noetic Idea as the correlative basic concept of formal noetics. We add that to the just-characterized judgment-Idea, which makes up the fundamental concept of formal logic (the discipline of the mathesis universalis related to predicative meanings), there stands correlatively opposed the noetic Idea: "the judgment" in a second sense, namely understood as judging in general, in eidetic and purely-form-determined generality. It is the fundamental concept of the formal noetic doctrine of the right of judging. So there are two formal disciplines: formal logic (mathesis), which studies the noematic proposition (the judgment as meant-content), and formal noetics (the doctrine of the right of judging), which studies the noetic judging (judging as act-form). Each has its fundamental concept, the proposition (noematic) and the judging (noetic).

He generalizes to the modal variants of judgment and distinguishes two concepts of judgment-content. All that we have just carried out holds also for other noetic experiences, for example, of course, for all that are essence-related to the judgments as predicative certainties: thus the corresponding surmises, conjectures, doubts, also rejections; whereby the agreement can go so far that in the noema an everywhere identical sense-content appears, which is only equipped with different "characterizations." The "same" S is P, as noematic core, can be "content" of a certainty, of a surmising-as-possible, or of a conjecturing, and so on. In the noema the "S is P" does not stand alone; rather, as it is there thought out as content, it is a non-self-sufficient; it is in each case conscious in changing characterizations that the full noema cannot do without: it is conscious in the character of the "certain" or the "possible," the "probable," the "null," and the like, characters to which the modifying quotation mark belongs throughout, and which as correlates are specially coordinated to the noetic experience-moments of holding-for-possible, holding-for-probable, holding-for-null, and the like. There separate hereby, as one sees at the same time, two fundamental concepts of "judgment-content," and likewise of surmise-content, question-content, and so on. Not seldom the logicians use the talk of judgment-content so that obviously (though without the so necessary distinction) the noetic or noematic-logical concept of judgment is meant, the two concepts, that is, a content that the judgment can have in common, identically, with a surmise, a question, and other act-noemata or noeses. This is the doctrine of the doxic modalities and the sense-core. The same sense-core "S is P" can be characterized as certain, possible, probable, doubtful, null, and so on. These characters are correlated to the noetic modes (holding-for-certain, \-possible, etc.). So the noema has a sense-core (the "S is P") plus a position-character (certain/possible/etc.). And the "content" common across these modes (and across judgment, surmise, question) is the sense-core. This anticipates the systematic treatment of the doxic modalities (§§103ff.) and the sense-core as the identical content underlying the modal variations.

### § 95\. The Analogous Distinctions in the Emotional and Volitional Sphere

Husserl extends the noesis-noema structure to feeling and will, noting the layering. Analogous expositions hold then, as one easily convinces oneself, for the emotional and volitional sphere, for experiences of liking and disliking, of valuing in every sense, of wishing, deciding, acting; all these are experiences that contain manifold and often multiple intentional layerings, noetic and accordingly also noematic. Thereby the layerings are, generally speaking, such that uppermost layers of the total phenomenon can fall away without the rest ceasing to be a concretely complete intentional experience, and that conversely a concrete experience can take on a new noetic total-layer; as when, for example, on a concrete presentation a non-self-sufficient moment "valuing" layers itself, or conversely falls away again. So the emotional and volitional acts (valuing, wishing, willing) have the same noesis-noema structure, but with founded layering. A valuing is founded on a presenting (one values what one presents); the valuing-layer can be added to or removed from the underlying presentation. So these acts are multi-layered: a founding presentation-layer plus a founded valuing- or willing-layer.

He analyzes the value-noema and introduces the distinction of mere thing and value-objectity. When in this way a perceiving, phantasying, judging, and the like founds a layer of valuing that wholly covers it, then we have in the founding-whole, designated according to the highest level as a concrete value-experience, different noemata, or senses. The perceived as such belongs, as sense, specially to the perceiving, but it enters into the sense of the concrete valuing, founding its sense. We must accordingly distinguish: the objects, things, properties, states-of-affairs that stand in the valuing as valued, or the corresponding noemata of the presentations, judgments, and the like that found the value-consciousness; on the other hand, the value-objects themselves, the value-situations themselves, or the noematic modifications corresponding to them, and then in general the complete noemata belonging to the concrete value-consciousness. This analyzes the value-noema. A concrete valuing is founded on a presenting; so its noema has two strata: the underlying sense (the mere thing valued, the perceived-as-such) and the value-sense proper (the thing as valuable, the value-object). So the value-noema includes both the mere thing and its value-character (Wertheit).

He introduces relative terms to keep the strata distinct. For clarification, it is good (here and in all analogous cases) to introduce distinguishing relative terms, to keep valued object and value-object, valued state-of-affairs and value-state-of-affairs, valued property and value-property (which is itself again double-sensed) better separated. We speak of the mere "thing" (Sache) that is valued, that has value-character, Wertheit; over against this, of the concrete value itself or the value-objectity. Likewise parallel, of the mere state-of-affairs, or the mere situation, and the value-state-of-affairs, or the value-situation, namely where the valuing has a state-of-affairs-consciousness as founding substrate. The value-objectity implies its thing; it brings in, as a new objective layer, the Wertheit. The value-state-of-affairs harbors in itself the belonging mere state-of-affairs, the value-property, likewise the thing-property and beyond that the Wertheit. This fixes the value-terminology. The "mere thing" (Sache) is the value-free substrate; the "value-objectity" is the thing plus its value-character (Wertheit). The value-objectity implies and includes the mere thing, adding the value-layer. So value is a founded objective layer: the valuable thing is the mere thing with value-character added.

He applies the noema-distinction to value: the valued-as-such, with the value-being bracketed. It is further also here to be distinguished between the value-objectity simpliciter and the value-objectity in quotation marks, which lies in the noema. As to the perceiving the perceived as such stands opposed in a sense that excludes the question of the truth-being of the perceived, so to the valuing the valued as such, and again so that the being of the value (of the valued thing and its truly being-value) remains out of question. All actual positings are to be switched off for the grasping of the noema. And again it is well to note that to the full "sense" of the valuing belongs the what of it with the whole fullness in which it is conscious in the relevant value-experience, and that the value-objectity in quotation marks is not without further ado the full noema. So the value-noema (the valued-as-such, the value-objectity "in quotation marks") brackets the question whether the thing really is valuable, just as the perception-noema brackets whether the perceived really exists. The full value-noema is the whole valued-what in its fullness, of which the bracketed value-objectity is the core.

He extends to the will, giving the will-noema. Likewise the carried-out distinctions transfer in the volitional sphere. On the one side we have the deciding, which we in each case carry out, with all the experiences it requires as substrate and which, taken in concretion, it encloses in itself. To it belong many kinds of noetic moments. The will-positings have underlying them valuing positings, thing-positings, and the like. On the other side we find the decision (Entschluss) as a proper kind of objectity specifically belonging to the volitional domain, and it is one evidently founded in other such noematic objectities. If we, as phenomenologists, switch off all our positings, then again there remains to the will-phenomenon, as phenomenologically pure intentional experience, its "willed as such," as a noema proper to the willing: the "will-meaning," and exactly as it is "meaning" in this will (in the full essence), and with all that, what and "whither" is willed. So the will has its noema: the willed-as-such (the will-meaning), the decision as a founded objectity, with the act of willing bracketed. The volitional sphere has the same noesis-noema structure, founded on valuing and thing-positing.

He discusses the term "meaning" (Meinung) and confines it to the narrower spheres. We just said "meaning" (Meinung). This word forces itself here upon us, just as the words "sense" and "signification." To the meaning or intending corresponds then the meaning (Meinung), to the signifying the signification (Bedeutung). However, these words are all afflicted with so many equivocations through transference, and not least with such as stem from the gliding-over into these correlative layers, whose scientific separation is to be strictly carried out, that with respect to them the greatest caution is in place. Our considerations now move in the widest compass of the essence-genus "intentional experience." The talk of "meaning," however, normally restricts itself to narrower spheres, which at the same time function as sub-layers of the phenomena of the wider. As a term, the word (and the expressions related to it) will therefore be able to come into consideration only for these narrower spheres. For the generalities, our new terms and the appended example-analyses certainly do us better service. This is a terminological caution. "Meaning" (Meinung), "signification" (Bedeutung) are tempting words for the noema, but they are equivocal and normally restricted to the narrower (predicative, linguistic) spheres. So Husserl reserves them for those narrower spheres and uses the new terms (noesis, noema, sense) for the general structures. (This anticipates §§124ff., where Sinn and Bedeutung are distinguished, Bedeutung being reserved for the expressed, linguistic-conceptual sense.)

### § 96\. Transition to the Further Chapters. Concluding Remarks

Husserl explains why he has taken such care with the noesis-noema distinction. We have bestowed such great care on the general working-out of the distinction between noesis (that is, the concretely complete intentional experience, designated under emphasis of its noetic components) and noema, because its grasping and mastery is of the greatest reach for phenomenology, indeed for its right grounding downright decisive. At the first moment it seems to be a matter of something self-evident: every consciousness is consciousness of something, and the consciousness-modes are very different. On closer approach, however, we felt the great difficulties. They concern the understanding of the being-mode of the noema, the way in which it is supposed to "lie" in the experience, to be "conscious" in it. They concern quite especially the clean separation between what, in the way of reell components, is a matter of the experience itself, and what is a matter of the noema, to be measured to it as its own. Also the correct articulation in the parallel construction of noesis and noema makes afterward still enough difficulties. This states the stakes and the difficulties. The noesis-noema distinction is decisive for grounding phenomenology. It looks self-evident but harbors great difficulties: the being-mode of the noema (how it "lies in" the experience without being a reell part), the separation of the reell (the experience's own parts) from the noematic (the object-as-meant), and the parallel articulation of the two sides. These are the hard problems the following chapters address (the structure of the noema, §§97ff.).

He notes the special difficulty of carrying the distinctions into the emotional acts. Even when we have already happily carried out main pieces of the distinctions belonging here at the presentations and judgments, where they first present themselves, and for which logic delivers valuable but not remotely sufficient preparatory work, it takes some effort and self-overcoming to bring the parallel distinctions at the emotional acts not only to postulate and assert, but really to clear givenness. So the noesis-noema distinction is easiest to see in the presentation and judgment spheres (where logic has done preparatory work), and hardest in the emotional sphere, where it must be brought to clear givenness by effort, not merely postulated. This is why §§94–95 took such care with the emotional and volitional analogues.

He states the aim of the following chapters and the method. It cannot here, in the connection of our merely upward-leading meditations, be the task to carry out pieces of phenomenology systematically. Still, our goals require going deeper than before into the matters and sketching beginnings of such investigations. That is necessary in order to bring the noetic-noematic structures so far to clarity that their significance for the problematic and methodology of phenomenology can become understandable. A content-rich representation of the fruitfulness of phenomenology, the greatness of its problems, the kind of its procedure, can only be won if domain after domain is really entered and the problem-breadths belonging to them are made visible. But each such domain is really entered, and made perceptible as a firm working-ground, only through the carrying-out of phenomenological separations and clarifications, with which also the sense of the problems to be solved here can first become understandable. This states the method of the rest of the book. The aim is not a complete system but enough of the noetic-noematic structures to show their significance for phenomenology's problems and method. And the only way to convey phenomenology's fruitfulness is to actually enter domain after domain, carrying out the concrete separations and clarifications. So the following chapters give worked specimens of phenomenological analysis, not a finished system.

He closes with the famous statement that there is no royal road into phenomenology. Strictly to this style our further-following analyses and problem-indications are to bind themselves, as in part the previous ones have already done. We hold ourselves, however many-shaped the treated materials may appear to the novice, only in restricted spheres. Naturally we prefer what lies relatively near the entrances into phenomenology, and what is unconditionally necessary in order to be able to follow through systematic main lines. Everything is difficult; it requires laborious concentration on the givennesses of the specifically-phenomenological essence-intuition. A "royal road" into phenomenology, and so also into philosophy, there is not. There is only the one that its own essence prescribes. This is the memorable close of the chapter. Phenomenology stays in restricted spheres, near the entrances, doing what is necessary to follow the main lines. Everything is difficult, requiring concentrated essence-intuition. And there is no "royal road" (königsweg) into phenomenology or philosophy, no shortcut: only the single, laborious way prescribed by phenomenology's own essence (the way of intuitive essence-analysis). With this, the Third Chapter closes, having established the noesis-noema correlation across perception, judgment, feeling, and will. The Fourth Chapter (§§97ff.) turns to the detailed structure of the noesis and the noema, the reell and the intentional, the sense and the object.

Husserl ends the chapter with a memorable remark on phenomenology as a beginning science. Phenomenology presents itself in these presentations as a beginning science. How much of the results of the analyses here attempted is definitive, only the future can teach. Surely much of what has been described will, sub specie aeterni, have to be described otherwise. But one thing may and must be striven for: that at each step one faithfully describe what one, from one's viewpoint and after most earnest study, really sees. The procedure is that of a research-traveler in an unknown part of the world, who carefully describes what presents itself to him on his untrodden ways, which will not always be the shortest. He may be filled with the secure consciousness of bringing to expression what, according to time and circumstances, had to be expressed, and what, because it is faithful expression of the seen, constantly retains its value, even if new researches will require new descriptions with manifold improvements. In the same disposition Husserl wants, in what follows, to be a faithful describer of the phenomenological formations, and otherwise to keep the habitus of inner freedom even toward his own descriptions. This humility-and-honesty statement closes the chapter. Phenomenology is a beginning science; its results are provisional, open to future correction. The image is the research-traveler in unknown territory, faithfully describing what presents itself on untrodden, not-always-shortest paths. The faithful description of the seen keeps its value even when later improved. And one must keep "inner freedom even toward one's own descriptions": not dogmatizing one's results, but remaining open to re-seeing and correcting. This sets the tone for the worked analyses of the Fourth Chapter.

## Fourth Chapter: On the Problematic of the Noetic-Noematic Structures

### § 97\. The Hyletic and Noetic Moments as Reell, the Noematic as Non-Reell Experience-Moments

Husserl resumes the reell/intentional distinction and restricts to the simplest acts. We used, in the previous chapter, in introducing the distinction between noetic and noematic, the talk of reell and intentional analysis. Let us link up here. A phenomenologically pure experience has its reell components. Let us restrict ourselves, for simplicity, to noetic experiences of the lowest level, hence to such as are not, through several noetic layers built one over another, complex in their intentionality, as we found with the thought-acts, the emotional and volitional acts. So the analysis takes a simple act (a sense-perception), not a founded one (judgment, valuing), to isolate the reell components cleanly. A pure experience has reell components (its real parts); the task is to distinguish these from the noematic (non-reell).

He gives the tree-perception example and poses the question. Let us take as example a sensuous perception, the simple tree-perception that we have, just now looking out into the garden, when in a unity of consciousness we consider this tree there, which now stands quietly, then appears moved by the wind, and which presents itself in very different appearance-modes insofar as we, during our steady consideration, change our spatial position to it, stepping to the window, or merely altering the head- or eye-position, at the same time relaxing and re-tensing the accommodation, and so on. The unity of one perception can in this way encompass a great manifold of modifications, which we, as naturally attuned observers, ascribe now to the actual object as its changes, now to a real and actual relation to our real psychophysical subjectivity, and finally to this itself. It is now a matter of describing what of this remains as phenomenological residuum when we reduce to "pure immanence," and what may thereby count as a reell component of the pure experience, and what not. This sets up the analysis. One perception of the tree contains a great manifold (the tree appears in many ways as I move, turn my head, refocus). Naturally I assign these variations to the object, to my relation to it, or to myself. The question is: after the reduction, what is a reell component of the pure experience, and what is not?

He states the result: the noema (the "perceived tree as such") is not reell in the perception, any more than the real tree. And there it is to be made wholly clear that, though to the essence of the perception-experience in itself the "perceived tree as such" belongs, or the full noema, which is not touched by the switching-off of the actuality of the tree itself and of the whole world; but that, on the other hand, this noema with its "tree" in quotation marks is just as little reell contained in the perception as the tree of actuality. This is the central thesis of §97. The noema (the perceived-tree-as-such) belongs to the essence of the perception, and survives the bracketing of the real tree. But the noema is not a reell component of the perception, any more than the real tree is. So the noema is neither the real tree (bracketed) nor a real part of the experience; it is the intentional correlate, belonging to the experience but not reell in it. This is the precise being-status of the noema: it belongs essentially to the experience yet is not a real part of it.

He asks what is reell, recalling the hyletic and noetic components. What do we find reell in it as pure experience, contained in it as in the whole its parts, its pieces and unsegregable moments? We have already occasionally highlighted such genuine, reell components under the titles of stuff-like and noetic components. Let us contrast them with the noematic stocks. So the reell components are the hyletic (stuff-like) and noetic moments (§85). The task now is to contrast these reell components with the noematic (the noema), using the example of color.

He distinguishes the noematic (objective) color from the reell sensation-color. The color of the tree-trunk, purely as the perceptually conscious, is exactly "the same" as the one we took, before the phenomenological reduction, as that of the actual tree (at least as "natural" humans and before the mixing-in of physical knowledge). This color now, set in the bracket, belongs to the noema. But it does not belong as a reell component to the perception-experience, although we also find in it "something like color": namely the "sensation-color," the hyletic moment of the concrete experience, in which the noematic, or "objective," color "adumbrates" itself. This is the crucial distinction (from §41, now sharpened). The appearing color of the tree-trunk (the objective color) belongs to the noema; it is not a reell part of the experience. The reell part is the sensation-color (the hyletic datum), in which the objective color adumbrates itself. So there are two "colors": the noematic (objective, appearing) color, which is in the noema, and the reell sensation-color (the hyletic datum), which is in the experience. The objective color adumbrates itself through the sensation-color.

He describes the adumbration: one noematic color, a manifold of sensation-colors. Thereby the one and same noematic color, which is conscious in the continuous unity of a changeable perception-consciousness as an identical, in itself unchanged, adumbrates itself in a continuous manifold of sensation-colors. We see a tree unchanged as to color, as to its, the tree-color, while the eye-positions, the relative orientations manifoldly change, the glance ceaselessly wanders over the trunk, the branches, while we at the same time step nearer and so in various ways bring the perception-experience into flux. If we carry out the sensation-reflection, directed at the adumbrations, then we grasp them as evident givennesses, and in perfect evidence we can, alternating in attitude and attention-direction, set them and the corresponding objective moments also in relation, recognize them as corresponding, and thereby also see at once that, for example, the adumbration-colors belonging to any fixed thing-color relate to it as continuous "manifold" to "unity." This is the adumbration structure (§41). The objective color stays one and identical (the tree's color does not change), while the sensation-colors form a continuous manifold (as I move, the sensed color-data vary). Reflection on the sensations grasps these adumbrations as evident givennesses and sees that they relate to the objective color as manifold to unity. So unity (the one objective color, noematic) and manifold (the many sensation-colors, reell) are correlated.

He states the essential law that the appearing object depends on the hyletic content. We gain even, in carrying out the phenomenological reduction, the general essence-insight that the object tree in a perception in general can appear as objectively so determined, as it appears in it, only then, if the hyletic moments (or, if it is a continuous perception-series, if the continuous hyletic changes) are precisely these and no others. Therein lies that every change of the hyletic content of the perception, if it does not directly cancel the perception-consciousness, must at least have the result that the appearing becomes an objectively "other," be it in itself, be it in the manner of orientation belonging to its appearance, and the like. This is an essential-lawful dependence. The object can appear as it does only if the hyletic content is precisely what it is. Any change in the hyletic content either cancels the perception or makes the appearing object (or its orientation) objectively other. So the hyletic content and the noematic appearing are essence-lawfully correlated: the object-as-appearing depends on the sensation-data through which it appears.

He fixes the result: unity and manifold belong to totally different dimensions, hyletic reell, the adumbrated noematic. With all this it is also absolutely doubtless that here "unity" and "manifold" belong to totally different dimensions, and indeed all the hyletic belongs to the concrete experience as a reell component, whereas the in it as manifold "presenting," "adumbrating" belongs to the noema. This fixes the dimensions. The hyletic (the manifold of sensation-data) is a reell component of the experience. The adumbrated (the unity that presents itself through the manifold, the objective color) belongs to the noema. So reell (hyletic) and noematic (adumbrated) are totally different dimensions: the manifold of data is in the experience, the unity they present is in the noema.

He adds that the appearing (the apprehension, the sense-giving) is also reell. But the stuffs are, we said earlier, "animated" by noetic moments; they undergo (while the ego is turned not to them but to the object) "apprehensions," "sense-givings," which we grasp in reflection precisely at and with the stuffs. There results with respect to this at once: not only the hyletic moments (the sensation-colors, \-tones, and so on), but also the apprehensions animating them, hence both in one, also the appearing of the color, the tone, and so of every quality of the object, belongs to the "reell" stock of the experience. This is an important clarification. The reell components include not only the hyletic data but also the noetic apprehensions (sense-givings) that animate them, and so the appearing itself (the appearing of the color through the data). So the "appearing of the color" (the act of the color presenting itself) is reell, while the "appearing color" (the objective color that appears) is noematic. The appearing (act) is reell; the appearing (object) is noematic.

He states the general law: to every objectively-described component corresponds a reell component, but only insofar as description holds to the object. It now holds generally: in itself the perception is perception of its object, and to every component that the "objectively" directed description highlights at the object, there corresponds a reell component of the perception; but, well noted, only insofar as the description holds itself faithfully to the object, as it "stands there" in this perception itself. All these noetic components we can also designate only by recurring to the noematic object and its moments; thus say for example: consciousness, more closely perception-consciousness of a tree-trunk, of the color of the trunk, and so on. This is the noetic-noematic correspondence. To each moment of the object (as described) corresponds a reell (noetic) component of the perception (the apprehension of that moment). But the noetic components can be described only by recurring to the noematic object: we describe the apprehension as "consciousness of the tree-trunk, of its color." So the noetic side is described via the noematic side (we name the acts by their objects).

He notes the total difference between the reell experience-unity and the noematic unity. On the other hand, our consideration showed that the reell experience-unity of hyletic and noetic components is a totally other than the "in it conscious" of the components of the noema; and again than the unity that all those reell experience \[components form, and the unity of the noema with the experience\]. So the reell unity (the hyletic and noetic components really united in the experience) is totally different from the noematic unity (the components of the noema, conscious "in" the experience but not reell). There are different kinds of unity: the reell unity of the experience's parts, and the intentional unity of the noema. The experience really contains its hyletic and noetic parts; it intentionally "contains" (is conscious of) its noema, which is not a reell part. This sharp distinction of reell containment from intentional "containment" is the heart of §97 and the precise being-status of the noema.

### § 98\. Mode of Being of the Noema. Form-Doctrine of the Noeses. Form-Doctrine of the Noemata

Husserl states the being-mode of the noema: non-self-sufficient, its esse in its percipi, but not Berkeley's. (The section opens by establishing that the noema is non-self-sufficient.) The noema is a throughout non-self-sufficient one. Its esse consists exclusively in its "percipi," only that this proposition holds nothing less than in the Berkeleyan sense, since the percipi here does not contain the esse as a reell component. This is a precise and important formula. The noema is non-self-sufficient: it cannot exist on its own, only as the correlate of a noesis. So its esse (being) is its percipi (being-perceived, being-conscious): the noema is only as the perceived-as-such of a perceiving. But this is not Berkeley's esse est percipi. Berkeley meant the object is a bundle of ideas in the mind (a reell mental content). Husserl means the opposite: the noema's percipi does not contain its esse as a reell component. The noema is the perceived-as-such, correlative to but not reell in the perceiving. So Husserl's formula marks the noema's dependence on consciousness (its esse is its percipi) while denying that the noema is a mental content (against Berkeley, §55). This is the precise sense in which the noema "depends on" consciousness without being "in" the mind.

He transfers this to the eidetic level. This transfers naturally into the eidetic mode of consideration: the Eidos of the noema points to the Eidos of the noetic consciousness; both belong eidetically together. The intentional as such is what it is as intentional of the so-and-so constituted consciousness that is consciousness of it. So at the eidetic level, the essence of the noema points to the essence of the noesis: they belong together by essential law. The noema-essence is essentially the noema-of a certain noesis-essence. So noesis and noema are an essential correlative pair, neither thinkable without the other.

He notes that despite its non-self-sufficiency the noema can be studied for itself, and projects two form-doctrines. Despite this non-self-sufficiency, the noema can be considered for itself, compared with other noemata, investigated as to its possible reshapings, and so on. One can sketch a general and pure form-doctrine of the noemata, to which would stand correlatively opposed a general and not less pure form-doctrine of the concrete noetic experiences with their hyletic and specifically noetic components. So there can be two correlative formal disciplines: a form-doctrine of the noemata (the formal study of the objective senses and their structures) and a form-doctrine of the noeses (the formal study of the acts with their hyletic and noetic components). These are correlative but distinct sciences. (The form-doctrine of the noemata is essentially what becomes Husserl's "transcendental logic" of the noematic; the noetic form-doctrine its act-side correlate.)

He warns the two form-doctrines are not mere mirror-images. Naturally these two form-doctrines would by no means relate to each other so to speak like mirror-images, or pass into each other by a mere change of sign; somewhat so that we substituted for each noema N "consciousness of N." That already follows from what we carried out above with respect to the belonging-together of unitary qualities in the thing-noema and their hyletic adumbration-manifolds in the possible thing-perceptions. This is a crucial warning. The noetic and noematic form-doctrines are not trivial mirror-images: one cannot just prefix "consciousness of" to each noema to get the noesis. The reason: one noema (the unity, e.g. the objective color) corresponds to a manifold of hyletic adumbrations and noetic apprehensions. So the correspondence is unity-to-manifold, not one-to-one. The two sides are correlated but structurally different (the noema is a field of unities, the noesis a field of constituting manifolds).

He develops the manifold/unity asymmetry through the apprehension-differences. It might now seem that the same must hold also with respect to the specifically noetic moments. One could in particular point to those moments which make it that a complex manifold of hyletic data, say color-data, touch-data, and so on, wins the function of a manifold adumbration of one and the same objective thing. It need only be recalled that in the stuffs themselves, as to their essence, the relation to the objective unity is not unambiguously prefigured; rather, the same stuff-complex can undergo multiple, discretely into-one-another-jumping apprehensions, according to which different objectivities are conscious. Is it not thereby already clear that in the animating apprehensions themselves, as experience-moments, essential differences lie and differentiate themselves with the adumbrations they follow, and through whose animation they constitute "sense"? Thus one would draw the conclusion: a parallelism between noesis and noema is indeed present, but so that the formations on both sides and in their essential mutual-correspondence must be described. The noematic is the field of unities, the noetic that of "constituting" manifolds. The manifold "functionally" unifying and at the same time unity-constituting consciousness shows in fact never identity where in the noematic correlate identity of the "object" is given. This develops the asymmetry. The same hyletic stuff can be apprehended in different ways (yielding different objects), so the apprehensions carry essential differences. The conclusion drawn: there is a parallelism, but the noematic side is the field of unities (the identical objects), the noetic side the field of constituting manifolds. And the constituting consciousness never shows identity where the noematic correlate shows identity of the object.

He gives the tree-example of unity (noematic) versus non-identity (noetic). Where, for example, different sections of a lasting, thing-unity-constituting perceiving show an identical, this one, in the sense of this perceiving unchanged tree, giving itself now in this orientation, then in that, now from the front, then from the back, with respect to the visually grasped properties of some place first unclearly and indeterminately, then clearly and determinately, and the like, there the object findable in the noema is conscious as an identical in the literal sense, but the consciousness of it is, in the different sections of its immanent duration, a non-identical, a merely connected, continuously unitary one. This is the key contrast. Across a continuous perceiving, the noematic object is literally identical (the one same tree). But the consciousness of it (the noesis) is not identical across the duration: it is a continuously connected but changing manifold (different apprehensions, orientations, clarity-levels). So noematic identity corresponds to noetic non-identity (a continuous manifold). This is the unity/manifold asymmetry: one identical noematic object, a manifold of constituting noeses.

He cautions that the conclusions are not quite correct, and distinguishes the parallelisms. However much that is correct is said in all this, the drawn conclusions are yet not quite correct, as indeed in these difficult questions the greatest caution is commanded. The parallelisms existing here, and there are several of them, which only too easily mix into one another, are afflicted with great and still very clarification-needing difficulties. We must carefully keep in view the difference between concrete noetic experiences, the experiences together with their hyletic moments, and the pure noeses, as mere complexes of noetic moments. Again we must keep distinguished: the full noema and, for example, in the case of perception, the "appearing object as such." This warns against conflating several distinct parallelisms. One must distinguish: concrete noetic experiences (with hyle) versus pure noeses (noetic moments alone); and the full noema versus the appearing object as such (a core of the noema). The parallelisms among these are several and easily confused.

He shows that the appearing object is itself a unity of noematic manifolds, not just of noetic ones. Take this "object" and all its objective "predicates," the noematic modifications of the predicates of the perceived thing simply posited as actual in normal perception, then it and these predicates are indeed unities over against manifolds of constituting consciousness-experiences (concrete noeses). But they are also unities of noematic manifolds. We recognize this as soon as we draw the noematic characterizations of the noematic "object" (and its "predicates"), which we so far badly neglected, into the circle of attention. So certainly, for example, the appearing color is a unity over against noetic manifolds and specially of such of noetic apprehension-characters. But closer investigation shows that to all changes of these characters, if not in the "color itself," which there constantly appears, yet in its changing "givenness-mode," for example in its appearing "orientation to me," noematic parallels correspond. So in noematic "characterizations" noetic ones quite generally mirror themselves. This is a refined and important point. The appearing object (the noematic core) is a unity not only over against noetic manifolds but over against noematic manifolds too. The color itself stays one, but its givenness-mode (its appearing orientation) varies, and these are noematic variations. So the noetic manifold (the changing apprehensions) is mirrored in a noematic manifold (the changing givenness-modes of the appearing object). The identity is at the level of the object/core; the manifold is at the level of the givenness-modes, on both the noetic and the noematic side.

He announces the program of analyzing the noetic-noematic parallels. How that is the case will now, and not merely for the perception-sphere here exemplarily preferred, have to be a theme of comprehensive analyses. We will analyze the different consciousness-kinds with their manifold noetic characters in turn and investigate them according to the noetic-noematic parallels. So the following sections (§§99ff.) will analyze the various act-kinds and their noetic characters, tracing the noetic-noematic parallels throughout.

He draws the crucial distinction between two parallelisms, with the Spinozist tag. We must impress on ourselves in advance that the parallelism between the unity of the noematically so-and-so meant object, the object in the "sense," and the constituting consciousness-formations ("ordo et connexio rerum, ordo et connexio idearum") must not be confused with the parallelism of noesis and noema, in particular understood as parallelism of noetic and corresponding noematic characters. To this latter parallelism the now-following considerations apply. This is a decisive clarification. There are two different parallelisms. The first is the constitution-parallelism: the identical object (the unity in the sense) versus the constituting consciousness-formations (the manifold of acts that constitute it). Husserl tags this with the Spinozist formula ordo et connexio rerum / ordo et connexio idearum (the order of things and the order of ideas), the object-pole versus the constituting acts. The second is the noesis-noema parallelism proper: noetic characters versus corresponding noematic characters (the act-features and the object-as-given features that mirror each other). These two parallelisms must not be confused: the first is object versus constituting manifold; the second is noetic character versus noematic character. The following sections treat the second (the noesis-noema parallelism of characters). This distinction is subtle but essential for the rest of the chapter: the noema (the object-in-the-how) has both a core (the object/sense, the first parallelism's unity-pole) and characters (which mirror noetic characters, the second parallelism). §99 turns to the noematic core and its characters in the sphere of presentations and re-presentations.

### § 99\. The Noematic Core and its Characters in the Sphere of Presentations and Re-presentations

Husserl states the task of reaching the full noema, noting that what was treated so far is only a central core. It is our task to widen considerably the circle of what has been shown in the two parallel series of noetic and noematic occurrences, in order to reach the full noema and the full noesis. What we have so far chiefly had in view, though still without an inkling of what great problems are enclosed in it, is just a central core, and not even an unambiguously bounded one. So the analyses so far reached only the noematic core (the objective sense, §91); the task now is to add the surrounding characters that complete the full noema. The core is not even sharply bounded by itself; it needs the characters.

He recalls the objective sense as the identical across act-types, and isolates the givenness-modes as characters. We recall first that "objective sense" which resulted for us above (back-reference to §91), through comparison of noemata of different-kind presentations, of perceptions, memories, image-presentations, and the like, as a describable with purely objective expressions, and even mutually with identical ones in the favorably chosen limit-case that a completely alike, alike-oriented, in every respect alike-apprehended object, for example a tree, presents itself perceptually, memory-wise, image-wise, and so on. Over against the identical "appearing tree as such" with the identical "objective" how of the appearing, there remain the differences of the givenness-mode changing from intuition-kind to intuition-kind and according to other presentation-kind. This isolates the characters from the core. The core is the "appearing tree as such" with its objective how (the tree exactly as it looks, in this orientation, with these features), identical across perception, memory, image-presentation. What differs is the givenness-mode: the same tree is given perceptually, or memory-wise, or image-wise. So the givenness-mode is a character at the core, distinct from the core itself.

He names these characters and stresses they are noematic, ideell not reell. That identical is once conscious "originarily," another time conscious "memory-wise," then again "image-wise," and so on. With this, however, characters designate themselves at the "appearing tree as such," findable in the glance-direction upon the noematic correlate and not in that upon the experience and its reell stock. There express themselves with this not "modes of consciousness" in the sense of noetic moments, but modes in which the conscious itself and as such gives itself. As characters at the so-to-speak "ideell," they are themselves "ideell" and not reell. This fixes the status of these characters. The givenness-modes (originary / memory-wise / image-wise) are noematic characters: they are found by looking at the noematic correlate (the appearing tree), not at the experience's reell stock. They are not noetic moments (modes of the act) but modes in which the object gives itself. And as characters at the ideell (the noema), they are themselves ideell (irreal), not reell. So the givenness-mode is a noematic character, a mode of self-giving of the object, irreal like the noema itself.

He distinguishes two series of these characters. On closer analysis one notices that the exemplarily named characters do not belong to one series. So the givenness-mode characters fall into different series, which Husserl now distinguishes: the reproductive modification (memory) and the imaging modification (image), each with its own structure.

He analyzes the first series: the simple reproductive modification (memory as "modification of perception"). On the one hand we have the simple reproductive modification, the simple re-presentation, which gives itself in its own essence, remarkably enough, as modification of another. The re-presentation points back to perception in its own phenomenological essence: for example, the remembering of something past implies, as we already remarked earlier, the "having-perceived"; so in a certain way the "corresponding" perception (perception of the same sense-core) is conscious in the memory, but yet not really contained in it. The memory is precisely in its own essence "modification of" perception. Correlatively, the as-past characterized gives itself in itself as "present having-been," hence as a modification of the "present," which as unmodified is precisely the "originary," the "bodily present" of perception. This is the structure of memory as reproductive modification. Memory gives itself as a "modification of another" (of perception): it implies a "having-perceived" (the corresponding perception is conscious in it, but not really contained). So memory is essentially "modification of perception." Correlatively, on the noematic side, the "past" gives itself as a modification of the "present," whose unmodified form is the "originary," "bodily present" of perception. So perception (the originary, the present) is the unmodified, and memory (the past) is its reproductive modification.

He analyzes the second series: the imaging (pictorializing) modification, with the Bild/Abgebildetes pair. On the other hand, the imaging modification belongs to another modification-series. It re-presents "in" an image. But the image can be an originarily appearing one, for example the "painted" image (not, say, the thing painting, the one of which it is said that it hangs on the wall, footnote to §111), which we grasp perceptively. But the image can also be a reproductively appearing one, as when we have image-presentations in memory or free phantasy. This analyzes imaging. The imaging modification presents its object "in" an image (a depiction). The image-vehicle can be originarily appearing (a painted picture we perceive) or reproductively appearing (an image in phantasy). The footnote distinguishes the painted image (the depicting appearance) from the thing painting (the physical canvas on the wall), a distinction developed at §111.

He notes that the imaging characters presuppose complexions (the Bild/Abgebildetes distinction) and harbor a pair of characters. At the same time one observes that the characters of this new series are not only back-related to those of the first, but also presuppose complexions. The latter with respect to the distinction, noematically belonging to the essence of consciousness, between "image" (Bild) and "imaged" (Abgebildetes). One sees thereby also that here the noema harbors in itself in each case a pair of characters pointing to one another, though belonging to different presentation-objects as such. This is the key structural point about imaging. Image-consciousness involves a pair: the image (Bild, the depicting) and the imaged (Abgebildetes, the depicted). These point to one another (the image is image of the imaged). So the imaging noema harbors a pair of mutually-pointing characters, belonging to two presentation-objects (the image-object and the depicted object). This is more complex than simple memory (which has a single back-reference to perception).

He adds the third type: sign-presentations, with the Zeichen/Bezeichnetes pair. Finally, a closely related and yet new type of modifying noematic characters (to which, as everywhere, parallel noetic ones correspond) is offered us by the sign-presentations with the analogous opposition of sign (Zeichen) and signified (Bezeichnetes); whereby again presentation-complexes and, as correlates of their peculiar unity as sign-presentations, pairs of noematically belonging-together characterizations at noematic object-pairs appear. So sign-consciousness (e.g. a name signifying a thing) involves a pair: the sign (Zeichen) and the signified (Bezeichnetes), analogous to image/imaged. The sign-presentation harbors a pair of mutually-pointing characters at two objects (the sign-object and the signified object).

He notes that both image and sign give themselves as "modification of" something. One notices also that, as the "image" in itself, according to its sense as image, gives itself as modification of something that without this modification would stand there precisely as bodily or re-presented self, exactly so the "sign," but in its way, likewise as modification of something. So both image and sign are "modifications of" something: they present their object indirectly (through an image or sign), as a modification of the direct (bodily or re-presented) self-givenness. So memory, image, and sign are all "modifications," each in its way, of the unmodified self-givenness (perception). This completes the survey of the givenness-mode characters in the presentation-sphere.

### § 100\. Essence-Lawful Level-Formations of Presentations in Noesis and Noema

Husserl states that all these modification-types admit ever-new level-building, nesting in infinitum. All so-far-treated types of presentation-modifications are accessible to ever-new level-building, such that the intentionalities in noesis and noema build level-wise on one another, or rather nest into one another in a unique way. So the modifications (memory, image, sign) can be iterated, building levels: a memory of a memory, an image in a phantasy, and so on, nesting indefinitely. This nesting is a key structural feature of consciousness.

He gives the example of memories in memories. There are simple re-presentations, simple modifications of perceptions. But there are also re-presentations of second, third, and essentially of arbitrary level. As example memories "in" memories can serve us. Living in memory, we "perform" an experience-connection in the mode of re-presentation. Of this we convince ourselves by reflecting "in" the memory (which is itself a re-presentation-modification of an originary reflecting), and then we find the experience-connection characterized as memory-wise "lived-having-been." Among the so-characterized experiences, whether we reflect on them or not, memories can themselves appear, characterized as "lived-having-been memories," and the glance can be directed through them onto the remembered of second level. In the secondarily modified experience-connection memories can again appear, and so ideally in infinitum. This is the structure of nested memory. When I live in a memory, I re-present a past experience-connection. Reflecting "in" the memory, I find it characterized as "lived-having-been." Within that remembered connection, there can be further memories (memories I had then, characterized as "lived-having-been memories"), through which I reach the second-level remembered. And this nests without end (a memory of having remembered something, etc.).

He notes that a mere change of sign translates all this into phantasy. A mere change of sign (whose peculiarity we shall still learn to understand) translates all these occurrences into the type free phantasy; there result phantasies in phantasies, and so in arbitrary nesting-level. This anticipates the neutrality-modification (§§109ff.). A "mere change of sign" (the neutralizing of the positing) turns memory-nesting into phantasy-nesting: phantasies in phantasies, nested indefinitely. (The "change of sign" here is the shift from positing memory to non-positing phantasy, a structure Husserl will analyze as the neutrality-modification.)

He notes the mixtures. Likewise then further mixtures. Not only that every re-presentation, according to its essence, harbors in itself, with respect to its next-lower level, re-presentation-modifications of perceptions, which step into the grasping glance through the wonderful reflection in the re-presentation; we can, in the unity of a re-presentation-phenomenon, find beside re-presentations of perceptions at the same time such of memories, expectations, phantasies, and so on, whereby the relevant re-presentations themselves can be of each of these types. And all that in different levels. So the nesting allows mixtures: within one re-presentation, there can be re-presentations of perceptions, memories, expectations, phantasies, at various levels, in combination. The structure of nested and mixed re-presentations is very rich. (This is the basis of Husserl's analyses of memory, expectation, phantasy, and image-consciousness in the time-consciousness and phantasy manuscripts.)

### § 101\. Characters of Levels. The Different Re-presentations

Husserl establishes that the image and signified are conscious-but-transcendent unities, so their characters cannot be reell. The image and the signified, in abstraction from the characterizations belonging to them, "image for," "imaged," "sign for," "signified," are evidently in the experience conscious but to it transcendent unities. But if that is so, then characters that appear at them consciousness-wise and are grasped, in the glance-attitude upon them, as their properties, can impossibly be regarded as reell experience-moments. This is an important consequence. The image-object and the signified-object (abstracting from the "image for" / "sign for" characters) are conscious unities transcendent to the experience (not reell parts of it, like the perceived tree). So the characters appearing at them (their properties) cannot be reell experience-moments either. The characters belong to the noematic (transcendent) objects, not to the reell experience.

He states the universal separation to be made, re: the noematic core (the bearer of characters) and the characters themselves. However the two, what is reell experience-stock and what is conscious in it as non-reell, stand to one another, may carry still such difficult problems, the separation we must everywhere make, and indeed both with respect to the noematic core, the "intentional object as such" (and taken in its "objective" givenness-mode), which appears as the respective bearer of the noematic "characters," and with respect to the characters themselves. This fixes the structure of the full noema. One must everywhere separate the reell experience-stock from the non-reell conscious (the noema). And within the noema, one must distinguish: the noematic core (the "intentional object as such" in its objective givenness-mode), which is the bearer of characters, and the characters themselves (which attach to the core). So the full noema \= core (the object-as-such in its objective how) \+ characters (the givenness-modes, and, as §103 will add, the being-characters).

### § 102\. Transition to New Dimensions of Characterization

Husserl announces that there are still other characters, ordering into fundamentally different genera and dimensions. But of such characters always adhering to the noematic core there are still quite others, and of very different kinds as to how they belong to it. They order themselves into fundamentally different genera, so to speak fundamentally different dimensions of characterization. So besides the givenness-mode characters (originary / memory-wise / image-wise), there are further characters of fundamentally different kinds, ordering into different genera or dimensions of characterization. (The next dimension is the being-characters / doxic modalities, §103; later come the attentional characters, the neutrality-character, and others.)

He stresses the universal phenomenological reach of all these characters. Let it be pointed out from the start that all the characters here to be indicated or already indicated (all titles for necessary analytic-descriptive researches) are of universal phenomenological reach. Although we treat them at first under preference of the relatively simplest-built intentional experiences, which a determinate and fundamental concept of "presentation" comprises, and which make up necessary substrates for all other intentional experiences, the same fundamental genera and differences of characters are found also with all these founded, and so with all intentional experiences whatever. This is the generality-claim. The characters (givenness-modes, being-characters, etc.) are studied first on the simplest acts (the "presentations," Vorstellungen, the foundational acts), but they hold for all intentional experiences, including the founded ones (judgments, valuings, willings). So the doctrine of the noematic core and its characters is universal.

He states the master-structure: always a noematic core that must be characterized by differences from each genus. Thereby the situation is that always and necessarily a noematic core, an "object-noema" (Gegenstandsnoema), is conscious, which must be characterized somehow, and indeed according to these or those (themselves mutually exclusive) differences from each genus. This is the culminating structural thesis. Every noema has a noematic core (an object-noema, the object-as-such), which must be characterized, and the characterization draws, from each genus (givenness-mode, being-mode, etc.), one of the mutually-exclusive differences. So every noema is: a core (object-noema) \+ a characterization from each dimension (a givenness-mode: originary or memory-wise or...; a being-mode: certain or possible or...; etc.). The core is necessarily characterized in every dimension; it cannot be a bare core without characters. This is the full structure of the noema: an object-core bearing, in each dimension of characterization, one determinate character. §103 takes up the most important of these dimensions, the being-characters (the doxic modalities).

### § 103\. Belief-Characters and Being-Characters

Husserl introduces a new dimension: the being-characters, with their noetic correlate the belief-characters. If we now look around for new characters, we become aware first that with the previously treated groups of characters the obviously totally different-kind being-characters (Seinscharaktere) combine. Noetic characters relating correlatively to being-modes, "doxic" or "belief-characters" (Glaubenscharaktere), are, with the intuitive presentations, for example the perception-belief reell enclosed in normal perception as "Gewahrung," and more closely, say the perception-certainty; to it corresponds as noematic correlate at the appearing "object" the being-character, that of the "actual" (wirklich). This introduces the doxic dimension. Besides the givenness-mode characters (originary/memory-wise), there is a new dimension: the being-characters (noematic) and their correlative belief-characters (noetic). In normal perception there is reell a perception-belief (Wahrnehmungsglaube), more precisely a perception-certainty; its noematic correlate is the being-character "actual" (the object is given as actually existing). So the act believes, and correlatively the object is characterized as actual.

He extends to memory and notes these are positing ("thetic") acts. The same noetic, or noematic, character shows the "certain" re-presentation, the "sure" memory of every kind of what-has-been, of what now is, of what will be in the future (so in fore-remembering expectation). They are being-"positing" acts, "thetic." But with this expression it is to be noted that, although it points to an act, to a position-taking in a special sense, precisely this is to remain out of consideration. So memory and expectation, in their certain mode, also have the belief-character (certainty) and the being-character (actual). These are "positing" (setzende), "thetic" acts: they posit their object as being. Husserl notes that "thetic" here does not emphasize the act-performance (the position-taking as an ego-act) but the positing-character as such.

He describes the modalization of certainty into the modalities. The perceptually or memory-wise appearing had, in the so-far considered sphere, the character of the "actual" being simpliciter, of the "certain" being, as we also say in contrast to other being-characters. For this character can modify itself, possibly transform itself at the same phenomenon through actual modifications. The mode of "certain" belief can pass over into that of mere surmise (Anmutung) or conjecture (Vermutung), or of the question and the doubt; and accordingly the appearing (and, with respect to that first dimension of characterizations, characterized as "originary," "reproductive," and the like) has taken on the being-modalities of the "possible," the "probable," the "questionable," the "doubtful." This is the doctrine of modalization. The certainty (the *Urdoxa*) can modalize: belief-certainty passes into surmise, conjecture, question, doubt. Correlatively, the being-character "actual/certain" modalizes into "possible," "probable," "questionable," "doubtful." So the being-modalities are modifications of the basic certainty. And these modalizations attach to the same object (which keeps its givenness-mode character, originary or reproductive).

He gives the worked example of the figure in the forest-dark. For example: a perceived object stands at first in simple self-evidence, in certainty. Suddenly we become doubtful whether we have not fallen victim to a mere "illusion," whether the seen, heard, and the like is not "mere appearance." Or the appearing keeps its being-certainty, but we are uncertain with respect to some property-complex. The thing "intimates itself" (mutet sich an) as a human being. Then a counter-surmise sets in, it could be a moved tree, which in the forest-dark looks similar to a moving human.
