But now the "weight" of the one "possibility" becomes considerably greater; we decide for it, say in the manner that we determinately conjecture: "it was in any case a tree after all." This is the famous example. A perceived object, first certain, suddenly becomes doubtful (illusion? mere appearance?). Or it keeps its being-certainty but a property becomes uncertain: it "looks like" a man, then a counter-surmise arises (a wind-moved tree in the forest-dark, looking like a man), and the "weight" of one possibility grows until we decide ("it was a tree after all"). This concrete example shows the modalities (certainty → doubt → surmise → counter-surmise → weighted possibility → decision) playing out at a single perceptual phenomenon, within intuition, without any conceptual thinking.

He notes the modalities change likewise in memory, within intuition, without predicative thinking. Likewise the being-modalities change, and much more frequently, in memory, and indeed so that they to a large extent establish and exchange purely in the frame of the intuitions, or dark presentations, without participation of any "thinking" in the specific sense, without "concept" and predicative judgment. This is an important point. The modalities operate at the pre-predicative, intuitive level. The shift from certainty to doubt to conjecture happens within perception and memory, in the intuitions themselves, without any concept or predicative judgment. So modalization is a pre-logical, pre-conceptual phenomenon (the basis of Husserl's genetic doctrine of judgment in Experience and Judgment, where pre-predicative modalization founds predicative logic).

He flags further characters and problem-groups, deferring them. One sees at the same time that the relevant phenomena still suggest many kinds of studies, that still various characters here appear (like the "decided," the "weights" of the possibilities, and the like), and that in particular also the question after the essential substrates of the respective characters, after the whole essence-lawfully regulated construction of the noemata and noeses, demands deeper investigations. For us it is here, as elsewhere, enough to have set out the problem-groups. So Husserl flags further characters (the "decided," the "weights" of possibilities) and the deeper questions (the essential substrates, the lawful construction of noema and noesis), but defers them. The aim is to mark the problem-groups, not to resolve them.

### § 104\. The Doxic Modalities as Modifications

Husserl draws out the specifically intentional sense of "modification" in the belief-series, identifying certainty as the unmodified primal form. With respect to the series of belief-modalities specially occupying us, let it be pointed out that in it again the distinguished, specifically intentional sense of the talk of modification comes into validity, which we made clear to ourselves in the analysis of the previous series of noetic, or noematic, characters. In the present series, belief-certainty obviously plays the role of the unmodified, or, as we would here have to say, the "unmodalized primal form of the belief-mode." Accordingly in the correlate: the being-character simpliciter (the noematic "certain" or "actual" being) functions as the primal form of all being-modalities. This is the key structural claim. Belief-certainty is the unmodified primal form (Urform) of belief; the other modes (surmise, conjecture, doubt) are its modifications. Correlatively, the being-character "actual/certain" is the primal form of all being-modalities; "possible," "probable," etc. are its modifications.

He shows the modalities point back to the primal form. In fact all the being-characters welling up from it, the specifically so-to-be-called being-modalities, have in their own sense a back-relation to the primal form. The "possible" says in itself as much as "possibly being," the "probable," "doubtful," "questionable" as much as "probably being," "doubtfully and questionably being." The intentionality of the noeses mirrors itself in these noematic relations, and one feels again pressed to speak outright of a "noematic intentionality" as "parallel" to the noetic and properly so-called one. This transfers then to the full "propositions," that is, to the unities of sense-core and being-character. This shows the back-reference. Each modality contains the primal form in its own sense: "possible" means "possibly being," "probable" means "probably being." So the modalities essentially refer back to being (the primal form). And this back-reference is itself a noematic relation, mirroring the noetic intentionality, so that one can speak of a "noematic intentionality" parallel to the noetic. This applies to the full "propositions" (Sätze), the unities of sense-core and being-character. (The "proposition" here is the noematic unity of core \+ being-character, treated more fully in the Fourth Part, §§133ff.)

He notes the convenient terminological use of "being-modality" and "doxic modality." It is, by the way, convenient to use the term being-modality for the whole series of these being-characters, hence also to include the unmodified "being," wherever it is to be considered as a member of this series; somewhat as the arithmetician includes the One under the name number. In the same sense we generalize the sense of the talk of the doxic modalities, under which we will, moreover often in conscious double-meaning, comprise the noetic and noematic parallels. So "being-modality" covers the whole series including the unmodified being (as "1" is a number), and "doxic modality" covers both the noetic belief-modes and the noematic being-modes (often deliberately ambiguously, since they are parallel).

He warns against equivocations of "certain," especially the affirmation-sense. It is further, in the designation of the unmodalized being as "certain being," to attend to the equivocations of the word "certain," and not only in the respect that it sometimes says the noetic, sometimes the noematic "being-certain." It serves, for example, also (and that is here very confusing) to express the correlate of affirmation, the "yes" as counterpart to the "no" and "not." That must here remain strictly excluded. Word-meanings shift constantly in the frame of the logically-immediate equivalence. But it is our concern to set out the equivalences everywhere, and to separate sharply what lies, as essence-different phenomena, behind the equivalent concepts. This is a terminological caution. "Certain" is equivocal: it can mean the doxic certainty (the unmodalized belief, the topic here), but also the affirmation (yes, as opposed to no), which is a different phenomenon (treated at §106). These must be kept apart. The task is to set out the equivalences but distinguish the essence-different phenomena behind them.

He introduces the term *Urdoxa* for belief-certainty, marking its special position. Belief-certainty is belief simpliciter, in the pregnant sense. It has, according to our analyses, in fact a highly remarkable special position in the manifold of acts that are all comprised under the title belief, or "judgment," as is also frequently but in very unsuitable manner said. It needs a proper expression that takes account of this special position and extinguishes every memory of the usual equating of certainty and the other belief-modes. We introduce the term primal belief (Urglaube) or Urdoxa, with which the intentional back-relatedness of all "belief-modalities" set out by us is appropriately expressed. This introduces the Urdoxa. Belief-certainty has a special position: it is not one mode among coordinate modes but the primal form to which all others refer back. To mark this, Husserl introduces the term Urglaube or Urdoxa (primal belief). The Urdoxa is the foundational belief-certainty from which all modalities derive.

He extends "doxic modality" to all modifications grounding in the Urdoxa, and rejects the coordinate-species view. We add that we will use this latter expression (or "doxic modality") for all intentional modifications grounding in the essence of the Urdoxa, also for those to be newly set out in the following analyses. The fundamentally false doctrine, according to which a genus "belief" (or "judgment") differentiates itself only in certainty, conjecture, and so on, as if it were a matter of a series of coordinate species (wherever one may break off the series), as in the genus sense-quality color, tone, and so on are coordinate species, hardly needs any more criticism for us. Moreover we must, here as elsewhere, deny ourselves following the consequences of our phenomenological establishments. This rejects the coordinate-species view. The modalities (certainty, conjecture, doubt) are not coordinate species of a genus "belief" (the way color and tone are coordinate species of sense-quality). Rather, certainty (the Urdoxa) is the primal form, and the others are its modifications. So the relation is foundation/modification, not coordinate species. Husserl defers the consequences of this for logic.

### § 105\. Belief-Modality as Belief, Being-Modality as Being

Husserl shows the double glance-direction belonging to higher-level intentionalities. If we speak, with respect to the above-described highly remarkable situations, of an intentionality with which the secondary modi back-relate to the Urdoxa, then the sense of this talk demands the possibility of a multiple glance-direction of a kind that belongs in general to the essence of the intentionalities of higher level. This possibility in fact exists. We can, on the one hand, for example living in the probability-consciousness (in the conjecturing), look at what is probable; on the other hand at the probable itself and as such, that is, at the noematic object in the character that the conjecture-noesis conferred on it. This describes the double glance. In a conjecturing (probability-consciousness), one can look either at what is probable (the object) or at the probable-as-such (the object in its probability-character). This double glance-direction belongs to higher-level intentionalities (those with a modalized character).

He shows the modal character itself becomes, in the second glance, a being. But the "object" with its sense-stock and with this probability-character is, in the second glance-setting, given as being: in relation to it the consciousness is accordingly simple belief in unmodified sense. Likewise we can live in the possibility-consciousness (in the "surmise"), or in questioning and doubting, the glance directed at what is conscious to us there as possible, questionable, doubtful. But we can also look at the possibilities, questionablenesses, doubtfulnesses as such, and possibly, explicating, grasp and predicate at the sense-object the possible-being, questionable-being, doubtful-being: it is then given as being in the unmodified sense. This is the key result. When I turn the glance to the probability-character itself (the probable-as-such), it is given as being: the consciousness of it is simple (unmodified) belief. So the modalized character (probable, possible, doubtful), when made an object, is itself given as a being (a probable-being, a possible-being). The modality, looked at directly, becomes a being-object of an unmodified belief. So every modalization can be "objectified" into a being (the possibility becomes a possible-being, posited in certainty).

He states the universal essence-law: every experience functions as belief-consciousness in the sense of the Urdoxa. So we will be able to establish quite generally the highly remarkable essence-peculiarity that every experience, in relation to all the noetic moments that constitute themselves through its noeses at the "intentional object as such," functions as belief-consciousness in the sense of the Urdoxa; or, as we can also say: every addition of new noetic characters, or every modification of old ones, constitutes not only new noematic characters, but there constitute themselves thereby eo ipso for the consciousness new being-objects; to the noematic characters correspond predicable characters at the sense-object, as actual and not merely noematically modified predicabilia. These propositions will gain still in clarity when we have made ourselves familiar with new noematic spheres. This is the universal doxic thesis. Every experience functions as belief-consciousness in the sense of the Urdoxa: the noematic characters constituted by its noeses can always be "objectified" into being-objects (the possibility becomes a possible-being, the value becomes a value-being). So every noematic character corresponds to a predicable character at the sense-object (an actual predicate, e.g. "is possible," "is valuable"). This is the doxic-positing power of consciousness: every character can be turned into a being-predicate of an object posited in the Urdoxa. (This grounds the universality of logic: every act-character can become a logical predicate, so the doxic/logical sphere underlies all consciousness.)

### § 106\. Affirmation and Negation with their Noematic Correlates

Husserl introduces negation and affirmation as a new back-relating modification. Again a new back-relating modification, and indeed one of possibly higher level by virtue of its essential intentional back-relation to every kind of belief-modalities, is the rejection (Ablehnung), as well as the agreement (Zustimmung) analogous to it. More specially expressed, negation (Verneinung) and affirmation (Bejahung). Every negation is negation of something, and this something points us back to some belief-modality. Noetically, then, the negation is "modification" of some "position"; that says not of an affirmation, but of a "positing" in the extended sense of some belief-modality. This introduces negation. Negation (and affirmation) is a new back-relating modification, of higher level, since it relates to any belief-modality. Every negation is negation of something (some posited content); so negation is a modification of a "position" (a positing, in the extended sense covering any belief-modality, not just affirmation).

He describes the noematic achievement of negation: the "crossing-out," the character "not." Its new noematic achievement is the "crossing-out" (Durchstreichung) of the corresponding positional character; its specific correlate is the crossing-out-character, that of the "not." Its negation-stroke goes through a positional, more concretely expressed, through a "proposition" (Satz), and indeed by virtue of the crossing-out of its specific proposition-character, that is, its being-modality. Precisely thereby this character and the proposition itself stand there as "modification" of another. Differently expressed: through transformation of the simple being-consciousness into the corresponding negation-consciousness, in the noema the simple character "being" becomes the "not-being." This describes negation noematically. Negation "crosses out" the positional character (the being-modality) of a proposition; its correlate is the character "not." The negation-stroke goes through the proposition, crossing out its being-character. So the proposition stands as a "modification" of another (the un-negated). On the noematic side, "being" becomes "not-being." So negation is the crossing-out of being, yielding not-being, and affirmation (its counterpart) is the confirming of being.

He notes affirmation as the counterpart. (The section's title pairs affirmation with negation.) Affirmation (Bejahung, Zustimmung) is the analogous counterpart: the confirming, the "yes," as opposed to the negation's "no"/"not." Both are back-relating modifications of a prior positing (any belief-modality). So affirmation and negation are a pair of higher-level doxic modifications: affirmation confirms the positing (yes, it is), negation crosses it out (no, it is not). These are the doxic acts underlying the logical operations of affirmation and negation. The noematic correlates are the characters "being" (confirmed) and "not-being" (crossed out). This completes the analysis of the doxic modalities and their being-correlates, and prepares the analysis of the further modifications (the neutrality-modification, §§109ff.) and the doctrine of reason. §107 notes that these modifications are interwoven and iterable, continuing the survey of the noetic-noematic structures.

### § 107\. Iterated Modifications

Husserl draws the consequence that the doxic modifications can be iterated without end. What we have already appropriated from the beginnings of such analysis suffices to carry out at once the following advance of insight. Since every negate and affirmate is itself a being-object, it can, like everything conscious in a being-mode, be affirmed or negated. There results therefore, in consequence of the being-constitution newly carried out at each step, an ideally infinite chain of iterated modifications. So in the first level the "not-not-being," the "not-impossible," "not-unquestionable," "not-improbable-being," and so on. This applies the result of §105 (every character becomes a being-object) to negation and affirmation. Since a negate (the not-being) is itself a being-object, it can be negated again (the not-not-being), and so on without end. So iteration generates an infinite chain: not-being, not-not-being, not-impossible, and so forth.

He extends the iteration to all the being-modalities. The same holds, as is immediately to be surveyed, for all the earlier-discussed being-modifications. That something is possible, probable, questionable, and so on, can itself again be conscious in the mode of possibility, probability, questionability; to the noetic formations correspond the noematic being-formations: it is possible that it is possible, that it is probable, questionable; it is probable that it is possible, that it is probable; and so in all complications. To the higher-level formations then correspond again affirmates and negates, which are again modifiable, and so it goes, ideally speaking, in infinitum. So the modalities iterate and combine: "it is possible that it is probable," "it is probable that it is possible," and these can themselves be affirmed or negated, without end. The chain of iterated modalities is ideally infinite.

He notes this is not mere verbal repetition, pointing to probability-theory. It is here a matter of nothing less than mere verbal repetitions. Let only the probability-doctrine and its applications be recalled, where possibilities and probabilities are constantly weighed, denied, doubted, conjectured, asked after, established, and so on. So the iteration is not a verbal game but a real structure, exhibited in probability-theory (which constantly compounds and modalizes probabilities). The iterated modalities have genuine phenomenological and logical content.

He distinguishes the two senses of "modification" (actual operation versus essence-peculiarity). But always it is to be noted that the talk of modifications here relates, on the one hand, to a possible transformation of the phenomena, hence to a possible actual operation; on the other hand, to the much more interesting essence-peculiarity of noeses, or noemata, \[to be modifications of one another by essential law\]. This is an important methodological clarification (which recurs throughout). "Modification" has two senses: (i) an actual operation (an act I perform, transforming one phenomenon into another, e.g. actually negating a belief); and (ii) the essence-peculiarity (the lawful relation by which one noesis/noema is in itself a "modification of" another, regardless of any actual operation). The second is the more interesting: the modifications are essential relations among the noematic forms, not merely acts one happens to perform.

### § 108\. The Noematic Characters Not "Reflection-Determinations"

Husserl insists again that noesis and noema must be distinguished, and warns against a psychologistic misreading. It is necessary that we, with each new group of noeses and noemata that we have brought to clear consciousness, also assure ourselves anew of the fundamental insight so very contrary to the psychologistic thought-habits: that precisely between noesis and noema must really and correctly be distinguished, exactly as faithful description demands. Even if one has already found one's way into the purely immanent essence-description (which so many who otherwise praise description do not manage) and has brought oneself to grant every consciousness an intentional object, as belonging to it and immanently describable, there is still the great temptation to apprehend the noematic characters, and quite especially the last-treated ones, as mere "reflection-determinations." This sets up the error to be refuted. Even those who accept the noema may be tempted to treat the noematic characters (being, not-being, possible, etc.) as "reflection-determinations": determinations that accrue to the object only by reflecting back on the act. Husserl will refute this.

He explains the error and exposes it as mere construction. We understand, recalling the common narrow concept of reflection, what that says: determinations that accrue to the intentional objects by these being back-related to the consciousness-modes in which they are precisely consciousness-objects. So the negate, the affirmate, and the like, is supposed to result from this, that the "judgment"-object is characterized, in relating reflection upon the negating, as negated, in reflection upon the affirming as affirmed, likewise in reflection upon the conjecturing as more probable, and so everywhere. That is mere construction (footnote to the Sixth Investigation, §44), which betrays itself in its perversity already in this, that, if these predicates really were only relating reflection-predicates, they could be given only in the actual reflecting upon the act-side and in relating to it. Evidently, however, they are not given through such reflection. This is the refutation. The "reflection-determination" view says: the object is "negated" only because I reflect on my negating and relate it back. But then these characters could be given only in actual reflection on the act. Yet they are not given that way: I grasp the not-being directly at the object, without reflecting on my act. So the characters are not reflection-determinations.

He states the positive point: the characters are grasped directly at the correlate. We grasp what is the proper matter of the correlate directly in the glance-direction precisely upon the correlate. At the appearing object as such we grasp the negates, affirmates, the possible and questionable, and so on. We thereby in no way look back upon the act. Conversely, the noetic predicates arising through such reflection have nothing less than the same sense as the relevant noematic predicates. This is the decisive point. The being-characters (not-being, possible, etc.) are grasped directly at the appearing object, with no glance back at the act. And the noetic predicates (reached by reflecting on the act) are not the same in sense as the noematic predicates. So the noematic characters belong to the object-as-given, not to the act, and are grasped directly. (This confirms the reality and independence of the noematic dimension.)

He connects this to the truth-relation: not-being is only equivalent, not identical, to validly-negated-being. With this is connected that also from the standpoint of truth, not-being is obviously only equivalent and not identical with "validly being-negated," possible-being with "in valid manner being-held-for-possible," and the like. This is an important logical point. "Not-being" (a noematic character) is only equivalent to, not identical with, "validly negated-being" (a noetic-epistemic fact). The object's not-being and the act's valid negating are correlated but distinct. So the noematic and noetic predicates, though equivalent, are essence-different.

He confirms with the stereoscope example and the honest-description maxim. Also the natural speech, undisturbed by any psychological prejudices, gives testimony here for us, if we still needed it. Looking into the stereoscope, we say, this appearing pyramid is "nothing," is mere "appearance." The appearing as such is the evident subject of the predication, and to it (which, however, a thing-noema is nothing less than a thing) we ascribe what we find at it itself as character: precisely the nullity. One must here, as everywhere in phenomenology, only have the courage to take what is really to be seen in the phenomenon, instead of reinterpreting it, precisely as it gives itself, and to describe it honestly. All theories have to direct themselves accordingly. This confirms the point. In the stereoscope, the appearing pyramid is "nothing," "mere appearance": we predicate the nullity of the appearing-as-such (the noema), not of our act. So the nullity is a character at the noema, directly grasped. The maxim: have the courage to take what is seen as it gives itself, describe it honestly, and let all theories conform to it. (This is the anti-constructivist methodological refrain of phenomenology, §§87, 90, 96.)

### § 109\. The Neutrality-Modification

Husserl introduces a new and supremely important modification, isolated from all the previous. Among the modifications to be related to the belief-sphere we have still to designate a highly important one, which takes a completely isolated position, so by no means may be put in one series with those discussed above. The peculiar way in which it relates to the belief-positings, and the circumstance that it first shows itself in its peculiarity at deeper investigation, as one not at all specially belonging to the belief-sphere, rather as a highly significant general consciousness-modification, justifies it if we devote at this place a detailed consideration to it. We will thereby also find occasion to bring to discussion a kind of genuine belief-modification still lacking to us, with which the new one in question is easily mixed: that of the assumptions. This introduces the neutrality-modification. It is not one belief-modality among others (certainty, doubt, etc.) but a completely different, general consciousness-modification, applicable to all acts. It is easily confused with the "assumptions" (Annahmen), which Husserl will distinguish.

He characterizes the neutrality-modification: it neutralizes, performs nothing, is the counterpart of all performing. It is now a matter for us of a modification that in a certain way completely cancels, completely deprives of force, every doxic modality to which it is related, but in a totally other sense than the negation, which moreover, as we saw, has in the negate its positive achievement, a not-being that is itself again being. It does not cross out, it "performs" nothing, it is the consciousness-counterpart of all performing: its neutralization. It lies enclosed in every refraining-from-performing, setting-out-of-action, "bracketing" it, "leaving-it-undecided" and now having-it-"undecided," "thinking-oneself-into" the performing, or "merely thinking" the performed, without "joining in." This characterizes neutralization. Unlike negation (which performs something — a not-being), the neutrality-modification performs nothing: it neutralizes the positing, sets it out of action, brackets it. It is the "mere thinking" of something without "joining in" the positing. So a neutralized belief is a believing that does not actually believe, a thinking-of without positing.

He notes the difficulty of naming it, and isolates it from arbitrary acts. Since this modification has never been scientifically set out and accordingly also not terminologically fixed (one has, where one touched it, always mixed it with other modifications), and since for it also in general language an unambiguous name is lacking, we can only come at it circumscriptively and step-wise through exclusions. For all the expressions just put together for preliminary indication contain something excessive in sense. Everywhere an arbitrary doing is co-designated, while it should not at all matter. We exclude it therefore. The result of this doing has in any case a peculiar content that can be considered in itself, apart from the fact that it "stems" from it (which of course would also be a phenomenological datum), as it is indeed also possible and occurs in the experience-connection without such arbitrariness. This isolates the neutrality-modification from any act. The expressions used (bracketing, refraining, thinking-oneself-into) all suggest a voluntary act, but the neutrality-modification is not essentially an act: it can occur without any arbitrary doing. So one must consider the neutralized content in itself, apart from any performing.

He describes the neutralized positing: the positing-character become powerless. If we thus exclude from the leaving-undecided all the voluntary, but understand it also not in the sense of a doubtful or hypothetical, then there remains a certain "having-undecided," or better still, a "having-standing-there" of something that is not "actually" conscious as standing-there. The positing-character has become powerless. The belief is now seriously no belief any more, the conjecturing not seriously conjecturing, the negating not seriously negating, and so on. It is "neutralized" believing, conjecturing, negating, and the like, whose correlates repeat those of the unmodified experiences, but in radically modified manner: the being simpliciter, the possible-, probable-, questionable-being, likewise the not-being and each of the other negates and affirmates, is consciousness-wise there, but not in the manner of the "actual," rather as "merely thought," as "mere thought." This describes neutralization precisely. In the neutralized positing, the positing-character is powerless: the belief is not seriously believing, the negating not seriously negating. The correlate is "merely thought," not "actual." So neutralization gives the content as merely-thought, without any serious positing.

He connects the neutrality-bracket to the phenomenological bracket. Everything has the modifying "bracket," closely related to that of which we earlier spoke so much, and which is so important for the way-preparation to phenomenology. The positings simpliciter, the non-neutralized positings, have as correlate-results "propositions," which are throughout characterized as "being." The possibility, probability, questionability, the not-being and the yes-being, all that is itself something "being": namely as such characterized in the correlate, as the "meant" in consciousness. The neutralized positings, however, differ essentially in that their correlate contains nothing positable, nothing actually predicable; the neutral consciousness plays in no respect for its conscious the role of a "belief." This connects the neutrality-modification to the epoché. The phenomenological bracketing (§§31–32) is closely related to the neutrality-modification: both set the positing out of action. But there is a difference (developed at §110): the non-neutralized positings yield "propositions" characterized as being (the possibility is itself a being); the neutralized positings yield nothing positable, nothing predicable — the neutral consciousness is in no way a belief. So the neutralized content cannot be predicated of (one cannot judge "it is so"), because there is no positing in it.

### § 110\. Neutralized Consciousness and the Adjudication of Reason. The Assuming

Husserl states the decisive mark: neutralized noeses fall outside the adjudication of reason. That here really an incomparable peculiarity of consciousness lies present shows itself in this, that the proper, non-neutralized noeses by their essence underlie an "adjudication of reason" (Rechtsprechung der Vernunft), while for the neutralized ones the question after reason and unreason gives no sense. This is the decisive mark of the neutrality-modification. A real (non-neutralized) positing is subject to the adjudication of reason: it can be valid or invalid, justified or not (a belief can be true or false, a conjecture warranted or not). But a neutralized positing is not subject to reason at all: it makes no sense to ask whether a mere thinking-of is true or false, justified or not. So neutralization removes the content from the jurisdiction of reason. (This connects to the theory of reason, the Fourth Part: reason adjudicates positings, but the neutralized has no positing to adjudicate.)

He states it correlatively for the noemata. Likewise, correlatively, for the noemata. Everything noematically characterized as being (certain), as possible, presumable, questionable, null, and so on, can be characterized "validly" or "invalidly" so, it can be "in truth" being, possible, null, and so on. On the contrary, the mere thinking-oneself "posits" nothing, it is no positional consciousness. The "mere thought" of actualities, possibilities, and so on, "pretends" nothing, it is neither to be acknowledged as correct nor to be rejected as incorrect. This restates it noematically. A real noematic positing (being, possible) can be valid or invalid, true or false. But the merely-thought posits nothing, pretends nothing, and so is neither correct nor incorrect. The neutralized noema is outside truth and falsity.

He distinguishes the neutrality-modification from the assuming (Annahme). Of course every mere thinking-oneself can be transferred into an assuming (Annehmen), positing-down (Ansetzen), and this new modification (just like that of the thinking-oneself) underlies the unconditionally free arbitrariness. But assuming is again something like positing, the assumption (Ansatz) again a kind of "proposition," only that it is a quite proper modification of the belief-positing, standing over against and aside from the main series treated above. It can enter into the unity of rationally-to-be-judged positings as member (its assumption as hypothetical "antecedent" or consequent) and thereby \[be subject to reason\]. This is the crucial distinction. The "assuming" (Annahme, e.g. the antecedent of a hypothetical, "suppose that S is P") is not the neutrality-modification. The assuming is still a kind of positing (a proper belief-modification): it can enter into rational judgment (as the antecedent of an inference) and so is subject to reason. The neutrality-modification, by contrast, posits nothing and is outside reason. So one must not confuse the merely-thought (neutralized, outside reason) with the assumed (a positing, hypothetical, within reason). This is a fundamental error to avoid (against, e.g., Meinong's theory of assumptions, which Husserl thought conflated them).

He warns of two equivocations in "think." It is a fundamental error to mix the one and the other, and to overlook the equivocation lying in the talk of mere thinking-oneself, or of mere thought. To this comes the likewise disturbing equivocation that lies in the word think, insofar as it is sometimes related to the distinguished sphere of the explicating, conceiving, and expressing thinking, to the logical thinking in a specific sense, and sometimes to the positional as such, which, as we just had it here in view, asks after no explicating and conceiving predicating. So "think" is doubly equivocal: (i) "merely think" can mean neutralized (outside reason) or assumed (hypothetical, within reason); (ii) "think" can mean logical-conceptual thinking (explicating, predicating) or the positional as such (the bare positing, pre-predicative). These equivocations must be sorted out.

He notes all this is found in the sphere of sensuous intuition. All the discussed occurrences we find in the sphere, at first preferred by us, of the mere sensuous intuitions and their modifications into dark presentations. So the neutrality-modification and the assuming are found already at the level of sensuous intuition (perception, memory, phantasy), not only at the level of predicative thought. This keeps the analysis at the pre-predicative level.

### § 111\. The Neutrality-Modification and Phantasy

Husserl warns against confusing the neutrality-modification with phantasy, while noting phantasy is itself a neutrality-modification. But still another dangerous equivocation of the expression "merely-think-oneself" comes into question, or a very near-lying confusion is to be warded off, namely that between neutrality-modification and phantasy. The confusing and really not easily disentangled thing lies here in this, that phantasy itself is in fact a neutrality-modification, that it, despite the peculiarity of its type, is of universal significance, applicable to all experiences, that it plays its role also in most formations of the thinking-oneself and yet must be distinguished from the general neutrality-modification with its manifold formations following all positing-kinds. This is the crucial and subtle point. Phantasy is itself a neutrality-modification (a phantasying does not posit its object as actual). But phantasy is not the general neutrality-modification; it is one specific kind. So one must distinguish phantasy (a specific neutralizing) from the general neutrality-modification (which applies to all positing-kinds).

He defines phantasy as the neutrality-modification of positing re-presentation (memory). More closely carried out, phantasying in general is the neutrality-modification of "positing" re-presentation, hence of memory in the widest conceivable sense. Here it is to be noted that in ordinary speech re-presentation (reproduction) and phantasy go through one another. We use the expressions so that, taking account of our analyses, we leave the general word re-presentation without indication in respect of whether the relevant "positing" is a proper or a neutralized one. Then re-presentations in general divide into the two groups: memories of every kind and their neutrality-modifications. That this division nonetheless cannot count as a genuine classification will show itself further on. This defines phantasy precisely. Re-presentation (Vergegenwärtigung) is the genus; it divides into positing re-presentation (memory, which posits its object as having been) and neutralized re-presentation (phantasy, which does not posit). So phantasy is the neutralized memory: the re-presentation with the positing neutralized. (Husserl notes this division is not a genuine classification, for reasons developed later.)

He relates every experience to its memory- and phantasy-modifications. On the other hand, every experience in general (every so-to-speak really living one) is a "present-being" experience. To its essence belongs the possibility of reflection upon it, in which it is necessarily characterized as certain and present being. Accordingly, to every experience, as to every originarily conscious individual being, corresponds a series of ideally possible memory-modifications. To the living-through, as originary consciousness of the experience, correspond as possible parallels memories of it, hence also as their neutrality-modifications phantasies. So for every experience, and however it may stand with the glance-direction of the pure ego. This generalizes. Every experience can be remembered (its memory-modification) and, neutralizing the memory, phantasied (its phantasy-modification). So every experience has both a memory-double and a phantasy-double.

He gives the example: in phantasy, the giving perception is itself phantasied. To illustrate: as often as we have re-presented any objects — let us at once assume it is a mere phantasy-world, and we are attentively turned to it — then it counts as belonging to the essence of the phantasying consciousness that not only this world, but also at the same time the perceiving "giving" it is phantasied. To it we are turned, to the "perceiving in phantasy" (that is, the neutrality-modification of memory) only then, when we, as earlier discussed, "reflect in the phantasy." This is an important structural point. When I phantasy a world, the perceiving that "gives" that world is itself phantasied (a phantasied perceiving). I am turned to the phantasy-world; I reach the phantasied perceiving only by reflecting "in" the phantasy. So phantasy carries within it a phantasied (neutralized) perceiving of the phantasy-world.

He distinguishes the phantasy-modification (of any experience) from the neutrality-modification (of any positing). It is, however, of fundamental significance not to confuse this ideally always-possible modification, which would transfer every experience, also the phantasying one itself, into the exactly corresponding mere phantasy or, what is the same, into the neutralized memory, with that neutrality-modification that we can oppose to every "positing" experience. In this respect memory is a quite special positing experience. Another is normal perception, again another the perceptive or reproductive possibility-, probability-, questionability-consciousness, the consciousness of doubt, of negation, affirmation, assumption, and so on. This sharpens the distinction. The phantasy-modification turns any experience into its phantasy (neutralized memory). The general neutrality-modification can be applied to any positing experience (perception, doubt, negation, etc.). Memory is just one positing experience among many; phantasy is its neutralization. But the general neutrality-modification neutralizes all of them. So phantasy (neutralized memory) is a special case; the general neutrality-modification is broader.

He gives the Dürer example: the neutrality-modification of normal perception is the image-object consciousness. We can, for example, convince ourselves that the neutrality-modification of normal, in unmodified certainty positing perception is the neutral image-object consciousness that we find as component in the normal considering of a perceptively presented depicting world. Let us try to make this clear to ourselves: let, say, the Dürer engraving "Knight, Death, and Devil" be considered. This is the famous Dürer example, distinguishing three levels. (i) Normal perception, whose correlate is the thing "engraving-sheet," this sheet in the portfolio. (ii) The perceptive consciousness in which, in the black lines, colorless little figures "knight on the horse," "death," and "devil" appear. To these we are not turned, in the aesthetic consideration, as objects; turned we are to the realities presented "in the image," more precisely, to the "depicted" realities, the knight of flesh and blood, and so on. (iii) The consciousness mediating and making possible the depicting, of the "image" (the little gray figures, in which by virtue of the founded noeses another presents itself depictingly through similarity), is now an example for the neutrality-modification of perception. This depicting image-object stands before us neither as being, nor as not-being, nor in any other positing-modality; or rather, it is conscious as being, but as quasi-being in the neutrality-modification of being. This is the analysis of image-consciousness. Three levels: (i) the physical engraving-sheet (normally perceived, posited as real); (ii) the depicting image-object (the gray figures, perceived but not posited as real — the "image"); (iii) the depicted (the flesh-and-blood knight, to which we are aesthetically turned). The image-object (level ii) is the neutrality-modification of perception: it appears, but is neither posited as being nor as not-being — it is "quasi-being," neutralized. So aesthetic image-consciousness involves a neutralized perception (the image-object given as quasi-being).

He notes the depicted too is neutralized in pure aesthetic comportment, and that neutralization is not a privation. Likewise, however, also the depicted, if we comport ourselves purely aesthetically and take the same again as "mere image," without conferring on it the stamp of being or not-being, of possible- or presumable-being, and the like. That says, however, as is evident, no privation, but a modification, precisely that of neutralization. We may only not present it to ourselves as a re-forming operation attached to a prior positing. That it can occasionally also be. But it need not be. This completes the image-analysis. In pure aesthetic comportment, the depicted (the knight) too is taken as "mere image," neutralized (not posited as being or not-being). Neutralization is not a privation (a lack) but a positive modification. And it need not be a re-forming operation following a prior positing; it can occur originally (the image-object is given as neutralized from the start, without first being posited and then neutralized).

### § 112\. The Iterability of the Phantasy-Modification. The Non-Iterability of the Neutrality-Modification

Husserl states the radical difference: phantasy is iterable, neutralization is not. The radical difference between phantasy, in the sense of neutralizing re-presentation, and neutralizing modification in general shows itself — to highlight sharply this decisive point of difference — in that the phantasy-modification as re-presentation is iterable (there are phantasies of arbitrary level: phantasies "in" phantasies), while the repetition of the "operation" of neutralization is essentially excluded. This is the decisive criterion distinguishing phantasy from the general neutrality-modification. Phantasy is iterable: there are phantasies in phantasies, at any level (§100). But the neutralization-operation cannot be repeated: neutralizing a neutralized consciousness yields nothing new (a neutralized neutral is just the neutral). So phantasy (iterable) and the general neutrality-modification (non-iterable) are essentially different, even though phantasy is a kind of neutralization.

He anticipates resistance and locates the source of the error. Our assertion of the possibility of iterated reproductive (as well as depicting) modifications will probably meet rather general contradiction. That will first change when practice in the genuine phenomenological analysis is more widespread than is at present still the case. As long as one treats experiences as "contents" or as psychic "elements," which despite all fashionable contestation of the atomizing and reifying psychology are yet regarded as a kind of little things; as long as one accordingly believes one can find the difference between "sensation-contents" and corresponding "phantasy-contents" only in factual marks of "intensity," "fullness," and the like, it cannot get better. This locates the error. The resistance to iterated phantasy comes from treating experiences as little thing-like contents, and treating the phantasm as a faded sensation (differing only in intensity/fullness). This is the sensualist error (the reifying psychology). One must instead see that the difference is a consciousness-difference.

He states the positive point: the phantasm is phantasy of the sensation-datum, a consciousness-difference. One would have to first learn to see that here it is a matter of a consciousness-difference, that therefore the phantasm is not a mere anemic sensation-datum, but according to its essence phantasy of the corresponding sensation-datum; further, that this "of" can in no way come in through any so-extensive thinning of the intensity, of the content-fullness, and so on, of the relevant sensation-datum. This is the key correction. The phantasm (the phantasy-content) is not a faded sensation but phantasy of the sensation-datum: it has the intentional "of" (it points to the corresponding sensation). No degree of thinning a sensation makes it a phantasm; the difference is intentional (consciousness-of), not a matter of intensity. So phantasy is an intentional modification, not a weak sensation.

He states what the trained phenomenologist sees: phantasy-levels and the gap to perception. Whoever is practiced in consciousness-reflections (and beforehand has learned at all to see the givennesses of intentionality) will see the consciousness-levels that are present in the phantasies in phantasies, or the memories in memories or in phantasies, without further ado. He will then also see what lies in the essence-kind of these level-formations: namely, that every phantasy of higher level can be freely transferred into a direct phantasy of what is mediately phantasied in that, while this free possibility does not hold in the transition from the phantasy to the corresponding perception. Here there is for spontaneity a gulf, which the pure ego can overstep only in the essentially new form of realizing acting and creating (to which also the arbitrary hallucinating is to be reckoned). This is the concluding structural insight. In nested phantasy, one can always "flatten" a higher-level phantasy into a direct phantasy of what it mediately phantasies (a phantasy of a phantasied tree becomes a direct phantasy of the tree). But one cannot freely pass from phantasy to perception: there is a gulf. To get from phantasy to perception requires a wholly new kind of act — realizing action and creating (including arbitrary hallucinating). So phantasy and perception are separated by a gulf that mere spontaneity cannot cross; only realizing action (making the phantasied actual, or hallucinating) crosses it. The footnote notes that the Logical Investigations already treated, in the main, the points so far developed in the doctrine of the neutrality-modification (especially as regards the image-object). This completes the core of the neutrality-modification doctrine; §113 turns to positionality and neutrality as actuality and inactuality, deepening the analysis.

### § 113\. Actual and Potential Positings

Husserl distinguishes actual from potential positing, introducing "positional consciousness." Our considerations on neutrality-modification and positing press toward important continuations. We have used the talk of "positing" consciousness in a wide sense that necessarily needs a differentiation. Let us separate actual and potential positing, and use as the general title, which we nonetheless cannot do without, "positional consciousness." So "positing" was used loosely; it must be split into actual positing (a positing actually performed) and potential positing (a positing not performed but available). The general title for both is "positional consciousness."

He relates this to the attention-distinction but warns they do not coincide. The difference between actuality and potentiality of the positing stands in near relation to the earlier-discussed actuality-differences of attention and inattention (back-reference to §§35, 37, 92), but by no means coincides with them. Through regard to the neutrality-modification, a doubleness enters into the general distinction between actuality and inactuality of the attentional ego-turning, or a double-meaning into the concept of the talk of actuality, whose essence we must clarify. This is an important refinement. The positing-actuality (actual vs. potential positing) is related to but not the same as the attention-actuality (attended vs. unattended). The neutrality-modification introduces a doubleness: "actuality" can mean attentional actuality (being attended) or positing actuality (being actually posited). These must be distinguished.

He recalls the neutrality-contrast and adds the potentiality-contrast. The neutrality-modification met us in the contrast of the actual believing, conjecturing, and so on, with the peculiarly modified consciousness of the "merely thinking-oneself into" a believing, conjecturing, and so on; correlatively spoken, in the contrast of having the being, probable-being, and so on, "actually" before oneself or "actually posited," and having it not actually posited in the manner of a mere "standing-there." But from the start we also indicated the essentially different comportment of a non-neutral and neutral consciousness with respect to the potentiality of positings. From every "actual" consciousness, various positings potentially enclosed in it can be drawn, and these are then actual positings: in everything actually thetically meant there stick actual predicabilia. A neutral consciousness, however, "contains" in itself no kind of "actual" predicabilia. The unfolding through attentional actualities, through turnings to the various predicates of the conscious objective, yields nothing but neutral acts, or nothing but modified predicates. This is the key contrast. From an actual (positing) consciousness, one can draw out actual positings (actual predicates: in "the tree is green" one can draw "the green," "the trunk," etc. as actual predicabilia). From a neutral consciousness, unfolding yields only neutral acts (modified, non-actual predicates). So the potentiality of positing differs: a positing consciousness has a field of potential actual positings; a neutral consciousness has a field of potential neutral (shadow) positings.

He recalls the time-consciousness result and the experience's "perceived-like" being-character. In the considerations of the section before last (§111) it showed that every actual experience, as present-being — or, as we can also say, as the temporal unity constituted in phenomenological time-consciousness — carries its being-character with it in a certain way, similar to a perceived. To every actual experience-present corresponds ideally a neutrality-modification, namely a possible phantasy-experience-present exactly corresponding to it in content. Every such phantasy-experience is characterized not as actually present being, but as "quasi" present being. This recalls §111. Every actual experience has a being-character (it is present, like a perceived), and a corresponding phantasy-double (which is "quasi" present). So the experience itself is given as being (in inner time-consciousness), with a phantasy-counterpart that is quasi-being.

He states the result: the original time-consciousness functions like a perceiving, with a phantasy counterpart. It stands therewith in fact quite similarly as in the comparison of the noematic givennesses of an arbitrary perception with those of a phantasy (phantasy-consideration) ideally exactly corresponding to it: every perceived is characterized as "actually present being," every parallel phantasied as content-wise the same, but as "mere phantasy," as "quasi" present being. So: the original time-consciousness itself functions like a perception-consciousness and has its counterpart in a corresponding phantasy-consciousness. This is an important structural result. The inner time-consciousness (in which experiences are constituted) functions like a perceiving: it gives the experiences as actually present (posited). And it has a phantasy-counterpart (in which the experiences would be quasi-present). So the time-consciousness is the "perceiving" of the experiences.

He carefully qualifies: this time-consciousness is not a continuous immanent perceiving in the pregnant sense. This all-encompassing time-consciousness is, however, self-evidently no continuous immanent perceiving in the pregnant sense, that is, in that of an actually positing perceiving, which is itself an experience in our sense, a thing lying in immanent time, presently enduring, constituted in time-consciousness. In other words, it is self-evidently not a continuous inner reflecting, in which the experiences would become objective in the specific sense as posited, actually grasped as being. This is a crucial qualification (guarding the time-consciousness doctrine). The inner time-consciousness is not itself an actual immanent perceiving (which would be one more experience needing constitution, leading to regress). It functions "like" a perceiving (giving the experiences as present), but it is not an actual reflecting that posits the experiences as objects. So the experiences are constituted as present without being actually perceived/posited; the time-consciousness is a pre-reflective, non-positing awareness.

He distinguishes immanent reflection (actual positing) from this pre-reflective time-consciousness. Among the experiences there are distinguished ones, called immanent reflections, more specially immanent perceptions, which are directed at their objects actually being-grasping, being-positing. Beside them there are among the same also transcendently directed, in the same sense being-positing perceptions, the so-called outer ones. "Perception" in the normal word-sense says not only in general that some thing appears to the ego in bodily presence, but that the ego becomes aware of the appearing thing, grasps it as actually existing, posits it. This actuality of the existence-positing is, according to what was earlier carried out, neutralized in the perceptive image-consciousness. This distinguishes the levels. Immanent perception (reflection) actually posits its object as being. Outer perception does too (positing the thing as actually existing). But the image-consciousness neutralizes this positing (the image-object is not posited as being). So perception in the full sense is being-positing; the image-consciousness is its neutralization.

He gives the image-consciousness example of attention-actuality without positing-actuality. Turned to the "image" (not the depicted), we grasp as object nothing actual, but precisely an image, a fictum. The "grasping" has the actuality of the turning, but it is not "actual" grasping, but mere grasping in the modification of the "quasi," the positing is not actual positing, but in the "quasi" modified. Through turning the mental glance away from the fictum, the attentional actuality of the neutralized positing passes over into potentiality: the image still appears, but is not "attended," it is not — in the mode of the "quasi" — grasped. In the essence of this situation and its potentiality lie possibilities for actual glance-turnings, which here, however, never let actualities of positing arise. This is the key illustration. In image-consciousness, I can attend to the image-object (attentional actuality) without positing it as being (no positing actuality): the grasping is "quasi," neutralized. Turning away, the attention becomes potential. And no glance-turning to the image ever yields an actual positing (the image stays neutralized however I attend to it). So attention-actuality and positing-actuality come apart: one can actually attend to a neutralized object without positing it.

He extends to memory and phantasy. Similarly it stands when we compare "actual" (not neutral, really positing) memories with such in which the remembered, say through glance-turning-away, still appears but is no longer actual. The potentiality of the positing of the "still" appearing says here that through attentional actuality not only grasping cogitationes in general arise, but throughout "actually" grasping, actually positing ones. In the neutrality-modification of the memories, that is, the mere phantasies, we have again the attentional potentialities, whose transformation into actualities yields indeed "acts" (cogitationes), but throughout neutralized, throughout doxic positings in the mode of the quasi. The phantasied is conscious not as "actually" present, past, or future; it merely "hovers" before one as that, without actuality of positing. Mere glance-turning cannot remove this neutrality, just as little as in other cases it can produce positing-actuality. This extends the point to memory/phantasy. Actual memory, when attended, yields actual positings (the remembered posited as having been). Phantasy (neutralized memory), when attended, yields only neutralized positings (the phantasied as quasi-being). And attention cannot convert the one into the other: glance-turning cannot make a phantasy into a positing memory.

He illustrates with the perception-background as a unity of potential positings. Every perception has, this can serve us for further illustration, its perception-background. The specially grasped thing has its perceptively co-appearing thingly environment, lacking special existence-theses. It too is "actually-being" environment, it is so conscious that — in the sense of an essence-possibility — actually being-positing glances can direct themselves upon it. It is in a certain way a unity of potential positings. Likewise with memory regarding its memory-background; or also with perception, or memory, regarding its halo of retentions and protentions, of back-memories and fore-memories, which press forward in greater or lesser fullness and changing in their clarity-degrees, but are not performed in the form of actual theses. In all these cases the actualization of the "potential positings" through corresponding glance-turnings (attentional actuality) leads necessarily to ever new actual positings, and that belongs to the essence of these situations. This is the structure of the positing-background. The perceptual background (the co-appearing environment, the retentional/protentional halo) is a unity of potential positings: it is given as actually-being, available for actual positing whenever I turn to it. Turning to any background-item yields an actual positing. So the background is a field of potential actual positings.

He contrasts the neutral case: the background of the image/phantasy yields only modified positings. But if we pass over to the parallel neutrality-modifications, then everything translates into the modification of the quasi, also the "potentiality" itself. Attentional backgrounds also has (and necessarily) the image-object or phantasy-object. Again "background" is a title for potential turnings and "graspings." But the production of the actual turning here in principle leads not to actual positings, but always only to modified ones. This contrasts the neutral case. The image-object and phantasy-object also have a background (a halo of co-phantasied items), which is a field of potential turnings. But turning to them yields only modified (neutralized) positings, never actual ones. So the neutral consciousness has a field of potential neutral positings, paralleling the positing consciousness's field of potential actual positings.

He extends to the modal theses and notes that living "in" them performs other theses but not the doxic Urtheses. Likewise it stands, what especially interests us here, with modal variations of the specific belief-theses (the doxic Urtheses) with the conjectures, surmises, questions, and so on, likewise with the negations and affirmations. The correlates conscious in them, the possibility, probability, the not-being, and the like, can experience doxic positing and thereby at the same time specific "objectification," but while we "live" "in" the conjecturing, questioning, rejecting, affirming, and the like, we perform no doxic Urtheses — although indeed other "theses," in the sense of a necessary generalization of the concept, namely conjecture-theses, questionability-theses, negation-theses, and so on. But at any time we can perform the corresponding doxic Urtheses; in the essence of the phenomenological situations grounds the ideal possibility of actualizing the potential theses enclosed in them (back-reference to §105). This is the key point about modalities. When I live "in" a conjecturing (or questioning, negating), I perform a "thesis" (a conjecture-thesis) but not a doxic Urthesis (a simple belief-positing). However, I can always convert it into a doxic Urthesis: I can turn the glance to the probability and posit it as being ("it is probable that..."). So every modal act contains a potential doxic Urthesis, actualizable at will. This is the doxic potentiality grounding all acts (the basis of the Urdoxa's universality, §117).

He closes §113 noting the neutral case again translates everything. This actualization leads now, if it was from the start a matter of actual theses, always again to actual theses, as potentially enclosed in the starting-theses. If we translate the starting-theses into the language of neutrality, then the potentiality also translates into the same. If we perform conjectures, questions, and the like, in mere phantasy, then everything carried out before remains, only with changed sign. All the doxic theses and being-modalities to be drawn out, through the possible glance-turnings of attention, from the original act, or act-noema, are now neutralized. This closes §113. In the neutral case (conjecturing in phantasy), everything is the same but with "changed sign": the potential theses are neutralized. So the positing/neutral distinction runs through the whole structure of actuality and potentiality.

Husserl's footnote here delivers his verdict on Meinong. The footnote (attached to §112's close, bearing on this material) notes that the Logical Investigations already reached the right conception, especially regarding the relation to phantasy: the Fifth Investigation, §39, opposed "qualitative" and "imaginative modification," the former having the sense of the neutrality-modification discussed here. Husserl then explains why he could link only to his own older writings and not to Meinong's book On Assumptions (Über Annahmen, 1902), which treated closely related questions. In his judgment, that book — which shows such far-reaching coincidences with the parallel sections of the Logical Investigations, in matter and theoretical thought — brought no real advances beyond his own attempts, neither materially nor methodically; many thought-motives on which he still lays weight are not heeded there, and the very confusions clarified in his last expositions (the conflation of the neutrality-modification with the assuming, §110) make up the main core of Meinong's conception of assumptions. So Husserl judges that Meinong's theory of assumptions rests precisely on the confusion (neutrality-modification vs. assuming) that §§109–110 expose.

### § 114\. Further on the Potentiality of the Thesis and the Neutrality-Modification

Husserl states that the positing/neutral distinction runs through inattentional consciousness too. The difference of non-neutral and neutral consciousness concerns, according to the analyses carried out, not merely consciousness-experiences in the attentional mode of the cogito, but also in that of attentional inactuality. It manifests itself then in the double comportment of these consciousness-"backgrounds" at their attentional transformation into "foregrounds," more precisely spoken, at their transformation into attentional actualities, with which the original experience passes over into a doxic cogito, indeed into an Urdoxa. That is self-evidently possible under all circumstances; for to the essence of every intentional experience belongs the possibility of "looking at" its noeses as well as its noemata, the noematically constituted objectivities and their predicates — to grasp them positing in the manner of the Urdoxa. This generalizes the distinction. The positing/neutral difference holds even for inattentional (background) consciousness: it shows in how the background behaves when attended (transformed into a foreground/cogito). And it is always possible to "look at" the noeses or noemata of any experience and posit them in the Urdoxa (the universal doxic potentiality, §105).

He states the central thesis: the neutrality-modification is a fundamental peculiarity of all consciousness. The situation is, as we can also say, that the neutrality-modification is not a special modification attached to the actual theses, the only ones that are really theses, but concerns a fundamental-essential peculiarity of all consciousness whatever, which expresses itself in the comportment toward the actual urdoxic positability or non-positability. Hence the necessity to show it precisely at the actual Urpositings, or at the modification they undergo. This is the central thesis. The neutrality-modification is not a local modification of theses but a fundamental feature of all consciousness: every consciousness is either positional (its doxic potentiality leads to actual positings) or neutral (it leads only to neutralized positings). This is why it had to be shown at the actual Urpositings (where the contrast is clearest).

He states the radical division of all consciousness, with the cogito-counterpart structure. More closely determined, it is a matter of the following: Consciousness whatever, whatever kind and form it may be, is shot through with a radical division. First, as we know, to every consciousness in which the pure ego does not from the start live as "performing" it, which thus does not from the start have the form of the "cogito," belongs the essentially-possible modification that transfers it into this form. There exist now two fundamental possibilities in the manner of the consciousness-performance within the mode cogito, or otherwise expressed: To every cogito belongs an exactly corresponding counterpart such that its noema has in the parallel cogito its exactly corresponding counter-noema. This is the radical division. Every cogito has an exactly corresponding counterpart-cogito, whose noema is the counter-noema (the neutralized version). So consciousness is doubled: every act has its neutral shadow, every noema its neutralized counter-noema.

He describes the relation of the parallel acts: actual versus shadow. The relation of the parallel "acts" consists in that the one of the two is an "actual" act, the cogito an "actual," "actually positing" cogito, while the other is a "shadow" of an act, an improper, a not actually positing cogito. The one really performs, the other is mere mirroring of a performing. To this corresponds the radical difference of the correlates: on the one side the constituted noematic performance that has the character of the unmodified, actual performance, on the other the "mere thought" of the exactly corresponding performance. This describes the doubling. The positing act really performs (posits its object); the neutral act is a "shadow," a mere mirroring of a performing (it does not really posit). Correlatively, the positing noema is an actual performance; the neutral noema is the "mere thought" of that performance. So the neutral act/noema is the shadow/mirror of the positing act/noema.

He states that the actual and modified are exactly alike yet not of the same essence, introducing the "counter-essence." The actual and modified correspond ideally absolutely exactly and are yet not of the same essence. For the modification transfers to the essences: to the originary essence corresponds its counter-essence as "shadow" of the same essence. Naturally one may not bring into the figurative talk of shadow, mirroring, image anything of mere appearance, anything of deceptive opinion, and the like, with which indeed actual acts, or positional correlates, would be given. This is a subtle and important point. The positing and neutral acts are exactly alike in content, yet not of the same essence: the modification transfers to the essence itself. So there is the originary essence (e.g. perceiving) and its "counter-essence" (the neutralized perceiving), the "shadow" of the same essence. The figurative talk of "shadow"/"mirror" must not suggest mere appearance or deception (which would themselves be positing phenomena). The neutral is not an illusion but a genuine (neutralized) modification.

He reminds that this is not the phantasy-modification. Before the other so-near-lying confusion of the modification here in question with the phantasy-modification, which likewise creates to every experience — as experience-present in inner time-consciousness — a counterpart, its phantasy-image, need not be warned anew. So the general neutrality-modification (the positing/neutral doubling) must not be confused with the phantasy-modification (which creates a phantasy-image of every experience), as established at §§111–112.

He states the two fundamental propositions for the doxic domain. The radical division of the intentional experiences into two classes, which stand to one another like actuality and powerless mirroring of noematic performing, manifests itself to us here (at our starting-point from the doxic domain) in the following fundamental propositions: Every cogito is in itself either a doxic Urpositing or not. But by virtue of a lawfulness again belonging to the general fundamental essence of consciousness whatever, every cogito can be transferred into a doxic Urpositing. This sets up the universal doxic thesis. Every cogito either is a doxic Urpositing or can be converted into one. So the doxic Urpositing (the simple belief) underlies all consciousness: every act can be turned into a believing (the Urdoxa, §117).

He describes how every thetic character becomes a being-character. That, however, in manifold manner and in particular so, that every "thetic character" in the widest sense, which constitutes itself as correlate of a noetic "thesis" (in correspondingly widest sense) belonging to the cogito in the noema of this cogito, experiences the transformation into a being-character and so takes on the form of a being-modality of the widest sense. In this manner the character of the "probable," which is the noematic correlate of the conjecturing, specifically of the "act-character," the "thesis" of the conjecturing as such, transforms itself into the probable-being; likewise the noematic character of the "questionable," this specific correlate of the questionability-thesis, into the form of the questionable-being, the negation-correlate into the form of the not-being: nothing but forms that have so to speak taken on the stamp of the actual doxic Urthesis. This describes the doxic objectification. Every thetic character (probable, questionable, not) can be transformed into a being-character (probable-being, questionable-being, not-being) by performing the doxic Urthesis on it. So the modalities become being-modalities, "stamped" by the Urdoxa. (This is the result of §105, now systematized.)

He extends "thesis" to all act-spheres (feeling, wish, will). That goes, however, still further. We will find grounds to extend the concept of the thesis over all act-spheres and so, for example, to speak of liking-theses, wish-theses, will-theses, with their noematic correlates "pleasing," "wished," "practically obligatory," and the like. Also these correlates take on, through the a priori possible transference of the relevant act into a doxic Urthesis, the form of being-modalities in an extremely extended sense: thus the "pleasing," "wished," "obligatory," and so on, becomes predicable; for in the actual Urbelief-positing it is conscious as pleasingly being, wishedly being, and so on (back-reference to §105). This extends the thesis-concept universally. Not only the doxic acts but all acts (feeling, wishing, willing) have "theses" (liking-theses, etc.) with noematic correlates (pleasing, wished, obligatory). And all these correlates can be objectified into being-predicates (pleasingly-being, etc.) by the doxic Urthesis. So every act-character can become a logical predicate (the universality of the doxic/logical sphere, grounding the universality of logic, §117).

He notes the transference preserves the noema's essence except for the givenness-mode. The transference is, however — in these examples — so to understand that it preserves the noema of the original experience according to its whole essence, only apart from the givenness-mode changing lawfully with the transference. But this point will still need a supplement (forward-reference to §117). So objectifying a value-character into a being-predicate preserves the noema's content (the value stays), changing only the givenness-mode (now given as a being). Husserl flags a needed supplement (§117, on the founding role of the doxic).

He states the radical division of cases: the Urdoxa actual or its powerless counterpart. The cases now divide radically by this, that the respective Urdoxa is either an actual one, so to speak an actually believed belief, or its powerless counterpart, the mere "thinking-oneself" (of being simpliciter, possible-being, and so on). What that doxic transformation of the respective original experience yields from itself, whether unfoldings of its noematic stocks into actual doxic Urpositings, or exclusively into urdoxic neutralities, that is absolutely firmly predetermined through the essence of the relevant intentional experience. From the start, therefore, in the essence of every consciousness-experience a firm sum of potential being-positings is prefigured, and indeed, according as the relevant consciousness is from the start constituted, a field of possible actual positings or possible neutral "shadow-positings." This states the radical division. Every experience is, by its essence, either positional (its doxic potentiality yields actual positings) or neutral (it yields only neutral shadow-positings). This is firmly predetermined by the experience's essence. So each experience has a field of potential positings, either actual or neutral.

He fixes the terminology: positional and neutral. And again: so constituted is consciousness whatever, that it is of a double type: primal-image and shadow, positional consciousness and neutral. The one characterized by this, that its doxic potentiality leads to actually positing doxic acts, the other by this, that it lets only shadow-images of such acts, only neutrality-modifications of such, proceed from itself; in other words, that it contains in its noematic stock nothing doxically graspable, or what is again equivalent, that it contains no "actual" noema, but only a counter-image of such. Only one doxic positability remains also to the neutral experiences: the one belonging to them as data of the immanent time-consciousness, determining them precisely as modified consciousness of a modified noema. The expressions "positional" and "neutral" shall from now on serve us terminologically. Every experience, whether it has the form of the cogito, whether it is in any special sense act or not, falls under this opposition. Positionality says therefore not presence or performance of an actual position, it expresses only a certain potentiality for the performance of actual positings. This fixes the terminology. Consciousness is of a double type: positional (its doxic potentiality leads to actual positings) and neutral (it yields only shadow-positings, contains no actual noema). The one exception: even neutral experiences can be doxically posited as data of inner time-consciousness (I can posit "there is this phantasy" — positing the phantasy-experience itself as being, though not its phantasied content). "Positional" and "neutral" become the fixed terms, applying to every experience. And "positionality" means not an actual position performed but a potentiality for actual positing. This completes the doctrine of positionality and neutrality. §115 applies these concepts and broadens the notions of act and performance, leading toward the founding role of the Urdoxa (§117).

### § 115\. Applications. The Extended Act-Concept. Act-Performances and Act-Stirrings

Husserl closes §114's terminological point, then confirms the universality of the positional/neutral difference. He notes that he includes the case where an experience is from the start a performed position under the concept of the positional experience, which is the less objectionable since by essential law to every performed positing belongs a plurality of potential positings.
