The difference between positionality and neutrality expresses, as has confirmed itself, no mere peculiarity referring to belief-positings, no mere kind of belief-modifications, such as conjecturing, questioning, and the like, or in other directions assumptions, negating, affirming are, hence not intentional variations of an Urmodus, of belief in the pregnant sense. It is in fact, as we had foretold, a universal consciousness-difference, which, however, for good reason appears, in our analytic course, linked to the difference, specially exhibited in the narrow sphere of the doxic cogito, between positional (that is, actual, real) believing and its neutral counterpart (the mere "thinking-oneself"). Highly remarkable and deep-lying essence-interweavings between act-characters of belief and all other kinds of act-characters, and so all consciousness-kinds whatever, came forward. This confirms the universality. The positional/neutral difference is not a belief-modification (like conjecture or doubt) but a universal consciousness-difference, shown most clearly in the doxic sphere but pervading all consciousness. And it reveals deep interweavings between belief-characters and all other act-characters.

He recalls the act-concept from §84 and the potentiality it presupposes. It is still important to take into account some earlier remarks (back-reference to §84). The cogito in general is the explicit intentionality. The concept of the intentional experience in general presupposes in so far already the opposition of potentiality and actuality, and indeed in the general meaning, as we can only recognize, in the transition to the explicit cogito and in the reflection upon the non-explicated experience and its noetic-noematic stocks, that it harbors intentionalities in itself, or noemata that belong to it. So, for example, with respect to the consciousness of the unattended but subsequently attendable background in perception, memory, and so on. The explicit intentional experience is a "performed" "I think." So the cogito (the explicit "I think") presupposes the potentiality/actuality distinction: we recognize that the non-explicit experience harbors intentionalities only by passing to the explicit cogito and reflecting. The background-consciousness (unattended but attendable) is the model.

He introduces the act-stirrings (Aktregungen) — acts not performed. The same can also pass over, by way of attentional changes, into a "non-performed" one. The experience of a performed perception, a performed judgment, feeling, will, does not disappear when attention turns "exclusively" to something new; wherein lies that the ego "lives" exclusively in a new cogito. The earlier cogito "fades away," sinks into the "dark," but it still always has a, though modified, experience-existence. Likewise cogitationes press up in the experience-background, now memory-wise or neutrally modified, now also unmodified. For example, a belief, an actual belief, "stirs"; we believe already "before we know it." Likewise under circumstances liking- or disliking-positings, desirings, also decisions are already living before we "live" "in" them, before we perform the proper cogito, before the ego judging, liking, desiring, willing "exercises itself." This introduces the act-stirrings. An act can be present without being performed: a belief "stirs" before I attend to it ("we believe before we know it"); a desire or decision can be alive before I "live in" it. So there are unperformed acts (act-stirrings), present and intentional but not performed by the ego.

He fixes the broadened act-concept: performed acts and act-stirrings. The cogito designates therefore in fact (and so we introduced the concept from the start) the proper act of perceiving, judging, liking, and so on. On the other hand, the whole construction of the experience in the described cases, with all its theses and noematic characters, is the same when this actuality is lacking. In so far we distinguish more distinctly performed acts and non-performed; the latter are either acts that have "fallen out of performance" or act-stirrings. The latter word we can quite well also use generally for non-performed acts in general. Such act-stirrings are lived with all their intentionalities, but the ego lives in them not as "performing subject." With this the act-concept broadens in a determinate and quite indispensable sense. This broadens the act-concept. "Act" in the strict sense is the performed cogito. But there are also non-performed acts: acts fallen out of performance, and act-stirrings (acts never yet performed). These are lived with all their intentionality, but the ego does not live in them as performing subject. So "act" broadens to include the non-performed.

He distinguishes act-performances (position-takings in the widest sense) from position-takings in the pregnant sense. The performed acts, or as it in a certain respect (namely with respect to the fact that it is a matter of processes) better is called, the act-performances make up the "position-takings" in the widest sense, while the talk of position-takings in the pregnant sense points back to founded acts, of the kind we will later discuss more closely: for example, to position-takings of hatred, or of the hating one to the hated, which on its side is for consciousness already constituted in noeses of lower level as existing person or thing; likewise would belong here position-takings of negation or affirmation to being-pretensions, and the like. This distinguishes two senses of "position-taking." In the widest sense, every act-performance is a position-taking. In the pregnant sense, "position-taking" means the founded acts (hatred toward an already-constituted person, negation toward a being-pretension): acts founded on lower acts that constitute their object. So the pregnant position-takings are the founded, higher-level acts.

He states that acts in the wider sense carry the neutral/positional difference. It is now clear that the acts in the wider sense just as the specific cogitationes carry in themselves the differences of neutrality and positionality, that they are, already before the transformation into cogitationes, noematically and thetically performing ones, only that we get their performances to view first through acts in the narrower sense, through cogitationes. The positings, or the positings in the mode of the "quasi," are already really present in them with the whole noeses to which these positings belong: presupposing the ideal case that they do not, in one with the transformation, also intentionally enrich themselves and otherwise change. In any case we can exclude these changes. This is the key point. The act-stirrings (non-performed acts) already carry the positional/neutral difference and already have their theses and noemata; we only get to see them by performing them (turning them into cogitationes). So the positing is already there in the act-stirring, before it is performed. Performing it merely makes it visible (apart from any enrichment the performing might add).

He recalls the doxic preference and the coincidence (Deckung) of every thetic character with a doxic thesis. Preferred in our whole discussions under the title neutrality were the doxic positings. The neutrality had its index in the potentiality. Everything rested on this, that every thetic act-character whatever (every act-"intention," for example the liking-intention, the valuing, willing intention, the specific character of the liking-, will-positing) harbors in its essence a character of the genus doxic thesis "coinciding" with it in a certain manner. According as the relevant act-intention is non-neutralized or neutralized, so is also the doxic thesis enclosed in it — which here was thought as Urthesis. This is the crucial coincidence-thesis. Every thetic act-character (liking, willing, etc.) harbors in its essence a coinciding doxic thesis. So a valuing harbors a doxic positing (of the value as being); a willing harbors a doxic positing (of the willed as being). And the neutrality of the act-character matches the neutrality of its enclosed doxic thesis. So the doxic is enclosed in every act-character.

He flags a coming restriction: the doxic modalities, not only the Urthesis, are enclosed in all theses. This preference of the doxic Urtheses will experience a restriction in the further analyses. It will become apparent that the essence-lawfulness we have exhibited demands a more exact determination, insofar as for now and more generally the doxic modalities (in the specific, also the assumptions encompassing sense) count, in place of the doxic Urtheses, as the "doxic theses" enclosed in all theses, or must step in for these. Within this general preference of doxic modalities whatever, the doxic Urthesis, the belief-certainty, then has the quite special preference that these modalities themselves are to be transformed into belief-theses, so that now again all neutrality has its index in the doxic potentiality in the distinguished, on the Urthesis back-related sense. This refines the thesis (anticipating §117). What is enclosed in every thesis is, more generally, a doxic modality (not just the Urthesis). But the Urthesis (belief-certainty) has the special preference that all modalities can be transformed into it. So the doxic potentiality, ultimately referred to the Urthesis, indexes all neutrality.

He notes the need for a broader basis and the confirmation to come. Now the (though with some desiderata) immediately in widest generality set-up, but only in special act-spheres made evident, propositions need a broader grounding-basis. The parallelism of noesis and noema we have not yet thoroughly discussed in all intentional domains. Precisely this main theme of our section presses also from itself to the extension of the analysis. In the performance of this extension, however, our general statements about the neutrality-modification will at the same time confirm and supplement themselves. So §§116–117 will broaden the basis (treating the founded acts) and thereby confirm and supplement the neutrality-doctrine.

### § 116\. Transition to New Analyses. The Founded Noeses and their Noematic Correlates

Husserl reviews what has been studied (the lower stratum) and presses to extend it. We have so far studied a series of general occurrences in the construction of the noeses and noemata within a large and yet very restricted frame — studied, of course, only in very modest measure, only so far as their determinate setting-off required and our guiding purpose, to procure for ourselves a general and yet content-rich representation of the problem-groups that the universal double-theme noesis and noema carries with it. Our studies related, however manifold complications they also drew in, to a mere under-layer of the experience-stream, to which still relatively simply-built intentionalities belong. We preferred (apart from the last fore-looking considerations) the sensuous intuitions, especially those of appearing realities, as well as the sensuous presentations (Vorstellungen) arising from them through the darkening and self-evidently united with them through genus-community. So the analyses so far treated only the lower stratum (the simple sensuous presentations). The task now is to extend to the founded (higher) acts (feeling, willing). The footnote notes that the firm delimitation of the widest concept of "presentation" is a task for systematic research, and points to the projected publications.

He notes the lower-stratum results recur at the higher level. The generality of the validity of our results presses itself, given the manner in which we conducted the investigation and made everything that could bind to the lower domain felt as incidental, at once forward, as soon as we extend the frame of research. We see then that all the differences between central sense-core (which, of course, very much needs further analysis) and the thetic characters grouping themselves around it recur, and likewise all modifications, which — like those of re-presentation, attention, neutralization — also attack the sense-core in their own ways, nonetheless leaving it its "identical." So the structure found at the lower level (sense-core surrounded by thetic characters; the modifications of re-presentation, attention, neutralization, which alter the givenness-mode but leave the core identical) recurs at the higher (founded) level.

He distinguishes two directions of advance: noetic syntheses and founded positing-kinds. We can now go forward in two different directions, on both sides leading to intentionalities founded in the presentations: either in the direction toward the noetic syntheses, or in that which leads us up to new-kind but founded "positing"-kinds. If we take the latter direction, we strike upon the (at first as simple as possible, that is, free from syntheses of lower or higher level) feeling, desiring, willing noeses, which are founded in "presentations," in perceptions, memories, sign-presentations, and so on, and show in their construction obvious differences of step-wise founding. This sets up two directions. (i) The noetic syntheses (the combining of acts into complex unities — collections, predications, etc., treated at §§118ff.). (ii) The founded positing-kinds (the feeling, desiring, willing acts, founded on presentations). §116 takes the second direction (the founded emotional/volitional acts); §118 will take the first (syntheses).

He prefers the positional forms and gives examples of founding. We now prefer everywhere for the total acts the positional forms (which, however, may not exclude neutral sub-stages), since what is to be said of them transfers suitably modified into the corresponding neutralizations. For example, an aesthetic liking is founded in a neutrality-consciousness of perceptive or reproductive content, a joy or sadness in a (non-neutralized) belief or a belief-modality, a willing or counter-willing likewise, but related to something valued as agreeable, beautiful, and the like. This gives examples of founding. Aesthetic liking is founded on a neutralized perception (we contemplate the image neutrally and like it); joy or sadness is founded on a positing belief (we are glad that something is so); willing is founded on a valuing. So the founded acts rest on lower acts (perceptions, beliefs, valuings), which may be positional or neutral.

He states the key result: new noetic moments bring new noematic moments, and a new sense-dimension. What interests us here, before all entering into the kinds of this construction, is that with the new noetic moments also new noematic moments appear in the correlates. On the one hand they are new characters, which are analogous to the belief-modes, but at the same time themselves, in their new content, possess doxological positability; on the other hand, with the new-kind moments also new-kind "apprehensions" combine, there constitutes itself a new sense, which is founded in that of the underlying noesis, at the same time enclosing it. The new sense brings a totally new sense-dimension in; with it constitute themselves no new determination-pieces of the mere "things," but values of the things, value-characters (Wertheiten), or concrete value-objectities: beauty and ugliness, goodness and badness; the use-object, the artwork, the machine, the book, the action, the deed, and so on. This is the central result. The founded acts (valuing, willing) bring new noematic moments: new characters (analogous to belief-modes, themselves doxically positable) and a new sense (founded on the underlying sense, enclosing it). The new sense brings a new sense-dimension: not new properties of the mere thing, but values of the thing (beauty, goodness), value-objectities (the artwork, the use-object). So valuing constitutes the value-object (the beautiful thing, the artwork) as a new founded objectity, on top of the mere thing.

He shows the founded noema has the same structure: a value-core surrounded by new thetic characters. Moreover, every full experience of the higher level shows in its full correlate a similar construction as we have beheld at the lowest level of noeses. In the noema of the higher level, say the valued as such, is a sense-core, surrounded by new thetic characters. The "value," the "pleasing," "joyful," and so on, functions similarly to the "possible," "probable," or possibly again: like "null" or "yes-actual" — although it would be wrong to put it in these series. This shows the parallel structure. The founded noema has a value-core (the valued-as-such) surrounded by new thetic characters (value, pleasing, joyful). These function analogously to the doxic modalities (possible, probable) but are not the same series. So the value-characters are a new dimension of thetic characters, parallel to but distinct from the doxic.

He shows the value-character is itself doxically positable and modalizable. Thereby the consciousness with respect to this new character is again a positional consciousness: the "value" is doxically positable as value being. The "being" belonging to the "value" as its characterization can further also be thought modalized, like every "being" or "certain": the consciousness is then consciousness of possible value, the "thing" intimates itself only as value; or also, it is conscious as presumably value, as not-value (which, however, does not mean as much as "valueless," as bad, ugly, and the like; simply the crossing-out of the "value" is expressed in the not-value). All such modifications attack the value-consciousness, the valuing noeses, not merely externally, but internally, as correspondingly the noemata. This shows the value-character's doxic positability and modalizability. The value can be posited as value-being, and modalized (possible value, presumable value, not-value). So the value-characters undergo the same doxic modalization as the being-characters. (And "not-value" is the crossing-out of value, not the positing of disvalue.)

He notes the attentional modifications multiply across the strata. Again there results a manifold of deep-going changes in the form of the attentional modifications, according as, in accordance with the multiplied essence-possibilities, the attending glance goes through the various intentional layers onto the "thing" and the thingly moments — which yields a connected system of modifications, which we already know as lower level — but then also onto the values, onto the constituted determinatenesses of higher level, through the apprehensions constituting them; again onto the noemata as such, onto their characters, or in the other reflection onto the noeses — and all that in the various specific modes of attending, noticing-in-passing, not-noticing, and the like. So the attentional glance can traverse the founded structure at many levels (the thing, the value, the noema, the noesis), yielding a rich system of attentional modifications.

He flags the difficult investigations needed. Extraordinarily difficult investigations are to be conducted, to unfold cleanly these complicated structures and bring them to full clarity, as for example how the "value-apprehensions" relate to the thing-apprehensions, how the new noematic characterizations (good, beautiful, and so on) relate to the belief-modalities, how they order themselves systematically in series and kinds, and what more of the like. So Husserl flags (but defers) the hard work of analyzing the founded structures (the value-apprehensions, the relation of value-characters to belief-modalities). This sets up §117, which completes the doctrine of the thesis and the Urdoxa.

### § 117\. The Founded Theses and the Conclusion of the Doctrine of the Neutralization-Modification. The General Concept of the Thesis

Husserl confirms that doxic positionality plays its role in the founded layers too. We now consider still the relation of the new noetic and noematic consciousness-layers to neutralization. We related this modification to the doxic positionality. This plays, as we easily convince ourselves, in the layers now come to setting-off in fact the role that we ascribed to it in advance in the widest act-sphere and specially discussed in that of the judgment-modalities. In the conjecture-consciousness "lies" the "presumable," "probable," positionally enclosed; likewise also in the liking-consciousness the "pleasing," in the joy-consciousness the "joyful," and so on. It lies therein, that is, it is accessible to the doxic positing, and therefore it is predicable. Accordingly every emotional consciousness with its new-kind founded emotional noeses falls under the concept of positional consciousness, as we had laid out this concept — with reference to doxic positionalities and finally to positional certainties. This confirms the doxic in the founded layers. The value-characters (pleasing, joyful) lie positionally enclosed in the founded acts, are doxically positable, and so predicable. So the founded emotional acts are positional consciousness.

He notes that relating neutralization to doxic positionality is in a way a detour. More closely seen, we will yet have to say that the relation of the neutrality-modification to the doxic positionality, so important insights underlie it, is in a certain way yet a detour. This flags a refinement. Relating neutralization to the doxic is correct but indirect (a detour), because the neutrality-modification really concerns all theses directly, not only via the doxic.

He establishes that the emotional/volitional acts are themselves positings (theses) in a widest unitary sense. Let us first make clear to ourselves that liking-acts (whether "performed" or not), likewise emotional and will-acts of every kind, are precisely "acts," "intentional experiences," and that to them in each case belongs the "intentio," the "position-taking"; or otherwise expressed: they are in a widest, but essentially unitary sense "positings," only precisely not doxic. In passing we said above quite correctly, act-characters in general are "theses" — theses in the extended sense and only in particular belief-theses or modalities of such. The essential analogy of the specific liking-noeses with the belief-positings is obvious, likewise of the wish-noeses, will-noeses, and so on. Also in valuing, wishing, willing something is "posited," apart from the doxic positionality that "lies" in them. That is also the source of all parallelizings of the various consciousness-kinds and all classifications of the same: one really classified the positing-kinds. This is the key generalization. The emotional/volitional acts are themselves positings (theses) in the widest sense (liking-theses, wish-theses, will-theses), not only the doxic positing enclosed in them. So "thesis" is a genus covering doxic and non-doxic (emotional, volitional) positings. And classifying consciousness-kinds is really classifying positing-kinds.

He states the archontic thesis structure of every intentional experience. To the essence of every intentional experience, whatever else may be findable in its concrete stock, belongs it, to have at least one, but as a rule several, in the manner of founding connected "positing-characters," "theses"; in this plurality is then necessarily one the so-to-speak archontic one, uniting all others in itself and ruling through them. This is the archontic-thesis doctrine. Every intentional experience has at least one, usually several, founded theses (positing-characters). Among these, one is "archontic" (ruling): the dominant thesis that unites and governs the others. (E.g. in a joy founded on a belief, the joy-thesis is archontic, ruling the underlying belief-thesis.) So the act has a layered thesis-structure with a ruling thesis.

He notes the generic community of all theses grounds the formal parallel disciplines. The highest genus-unity that unites all these specific "act-characters," the characters of the "positing," does not exclude essential and generic differentiatednesses. So the emotional positings are with the doxic as positings related, but by no means so belonging-together as all modalities of belief. With the generic essence-community of all positing-characters is that of their noematic positing-correlates (the "thetic characters in the noematic sense") given, and if we take the latter with their further noematic underlayers, the essence-community of all "propositions" (Sätze) eo ipso. Therein, however, ground ultimately the all-time felt analogies between general logic, general value-doctrine, and ethics, which, pursued into their last depths, lead to the constitution of general formal parallel disciplines, of formal logic, formal axiology, and formal practics. This is a far-reaching result. All theses share a generic essence (they are all positings), though with differences (the doxic modalities are more tightly bound than the emotional). Correlatively, all "propositions" (Sätze, the noematic positing-correlates) share an essence. This grounds the analogy between logic, value-theory, and ethics: there are three formal parallel disciplines — formal logic, formal axiology (value-theory), and formal practics (ethics) — corresponding to the doxic, the value-, and the will-theses. (This is the program of a formal axiology and practics parallel to formal logic, a major theme of Husserl's ethics lectures.)

He states the generalized thesis-proposition and extends positionality. We are therefore led back to the generalized title "thesis," to which we now relate the proposition: Every consciousness is either actually or potentially "thetic." The earlier concept "of the actual positing" and with it that of positionality experiences thereby a corresponding extension. Therein lies: Our doctrine of neutralization and its relation to positionality transfers to the extended concept of the thesis. This generalizes. Every consciousness is actually or potentially thetic (positing in the widest sense). So "positionality" extends to all theses, and the neutralization-doctrine applies to the extended thesis-concept.

He states the neutralization applies directly to all thetic characters. So to the thetic consciousness in general, whether it is now performed or not, belongs the general modification that we call the neutralizing, and indeed directly in the following manner. On the one side we have characterized the positional theses by this, that they are either actual theses or are to be transferred into actual ones; that they accordingly have "actually" positable noemata — actually positable in the extended sense. To this stand opposed the improper, the "quasi"-theses, the powerless mirrorings, incapable of taking into themselves any actual-thetic performings with respect to their, precisely neutralized, noemata. The difference between neutrality and positionality is a parallel noetic and noematic one; it concerns, as it is here grasped, all sorts of thetic characters directly, without the detour over the "positions," in the narrow and alone usual word-sense of the doxic Urpositings — at which it can yet alone exhibit itself. This is the refined result. The neutralization applies directly to all thetic characters (doxic, emotional, volitional), not only via the doxic. A positional thesis has actually-positable noemata; a neutralized thesis has powerless, non-positable noemata. The difference concerns all theses directly, though it is exhibited most clearly at the doxic Urpositings.

He explains the unique preference of the doxic, grounded in the things. That says, however, that the preference of these special doxic positings has its deep foundation in the things. According to our analyses, precisely the doxic modalities, and among them in special manner the doxic Urthesis, that of belief-certainty, have the unique preference that their positional potentiality overreaches the whole consciousness-sphere. By essential law, every thesis, of whatever genus, can, by virtue of the doxic characterizations belonging unremovably to its essence, be transformed into actual doxic positing. A positional act posits, but in whatever "quality" it posits, it also posits doxically; whatever is posited through it in other modes is also posited as being: only not actually. The actuality can, however, be essentially produced, in the manner of a principally possible "operation." Every "proposition," for example every wish-proposition, can therefore be transformed into a doxic proposition, and it is then in a certain way yet both in one: at once doxic proposition and wish-proposition. This is the unique preference of the doxic. The doxic potentiality overreaches all consciousness: every thesis (of any kind) can be transformed into an actual doxic positing, because every act also posits doxically (posits its object as being, though not actually). So a wish also posits the wished as being; this doxic positing can be actualized, yielding a doxic proposition that is at once a wish-proposition and a doxic one. So the doxic underlies all theses: every act harbors a doxic positing of its object as being.

He refines: the preference concerns the doxic modalities generally, the Urdoxa specially. Thereby the essence-lawfulness is, as we already indicated above, first that the preference of the doxic really concerns in general manner doxic modalities. For every emotional experience, every valuing, wishing, willing is in itself either characterized as being-certain or as being-intimated or as conjecturing, doubting valuing, wishing, willing. Thereby, for example, the value, if we are not set on the doxic positing-modalities, is precisely not actually posited in its doxic character. The value is conscious in the valuing, the pleasing in the liking, the joyful in the rejoicing, but at times so that we are in the valuing only not quite "sure"; or so that the thing intimates itself only as "value," as perhaps value, while we have not yet in the valuing taken sides for it. Living in such modifications of the valuing consciousness, we need not be set on the doxic. We can, however, become so, when we, say, live in the intimation-thesis and then pass over into the corresponding belief-thesis, which predicatively grasped now receives the form: "the thing might well be a value," or at a turn to the noetic side and to the valuing ego: "it intimates itself to me as value (perhaps value)." Likewise for other modalities. This refines the doctrine. The doxic enclosed in every act is, in general, a doxic modality (the valuing can be certain, or intimating, or conjecturing of its value). So the act harbors not just the Urdoxa but a doxic modality matching its own modal character. One can actualize this (turning the intimated value into "it might be a value").

He states the culminating result: doxic modalities and Urtheses lie in every act; every act harbors a "logical." In all thetic characters there stick in this manner doxic modalities and, when the mode is that of certainty, doxic Urtheses, coinciding with the thetic characters according to the noematic sense. Since this holds, however, also for the doxic variations, so lie (now no longer in noematic coincidence) also doxic Urtheses in every act. We can accordingly also say: Every act, or every act-correlate, harbors in itself a "logical," explicitly or implicitly. It is always logically to explicate, namely by virtue of the essential generality with which the noetic layer of "expressing" lets itself snuggle to everything noetic (or that of the expression to everything noematic). This is the culminating result of the doxic doctrine. Every act harbors doxic modalities, and ultimately doxic Urtheses (since every modality can be turned into certainty). So every act harbors a "logical" (a doxic-positing structure), explicitly or implicitly, and can be logically explicated (expressed in a proposition). This grounds the universality of logic: because every act harbors a doxic positing, every act can be expressed in a judgment, and logic (the doctrine of the doxic proposition) governs all consciousness. The "expressing" layer (§§124ff.) can attach to any noesis/noema. So the doxic/logical is the universal medium into which all consciousness can be translated. This is the deep ground of the Urdoxa's universality and of logic's universal reach.

He notes that neutralization affects the expressing too, and draws the great conclusion that all acts are objectivating. It is evident that with the transition into the neutrality-modification also the expressing itself and its expressed as such neutralizes itself. After all this it results that all acts whatever — also the emotional and will-acts — are "objectivating," originally "constituting" objects, necessary sources of various being-regions and thereby also of belonging ontologies. For example: the valuing consciousness constitutes the, over against the mere thing-world, new-kind "axiological" objectivity, a "being" of new region, insofar as precisely through the essence of the valuing consciousness in general, actual doxic theses as ideal possibilities are prefigured, which bring objectivities of a new-kind content — values — as "meant" in the valuing consciousness to setting-off. In the emotional act they are emotionally meant; they come, through actualization of the doxic content of these acts, to doxic and further to logically-explicit being-meant. This is the great conclusion of the doxic doctrine. Because every act harbors a doxic positing, every act is objectivating: every act constitutes an object. So valuing constitutes values (the axiological objectivity, a new being-region); willing constitutes practical objectivities. These are first meant emotionally/volitionally, then (by actualizing the doxic content) doxically, and finally logically-explicitly. So the emotional and volitional acts are genuine sources of objectivity, founding new being-regions (values, goods, works) and their ontologies.

He states that only the doxic cogito performs actual objectivation. Every non-doxically performed act-consciousness is in this manner potentially objectivating; the doxic cogito alone performs actual objectivation. Here lies the deepest of the sources from which the universality of the logical, finally that of the predicative judgment, is to be clarified (whereby we take in the not yet more closely treated layer of the meaning-wise expressing), and from there is also understood the last ground of the universality of the dominion of logic itself. This fixes the relation. The emotional/volitional acts are only potentially objectivating; the doxic cogito alone actually objectivates (turns the value into a posited value-object). This is the deepest source of the universality of the logical and of the predicative judgment, and the last ground of logic's universal dominion. (The "expressing" layer — the meaning-wise expressing — is the theme of §§124ff.)

He projects the formal and material disciplines of feeling and will. In further consequence is understood the possibility, indeed necessity, of formal and material noetic, or noematic and ontological disciplines essentially related to the emotional and will-intentionality. We will take up this theme later, until we have assured ourselves of some supplementary cognitions (forward-reference to the closing chapter of the Fourth Part). So there can be formal and material disciplines of the value- and will-spheres (formal axiology and practics, and their material counterparts), parallel to logic and the ontologies — the theme of the closing chapter of the Fourth Part. With this, the doctrine of the thesis, the Urdoxa, and the objectivating character of all acts is complete. §118 turns to the consciousness-syntheses (the second direction of advance, §116).

### § 118\. Consciousness-Syntheses. Syntactic Forms

Husserl turns to the second direction (§116): the synthetic forms of consciousness. If we now direct our attention into the second of the directions indicated above, onto the forms of the synthetic consciousness, then there enter into our horizon manifold formation-ways of experiences through intentional connection, which as essence-possibilities belong partly to all intentional experiences whatever, partly to the peculiarities of special genera of the same. Consciousness and consciousness binds itself not only together in general, it combines into one consciousness, whose correlate is one noema, which on its side is founded in the noemata of the combined noeses. This introduces the syntheses. Consciousness can combine into one consciousness with one noema, founded on the noemata of the combined acts. So synthesis is the combining of acts into a higher unity with a single founded noema (e.g. combining several presentings into one collecting, with the collection as its noema).

He sets aside the time-synthesis and the continuous syntheses, focusing on the articulated (polythetic) syntheses. We have here not aimed at the unity of the immanent time-consciousness, although it too, as the all-encompassing unity for all experiences of an experience-stream, and indeed as a unity of consciousness binding consciousness with consciousness, must be recalled. \[The time-synthesis, the Ursynthese of original time-consciousness, is set aside with its problematic, §81.\] We want now to speak of syntheses not in the frame of this time-consciousness, but in the frame of time itself, the concretely filled phenomenological time. On the other hand, we also do not enter into the indeed very important continuous syntheses, as such belong, for example, essentially to all space-thinghood constituting consciousness. We will later find sufficient occasion to get to know these syntheses more closely. Our interest we turn rather to the articulated syntheses, that is, to the peculiar ways how discretely set-off acts combine into an articulated unity, into that of a synthetic act of higher step-order. With a continuous synthesis we do not speak of an "act of higher order"; rather the unity (noetically, as noematically and objectively) belongs to the same order-step as the unified. This focuses the topic. Husserl sets aside (i) the time-synthesis (the Ursynthese, §81) and (ii) the continuous syntheses (which constitute spatial things, where the unity is of the same order as the unified, not a higher-order act). He focuses on the articulated (polythetic) syntheses: discrete acts combined into a higher-order synthetic act (e.g. a collecting, a relating). So "synthesis" here means the discrete, articulated, higher-order combination of acts.

He gives examples of higher-order synthetic acts in the will and feeling spheres. Examples of synthetic acts of higher step offer us in the will-sphere the relating willing "for the sake of another," likewise in the circle of feeling-acts the relating liking, the rejoicing "with regard to," or, as we likewise say, "for the sake of another." And so all similar act-occurrences at the various act-genera. Obviously also all acts of preferring belong here. So there are synthetic acts in every sphere: relating willing (willing one thing for the sake of another), relating feeling (rejoicing in one thing with regard to another), preferring (which relates one value to another). These are higher-order syntheses founded on simpler acts.

He singles out the universal group of syntheses: collecting, disjoining, explicating, relating. To closer consideration we want to subject another, in a certain way universal group of syntheses. It encompasses colligating (taking-together), disjoining (going to the "this or that"), explicating, relating syntheses, in general the whole series of syntheses that, according to the pure forms of the synthetic objectivities constituting themselves in them, determine the formal-ontological forms, and on the other hand, with respect to the construction of the noematic formations, mirror themselves in the apophantic meaning-forms of formal logic (the throughout noematically directed logic of propositions). This is the central group. The universal syntheses are: colligating (forming a collection — "A and B and C"), disjoining ("A or B"), explicating (laying out a whole into its parts), relating ("A is similar to B"). These syntheses constitute the formal-ontological forms (the categorial forms of objectivity: set, disjunction, relation) and mirror themselves in the apophantic meaning-forms of formal logic (the logical forms of propositions: conjunction, disjunction, predication). So these syntheses are the source of both formal ontology (the doctrine of categorial objects) and formal logic (the doctrine of propositional forms).

He notes this is an essentially closed group of unconditional generality. The relation to the formal ontology and logic already indicates that it is here a matter of an essentially closed group of syntheses, to which an unconditional generality of possible application accrues with respect to the kinds of experiences to be combined, which on their side therefore may also be arbitrarily complex noetic unities. So these syntheses are universal: they can combine any experiences whatever (however complex), and they are an essentially closed group (the formal-syntactic forms). This is why they ground formal logic and formal ontology (which apply to any objects whatever).

### § 119\. Transformation of Polythetic Acts into Monothetic

Husserl introduces the total-object of a synthetic consciousness. First, for all kinds of articulated syntheses, of polythetic acts, the following is to be noted: Every synthetically-unitary consciousness, however many particular theses and syntheses may be ordered into it, possesses the total-object belonging to it as synthetically-unitary consciousness. Total-object it is called over against the objects that belong intentionally to the synthetic members of lower or higher step, insofar as they all also contribute to it in the manner of founding and order themselves into it. Every peculiar self-delimiting noese, even if it is a non-self-sufficient layer, contributes its part to the constitution of the total-object; as for example the moment of valuing, which is non-self-sufficient, since it is necessarily founded in a thing-consciousness, \[constitutes\] the objective value-layer, that of the "value-character." Such new layers are also the specifically synthetic ones of the universal consciousness-syntheses just designated, that is, all the forms that specially stem from the synthetic consciousness as such, hence the connection-forms and the synthetic forms adhering to the members themselves (insofar as they are drawn into the synthesis). This introduces the total-object. A synthetic consciousness has a total-object (e.g. the collection "A and B and C"), to which all the members contribute. The synthetic forms (the connection-forms, like "and," and the forms adhering to the members, like "first member") are new layers constituting the total-object. So the collection is a new objectity, constituted by the colligating synthesis, with the members as its founding parts.

He distinguishes the polythetic (many-rayed) from the monothetic (single-rayed) consciousness. In the synthetic consciousness, we said, a synthetic total-object constitutes itself. It is, however, therein in quite other sense "objective" than the constituted of a simple thesis. The synthetic consciousness, or the pure ego "in" it, directs itself many-rayed onto the objective, the simply thetic consciousness in one ray. So the synthetic colligating is a "plural" consciousness, it takes one and one and one together. Likewise constitutes itself in a primitive relating consciousness the relation in a two-rayed \[consciousness\]. This is the key distinction. A simple (monothetic) consciousness directs itself in one ray at its object. A synthetic (polythetic) consciousness directs itself many-rayed (the colligating is "plural" — one and one and one; the relating is two-rayed — relating A to B). So the polythetic act has multiple rays (one per member), while the monothetic has one.

He states the central law: the polythetic can be transformed into a monothetic, making the synthetic unity into an object. \[The transformation:\] the many-rayed consciousness can be transformed into a single, simple, one-ray consciousness, which makes the in the former synthetically constituted "objective" in a "monothetic" act in the specific sense. So the synthetically constituted collection becomes in the distinguished sense objective; it becomes the object of a simple doxic thesis in the back-relation of a simple thesis onto the just-now originally constituted collection, hence in a peculiar noetic linking of a thesis onto the synthesis. In other words: the plural consciousness can be essentially transferred into a singular one, which draws out of it the manifold as one object, as a single thing; the manifold can now on its side be connected with other manifolds and other objects, set in relation to them, and so on. This is the law of nominalization (monothetic transformation). The many-rayed (polythetic) collecting can be transformed into a single-rayed (monothetic) act that takes the collection as one object. So "A and B and C" (colligated, many-rayed) becomes "the collection {A, B, C}" (one object, single-rayed), which can then itself be a member of further syntheses (related to other collections, etc.). This monothetic transformation is the source of the objectification of categorial forms: the synthesis, performed many-rayed, is then grasped single-rayed as one object.

He extends to disjunction and relation, and states the evidence-requirement. The situation is obviously the same for the disjoining consciousness, built quite analogously to the colligating, and its ontic, or noematic, correlates. Likewise can from the relating consciousness the synthetically-originally constituted relation be drawn out in a linked simple thesis and made into an object in the distinguished sense, and as such compared with other relations, in general used as subject of predicates. Thereby it is, however, to be brought to complete evidence that the simply objectified and the synthetically-unitary is really the same, and that the subsequent thesis, or drawing-out, imputes nothing to the synthetic consciousness, but grasps what this gives. Evident, of course, is also the essentially different givenness-mode. This extends and qualifies. The same holds for disjunction and relation: the relation, constituted two-rayed, can be objectified single-rayed (the relation "similarity" becomes an object, predicated of). And the objectified is really the same as the synthetically-constituted (the monothetic grasping does not falsify or add; it grasps what the synthesis gives), though the givenness-mode differs (single-rayed vs. many-rayed). So nominalization is faithful: it makes the synthesis into an object without distorting it.

He states the logical law of nominalization. In logic this lawfulness manifests itself in the law of "nominalization," according to which to every proposition and every partial form distinguishable in the proposition a nominal corresponds: to the proposition itself, say "S is p," the nominal that-clause; for example, at the subject-place of new propositions, to the "is p" the p-being, to the relation-form "similar" the similarity, to the plural-form the plurality, and so on. This is the law of nominalization in logic. Every proposition and every part-form can be "nominalized" (turned into a noun, a subject of further propositions): "S is p" becomes "that S is p" (the that-clause); "is p" becomes "p-being"; "similar" becomes "similarity"; the plural becomes "plurality." So logic mirrors the monothetic transformation: every syntactic form can be nominalized into an object-term. (The footnote points to the first attempts in the Logical Investigations, Fifth Investigation §§34–36 and Sixth §49.)

He states the great result: the nominalized concepts form the categorial modifications of the Idea of objectivity, the material of formal ontology. The concepts arising from the "nominalizations," thought as determined exclusively through the pure forms, form formal-categorial modifications of the Idea of objectivity in general and deliver the fundamental concept-material of formal ontology and, enclosed therein, all formal-mathematical disciplines. This proposition is, for the understanding of the relation of formal logic as logic of the apophansis and the universal formal ontology, of decisive significance. This is the culminating result. The nominalized forms (collection, plurality, relation, that-being) are the formal-categorial modifications of the Idea of "object in general" — the fundamental concepts of formal ontology (and of all formal mathematics). So formal ontology (the doctrine of the categorial forms of objectivity) arises from the nominalization of the synthetic forms. And this clarifies the relation between formal logic (the logic of the apophansis, of propositions) and formal ontology (of objects): the two are correlative, the propositional forms (logic) and the nominalized object-forms (ontology) springing from the same syntheses. (This is the central thesis of Husserl's Formal and Transcendental Logic: formal logic and formal ontology are two sides of one formal mathesis.)

### § 120\. Positionality and Neutrality in the Sphere of the Syntheses

Husserl applies the positional/neutral distinction to the syntheses. All proper syntheses, and such we had constantly in view, build themselves on simple theses, the word understood in that general sense that we fixed above, which encompasses all "intentions," all "act-characters"; and they themselves are theses, theses of higher step. All our establishments about actuality and inactuality, about neutrality and positionality transfer themselves accordingly, as needs no exposition, to the syntheses. So the syntheses are themselves theses (of higher step), built on simple theses. So the positional/neutral and actual/potential distinctions apply to them too (a colligating can be positional or neutral, actual or potential).

He flags the question of how the founding and founded theses relate in positionality. A closer investigation would here, on the other hand, be necessary, to establish in which various ways the positionality and neutrality of the founding theses relates to that of the founded theses. So the relation between the positionality of the founding acts and that of the founded synthesis is a question (taken up below).

He notes that a positional higher thesis does not require positional lower theses, with examples. Generally, and not only for the special founded acts that we call syntheses, it is clear that one cannot without further ado say a positional thesis of higher step presupposes nothing but positional theses in the lower steps. So an actual essence-intuition is a positional and not a neutralized act, which is founded in some exemplarily intuiting consciousness, which on its side may very well be a neutral, a phantasy-consciousness. Similar holds of an aesthetic liking with respect to the appearing liking-object, of a positional image-consciousness with respect to the depicting "image." This is an important point. A positional higher act can rest on a neutral lower act. Examples: an actual essence-intuition (positional — it really posits the essence) can be founded on a phantasy (neutral — the example is merely imagined, §70); an aesthetic liking (positional — I really like it) rests on a neutral image-consciousness (the depicted is neutralized). So positionality of the higher does not require positionality of the lower; a real positing can be founded on a neutral (merely-imagined) act. (This is why eidetic intuition on phantasy examples yields real, positional essence-knowledge, §70.)

He states the contrary rule for the proper (logical) syntheses: they depend on the positionality of the founding theses. If we now consider the group of syntheses interesting us, then we recognize at once that in it every synthesis is, according to its positional character, dependent on that of the founding noeses; more precisely, that it is positional (and can only be so) when all the sub-theses are positional, and neutral when they are not. This is the contrary rule for the logical syntheses. The colligating, disjoining, relating syntheses are positional only if all their sub-theses are positional, and neutral if not. So (unlike essence-intuition or aesthetic liking) these syntheses inherit their positionality from their members: a real colligating requires all members really posited; if any member is neutralized, the whole colligating is neutralized. So the logical syntheses depend on the positionality of their founding acts.

He illustrates with colligating and notes the role of assumptions. A colligating, for example, is either really colligating or colligating in the mode of the "quasi," it is really or neutralized thetic. In the one case all the acts related to the single collection-members are actual theses, in the other case not. Likewise it stands with all the other syntheses of the class mirroring itself in the logical syntaxes. Pure neutrality can never function for positional syntheses; it must at least experience the transformation into "assumptions," say into hypothetical antecedents or consequents, into hypothetically posited nominals, as for example "the pseudo-Dionysius," and what more of the like. This illustrates and qualifies. A real colligating requires all members really posited; a neutralized colligating has merely-quasi members. For a positional synthesis to incorporate a non-posited member, that member must become an assumption (a hypothetical positing, §110), not a pure neutrality: e.g. a hypothetical antecedent ("if S is p"), or a hypothetically posited nominal ("the pseudo-Dionysius" — a name posited hypothetically, not asserted to refer). So the logical syntheses work with positings and assumptions, not pure neutralities. This distinguishes the logical syntheses (which require positing or assuming) from the founded acts like essence-intuition (which can rest on pure neutrality). §121 takes up how these syntheses come to express themselves in the syntactic forms of propositions, and the doxic syntaxes in the emotional and will-sphere.

### § 121\. The Doxic Syntaxes in the Emotional and Will-Sphere

Husserl asks why the syntheses express themselves in propositional forms, and gives the obvious first answer. If we now ask how the syntheses of this group come to express themselves in the syntactic forms of the assertion-propositions that the logical form-doctrine of propositions systematically develops, then the answer lies at hand. They are precisely, one will say, doxic syntheses, or, as we, recalling the logical-grammatical syntaxes in which they imprint themselves, also say: doxic syntaxes. To the specific essence of the doxic acts belong these syntaxes of the "and," or the plural-forms, the syntaxes of the "or," of the relating positing of a predicate on the under-ground of a subject-positing, and so on. That "belief" and "judgment" in the logical sense belong closely together (if one does not want outright to identify them), that belief-syntheses find their "expression" in the forms of the assertion-propositions, that no one will doubt. So the first answer: the syntheses (and, or, predication) are doxic syntaxes, belonging to the essence of the doxic acts, which express themselves in the logical propositional forms. But Husserl will show this is not the whole truth.

He states the basic fact: the syntheses belong also to the non-doxic theses (the axiological and practical "and"). So correct as this also is, it is yet to be seen that the designated conception does not comprise the whole truth in itself. These syntheses of the "and," of the "or," of the "if" or "because" and "so," in short, the syntheses giving themselves at first as doxic, are not at all merely doxic. It is a fundamental fact that such syntheses also belong to the own essence of the non-doxic theses, and indeed in the following sense. There are undoubtedly such things as collective joy, collective liking, collective willing, and so on. Or, as I am accustomed to express it, there is, beside the doxic "and" (the logical), also an axiological and practical "and." The same holds of the "or" and all syntheses belonging here. This is the key claim. The syntactic forms (and, or, if-then) are not exclusively doxic. There is also an axiological "and" (collective liking) and a practical "and" (collective willing). So the syntheses belong to the emotional and volitional acts too, in their own right. (The footnote notes Husserl struck on this fact over a decade ago, in trying to realize the idea of a formal axiology and practics as analogue of formal logic.)

He gives the mother-and-children example: a genuinely plural loving. For example: the mother who lovingly looks upon her flock of children embraces in one act of love each child singly and all together. The unity of a collective act of love is not a love and a collective presenting added to it, even if ordered to it as necessary underlayer of the love. Rather the loving itself is collective, it is just as many-rayed as the presenting "underlying" it and possibly plural judging. We may outright speak of a plural loving, exactly in the sense as of a plural presenting or judging. The syntactic forms enter into the essence of the emotional acts themselves, namely into the thetic layer specifically peculiar to them. This is the decisive example. The mother's love is itself collective (plural, many-rayed): she loves each child and all together in one act of love. This is not a single love plus a collective presenting; the loving itself is plural. So the syntactic forms (the colligating) enter the essence of the emotional act itself, into its own thetic layer. So there is a genuinely axiological synthesis (a plural loving), not just a doxic synthesis underlying a simple feeling.

He relates this to the essence-kinship of doxic and non-doxic theses. Now we recall the above-investigated essence-kinship of doxic theses and theses in general. In every thesis whatever lies, according to what it, for example as this love-intention, noematically performs, a parallel doxic one hidden. Obviously the parallelism between the syntaxes belonging to the sphere of the doxic thesis and those belonging to all other theses (the parallelism of the doxic "and," "or," and so on, with the value-wise and will-wise) is a special case of the same essence-kinship. For the synthetic emotional acts — namely synthetic with respect to the syntactic forms here discussed — constitute synthetic emotional objectivities, which through corresponding doxic acts come to explicated objectivation. This connects to §117. Every thesis harbors a parallel doxic thesis (§117); so every axiological synthesis (the plural loving) harbors a parallel doxic synthesis (the plural judging). The synthetic emotional acts constitute synthetic emotional objectivities (the loved flock as a love-collective), which are objectivated by corresponding doxic acts.

He gives the love-collective and its noematic love-characters. The loved flock of children as love-object is a collective; that says, in correlative turn of the above-carried-out, not only a thingly collective and a love added to it, but a love-collective. As in noetic respect a love-ray going out from the ego divides into a bundle of rays, each of which goes onto a single object, so there distribute themselves onto the love-collective as such as many noematic love-characters as objects are in each case colligated, and there are just as many positional characters, which combine synthetically into the noematic unity of one positional character. This describes the love-collective noematically. The love-collective (the loved flock) bears, on the noematic side, as many love-characters as there are children, which combine into one positional character. So the emotional synthesis has its own noematic structure, paralleling the doxic.

He states the parallel-forms thesis and the noematic transfer. We see that all these syntactic forms are parallel-forms, that is, that they belong both to the emotional acts themselves with their specific emotional components and emotional syntheses, and to the doxic positionalities parallel to them and essence-united with them, which are to be drawn out of them through suitable glance-turnings onto the respective lower-steps and upper-steps. Naturally this transfers from the noetic to the noematic sphere. The axiological "and" harbors essentially a doxic in itself, every axiological syntactic form of the group here considered a logical: quite as every simple noematic correlate includes a "being" or another being-modality and as its substrate the form of the "something" and the otherwise belonging forms in itself. This is the parallel-forms thesis. The syntactic forms are parallel-forms: they belong both to the emotional acts (the axiological "and") and to the parallel doxic positionalities (the logical "and"), drawable from them. So the axiological "and" harbors a doxic "and"; every axiological syntactic form harbors a logical one — just as every noematic correlate harbors a "being" and the form "something." So the logical (doxic) syntax underlies every emotional syntax, drawable by a glance-turning.

He notes living emotionally vs. doxically turning, and the role for the origin of ethical/aesthetic concepts. At any time it is the matter of special, essence-possible glance-turnings and co-enclosed thetic or synthetic-doxic procedures, to form, out of an emotional act in which we so to speak only emotionally live, hence without actualizing the doxic potentialities, a new act in which the for-now only potential emotional objectivity transforms itself into an actual, doxically and possibly explicitly explicated one. It is thereby possible and in empirical life very usual that we, for example, look upon several intuitive objects, doxically positing them; that we thereby at the same time perform a synthetic emotional act; say a unity of collective liking, or that of a selecting emotional act, a preferring liking, a setting-back disliking; while we do not at all pass over to turning the whole phenomenon doxically. We do it, however, when we make an assertion, for example about our liking of the plurality or of one out of the plurality, about the preferability of the one over the others, and the like. This describes the two attitudes. We can live emotionally (in the collective liking) without actualizing the doxic potentiality. We actualize it (turn doxic) when we make an assertion about the liking. Husserl notes the importance of these analyses for the essence of axiological and practical objectivities and for the problems of the "origin" of ethical, aesthetic, and kindred concepts (the genetic grounding of ethics and aesthetics). And he reminds that the task here is to mark the problems, not solve them.

### § 122\. Modes of Performance of the Articulated Syntheses. "Theme"

Husserl introduces the modes of performance, beginning with the originary production and the ego's spontaneity. To the realm of the theses and syntheses belongs an important group of general modifications, whose briefly-indicating discussion we best attach here at once. A synthesis can be performed step by step, it becomes, it arises in originary production. This originarity of the becoming in the consciousness-stream is a quite peculiar one. The thesis and synthesis becomes, in that the pure ego does the step and every new step actually; it itself lives in the step and "steps forth" with it. The positing, positing-thereon, fore- and after-positing, and so on, is its free spontaneity and activity; it lives in the theses not as passive being-therein, but they are out-radiations from it as a primal-source of productions. Every thesis begins with an onset-point, with a punctual origin-positing; so the first thesis, as every further in the connection of the synthesis. This "onset" belongs precisely to the thesis as such, as a remarkable mode of originary actuality. It is something like the fiat, like the onset-point of the willing and acting. This introduces the originary production. A synthesis is performed step by step, in originary production, by the ego's free spontaneity. Each thesis begins with an onset-point (a punctual origin-positing), like a fiat (the "let it be" of willing). So the ego actively produces each thesis from itself, as out-radiations from its primal source.

He distinguishes the general onset-mode from the special will-act. But one may not confuse general and special. The spontaneous self-deciding, the willful, executing doing is one act beside other acts; its syntheses are special syntheses among others. But every act, of whatever kind, can set in, in this spontaneity-mode of the so-to-speak creative beginning, in which the pure ego has its entrance as subject of the spontaneity. This distinguishes. The will-act (deciding, doing) is one special act. But the onset-mode (the creative beginning, the ego's spontaneous entrance) is general — every act can begin in this spontaneous mode. So the fiat-like onset is not special to willing; it is the general mode of the ego's spontaneous production of any act.

He describes the transition from onset to "having in grip." This mode of onset passes over at once and by an essence-necessity into another mode. For example, the perceiving grasping, seizing changes at once and without break into the "having in grip." So after the onset (the grasping), the act passes into "having in grip" (Im-Griff-haben): the object, once grasped, is held in grip. So there is an onset-mode (the active grasping) and a having-in-grip mode (the holding of what is grasped).

He describes the "still holding in grip" across a synthesis (the theme). A again new modal change attaches when the thesis was mere step for a synthesis, when the pure ego performs a new step, and when it now, in the continuous unity of the synthetic consciousness, "still" "keeps" "in grip" what it just had in grip: grasping the new thematic object, or rather grasping a new member of the total-theme as primary theme, but still holding the previously grasped member, as belonging to the same total-theme. For example colligating I do not let go what I just perceivingly grasped, in that I turn the grasping glance to the new object. Performing a proof, I run through the premise-thoughts in steps; no synthetic step do I give up, what I have won I do not lose out of grip, but the actuality-mode has changed essentially with the performance of the new thematic Uractuality. This describes the theme-structure. In a synthesis (colligating, proving), the ego grasps a new member (the primary theme) while still holding the previous members in grip (as belonging to the same total-theme). So there is a primary theme (the currently grasped) and the still-held members (the co-theme). This is the structure of thematic consciousness: the moving focus (primary theme) against the retained held members (the total-theme). (This is the basis of Husserl's later analyses of theme and thematic field, e.g. in Experience and Judgment.)

### § 123\. Confusion and Distinctness as Modes of Synthetic Acts

Husserl introduces the distinction of confused and distinct performance, with the example of a faintly-recalled proof. \[The section describes how a synthetic formation can be given confusedly — as a unitary but unarticulated whole — or distinctly, step by step.\] A confused (verworren) re-memory, say of a proof, comes up at first as a unitary but unarticulated background. Then an ego-glance directs itself single-rayed onto it, grasping the relevant noematic objectivity in an unarticulated grip. Now a new process can set in, the confused re-memory passes over into a distinct and clear one: step by step we remember the proof-course, we produce the proof-theses and syntheses "again," we run through the stages of yesterday's conversation "again," and the like. This introduces the confusion/distinctness distinction. A synthetic formation (a proof, a conversation) can be given confusedly (as an unarticulated whole, grasped single-rayed) or distinctly (articulated step by step, the syntheses produced "again"). So one can have a proof "confusedly" (as a vague unitary grasp) and then make it distinct (re-producing each step).

He notes the reproduction is inessential; the confused-to-distinct passage is general. Naturally such reproduction in the manner of re-memory, of re-production of the "earlier" originary productions, is something extra-essential. We have, say, a new theoretical idea for the carrying-out of a complicated theory first confusedly-unitary, then in freely-actively performed steps brought to unfolding and transformed into synthetic actualities. All that indicated is self-evidently to be related in the same way to all act-kinds. So the confused-to-distinct passage is not tied to memory: a new idea too can be had first confusedly (as a unitary hunch) and then made distinct (unfolded into articulated steps). This holds for all act-kinds. So confusion and distinctness are general modes: a synthetic formation can be grasped confusedly (unarticulated) or distinctly (articulated).

He states the importance of the confusion/distinctness distinction for the phenomenology of expressions, with the reading example. This important difference between confusion and distinctness plays in the phenomenology of the "expressions" to be discussed further on, explicit presentations, judgments, emotional acts, and so on, an important role. One need only think of the manner how we are accustomed to grasp the always already very complex synthetic formations that make up the "thought-content" of our respective reading, and consider what, in the understanding of the read, comes to really originary actualization with respect to this so-called thoughtful underlayer of the expressions. This is the bridge to §124. The confusion/distinctness distinction matters for the phenomenology of expressions: when reading, we grasp the complex thought-content largely confusedly (not re-producing each thought distinctly). So most of our understanding of expressions operates with confused (non-distinct) graspings, only some of which we actualize distinctly. (This is the basis of Husserl's distinction between distinct and confused judging, central to Formal and Transcendental Logic.)

### § 124\. The Noetic-Noematic Layer of the "Logos." Meaning (Bedeuten) and Signification (Bedeutung)

Husserl introduces the expressing layer (the "logical" acts) and the noesis-noema parallelism in it. With all so-far considered acts interweave the expressing, the in the specific sense "logical" act-layers, at which no less the parallelism of noesis and noema is to be made evident. The general and unavoidable ambiguity of the manners of speaking, which is conditioned by this parallelism and shows itself effective everywhere where the relevant relations come to language, does so naturally also in the talk of expression and signification (Bedeutung). The ambiguity is only dangerous so long as one does not recognize it as such, or has not separated the parallel structures. If that has happened, then we have only to take care that it is in each case beyond doubt to which of the structures the talk is to be related. This introduces the expressing (logical) layer. The expressing acts (the layer of language/meaning) interweave with all other acts, and have their own noesis-noema parallelism. The term "signification" (Bedeutung) is ambiguous (noetic meaning-act vs. noematic meaning-content), but the ambiguity is harmless once the parallel structures are separated.

He sets aside the sensuous side of the expression, focusing on meaning. We link up to the known distinction between the sensuous, so-to-speak bodily side of the expression and its non-sensuous, "spiritual" side. On the closer discussion of the former we need not enter; likewise not on the manner of the unification of both sides. Self-evidently these too are titles for not unimportant phenomenological problems. We look exclusively onto "meaning" (Bedeuten) and "signification" (Bedeutung). So Husserl sets aside the sensuous side of the expression (the word-sound) and the problem of how the two sides unite, focusing on the meaning (the spiritual side). (The expressing layer \= the meaning-act and its meaning-content, abstracting from the physical word.)

He extends "signification" from the linguistic sphere to the whole noetic-noematic sphere, distinguishing Sinn from Bedeutung. Originally these words have only relation to the linguistic sphere, to that of "expressing." It is, however, nearly unavoidable and at the same time an important cognition-step, to extend the signification of these words and suitably to modify it, whereby it finds in a certain way application to the whole noetic-noematic sphere: hence to all acts, whether these are now interwoven with expressing acts or not. So we too have constantly spoken of "sense" (Sinn) — a word that is, however, in general used equivalently with "signification" — at all intentional experiences. For distinctness' sake we want to prefer the word signification (Bedeutung) for the old concept, and in particular in the complex talk "logical" or "expressing" signification. The word sense (Sinn) we use as before in the more comprehensive width. This is the crucial terminological decision. Husserl reserves Bedeutung (signification) for the narrower, linguistic-expressing sphere (the "logical" or "expressing" signification), and Sinn (sense) for the wider sphere (the noematic core of any act, expressed or not). So every act has a Sinn (noematic core); only the expressed acts have a Bedeutung (linguistic meaning). Bedeutung is the expression of Sinn. (This is the standard Husserlian distinction: Sinn is the general noematic sense; Bedeutung is the specifically linguistic/conceptual meaning.)

He gives the example: explication of a perception ("this is white") needs no expression. Let there stand, to link up to an example, in the perception an object there, with a determinate sense, monothetically posited in the determinate fullness. We perform, as such normally tends to attach to the first, simple perception-grasping without further ado, an explicating of the given and a relating in-one-positing of the lifted-out parts or moments: say according to the schema "this is white." This process requires not the least of "expression," neither of expression in the sense of word-sound, nor of such as word-meaning, which latter can here indeed also be present independently of the word-sound (as when this is "forgotten"). This is the example. The explication of a perception ("this is white") can be performed without any expression (no word-sound, not even word-meaning). So there is a pre-expressive explicating: the perception is articulated ("this — white") before and independently of any language. So the expressing layer is a further, added layer, not necessary for the underlying explication.

He states the result: every "meant as such" is expressible by significations, and "logical signification is an expression." But if we have "thought" or asserted: "this is white," then a new layer is there, united with the purely perceptually "meant as such." In this manner also everything remembered, phantasied as such is explicable and expressible. Every "meant as such," every meaning (Meinung) in the noematic sense (and indeed as noematic core) of an arbitrary act is expressible through "significations." Generally we therefore set up: Logical signification is an expression. This is the key result. When we "think" or assert "this is white," a new layer (the expressing) is added to the perceptual "meant as such." Every noematic core (every Sinn) is expressible through significations (Bedeutungen). So: logical signification is an expression — the Bedeutung is precisely the expressing of the Sinn. So the expressing layer takes any noematic core and expresses it conceptually.

He describes the expressing layer as a medium that mirrors all intentionality conceptually. The word-sound can be called expression only because the signification belonging to it expresses; in it lies the expressing originally. "Expression" is a remarkable form that lets itself adapt to all "sense" (the noematic "core") and raises it into the realm of the "Logos," of the conceptual and thereby of the "general." Thereby the last words are understood in a quite determinate signification, which is to be separated from other significations of these words. In general, with what was just indicated, a great theme of phenomenological analyses is designated, which are fundamental for the essence-clarification of logical thinking and its correlates. In noetic respect, under the title "expressing" a special act-layer is to be designated, to which all the other acts are to adapt peculiarly and with which they are remarkably to fuse, precisely so that every noematic act-sense, and consequently the relation to objectivity lying in it, imprints itself "conceptually" in the noematic of the expressing. A peculiar intentional medium is present, which by its essence has the distinction of mirroring every other intentionality according to form and content, of depicting it in its own coloring and thereby imprinting on it its own form of "conceptuality." This describes the expressing layer. The expressing is a special act-layer that adapts to any sense (any noematic core) and raises it into the realm of the Logos (the conceptual, the general). It is a medium that mirrors every intentionality (form and content), depicting it conceptually and imprinting on it the form of conceptuality. So the expressing layer takes any act's sense and renders it conceptually, in general (universal) form. (This is the constitution of the conceptual/linguistic from the pre-conceptual sense.)

He cautions about the mirror-metaphor and flags the difficult problems. Yet these self-imposing talks of mirroring or depicting are to be taken with caution, since the figurativeness mediating their application could easily mislead. Extraordinarily difficult problems attach to the phenomena belonging to the titles meaning and signification (footnote: as one sees from the second volume of the Logical Investigations, in which they form a main theme). This cautions and flags the difficulty. The mirror/depicting metaphor for the expressing layer must be used carefully (the expressing does not literally "copy" the sense). And the phenomena of meaning and signification harbor extraordinarily difficult problems (the main theme of the Logical Investigations, vol. II). So §124 opens the analysis of the expressing/logical layer (the constitution of conceptual meaning from sense), to be continued in §§125–127. With this, the relation of Sinn (the universal noematic core) to Bedeutung (the expressing, conceptual signification) is fixed, and the "logical" layer is located as a founded, universal medium expressing all sense conceptually.

He locates the problems and notes the two ways into phenomenology.
