Since every science, according to its theoretical content, according to all that is "doctrine" in it (theorem, proof, theory), objectivates itself in the specifically "logical" medium, in that of the expression, the problems of expression and signification are for the philosopher and psychologist guided by general logical interests the nearest, and they are then also the first that, as soon as one seriously seeks to get to their ground, press toward phenomenological essence-researches. (A footnote notes that this was the way on which the Logical Investigations strove to penetrate into phenomenology; a second way, from the side of experience and the sensuous givennesses, pursued since the early 1890s, did not come to full expression in that work.) From there one is led to the questions how the "expressing" of "expressed" is to be understood, how explicit experiences stand to non-explicit, and what the latter experience in the supervening expressing: one will find oneself referred to their "intentionality," to the sense "immanent" to them, to "matter" and quality (act-character of the thesis); to the distinction of this sense and these essence-moments lying in the pre-expressive from the signification of the expressing phenomenon itself and its own moments. This locates the problems. The logical-expressive layer is where every science objectivates its theoretical content (theorems, proofs), so it is the nearest theme for the logically-minded. The questions: how expressing relates to the expressed, how explicit (expressed) experiences relate to non-explicit (pre-expressive) ones, and what the pre-expressive sense (the "matter" and quality, the noematic core and thetic character) becomes in the expressing. So one must distinguish the pre-expressive sense from the signification of the expression itself.

He states the key thesis: the expression-layer is not productive. The layer of the expression is — this makes up its peculiarity — apart from the fact that it lends expression to all other intentionalia, not productive. Or if one will: its productivity, its noematic performance, exhausts itself in the expressing and the with this newly-incoming form of the conceptual. This is the key thesis about expression. The expressing layer is not productive: it adds nothing to the underlying sense except the form of conceptuality (the expression). So the expression does not create new content; it only raises the existing sense into conceptual, general form. (The sense is given pre-expressively; the expression only renders it conceptually.)

He states that the expressing layer is essence-united with the expressed in thetic character, with only one thesis. Thereby the expressing layer is, according to the thetic character, perfectly essence-united with the expression-experiencing one, and it takes up in the coincidence so much its essence into itself that we call the explicit presenting itself presenting, the explicit believing, conjecturing, doubting itself and as a whole believing, conjecturing, doubting; likewise the explicit wishing or willing precisely wishing, willing. That also the difference between positionality and neutrality goes over into the explicit, is evident, and we have already mentioned it above. The expressing layer cannot have an otherwise-qualified positional or neutral thesis than the expression-experiencing one, and in the coincidence we find not two theses to be distinguished, but only one thesis. This is the unity-thesis. The expression shares the thetic character of the expressed (an explicit believing is itself a believing; the expression of a positing is positing, of a neutral is neutral). In the coincidence (Deckung) of expression and expressed, there is only one thesis (not two). So the expression does not add a second positing; it carries the same thesis as the expressed.

He notes the difficulty of the layering-image and the kinds of generality. The full clarification of the structures belonging here makes considerable difficulties. Already the recognition that really, after abstraction from the sensuous word-sound layer, there is still a layering of the kind we here presupposed — hence in every case (even in that of a so unclear, empty, merely verbal thinking) a layer of expressing meaning and an under-layer of expressed — is not easy, and even less the understanding of the essence-connections of these layerings. For the image of a layering must not be expected to bear too much: the expression is not something like an overlaid lacquer, or a garment drawn over; it is a spiritual forming, which exercises at the intentional under-layer new intentional functions and correlatively experiences from it intentional functions. Especially important is the understanding of the various kinds of "generality" that occur there: on the one hand that which belongs to every expression and expression-moment, even to the non-self-sufficient "is," "not," "and," "if," and so on; on the other hand the generality of the "general names" like "human," over against the proper names like "Bruno"; again that which belongs to a syntactically form-less essence in itself, in comparison with the just-touched and various generalities of the signification. This notes the difficulties. The layering-image is imperfect: the expression is not a lacquer or garment over the sense, but a spiritual forming that exercises new intentional functions on the under-layer. And there are various kinds of generality to distinguish: the generality of every expression-form (even "is," "and"); the generality of general names ("human" vs. "Bruno"); and the generality of a form-less essence. So §124 closes by flagging the hard problems of the expression-layer and the kinds of generality, which §§125–127 take further.

### § 125\. The Modes of Performance in the Logical-Expressing Sphere and the Method of Clarification

Husserl applies the actuality-modes (§122) to the expressing layer, in a double way. Obviously, for the clarification of the indicated difficulties, special regard is to be taken to the above-treated differences of the actuality-modes: the modalities of the act-performance, which concern, like all theses and syntheses, also the expressing ones. This, however, in a double way. On the one hand they concern the meaning-layer, the specifically logical one itself; on the other hand the founding under-layers. So the performance-modes (distinct/confused, §122–123) apply to the expressing layer in two ways: to the meaning-layer (the logical expression) itself, and to the founding under-layers (the expressed sense).

He describes distinct logical understanding and its lapse into confusion. In a reading we can perform every signification articulated and freely-actively, can thereby synthetically connect significations with significations in the prefigured way. We win, in this performance of the signification-acts in the mode of proper production, perfect distinctness of the logical understanding. This distinctness can pass over into confusions of all the above-described modes: the just-read proposition sinks into the dark, loses its living articulation, it ceases to be "still in grip" as our "theme." This describes the logical distinctness/confusion. Reading, we can perform the significations distinctly (articulated, freely produced), winning perfect distinct logical understanding. This can lapse into confusion (the proposition sinks into the dark, loses articulation). So the meaning-layer itself has distinct and confused modes.

He distinguishes the distinctness of the meaning-layer from that of the under-layer. Such distinctness and confusion is, however, to be distinguished from that which concerns the expressed under-layers. A distinct word- and sentence-understanding (or a distinct, articulated performance of the acts of asserting) is compatible with the confusion of the underlayers. This confusion says not merely unclarity, although it also says that. The under-layer can be a confused unitary (and is so mostly), which does not bear its articulation in itself actually, but owes it to the mere adaptation of the really articulated and in originary actuality performed layer of the logical expression. This is a crucial distinction. The distinctness of the meaning-layer (the words/sentences) is independent of the distinctness of the under-layer (the expressed sense). One can have a distinct sentence-understanding while the under-layer is confused (an unarticulated unity that owes its apparent articulation only to the expression adapting to it). So one can "understand" a sentence distinctly (verbally) while the underlying sense remains confused (not really intuited or articulated). This is the phenomenon of merely-verbal understanding.

He states the methodological consequence: the method of clarification needs supplementing for the assertion. This has a highly important methodological significance. We become aware that our earlier discussions about the method of clarification (back-reference to §67), with respect to the assertion, which is the life-element of science, need essential supplements. What is needed, to come from confused thinking to proper and fully-explicit cognizing, to the distinct and at the same time clear performance of the thought-acts, is now easily designated: First, all "logical" acts (those of meaning), so far as they were still performed in the mode of confusion, are to be transferred into the mode of originary spontaneous actuality, hence perfect logical distinctness is to be produced. Now, however, the analogue is also to be performed in the founding under-layer, all the non-living into living, all confusion into distinctness, but also all non-intuitiveness into intuitiveness. This supplements the method of clarification (§67). For scientific cognition (the assertion), one must produce both: (i) logical distinctness (the meaning-acts performed distinctly, in originary actuality) and (ii) the clarification of the under-layer (all confusion into distinctness, all non-intuitiveness into intuitiveness). So full cognition requires distinct meaning plus clear, intuitive, distinct under-layer. (This is the basis of the distinction between distinct evidence and clear/intuitive evidence in Formal and Transcendental Logic.)

He notes that only then does the clarification-method (§67) act, and that intuition transfers to synthetic acts. Only when we perform this work in the under-layer does — if not incompatibilities becoming visible in it make further work superfluous — the earlier-described method come into action; whereby it is to be reckoned that the concept of intuition, of the clear consciousness, transfers from the monothetic to the synthetic acts. This is an important point. The clarification (§67) acts only after the under-layer is made distinct, and only if no incompatibilities show up (if the thought is contradictory, clarification is superfluous — the contradiction shows it cannot be intuitively fulfilled). And the concept of intuition extends from simple (monothetic) to synthetic acts: a synthetic formation (a state-of-affairs, a relation) can be intuitively given (categorial intuition, from the Sixth Logical Investigation).

He distinguishes the kinds of evidence: pure-logical insight needs no clarity of the under-layer. For the rest it depends, as a deeper analysis shows, on the kind of evidence that is in each case to be won, or on the layer to which it is turned. All evidences relating to pure-logical relations, to essence-connections of noematic significations — hence those that we win from the fundamental laws of formal logic — require precisely the givenness of the significations, namely the givenness of the expressing propositions of the forms that the relevant signification-law prescribes. The non-self-sufficiency of the significations brings it with itself that the exemplification of the logical essence-formations mediating the evidence of the law also carries under-layers with itself, namely those that experience logical expression; but these under-layers need not be brought to clarity when it is a matter of a pure-logical insight. In corresponding modification this holds for all applied-logical "analytic" cognitions. This is a crucial result about evidence. For pure-logical insight (the laws of formal logic), one needs only the givenness of the significations (the propositions in their logical form) — the under-layer (the expressed sense) need not be clarified. So the law "if S is p and p is q, then S is q" can be evidently grasped from the propositional forms alone, without intuiting the matters S, p, q. So pure-logical (and analytic) evidence operates at the level of significations (distinctness), not requiring clarity of the under-layer. This distinguishes analytic evidence (distinctness of significations) from material evidence (clarity of the under-layer).

### § 126\. Completeness and Generality of the Expression

Husserl states the two things to note about the expression-relation: completeness and generality. (The section treats how far an expression expresses its under-layer.) Two things are to be noted. First the completeness of the expression: an expression can express its under-layer more or less completely, can leave moments of the under-layer unexpressed or express them all. So an expression may be more or less complete with respect to the sense it expresses (it may express only part of the sense, or all of it). Second the generality: the expression, by its essence, raises the sense into the conceptual, the general — but the generality of the expression and that of the expressed sense need not coincide. So the expression has its own generality, which may differ from the generality of the under-layer. (E.g. a general name "human" expresses a general sense; but one can also express an individual sense with general means.) These two features — completeness and generality — govern the expression-relation. \[The detailed treatment of completeness and generality, and how the expressing adapts to the under-layer, is developed in the section; the key results are that the expression need not express everything in the under-layer, and that the conceptual generality of the meaning is a distinct dimension from the generality of the expressed objectivity.\]

He notes the conceptual generality belongs to every expression. The conceptual generality belongs to every expression as such: even the expression of an individual ("this," "Socrates") raises it into the conceptual medium, gives it the form of a meaning. So all expression is conceptual (general in form), even when it expresses an individual. This is the "generality of the Logos": the expression imprints the form of conceptuality on whatever sense it expresses. So §126 establishes that the expressing layer (i) may be more or less complete and (ii) always imposes the form of conceptual generality, while the generality of the meaning is distinct from that of the expressed objectivity.

### § 127\. The Expression of the Judgments and the Expression of the Emotional Noemata

Husserl poses the unsolved problem: how the expression of judging relates to the expression of other acts. (The section takes up a problem that, in lack of the required phenomenological insights, has remained without solution:) the problem how the asserting, as expression of the judging, relates to the expressions of other acts. We have explicit predications, in which a "so it is\!" comes to expression. We have explicit conjectures, questions, doubts, explicit wishes, commands, and so on. Linguistically there offer themselves here partly peculiarly-built, but ambiguously to be interpreted, sentence-forms: to the assertion-propositions attach question-propositions, conjecture-propositions, wish-, command-propositions, and so on. The original dispute related to whether it is here a matter, apart from the grammatical word-sound and its historical forms, of coordinate signification-kinds, or whether not all these propositions are, according to their signification, in truth assertion-propositions. In the latter case all the belonging act-formations, for example such of the emotional sphere, which are in themselves no judgment-acts, could come to "expression" only on the detour over a judging founded in them. This poses the problem. There are explicit assertions ("so it is"), but also explicit questions, wishes, commands, with their own sentence-forms (question-propositions, wish-propositions). The dispute: are these coordinate kinds of signification (the wish-proposition a distinct kind), or are they all really assertion-propositions (so that a wish comes to expression only via a judgment founded in it)?

He criticizes the act-only framing and lists what is needed. But the whole relation of the problem to the acts, the noeses, is insufficient, and the constant overlooking of the noemata, onto which precisely at such signification-reflections the glance is directed, hindering to the understanding of the matters. In general it requires, to be able here only to penetrate to the correct problem-posings, the regard to the various structures we have shown: the general cognition of the noetic and noematic correlation as one going through all intentionalia, through all thetic and synthetic layers; likewise the separation of the logical signification-layer from the under-layer to be expressed through it; further the insight into the here, as otherwise in the intentional sphere, essence-possible reflection-directions and directions of modifications; especially, however, it requires the insight into the kinds how every consciousness is to be transferred into a judgment-consciousness, how out of every consciousness states-of-affairs of noetic and noematic kind are to be drawn. This criticizes and lists. The problem must not be posed only in terms of acts (noeses); the noemata must be considered. And it requires all the structures shown: the noetic-noematic correlation, the separation of the signification-layer from the under-layer, the reflection-directions, and especially the insight into how every consciousness can be turned into a judgment-consciousness (the universal doxic potentiality, §117).

He states the radical problem: is the expressing medium specifically doxic? The radical problem to which we are finally led back is, as emerges from the connection of the whole last series of problem-analyses, to be grasped thus: Is the medium of the expressing meaning, this peculiar medium of the Logos, a specifically doxic one? Does it not coincide, in the adaptation of the meaning to the meant, with the doxic lying in all positionality itself? This is the radical problem. Is the expressing meaning (the Logos-medium) specifically doxic? Does the expression coincide with the doxic positing that lies in every act (§117)? Husserl's answer (developed below) is yes: the expressing medium is doxic, and it coincides with the doxic enclosed in every positionality.

He develops the answer: the expression is doxic, with direct and indirect expression. Naturally that would not exclude that there are several ways of the expression, say of emotional experiences. One single one would be the direct, namely simple expression of the experience (or, for the correlative sense of the talk of expression: of its noema) through immediate adaptation of an articulated expression to the articulated emotional experience, whereby doxic coincides with doxic. The doxic form dwelling in the emotional experience according to all components would thus be it which makes possible the adaptability of the expression, as an exclusively doxothetic experience, to the emotional experience, which as such and according to all its members is manifoldly thetic, among which, however, necessarily also doxothetic. This is the resolution. The expression is doxic (doxothetic). It adapts to an emotional experience because the emotional experience harbors a doxic form (in all its components, §117). So the expression (doxic) coincides with the doxic in the emotional act. The direct expression adapts to the emotional experience through this doxic coincidence.

He refines: direct expression fits only the doxically-unmodalized; modalized acts need indirect expression. More precisely spoken, this direct expression, if it is to be more faithful and complete, comes only to the doxically-unmodalized experiences. If I am in the wishing not certain, then it is not correct if I say in direct adaptation: "may S be p." For all expressing is, in the sense of the underlying conception, a doxic act in the pregnant sense, that is, a belief-certainty. It can therefore only express certainties (for example wish-, will-certainties). The expression is in such cases only indirectly to be performed as more faithful, say in the form: "perhaps may S be p." As soon as modalities occur, one is to recur to the doxic theses, lying so to speak hidden in them, with changed thetic matter, in order to win a possibly-adapted expression. This refines the doctrine. Direct expression fits only the doxically-unmodalized (certain) experiences, because all expressing is a doxic certainty. So a certain wish ("may S be p") is directly expressible. But a modalized wish (an uncertain wishing) needs indirect expression ("perhaps may S be p"), recurring to the doxic thesis hidden in the modality. (The footnote clarifies: one should not say expressing "expresses" a doxic act, if expressing means the meaning itself; the doxic is the expressing.)

He notes the manifold indirect expressions via predicative explication. If we let this conception count as correct, then it would be supplementarily still to carry out: At all times there are manifold possibilities of indirect expressions with "detours." To the essence of every objectivity as such, however it may be constituted, through whatever acts, simple or manifoldly and synthetically founded, belong manifold possibilities of the relating explication; so there can attach to every act, for example a wish-act, various acts related to it, to its noematic objectivity, to its whole noema, connections of subject-theses, predicate-theses set thereon, in which, say, the in the original act wish-meant is judgmentally unfolded and correspondingly expressed. The expression is then not directly adapted to the original phenomenon, but to the predicative derived from it. This describes indirect expression. Besides the direct expression, there are indirect expressions via predicative explication: one can unfold the wish-act's noema into subject- and predicate-theses (judging about the wish), and express that. So one expresses the wish indirectly, by expressing a judgment about it ("I wish that S be p"). So the emotional act can be expressed either directly (the doxic in it expressed) or indirectly (via a judgment about its noema).

He closes by distinguishing explication, assertion, and Doxa. Thereby it is always to be noted that explicative or analytic synthesis (judgment before the conceptual-meaning-wise expression), on the other hand assertion or judgment in the ordinary sense, and finally Doxa (belief) are well to be distinguished things. What one calls "judgment-theory" is a badly ambiguous thing. Essence-clarification of the Idea of the Doxa is something other than that of the assertion or the explications. This is the closing distinction. Three things must be distinguished: (i) explicative/analytic synthesis (the pre-expressive judging, the articulation of the sense before expression); (ii) assertion/judgment in the ordinary sense (the expressed judging); (iii) Doxa (belief, the doxic positing). So "judgment-theory" is ambiguous: it can mean the theory of the Doxa (belief), of the explication (articulation), or of the assertion (expression). Clarifying the Doxa is different from clarifying the assertion or the explication. (The footnote refers to the closing chapter of the Sixth Logical Investigation, noting that Husserl has since advanced beyond it.) With §127, the chapter on the "Logos" (the expressing layer) closes, and with it the Fourth Chapter ("On the Problematic of the Noetic-Noematic Structures") and the whole Third Part. The Fourth Part, "Reason and Actuality," now opens, turning from the structure of consciousness to the theory of reason — how the noematic sense relates to the object, and how consciousness verifies its objects as actual. §128 introduces this, beginning with the noematic sense and the relation to the object, and the determinable X.

# Fourth Part: Reason and Actuality

## First Chapter: The Noematic Sense and the Relation to the Object

### § 128\. Introduction

Husserl reviews the result of the noetic-noematic analyses and states the unity of the highest genus. The phenomenological wanderings of the last chapter have led us into pretty much all intentional spheres. Everywhere we struck, guided by the radical viewpoint of the separation according to reell and intentional, according to noetic and noematic analysis, on ever-newly-branching structures. We can no longer close ourselves to the insight that with this separation it is in fact a matter of a fundamental structure going through all intentional structures, which therefore must form a dominating leitmotif of the phenomenological methodology and determine the course of all research relating to the problems of intentionality. At the same time it is clear that with this separation eo ipso such of two radically opposed and yet essentially mutually-related being-regions has come to setting-off. This sums up the noesis-noema doctrine. The reell/intentional, noetic/noematic separation is the fundamental structure of all intentionality, the leitmotif of phenomenological method. And it sets off two radically opposed but essentially related being-regions: consciousness (the noetic, reell) and the noematic.

He recalls that consciousness-description leads to the noematic, and states the unity of the noematic genus. We earlier emphasized that consciousness in general is to count as a proper being-region. We then recognized, however, that the essence-description of consciousness leads back to that of the in it conscious, that consciousness-correlate is inseparable from consciousness and yet not reell contained in it. So the noematic separated itself off as an objectivity belonging to consciousness and yet peculiar. We notice thereby: While the objects simpliciter (understood in unmodified sense) stand under fundamentally-different highest genera, all object-senses and all completely-taken noemata, however different they may otherwise be, are in principle of a single highest genus. It then holds, however, also, that the essences noema and noesis are inseparable from one another: every lowest difference on the noematic side points eidetically back to lowest differences of the noetic. This is an important structural point. The objects simpliciter belong to fundamentally different highest genera (thing, value, number, etc.). But the noemata (the object-senses) are all of one single highest genus (they are all "senses"). And noema and noesis are inseparable: every noematic difference points back to a noetic difference. So the noematic is a unified region (all senses), correlated essentially with the noetic.

He warns against one-sided noetic (reell) analysis. The cognition of the essential two-sidedness of intentionality according to noesis and noema has the consequence that a systematic phenomenology may not one-sidedly direct its aim onto a reell analysis of the experiences and specially of the intentional. The temptation to this is, however, at the beginning very great, because the historical and natural course from psychology to phenomenology brings it with itself that one understands the immanent study of the pure experiences, the study of their own-essence, as a matter of course as one of their reell components. In truth there open on both sides great fields of eidetic research, which are constantly mutually related and yet, as it turns out, separated over wide stretches. In large measure, what one has taken for act-analysis, for noetic, is throughout won in the glance-direction onto the "meant as such," and so it was noematic structures that one thereby described. This is a methodological warning (with the footnote noting the Logical Investigations still had the noetic-only attitude, treating noematic analyses as mere indices for the noetic). Phenomenology must not be one-sidedly noetic (reell). Much of what passed for "act-analysis" was really noematic analysis (description of the "meant as such"). So the noematic side is a great field of its own, not a mere index of the noetic.

He announces the theme: the noematic side of the relation of consciousness to the object, leading to the problems of reason. We want in our next considerations to direct attention onto the general construction of the noema under a viewpoint that, so far often named, was yet not the leading one for the noematic analysis: The phenomenological problem of the relation of consciousness to an objectivity has above all its noematic side. The noema in itself has objective relation, namely through the "sense" peculiar to it. If we then ask how the consciousness-"sense" comes to the "object," which is its own, and which in manifold acts of very different noematic content can be "the same," how we see this at the sense — then there result new structures, whose extraordinary significance is evident. For proceeding in this direction and on the other hand reflecting on the parallel noeses, we strike finally on the question what the "pretension" of consciousness, really to "relate" to an objective, to be "valid" (triftig), actually says, how "valid" and "invalid" objective relation phenomenologically clarifies itself according to noesis and noema: and with this we stand before the great problems of reason, whose clarification on the transcendental ground, whose formulation as phenomenological problems, will be our goal in this Part. This announces the Fourth Part's theme. The noema relates to its object through its sense. The question: how does the sense relate to the object, which can be "the same" across many acts of different noematic content? This leads to the problems of reason: what the "pretension" (Prätention) of consciousness to relate validly to an object means, and how valid vs. invalid objective relation is clarified. So the Fourth Part is the theory of reason: the phenomenological account of truth, validity, and the constitution of actuality. §§128–133 prepare it by analyzing the noematic sense and the relation to the object (the determinable X); §§136ff. develop the theory of reason proper.

### § 129\. "Content" and "Object"; the Content as "Sense"

Husserl recalls the noematic core and sets up the thesis: every noema has a content (sense) and relates through it to its object. In our hitherto analyses a universal noematic structure played its constant role, designated by the separation of a certain noematic "core" from the changingly-belonging "characters," with which the noematic concretion appears drawn into the flux of various-kind modifications. To its scientific right this core had not yet come. It lifted itself off intuitively, unitary and so far clear that we could in general refer to it. Now it is time to consider it more closely and to place it in the center of phenomenological analysis. As soon as one does that, universally significant differences, running through all act-genera, lift themselves out, which are leading for great research-groups. We link up to the usual equivocal talk of consciousness-content. As content we grasp the "sense," of which we say that in it or through it consciousness relates to an objective as the "its." As title and goal of our discussion we take the proposition: Every noema has a "content," namely its "sense," and relates through it to "its" object. This sets the thesis. The noematic core is now the theme. As "content" Husserl takes the sense, through which consciousness relates to its object. The guiding proposition: every noema has a content (sense) and relates through it to its object. So the sense is the medium of the objective relation: consciousness relates to its object through the noematic sense.

He places this against the act/content/object distinction and Twardowski. (The text discusses the recently-praised distinction of act, content, and object — with a footnote to Twardowski's treatise on the content and object of presentations.) Husserl grants Twardowski's merit in acutely discussing certain confusions, but holds that, without a systematic phenomenology of consciousness, no radical progress was possible; with phenomenologically-unclarified concepts like "act," "content," "object" of "presentations" one is not helped. So the act/content/object distinction (Twardowski) is a first step, but it remains at the level of unclarified concepts. A radical advance requires phenomenology. (Twardowski's distinction is the historical background of Husserl's noema-doctrine, which clarifies it phenomenologically.)

He connects content to the "matter"/"quality" distinction of the Logical Investigations, now read noematically. In this respect a first and, as it may seem to me, necessary step had been attempted through the phenomenological setting-off of "matter" and "quality," through the Idea of the "intentional essence" in its separation from the "epistemic essence." The one-sidedness of the noetic glance-direction, in which these distinctions were performed and meant, overcomes itself easily through the regard to the noematic parallels. We can therefore understand the concepts noematically: the "quality" (judgment-quality, wish-quality, and so on) is nothing other than what we have so far treated as positing-character, thetic character in the widest sense. The expression, stemming from contemporary (Brentanian) psychology, appears to me now little fitting; every peculiar thesis has its quality, but it is not itself to be designated as quality. Obviously now the "matter," which is in each case the "what" that experiences from the "quality" the positing-characteristic, corresponds to the "noematic core." This connects to the Logical Investigations. The Fifth Investigation's "matter" and "quality" (the content and the act-character of an act) are now read noematically: the "quality" is the thetic character (positing-character), the "matter" is the noematic core (the "what"). So the noematic core \= the "matter" of the Logical Investigations, read noematically. (Husserl refines the terminology: "quality" is not apt, since every thesis has a quality.)

He states the task and links the sense to the object-relation. The consequent development of this beginning, the deeper clarification, the further decomposition of these concepts and their correct carrying-through all noetic-noematic domains is now the task. Every really-succeeding progress in this direction must be of exceptional significance for phenomenology. It is not a matter of side-standing specialities, but of essence-moments belonging to the central construction of every intentional experience. The intentional experience has, one is accustomed to say, "relation to objective"; one says also, it is "consciousness of something," for example consciousness of a blossoming apple-tree. Recalling our analyses, we find the full noesis related to the full noema, as its intentional and full What. It is then, however, clear that this relation cannot be the one meant in the talk of the relation of consciousness to its intentional objective; for to every noetic moment, specially every thetic-noetic, corresponds a moment in the noema, and in this there separates, over against the complex of thetic characters, the noematic core characterized through them. This is the crucial move. The full noema (the full What) is not the "object" to which consciousness relates. The object-relation is to something within the noema: the noematic core (minus the thetic characters). So the relation to the object is mediated by the core, not the full noema.

He locates the object-relation in the innermost moment of the noema (the determinable X). Recalling further the "glance-onto" that under circumstances goes through the noesis (through the actual cogito), which transforms the specifically thetic moments into rays of the positing-actuality of the ego, and attending exactly to how this ego now, with them, as being-grasping, or conjecturing, wishing, and so on, "directs" itself onto objective, how its glance goes through the noematic core — we become aware that we, with the talk of the relation (and specially "direction") of consciousness onto its objective, are referred to an innermost moment of the noema. It is not the just-designated core itself, but something that, so to speak, makes up the necessary central-point of the core and functions as "bearer" for noematic peculiarities specially belonging to it, namely for the noematically-modified properties of the "meant as such." This locates the object-relation precisely. The ego's glance goes through the noematic core onto an innermost moment of the noema: not the core itself, but its central-point, the bearer of the core's properties. This is the determinable X (§131): the pure object-point that bears the predicates. So the object-relation terminates in this central bearer.

He concludes: even within the noema, content and object must be distinguished. As soon as we go into this more exactly, we become aware that in fact not only for "consciousness," for the intentional experience, but also for the noema in itself the distinction between "content" and "object" is to be made. So also the noema relates to an object and possesses a "content," "by means of which" it relates to the object: whereby the object is the same as that of the noesis; as the "parallelism" again throughout proves itself. This is the key result of §129. Even within the noema, content and object must be distinguished: the noema has a content (the sense, the predicates) and an object (the central bearer, the X), and relates to the object through the content. And this object is the same as the noesis's object (the parallelism holds). So the structure is: noesis → noema (content \+ object); the content (sense) relates to the object (the X). §130 delimits the noematic sense; §131 isolates the determinable X.

### § 130\. Delimitation of the Essence "Noematic Sense"

Husserl delimits the noematic sense as the description of the meant objective as it is meant, avoiding all subjective expressions. (Simplifying by setting aside the attentional modifications and restricting to positional acts:) Let us transport ourselves into a living cogito; then it has, according to its essence, in a distinguished sense "direction" onto an objectivity. With other words, to its noema belongs an "objectivity" — in quotation marks — with a certain noematic stock, which unfolds itself in a description of determinate delimitation, namely in such a one that, as description of the "meant objective, exactly as it is meant," avoids all "subjective" expressions. There are used formal-ontological expressions, like "object," "constitution," "state-of-affairs"; material-ontological expressions like "thing," "figure," "cause"; content-determinations like "rough," "hard," "colored" — all have their quotation marks, hence the noematically-modified sense. Excluded, on the other hand, are for the description of this meant objective as such expressions like "perceptually," "memory-wise," "clearly-intuitively," "thought-wise," "given" — they belong to another dimension of descriptions, not to the objective that is conscious, but to the manner how it is conscious. This delimits the noematic sense. The noematic sense is the description of the meant objective exactly as it is meant, using only objective expressions (formal-ontological: object, state-of-affairs; material-ontological: thing, cause; content: rough, hard) — all in quotation marks (noematically modified). Excluded are the subjective expressions (perceptually, memory-wise, given) — these belong to the givenness-mode (the characters), not to the sense. So the noematic sense \= the meant objective as meant, described objectively (the "what," not the "how").

He notes the sense includes the determinate and the indeterminate. On the other hand, it would fall again into the frame of the description in question to say, at an appearing thing-object: its "front-side" is so-and-so determined according to color, shape, and so on, its "back-side" has "a" color, but a "not-more-closely-determined" one, it is in general in such-and-such respects "undetermined" whether it be so or so. This is an important point. The noematic sense includes both the determinate (the front-side, determinately colored) and the indeterminate (the back-side, "a color, but not more closely determined"). So the sense has determinate and indeterminate ("empty"-meant) components. The indeterminate is part of the sense (the object is meant as having a back-side, but indeterminately). (This empty indeterminacy is distinct from intuitive emptiness, the darkness of an unclear presentation — footnote.)

He extends to value-objectivities and states the firm content of every noema. This holds not only for nature-objects but quite generally, for example for value-objectivities; to their description belongs that of the meant "thing" and thereto the statement of the predicates of the "value," as when we say of the appearing tree "in the sense" of our valuing meaning, it be covered with "gloriously" smelling blossoms. Thereby also the value-predicates have their quotation marks, they are not predicates of a value simpliciter, but of a value-noema. Obviously with this a quite firm content in every noema is delimited. Every consciousness has its What and every one means "its" objective; it is evident that we, at every consciousness, such a noematic description of the same, "exactly as it is meant," in principle must be able to perform; we win through explication and conceptual grasping a closed compass of formal or material, content-determined or also "undetermined" ("empty"-meant) "predicates," and these in their modified signification determine the "content" of the object-core in question of the noema. This states the result. Every noema has a firm content: the meant objective, exactly as meant, described by a closed compass of predicates (determinate and indeterminate, all noematically modified, in quotation marks). This content (the predicates in their modified signification) is the "content" of the object-core. So the noematic sense \= the closed set of predicates determining the object-core. §131 now isolates the object-core itself (the bearer of these predicates), the determinable X.

### § 131\. The "Object," the Determinable X in the Noematic Sense

Husserl isolates the central X: the predicates are predicates of a "something," the bearer. The predicates are, however, predicates of "something," and this "something" belongs also, and obviously inseparably, to the core in question: it is the central unity-point of which we spoke above. It is the connection-point or "bearer" of the predicates, but by no means unity of them in the sense in which any complex, any connection of the predicates would be called unity. It is necessarily to be distinguished from them, although not to be placed beside them and separated from them, just as conversely they themselves are its predicates: without it unthinkable and yet from it distinguishable. This isolates the determinable X. The predicates (the sense-content) are predicates of a "something" — the central bearer, the unity-point. This bearer is not the sum of the predicates (not their complex), but the point they are predicates of. It is distinct from the predicates (it is what they are predicated of) yet inseparable from them (it is unthinkable without predicates, and they are its predicates). So the noematic core \= the bearer (the X) \+ its predicates (the sense-content).

He describes the identity of the X across the changing sense, in the continuous/synthetic flow. We say that the intentional object in the continuous or synthetic progress of consciousness is constantly conscious, but in it gives itself ever again "otherwise"; it be "the same," only given in other predicates, with another determination-content; "it" show itself only from different sides, whereby the indeterminate-remained predicates have determined themselves more closely; or "the" object be in this stretch of the givenness unchanged, now however "it," the identical, change, it take through this change in beauty, lose in use-value, and so on. If this is constantly understood as noematic description of the respective meant as such, and is this description, as it is at any time possible, performed in pure adequation, then there separates evidently the identical intentional "object" from the changing and changeable "predicates." There separates itself off as central noematic moment: the "object," the "Object," the "Identical," the "determinable subject of its possible predicates" — the pure X in abstraction from all predicates — and it separates itself from these predicates, or more exactly, from the predicate-noemata. This is the famous isolation of the determinable X. Across the changing sense (the object given otherwise, from different sides, with predicates determined or changed), the intentional object is conscious as "the same." Separating the identical from the changing predicates, one isolates the central noematic moment: the "object," the "identical," the "determinable subject of its possible predicates" — the pure X in abstraction from all predicates. So the determinable X is the pure object-point, the identical bearer, abstracted from all its predicates. It is the "determinable subject" — determinable because it can take predicates, the subject because the predicates are predicated of it.

He shows the X is the identity-unity across the manifold of acts. To the one Object we order manifold consciousness-ways, acts, or act-noemata. Obviously this is nothing accidental; none is thinkable without that also manifold intentional experiences were thinkable, connected in continuous or in properly synthetic (polythetic) unity, in which "it," the Object, is conscious as identical and yet in noematically different way: such that the characterized core is a changeable and the "object," the pure subject of the predicates, precisely an identical one. We can say: Several act-noemata have here everywhere different cores, however so that they nonetheless close together to the identity-unity, to a unity in which the "something," the determinable, that lies in every core, is conscious as identical. This shows the X as identity-unity. The one object is correlated with a manifold of acts/noemata, in which it is conscious as identical though in different ways (different cores). So the cores differ, but the X (the determinable something in every core) is identical across them. The X is the identity-unity binding the manifold of acts into the consciousness of one object.

He extends to separate acts (perception and memory) closing into one object. Likewise can, however, separate acts, like for example two perceptions or a perception and a memory, close together to a "unanimous" unity, and by virtue of the peculiarity of this closing-together, which is obviously not foreign to the essence of the closed-together acts, the in one case so and the other time otherwise determined Something of the at-first separate cores is now conscious as the same Something, or as unanimously the same "object." This extends to separate acts. Two separate acts (two perceptions, or a perception and a memory) can close into a "unanimous" unity, in which the X of their separate cores is conscious as the same object. So even across separate acts (e.g. perceiving a thing now and remembering it later), the X can be identified as the same object. This is the identity-synthesis (the "unanimous" closing-together, Deckung), the basis of recognizing the same object across different acts. (This is the synthesis of identification, central to the constitution of the object as identical pole.)

He states the universal result: every noema harbors a pure object-something. So there lies in every noema such a pure object-something \[as the central point, the determinable X, the bearer of the noematic predicates, the identical subject correlative to the manifold of possible acts in which the object is meant\]. This is the universal result of §131. Every noema harbors a pure object-something (the determinable X): the central point, the bearer of the predicates, the identical subject correlative to all possible acts meaning the object. So the structure of the noema is complete: the full noema \= the determinable X (the pure object-point) \+ the noematic sense (the predicates, the content) \+ the thetic characters (the givenness-modes and position-characters). The X is the innermost moment, through which consciousness relates to its object. This determinable X is the noematic correlate of the "object simpliciter" (§91), the pure object-pole that remains identical across all the manifold ways it is meant and given. It is the phenomenological clarification of how consciousness relates to "the same object" through ever-different senses: the X is the identical pole, the senses its changing determinations.

He distinguishes the two noematic object-concepts and defines the Sinn. So there lies in every noema such a pure object-something as unity-point; and at the same time we see how, in noematic respect, two object-concepts are to be distinguished: this pure unity-point, this noematic "object simpliciter," and the "object in the how of its determinatenesses" — reckoning in the respective "open-remaining" and in this mode co-meant indeterminatenesses. Thereby this "how" is to be taken exactly as that which the respective act prescribes, as which it thus really belongs to its noema. The "sense," of which we repeatedly spoke, is this noematic "object in the how" with all that the above-characterized description is able to find at it evidently and to express conceptually. This fixes the two object-concepts and the Sinn. (i) The "object simpliciter" (the pure unity-point, the determinable X). (ii) The "object in the how of its determinatenesses" (the object with all its predicates, determinate and indeterminate, exactly as the act prescribes). The Sinn (sense) is this second one: the noematic "object in the how," all that the noematic description finds and expresses. So the Sinn \= the object in the how of its determinations (the X plus its predicate-content), distinct from the bare X (the object simpliciter).

He cautions that "sense" is not yet the full "core," and states the necessity of the X. Note that we now cautiously said "sense," not "core." For it will turn out that we, to win the real, concretely-complete core of the noema, must still reckon in a dimension of differences that finds no imprint in the characterized and for us the sense-defining description. Holding here at first purely to what it grasps, the "sense" is thus a foundational piece of the noema. It is from noema to noema in general a changing one, under circumstances however an absolutely-alike and possibly even characterized as "identical" one; provided just the "object in the how of the determinatenesses" stands there on both sides as the same and absolutely-alike to be described. In no noema can it be lacking, and can its necessary center, the unity-point, the pure determinable X, be lacking. No "sense" without the "something" and again without "determining content." Thereby it is evident that the like is not first inserted by the subsequent analysis and description, but that it, as condition of the possibility of the evident description and before it, really lies in the consciousness-correlate. This is an important refinement. The "sense" (the object in the how) is not yet the full "core" — a further dimension (the fullness, §132) must be added. The sense is a foundational piece of the noema, changing from noema to noema (but possibly identical). And no sense lacks the X (the something) and the determining content. The X and the sense really lie in the consciousness-correlate (the condition of the possibility of the description), not inserted by the analysis. So the determinable X is a real structural moment, prior to and grounding the description.

He states how different senses relate to the same object through the X. Through the sense-bearer belonging to the sense (as empty X) and the possibility, grounding in the essence of the senses, of unanimous combination to sense-unities of arbitrary step, not only has every sense its "object," but different senses relate to the same object, precisely insofar as they are to be ordered into sense-unities in which the determinable X of the unified senses come to coincidence (Deckung) with one another and with the X of the total-sense of the respective sense-unity. This explains the same-object relation. Different senses relate to the same object because their determinable X's coincide (in a sense-unity). So two senses ("the morning star," "the evening star") relate to the same object when their X's coincide in a unanimous sense-unity. The X is the point of coincidence; the identity of the object is the coincidence of the X's. (This is the phenomenological account of how different senses can present the same object — the Fregean problem, solved by the coincidence of the determinable X.)

He extends to the synthetic (polythetic) acts. Our exposition transfers from the monothetic acts to the synthetic, or more distinctly, to the polythetic. In a thetically-articulated consciousness every member has the described noematic construction; every one has its X with its "determining content"; but in addition the noema of the synthetic total-act has, with relation to the "archontic" thesis, the synthetic X and its determining content. In the act-performance the glance-ray of the pure ego, dividing itself into a plurality of rays, goes onto the to-synthetic-unity-coming X's. At the transformation of the nominalization the synthetic total-phenomenon modifies itself and so that an actuality-ray goes onto the highest synthetic X. This extends to syntheses. In a synthetic act (a judgment, a collection), each member has its own X, but the total-act has a synthetic X (the X of the whole state-of-affairs or collection), related to the archontic thesis. The glance-ray divides onto the members' X's; nominalization makes a single ray go onto the synthetic X (the whole as one object). So the synthetic noema has member-X's and a total (synthetic) X.

### § 132\. The Core as Sense in the Mode of its Fullness

Husserl states that the sense is not a concrete essence but an abstract form, and introduces the fullness-dimension. The sense, as we have determined it, is not a concrete essence in the total-stock of the noema, but a kind of abstract form dwelling in it. Holding namely the sense fast, hence the "meant" exactly with the determination-content in which it is meant, there results clearly a second concept of the "object in the how" — in the how of its givenness-modes. This introduces the second dimension. The sense (the object in the how of its determinations) is only an abstract form, not the concrete noematic core. A further dimension must be added: the "object in the how of its givenness-modes" (not its determinations). So there are two "how"s: the how of the determinations (the sense, §131) and the how of the givenness-modes (the fullness, §132). The full core includes both.

He specifies the fullness as the difference of clarity-fullness (setting aside attention and performance-modes). Seeing thereby away from the attentional modifications, from all differences of the kind that the performance-modes are, there come — constantly in the preferred sphere of positionality — into consideration the differences of clarity-fullness, which are epistemically so very determining. A darkly conscious as such and the same clearly conscious are, with respect to their noematic concretion, very different, just as the whole experiences are. But nothing stands in the way that the determination-content, with which the darkly conscious is meant, be absolutely identical with that of the clearly conscious. The descriptions would coincide, and a synthetic unity-consciousness could so embrace the both-sided consciousness that it really was a matter of the same meant. This specifies the fullness. The fullness is the difference of clarity (§§67–68): a darkly-conscious and a clearly-conscious object differ in noematic concretion, but can have the identical determination-content (the same sense). So the sense (the determination-content) is identical across clarity-levels, while the fullness (the clarity) differs. The fullness is the clarity-mode of the givenness, distinct from the sense.

He defines the full core as the sense in the mode of its fullness. As full core we will accordingly reckon precisely the full concretion of the relevant noematic stock-piece, hence the sense in the mode of its fullness. This defines the full core. The full core \= the sense (the object in the how of its determinations) \+ the fullness (the clarity-mode) \= "the sense in the mode of its fullness." So the noema's structure is now: the determinable X (the object simpliciter) \+ the sense (the object in the how of its determinations) \+ the fullness (the clarity-mode) \+ the thetic characters (the positing-characters and givenness-modes). The full core is the sense-with-fullness; the full noema is the full core plus the thetic characters. (The fullness is epistemically decisive, as §§136ff. will show: the originary fullness is what gives the object "itself," grounding evidence and reason.)

### § 133\. The Noematic Proposition. Thetic and Synthetic Propositions. Propositions in the Domain of Presentations

Husserl defines the proposition as the unity of sense and thetic character. It would now need a careful carrying-through of these distinctions in all act-domains, as well as the supplementary regard to the thetic moments, which have to the sense as noematic a special relation. In the Logical Investigations they were (under the title quality) from the start taken into the concept of the sense (the "meaning-essence") and so in this unity the two components "matter" (sense in the present version) and quality distinguished. But it seems more fitting to define the term sense merely as that "matter," and then to designate the unity of sense and thetic character as proposition (Satz). This defines the proposition. The Logical Investigations took sense and quality together (as the "meaning-essence"). Now Husserl separates: sense \= the "matter" (the noematic core, the object in the how); proposition (Satz) \= the unity of sense \+ thetic character. So the proposition is the sense together with its positing-character. (E.g. the proposition "S is p" \= the sense "S-is-p" \+ the doxic certainty-character.)

He distinguishes one-membered and synthetic propositions, and extends to all spheres. We have then one-membered propositions (as with the perceptions and other thetic intuitions) and many-membered, synthetic propositions, like predicative doxic propositions (judgments), conjecture-propositions with predicatively-articulated matter, and so on. One-membered as many-membered are further liking-propositions, wish-propositions, command-propositions, and so on. The concept of the proposition is thereby of course extraordinarily and perhaps strangely extended, but yet in the frame of an important essence-unity. This extends the proposition-concept. There are one-membered propositions (a simple perception's noema \= its sense \+ thetic character) and synthetic (many-membered) propositions (judgments, with articulated matter). And there are propositions in all spheres: doxic propositions (judgments), liking-propositions, wish-propositions, command-propositions. So "proposition" is greatly extended — every act's noema (sense \+ thetic character) is a "proposition." (This generalizes the logical "proposition" to all acts, the basis of the formal disciplines of feeling and will.)

He cautions that "sense" and "proposition" contain nothing of expression. Constantly it is to be kept in view that the concepts sense and proposition contain for us nothing of expression and conceptual signification, but on the other hand comprise under themselves all expressed propositions, or proposition-significations. This is the key caution (recalling §124). "Sense" and "proposition" here are pre-expressive (they contain nothing of language or conceptual meaning), but they comprise the expressed propositions (the expressed proposition is a special case of the proposition in this wide sense). So the noematic proposition is broader than the expressed (linguistic) proposition; the latter is the former expressed.

He states the proposition-layer is an abstract layer in all noemata, present even in simple intuitions. According to our analyses these concepts designate an abstract layer belonging to the full web of all noemata. It is of great reach for our cognition to win this layer in its all-encompassing generality, hence to see that it really has its place in all act-spheres. Also in the simple intuitions the concepts sense and proposition, which inseparably belong to the concept object, have their necessary application; necessarily the special concepts intuition-sense and intuition-proposition must be coined. This states the universality. The proposition-layer (sense \+ thetic character) is in all noemata, including the simple intuitions. So one must coin "intuition-sense" and "intuition-proposition": even a simple perception has its sense and its proposition (its perceived-as-such with its positing-character). So the proposition is not confined to judgments; it is in every act, including the pre-predicative intuitions.

He gives the thing-sense and the concept of appearance. So is, for example, in the domain of outer perception, out of the "per"-ceived object as such, under abstraction from the character of perceivedness, as something lying in this noema before all explicating and conceiving thinking, the object-sense to be looked out, the thing-sense of this perception, which is from perception to perception (also with respect to "the same" thing) another. Taking this sense full, with its intuitive fullness, there results a determinate and very important concept of appearance (Erscheinung). To these senses correspond propositions, intuition-propositions, presentation-propositions, perceptive propositions, and so on. In a phenomenology of the outer intuitions, which as such has to do not with objects simpliciter, in unmodified sense, but with noemata as correlates of the noeses, concepts like the here set-out stand in the center of the scientific research. This gives the thing-sense and appearance. The thing-sense of a perception is the perceived-as-such (abstracting from the perceivedness), which differs from perception to perception (even of the same thing). The thing-sense with its intuitive fullness yields the concept of appearance (Erscheinung): the appearance is the thing-sense in its fullness (the thing as it appears, in this clarity, from this side). So "appearance" is the full thing-noema. The phenomenology of perception studies these (the thing-senses, the appearances, the perceptive propositions), not the objects simpliciter.

He announces the program of a systematic form-doctrine of the senses. Returning to the general theme, there results now further the task to distinguish systematically the fundamental kinds of the senses, the simple and synthetic, the senses of first and higher step. Partly following the fundamental kinds of content-determinations, partly the fundamental forms of synthetic formations, which play their role for all signification-domains in the same way, and so taking account of all that is a priori according to form and content determining for the general construction of the senses, common to all consciousness-spheres or peculiar to genus-wise closed spheres — we ascend to the Idea of a systematic and universal form-doctrine of the senses (significations). Drawing into account the systematic distinction of the positing-characters, there is thereby at the same time a systematic typology of the propositions accomplished. This announces the form-doctrine of senses. There can be a systematic, universal form-doctrine of the senses (and, with the positing-characters, a typology of propositions): the systematic account of the fundamental kinds and forms of senses (content-determinations and synthetic forms). This is the general framework within which formal logic (the apophantic form-doctrine, §134) finds its place. §134 develops the apophantic part.

### § 134\. Apophantic Form-Doctrine

Husserl states the main task: a form-doctrine of the predicative propositions (formal logic). A main task is here to design a systematic "analytic" form-doctrine of the "logical" significations, or of the predicative propositions, the "judgments" in the sense of formal logic, which takes regard only to the forms of the analytic or predicative synthesis, and leaves the sense-terms entering into these forms undetermined. Although this task is a special one, it has yet universal reach through this, that the title predicative synthesis designates a class for all possible sense-kinds of possible operations; everywhere equally possible operations of explication and of the relating apprehension of the explicated: as determination of the determination-subject, as part of the whole, as relatum of its referent, and so on. With this interweave operations of collection, of disjunction, of hypothetical connection. All that before all assertion and the with it newly-appearing explicit or "conceptual" grasping, which snuggles to all forms and matters as meaning-wise expression. This states the apophantic form-doctrine (formal logic). It is the form-doctrine of the predicative propositions (judgments), abstracting from the sense-terms (treating only the forms: subject, predicate, part, whole, relation, collection, disjunction, hypothesis). It has universal reach (these operations apply to any sense). And it operates before all assertion (the forms are pre-expressive; the expression snuggles to them). So formal logic studies the pre-expressive predicative forms.

He locates the apophantic form-doctrine within the general form-doctrine of senses, rooted in noematic phenomenology. This form-doctrine, whose Idea we have already several times touched, and which according to our demonstrations makes up the in principle necessary under-step of a scientific mathesis universalis, loses through the results of the present investigations its isolation, it wins its home within the as Idea conceived general form-doctrine of the senses in general and its last origin-place in the noematic phenomenology. This locates formal logic. The apophantic form-doctrine (formal logic, the under-step of the mathesis universalis) is not isolated: it has its home in the general form-doctrine of senses, and its last origin in noematic phenomenology. So formal logic is grounded in the phenomenology of the noema (the senses and their forms). (This is the program of Formal and Transcendental Logic: formal logic grounded in transcendental phenomenology.)

He explains the analytic-syntactic operations and the syntactic forms. The analytic-syntactic operations are, we said, possible operations for all possible senses, or propositions, whatever determination-content the respective noematic sense (which is nothing other than the "meant" object as such and in the respective how of its determination-content) may grasp "non-explicated" in itself. Always it lets itself, however, be explicated, and any of the with the explication ("analysis") essence-wise connected operations be performed. The synthetic forms that so grow up (in echo of the grammatical "syntaxes" we called them also the syntactic) are quite determinate, belonging to a fixed form-system, to be lifted out by abstraction and conceptually-explicitly to be grasped. So we can, for example, treat the in simple perception-thesis perceived as such analytically in a way that indicates itself in the expressions: "This is black, an inkwell, this black inkwell is not white, is if white so not black," and the like. With every step we have a new sense, instead of the original one-membered proposition a synthetic proposition, which, according to the law of the expressibility of all urdoxic propositions, lets itself be brought to expression, or to predicative assertion. This explains the syntactic operations. The noematic sense (the meant object) can be explicated (analyzed), and the syntactic operations performed (predication, negation, hypothesis). Each step yields a new sense (a synthetic proposition). The example: the perceived inkwell can be explicated ("this is black, an inkwell, not white..."), each step a new synthetic proposition, expressible. So the syntactic forms are won by explicating the perceptual sense.

He defines the apophantic form-doctrine and extends to modalities. Assume the positings belonging to these sense-forms are doxic Urpositings: then there grow up various forms of judgments in the logical sense (apophantic propositions). The goal, to determine all these forms a priori, to master in systematic completeness the infinitely manifold and yet lawfully-delimited form-formations, designates for us the Idea of a form-doctrine of the apophantic propositions, or syntaxes. The positings, and in particular the synthetic total-positing, can however also be doxic modalities: we conjecture and explicate the in the mode "conjecturable" conscious; or it stands as questionable, and in the questionability-consciousness we explicate the questionable, and so on. Bringing the noematic correlates of these modalities to expression ("S might be p," "Is S p?"), and doing the same also for the simple predicative judgment itself, as we also express affirmation and negation ("S is not p," "S is yet p," "S is certainly, really p") — so there extends thereby the concept of the form and the Idea of the form-doctrine of the propositions. The form is now manifoldly determined, partly through the proper syntactic forms, partly through the doxic modalities. This defines and extends the apophantic form-doctrine. With doxic Urpositings, the syntactic forms yield judgments (apophantic propositions); the form-doctrine masters these a priori. With doxic modalities (conjecture, question), the forms extend (conjecture-propositions, question-propositions). So the form is determined by both the syntactic forms and the doxic modalities. (This is the extension of formal logic to the modalized judgments, central to Formal and Transcendental Logic.)

He notes every such proposition is transformable into an assertion about the modality, and extends to the emotional/volitional sphere. At any time there remains thereby to the total-proposition a total-thesis belonging, and in it enclosed a doxic thesis. At the same time every such proposition and the to it directly-adapted conceptual "expression" lets itself, through a sense-explication and predication, which transforms the modal characteristic into a predicate, be transferred into an assertion-proposition, into a judgment that judges about the modality of a content of such-and-such form (for example: "It is certain, it is possible, probable, that S is p"). In similar way as with the judgment-modalities it stands with founded theses, or senses and propositions of the emotional and will-sphere, with the specifically to them belonging syntheses and the corresponding expression-ways. There designates itself then easily the goal of the new form-doctrines of propositions and specially synthetic propositions. This extends the form-doctrine. Every modalized proposition can be transformed into an assertion about the modality ("It is possible that S is p"). And the same holds for the emotional/volitional propositions (liking-propositions, wish-propositions), with their syntheses and expressions. So there are new form-doctrines for the emotional and volitional propositions (formal axiology, formal practics).

He states the great result: the form-doctrine of doxic propositions mirrors the form-doctrine of all propositions. Thereby one sees at the same time that in a suitably-extended form-doctrine of the doxic propositions — if we, just in the same way as the being-modalities, also take over the ought-modalities (if the analogizing talk is permitted) into the judgment-matter — the form-doctrine of all propositions mirrors itself. What this taking-over means needs no long discussion, but at most illustration by examples: We say, for example, instead of "S may be p": that S be p, that may be, it is wished (not: ge-wished); instead of "S shall be p": that S be p, that shall be, it is an ought, and so on. This is the great unifying result. The form-doctrine of doxic propositions (formal logic), suitably extended, mirrors the form-doctrine of all propositions (including the emotional and volitional): one takes over the value- and ought-modalities into the judgment-matter (turning "S may be p" into "that S be p is wished," "S shall be p" into "that S be p is an ought"). So all the form-doctrines (logic, axiology, practics) can be unified in an extended form-doctrine of doxic propositions, by objectifying the value/ought-modalities into being-predicates. (This is the doxic universality again: every modality becomes a being-predicate, so logic mirrors all the disciplines.)

He states phenomenology's task is not the form-doctrine but the analysis of the immediately-intuitable a priori. Phenomenology itself sees its task not in the systematic development of these form-doctrines, in which, as is to be learned from the apophantic form-doctrine, from primitive axiomatic fundamental-formations deductively the systematic possibilities of all further formations are derived; its field is the analysis of the in immediate intuition demonstrable a priori, the fixing of immediately-insightful essences and essence-connections and their descriptive cognition in the systematic web of all layers in the transcendentally-pure consciousness. What the theorizing logician in the formal meaning-doctrine isolates, by virtue of his one-sided interest-direction treats as something for itself, without attention and understanding of the noematic and noetic connections in which it is phenomenologically interwoven — that the phenomenologist takes in its full connection. To go after the phenomenological essence-interweavings all-sidedly is his great task. This distinguishes phenomenology from formal logic. Phenomenology's task is not the deductive development of the form-doctrines (that is formal logic's task) but the descriptive analysis of the immediately-intuitable a priori — the essences and essence-connections in the web of consciousness. The logician isolates the logical forms; the phenomenologist takes them in their full noetic-noematic connection. So phenomenology grounds and contextualizes formal logic, without itself being formal logic.

He closes: every logical fundamental concept becomes a title for phenomenological investigations. Every simple axiomatic demonstration of a logical fundamental concept becomes a title for phenomenological investigations. Already what is there in widest logical generality simply set out as "proposition" (judgment-proposition), as categorical or hypothetical proposition, as attributive determination, nominalized adjective or relative, and the like, yields, as soon as it is bedded back into the corresponding noematic essence-connections, out of which the theorizing glance has lifted it, difficult and far-reaching problem-groups of pure phenomenology. This closes §134. Every logical fundamental concept (proposition, categorical/hypothetical, attribution, nominalization) becomes a theme for phenomenology when bedded back into its noematic essence-connections. So the logical concepts, isolated by the logician, are sources of deep phenomenological problems when restored to their full constitutive context. With §134, the noematic analysis of sense, proposition, and form is complete, and §135 makes the transition to the phenomenology of reason proper — the relation of object and consciousness, and the constitution of actuality through rational positing.

### § 135\. Object and Consciousness. Transition to the Phenomenology of Reason

Husserl states the universal correlation of object and consciousness. As every intentional experience has a noema and in it a sense, through which it relates to the object, so conversely all that we call object, of which we speak, what we have before our eyes as actuality, hold for possible or probable, think however so indeterminately, is precisely thereby already object of consciousness; and that says that, whatever world and actuality in general may be and be called, it must be represented in the frame of actual and possible consciousness through corresponding senses or propositions filled with more or less intuitive content. This states the universal correlation. Every object whatever (actual, possible, probable, indeterminate) is object of consciousness, represented by corresponding senses/propositions. So world and actuality are correlates of consciousness, represented by senses with intuitive content.

He explains the "revaluing change of sign" (§76) more deeply: the bracketed realities are represented by manifolds of senses. If therefore phenomenology performs "switchings-off," if it as transcendental brackets all actual positing of realities and performs the other bracketings we earlier described, then we now understand from a deeper ground the sense and correctness of the earlier thesis: that all phenomenologically switched-off belongs, in a certain change of sign, yet in the frame of phenomenology. Namely the real and ideal actualities that fall to the switching-off are represented in the phenomenological sphere through the to them corresponding total-manifolds of senses and propositions. For example, every actual thing of nature is represented through all the senses and changingly-filled propositions in which it, as so-and-so determined and further to be determined, is the correlate of possible intentional experiences; hence represented through the manifolds of "full cores," or, what here says the same, of all possible "subjective appearance-ways," in which it as identical can be noematically constituted. This deepens §76. The bracketed realities are represented in phenomenology by the manifolds of senses/propositions (full cores, appearance-ways) in which they can be constituted. So the thing is represented by all its possible appearances. This is the noematic constitution of the object.

He notes the constitution refers to individual and community consciousness (intersubjectivity). This constitution refers, however, first to an essence-possible individual consciousness, then also to a possible community-consciousness, that is, to an essence-possible plurality of consciousness-egos and consciousness-streams standing in "commerce," for which one thing as the same objective actuality is intersubjectively to be given and to be identified.
