This introduces intersubjectivity. The object is constituted not only for an individual consciousness but for a community of communicating egos, for which the thing is intersubjectively the same. So the objective actuality is the intersubjective correlate (developed in Cartesian Meditations and the Krisis). (Husserl flags but does not develop intersubjectivity here.)

He notes the noetic side: infinite manifolds of experiences, all conscious of "the same." On the other side correspond to every thing and finally to the whole thing-world with the one space and the one time the manifolds of possible noetic occurrences, the possible experiences relating to them of the singular individuals and community-individuals, experiences that as parallels of the just-considered noematic manifolds have in their essence the peculiarity of relating according to sense and proposition to this thing-world. So the unity of the thing stands over against an infinite ideal manifold of noetic experiences of a quite determinate and despite the infinity surveyable essence-content, all united in being consciousness of "the same." This states the noetic correlation. The thing (one unity) corresponds to an infinite manifold of noetic experiences (the experiences of all egos relating to it), all united as consciousness of the same. So the thing is the unity-pole of an infinite manifold of experiences (the constitution-parallelism, §98).

He states the strict lawfulness of the experience-sphere. All and everything is, however far we span the frame, and in whatever generality- and particularity-step we move — down to the lowest concretions — essence-wise prefigured. So strictly lawful is the experience-sphere according to its transcendental essence-construction, so firmly is every possible essence-formation according to noesis and noema determined in it, as any possible figure to be inscribed in space is determined through the essence of space — according to unconditionally-valid lawfulnesses. What here on both sides is called possibility (eidetic existence) is thus absolutely-necessary possibility, absolutely-firm member in an absolutely-firm structure of an eidetic system. This states the strict lawfulness. The experience-sphere is strictly lawful (like space): every possible noetic-noematic formation is determined by unconditionally-valid laws. So the eidetic possibilities are absolutely necessary, firm members of an eidetic system. (Phenomenology is a rigorous eidetic science, §75.)

He states the great turning-point: the question of actuality and reason. Reflecting more exactly on what the essence-connections between object and consciousness characterized in the foregoing consideration say or had to say, a doubleness becomes felt to us, and following it we notice that we stand before a great turning-point of our investigations. We order to an object manifolds of "propositions," or of experiences of a certain noematic content, and indeed so that through it syntheses of identification become a priori possible, by virtue of which the object can and must stand there as the same. The X in the different acts, or act-noemata, equipped with different "determination-content," is necessarily conscious as the same. But is it really the same? And is the object itself "actual"? Could it not be unreal, while yet the manifold unanimous and even intuition-filled propositions — propositions of whatever essence-content — ran off consciousness-wise? This is the great turning-point. The constitution of the object as identical X (the same across the manifold) does not yet settle whether the object is actual (really is) and whether the X is really the same (not merely meant as the same). The manifold of unanimous, intuition-filled propositions could run off even if the object were unreal (a coherent illusion). So the question of actuality (reality) and of reason (the validity of the positing) arises. This is the theme of the phenomenology of reason.

He states the reason-question and its phenomenological form. The consciousness, or consciousness-subject itself, judges about actuality, asks after it, conjectures, doubts it, decides the doubt and thereby performs "adjudications of reason." Must not in the essence-connection of the transcendental consciousness, hence purely phenomenologically, the essence of this right and correlatively the essence of "actuality" — related to all kinds of objects, according to all formal and regional categories — let itself be brought to clarity? This states the reason-question. Consciousness adjudicates about actuality (judges, doubts, decides). The phenomenological task: to clarify, purely phenomenologically, the essence of this "right" (the validity of the positing) and correlatively the essence of "actuality" — for all kinds of objects. So the phenomenology of reason clarifies validity and actuality as correlative phenomenological essences.

He distinguishes two senses of "constitution" (the narrower and the wider). In our talk of the noetic-noematic "constitution" of objectivities, for example thing-objectivities, lay thus an ambiguity. Preferably we thought in it in any case of "actual" objects, of things of the "actual world" or at least "a" actual world in general. What does then, however, this "actual" say for objects that are consciousness-wise yet only given through senses and propositions? In the wider sense, however, an object "constitutes" itself — "whether it be actual or not" — in certain consciousness-connections that bear in themselves an insightful unity, insofar as they essence-wise carry with themselves the consciousness of an identical X. This distinguishes two senses of constitution. (i) The narrower: the constitution of an actual object (which really is). (ii) The wider: the constitution of an object "whether actual or not" — the mere consciousness of an identical X (the object as meant, regardless of actuality). So "constitution" can mean the constitution of the object-as-meant (wider) or of the actual object (narrower). The reason-question is how the meant identity becomes actual identity.

He states the universal scope of the actuality-question. In fact the carried-out concerns not merely actualities in some pregnant sense. Actuality-questions stick in all cognitions as such, also in our phenomenological cognitions relating to possible constitution of objects: all have indeed their correlates in "objects" that are meant as "actually-being." When is, can everywhere be asked, the noematically "meant" identity of the X "actual" identity instead of "merely meant," and what says everywhere this "merely meant"? To the problems of actuality and the correlative of the reason-consciousness verifying it in itself we must therefore devote new considerations. This states the universal scope and announces the theme. The actuality-question is in all cognition (every cognition means its object as actually-being). The question: when is the meant identity actual, not merely meant? This is the problem of actuality and of the reason-consciousness that verifies it. So the phenomenology of reason (the Second Chapter) clarifies how consciousness verifies its objects as actual.

## Second Chapter: Phenomenology of Reason

Husserl opens the chapter with the correlation of actuality and rational verifiability. Speaking of objects simpliciter, one normally means actual, truly-being objects of the respective being-category. Whatever one then asserts of the objects — if one speaks rationally — must let the so-meant so-asserted be "grounded," "verified," directly "seen" or mediately "insightfully-seen." In principle stand in the logical sphere, in that of the assertion, "truly-" or "actually-being" and "rationally-verifiable-being" in correlation; and that for all doxic being- or positing-modalities. Self-evidently the here in question possibility of rational verification is understood not as empirical, but as "ideal," as essence-possibility. This is the master-thesis of the chapter. Truly-being (actual) and rationally-verifiable-being stand in correlation: an object is actual iff its being can be rationally verified (grounded, seen, insightfully seen). This holds for all doxic modalities (the possible is rationally-verifiable-as-possible, etc.). And the verifiability is ideal (essence-possibility), not empirical. So actuality is correlated with rational verifiability: to be actual is to be verifiable by reason. This is the core thesis of the phenomenology of reason.

### § 136\. The First Fundamental Form of Reason-Consciousness: Originarily-Giving "Seeing"

Husserl identifies the first form of reason-consciousness: originary givenness ("seeing"). Asking now what rational verification means, that is, in what the reason-consciousness consists, the intuitive re-presentation of examples and the beginning of essence-analysis performed on them offers us at once several distinctions: For the first, the distinction between positional experiences in which the posited comes to originary givenness, and such in which it does not come to such givenness: hence between "perceiving," "seeing" acts — in a widest sense — and not "perceiving" ones. So a memory-consciousness, say that of a landscape, is not originarily-giving, the landscape is not perceived, as when we really saw it. We thereby in no way want to have said that the memory-consciousness is without a right of its own: only just a "seeing" one it is not. This identifies the first form. The first reason-distinction: between positional experiences in which the posited comes to originary givenness (the "seeing," "perceiving" acts) and those in which it does not (e.g. memory, which gives the landscape but not originarily). So "seeing" (originary givenness) is the first fundamental form of reason-consciousness: the object given itself, in person. (Memory has its own right but is not "seeing.")

He extends to all positional spheres, with the arithmetic example. An analogon of this opposition phenomenology shows for all kinds of positional experiences: We can, for example, in "blind" way predicate that 2 \+ 1 \= 1 \+ 2, we can, however, perform the same judgment also in insightful way. Then the state-of-affairs, the synthetic objectivity corresponding to the judgment-synthesis, is originarily given, grasped in originary way. It is it no more after the living performance of the insight, which immediately darkens into a retentional modification. This extends the distinction. The originary-givenness/non-originary distinction holds for all acts, including judgments: one can judge "2+1=1+2" blindly (without insight) or insightfully (the state-of-affairs originarily given). After the insight darkens (into retention), it is no longer originarily given (though it has a reason-advantage over a thoughtless reproduction). So insight (the originary givenness of the state-of-affairs) is the "seeing" in the categorial sphere.

He locates the difference in the fullness-mode of the sense (not the sense itself). These differences do not concern the pure sense, or proposition; for this is in the members of every such example-pair an identical one and also consciousness-wise as identical at any time beholdable. The difference concerns the way how the mere sense, or proposition — which as mere abstractum in the concretion of the consciousness-noema demands a plus of completing moments — is filled or unfilled sense and proposition. This locates the difference. The difference (originary/non-originary) does not concern the sense (identical across the pair, blind and insightful) but the fullness-mode: whether the sense is filled (originarily given) or unfilled. So the sense is the same; the fullness differs. (The fullness, §132, is now seen to be the reason-decisive dimension.)

He specifies the fullness as the originary-giving (bodily) mode. Fullness of the sense alone does not make it out, it depends also on the how of the fulfillment. An experience-way of the sense is the "intuitive," whereby the "meant object as such" is intuitively conscious, and a specially distinguished case is thereby that the intuition-way is precisely the originarily-giving. The sense in the perception of the landscape is perceptively filled, the perceived object with its colors, forms, and so on (so far as they "fall into the perception") is conscious in the way of the "bodily." Similar distinctions we find in all act-spheres. This specifies the fullness. The fullness has a how: the intuitive fullness, and within it the originarily-giving (bodily) fullness. So the perceived landscape is given with bodily fullness (the object itself, in person). The originary fullness is the highest mode of givenness, the "seeing."

He states the reason-character belongs to the positing on the ground of originary fullness, and introduces motivation. In the setting onto the noema we find the character of bodiliness (as originary filledness) fused with the pure sense, and the sense with this character functions now as under-layer of the noematic positing-character, or what here says the same: of the being-character. A specific reason-character is, however, proper to the positing-character as a distinction that essence-wise then and only then accrues to it, when it is positing on the ground of a filled, originarily-giving sense and not merely in general of a sense. Here and in every kind of reason-consciousness the talk of belonging takes on a proper signification. For example: To every bodily-appearing of a thing belongs the positing, it is not only in general one with this appearing, it is with it peculiarly one, it is through it "motivated," and yet again not merely in general, but "rationally motivated." This is the key reason-thesis. The positing-character has a specific reason-character when it posits on the ground of an originarily-giving (filled) sense. The positing is then "rationally motivated" by the originary givenness: the bodily appearing motivates the positing. So reason is the motivation of the positing by the originary givenness: the object's giving-itself motivates (justifies) the positing of it as being. This is the phenomenological account of justification: the originary givenness is the original ground of right (Rechtsgrund) for the positing.

He states the originary givenness is the original ground of right. The same says: The positing has in the originary givenness its original ground of right (Rechtsgrund). In other givenness-ways the ground of right need not be lacking, but lacking is the advantage of the original ground, which in the relative estimation of the grounds of right plays its distinguished role. Likewise "belongs" the positing of the in essence-intuition originarily-given essence or essence-relation precisely to its positing-"matter," the "sense" in its givenness-mode. It is rational and as belief-certainty originally-motivated positing; it has the specific character of the "insightful." If the positing is a blind one, are the word-meanings performed on the ground of a dark and confusedly-conscious act-ground, then necessarily the reason-character of insight is lacking. This states the ground of right. The originary givenness is the original ground of right for the positing (the strongest justification). Other givenness-modes may have a ground of right (memory has some right) but not the original one. Insight (the originary givenness of an essence) is the rational, originally-motivated positing; blind positing lacks the reason-character. So reason grades the positings by their ground of right, the originary givenness being the highest.

He defines evidence as the unity of a reason-positing with its motivating ground. Insight, in general evidence (Evidenz) is thus a quite distinguished occurrence; according to its "core" it is the unity of a reason-positing with the it essence-wise motivating, whereby this whole situation can be understood noetically, but also noematically. Preferably the talk of motivation fits the relation between the (noetic) positing and the noematic proposition in its way of filledness. Immediately understandable is the expression "evident proposition" in its noematic signification. This defines evidence. Evidence is the unity of a reason-positing with its motivating ground (the originary givenness). It has a noetic and noematic side: noetically, the evident positing; noematically, the evident proposition (the proposition in its filled mode). So evidence is the rational positing motivated by the originary givenness — the having of the object itself as the ground of the positing. (This is Husserl's mature concept of evidence: not a feeling but the originary self-givenness of the object motivating the positing.)

He notes the double sense of "evidence" and of "fulfillment." The double sense of the word evidence in its application now to noetic characters, or full acts (for example evidence of the judging), now to noematic propositions (for example evident logical judgment, evident assertion-proposition) is a case of the general and necessary ambiguities of the expressions relating to moments of the correlation between noesis and noema. The phenomenological demonstration of their source makes them harmless and lets even their indispensability be recognized. It is still to be remarked that the talk of fulfillment has still a double sense lying in a quite other direction: Once it is "fulfillment of the intention," as a character that the actual thesis takes on through the special mode of the sense; the other time it is precisely the peculiarity of this mode itself, or the peculiarity of the relevant sense, to harbor in itself a "fullness" that rationally motivates. This clarifies the ambiguities. "Evidence" is noetic (the evident act) or noematic (the evident proposition). "Fulfillment" is either the character the thesis takes on (the intention fulfilled) or the fullness of the sense itself. These ambiguities are harmless once their source (the noesis-noema correlation) is seen. So §136 establishes evidence as the rational positing motivated by originary givenness — the first fundamental form of reason.

### § 137\. Evidence and Insight. "Originary" and "Pure," Assertoric and Apodictic Evidence

Husserl distinguishes evidence (assertoric seeing) from insight (apodictic seeing). The above-used example-pairs illustrate at the same time a second and third essential distinction. What we ordinarily call evidence and insight (or insightful-seeing), that is a positional doxic and thereby adequate-giving consciousness, which "excludes otherwise-being"; the thesis is through the adequate givenness in quite exceptional way motivated and in the highest sense act of "reason." That illustrates to us the arithmetic example. In that of the landscape we have indeed a seeing, but not an evidence in the ordinary pregnant word-sense, an "insight." More exactly considered, we notice at the contrasted examples a double difference: In the one example it is a matter of essence, in the other of individual; secondly the originary givenness in the eidetic example is an adequate, in the example from the experience-sphere an inadequate one. This distinguishes two differences. (i) Essence vs. individual: the arithmetic example is of an essence (the state-of-affairs 2+1=1+2), the landscape of an individual. (ii) Adequate vs. inadequate: the eidetic givenness is adequate (complete), the perceptual inadequate (one-sided). These two differences (which can cross) determine the kinds of evidence.

He states the essential insight: reason-consciousness is one highest genus, with seeing as a species. As regards the former difference, it is phenomenologically to be stated that the so-to-speak "assertoric" seeing of an individual, for example the "becoming-aware" of a thing or of an individual state-of-affairs, in its reason-character essentially distinguishes itself from an "apodictic" seeing, from the insightful-seeing of an essence or essence-relation; likewise, however, also from the modification of this insightful-seeing, which is performed by mixture of both, namely in the case of the application of an insight to an assertorically-seen and in general in the cognition of the necessity of the so-being of a posited individual. This distinguishes assertoric from apodictic seeing. Assertoric seeing: the seeing of an individual (a thing, an individual fact) — given but contingent. Apodictic seeing (insight): the insightful-seeing of an essence (a necessity) — given and necessary. And there is a mixed case: applying an insight to an assertorically-seen individual (cognizing the necessity of the so-being of an individual). So evidence divides into assertoric (of individuals) and apodictic (of essences, necessities).

He fixes the terminology: evidence (the genus), originary evidence, assertoric/apodictic, pure/impure. Evidence and insight are, in the ordinary pregnant sense, understood synonymously: as apodictic insightful-seeing. We want to separate the two words terminologically. We need throughout a more general word that in its signification spans the assertoric seeing and the apodictic insightful-seeing. It is to be regarded as a phenomenological cognition of greatest importance that both are really of one essence-genus and that, still more generally grasped, reason-consciousness in general designates a highest genus of thetic modalities, in which precisely the on originary givenness related "seeing" (in the extremely-widened sense) makes up a firmly-bounded kind. We choose for the most general concept the word evidence; for every through a motivation-relation onto originarity of the givenness characterized reason-thesis the expression originary evidence offers itself. It would further be between assertoric and apodictic evidence to distinguish and to the word insight to leave the special designation of this apodicticity. In further consequence pure insight and impure (for example cognition of the necessity of a factual, whose being itself need not even be evident) would be opposed; and likewise quite generally pure and impure evidence. This fixes the terminology. Evidence is the highest genus (all reason-consciousness). Originary evidence: the reason-thesis motivated by originary givenness. Assertoric evidence (of individuals) vs. apodictic evidence \= insight (of essences/necessities). Pure insight (the necessity and its ground both given) vs. impure (the necessity of a factual whose being is not itself evident). And pure vs. impure evidence generally. So the kinds of evidence are systematically distinguished. (The footnote-level point: formal/analytic vs. material/synthetic-a priori evidence is also flagged.)

### § 138\. Adequate and Inadequate Evidence

Husserl takes up the second difference: adequate vs. inadequate givenness, with the thing-perception. Let us now take regard to the second above-indicated difference of the evidence, which hangs together with that of adequate and inadequate givenness and gives us at the same time occasion to describe a distinguished type of "impure" evidence. The positing on the ground of the bodily appearance of the thing is indeed a rational one, but the appearance is always only a one-sided, "imperfect" appearance; as bodily conscious stands there not only the "properly" appearing, but simply this thing itself, the whole according to the total, although only one-sidedly intuitive and moreover manifoldly indeterminate sense. Thereby the "properly" appearing is not to be separated from the thing as a thing for itself; it forms its sense-correlate in the full thing-sense a non-self-sufficient part, which can have sense-unity and \-self-sufficiency only in a whole that necessarily harbors in itself empty-components and indeterminacy-components. This takes up the second difference. Thing-perception is rational but inadequate: the thing appears only one-sidedly (the front), yet the whole thing is meant (with empty and indeterminate components — the back, the inside). The properly-appearing (the front) is a non-self-sufficient part of the full thing-sense, which necessarily includes empty components. So thing-perception is essentially inadequate (one-sided, with empty horizons).

He states the essential law: a thing can appear only inadequately, so no thing-positing is final. In principle can a thing-real, a being of such sense, in a closed appearance only "inadequately" appear. With this hangs essence-wise together that no on such an inadequately-giving appearance based reason-positing can be "final," none "unsurpassable"; that none in its singularization is equivalent with the simple: "The thing is actual," but only equivalent with the: "It is actual" — presupposed that the progress of experience does not bring "stronger reason-motives," which present the original positing as one in the further connection "to be crossed-out." Rationally motivated is the positing thereby only through the appearance (the imperfectly-filled perception-sense) in and for itself, in its singularization considered. This is the key law. Because the thing appears only inadequately, no single thing-positing is final or unsurpassable: it is equivalent only to "it is actual (so far)," presupposing that further experience does not bring stronger motives to cross it out. So thing-positing is always provisional, defeasible: the single appearance motivates the positing only in its singularization, and further experience can overturn it. (This is the defeasibility of perceptual evidence, in contrast to the finality of adequate/apodictic evidence.)

He states the task of the phenomenology of reason in the inadequate sphere: the harmonious fulfillment and the discrepancy. The phenomenology of reason in the sphere of the in principle only inadequately to be given being-kinds (the transcendences in the sense of realities) has thus to study the various in this sphere a priori prefigured occurrences. It has to bring to clarity how the inadequate givenness-consciousness, how the one-sided appearing in continuous progress to ever-new, continuously into-one-another-passing appearances with one and the same determinable X behaves, what essence-possibilities here result; how here on the one hand a progress of the experiences is possible and constantly through the continuously fore-lying reason-positings rationally motivated: precisely the experience-course in which the empty-places of the preceding appearances fill themselves, the indeterminacies determine themselves more closely, and so constantly in the way of thoroughgoing unanimous fulfillment with its steadily self-increasing reason-power. On the other hand are the opposed possibilities to lay clear, the cases of fusions or polythetic syntheses of the discrepancy, the "other-determination" of the constantly as the same conscious X — other than it the original sense-giving of earlier perception-course together with its sense suffers crossing-out; how under circumstances the whole perception so to speak explodes and falls apart into "conflicting thing-apprehensions," thing-onsets; how the theses of these onsets cancel themselves and in this cancellation peculiarly modify themselves; or how the one thesis, unmodified remaining, conditions the crossing-out of the "counter-thesis." This states the task. The phenomenology of reason studies, in the inadequate (thing) sphere: (i) the harmonious fulfillment — the experience-course in which empty places fill, indeterminacies determine, with steadily increasing reason-power (the confirmation of the positing); and (ii) the discrepancy — the cases where further experience conflicts, the X is "other-determined," the perception "explodes" into conflicting apprehensions, and the original positing is "crossed out." So reason works through the ongoing confirmation or refutation of the thing-positing in the flow of experience.

He notes the weights of positings and the essence-analysis of motivation. More closely are also to be studied the peculiar modifications that the original reason-positings experience thereby, that they in the progress of unanimous fulfillment experience a positive phenomenological increase with respect to their motivating "power," that they constantly gain in "weight," hence indeed constantly and essentially contain weight, but a gradually different one. There are further the other possibilities to analyze thereupon, how the weight of positings suffers through "counter-motives," how they in doubt mutually "hold the balance," how a positing in the competition with one of "stronger weight" is "outweighed," "given up," and so on. To all that are of course the for the changes in the positing-characters essence-wise determining occurrences in the sense, as the belonging positing-matter, to be subjected to a comprehensive essence-analysis (for example the occurrences of the "conflict" or "competition" of appearances). For here as everywhere in the phenomenological sphere there are no accidents, no facticities, all is essence-wise determinedly motivated. This describes the weights. The reason-positings have "weight" (motivating power), which increases with unanimous fulfillment and is diminished by counter-motives; positings can balance (doubt), be outweighed, given up. All this is essence-wise motivated (no accidents). So reason is a graded, dynamic affair of weights, confirmations, and crossings-out, all essence-lawfully determined.

He states the universal correlation: to every region and category corresponds a fundamental type of originary evidence. Every region and category of pretended objects corresponds phenomenologically not only a fundamental kind of senses, or propositions, but also a fundamental kind of originarily-giving consciousness of such senses and belonging to it a fundamental type of originary evidence, which is essence-wise motivated through so-constituted originary givenness. Every such evidence — the word in our widened sense understood — is either adequate, in principle no more to be "strengthened" or to be "weakened," hence without graduality of a weight; or it is inadequate \[and thus capable of strengthening and weakening, of increase and decrease of weight\]. This states the universal correlation. To every region/category corresponds a fundamental type of originary evidence (the way objects of that region give themselves). And evidence is either adequate (final, without graduality — the eidetic and immanent spheres) or inadequate (defeasible, graded by weight — the thing/transcendent sphere). So the kinds of evidence are correlated with the regions: each region has its own evidence-type, adequate or inadequate. This grounds the regional theory of reason: each being-region is constituted through its own type of rational givenness.

He states that the evidence-type depends on the genus, so demanding the wrong perfection is countersense. Whether in a sphere this or that evidence-kind is possible depends on its genus-type; it is thus a priori prefigured, and the perfection that in one sphere (for example that of the essence-relations) belongs to evidence, to demand in other spheres that essence-wise exclude it, is countersense. This is an important methodological point. Whether adequate or inadequate evidence is possible in a sphere depends on the genus of objects in it (a priori). So it is countersense to demand of one sphere the perfection proper to another: e.g. to demand of thing-perception the adequacy (finality) proper to essence-insight, or to fault thing-perception for being inadequate, is to mistake an essential necessity for a defect. (This recalls §79: each kind of being has its own essentially necessary mode of givenness; to treat its peculiarity as a defect is countersense.) So the phenomenology of reason must respect the proper evidence-type of each region.

He notes the transference of "adequate"/"inadequate" from the givenness to the reason-positings. It is still to be remarked that we the original, on the givenness-mode related signification of the concepts "adequate" and "inadequate" onto the through them founded essence-peculiarities of the reason-positings themselves, precisely by virtue of this connection, may transfer — one of those unavoidable equivocations through transference, which are harmless as soon as one has recognized them as such, has fully-consciously distinguished the original and the derived. This closes §138. The terms "adequate"/"inadequate" apply primarily to the givenness (the adequate or inadequate appearing) and derivatively to the reason-positings founded on them (the adequate or inadequate evidence). This transference is a harmless equivocation once recognized. So §138 establishes the fundamental division of evidence into adequate (final, non-graded — the eidetic and immanent spheres) and inadequate (defeasible, graded by weight — the thing/transcendent sphere), correlated with the regions. §139 develops the interweaving of all the kinds of reason (theoretical, axiological, practical truth), leading toward the constitution of the regions and the thing as a Kantian Idea.

### § 139\. Interweavings of All Kinds of Reason. Theoretical, Axiological, and Practical Truth

Husserl states that a positing has its right when rational, and the proposition is correlatively justified. A positing, of whatever quality, has according to the so-far carried-out, as positing of its sense, its right when it is rational; the reason-character is precisely itself the character of rightness, which accrues to it essence-wise, hence not as accidental fact under the accidental circumstances of a factually-positing ego. Correlatively the proposition too is called justified: in the reason-consciousness it stands equipped with the noematic right-character, which again essence-wise belongs to the proposition as the so-qualified noematic thesis and this sense-matter. More exactly spoken, "belongs" to it a so-constituted fullness, which on its side grounds the reason-distinction of the thesis. This states the right-character. A positing is right (justified) when rational; the reason-character is the character of rightness, essence-wise (not an accident of a factual ego). Correlatively, the proposition is justified (bears the noematic right-character), grounded in its fullness. So rightness is an essence-character of the rational positing, not a contingent fact.

He distinguishes a proposition's having right in itself from something speaking for it. The proposition has here its right in itself. There can, however, also "something speak for a proposition," it can, without "itself" being rational, yet have part in reason. We recall, to stay in the doxic sphere, the peculiar connection of the doxic modalities with the Urdoxa (back-reference to §104): to it they all point back. Considering on the other hand the reason-characters belonging to these modalities, the thought presses from the start that they all, however different otherwise the matters and motivation-situations may be, point back so to speak to an Urreason-character that belongs to the domain of the Urbelief: to the case of the originary and finally perfect evidence. It becomes noticeable that between both kinds of pointing-back deep-lying essence-connections exist. This distinguishes two cases. (i) A proposition has its right in itself (it is rational). (ii) Something "speaks for" a proposition (it is not itself rational but has part in reason). And the reason-characters of the modalities point back to an Urreason-character of the Urbelief (the case of perfect evidence). So all reason points back to the Urdoxa's perfect evidence.

He gives the conjecture example and announces the back-running of all lines to the Urbelief. To indicate only the following: A conjecture can in itself be characterized as rational. Following the in it lying back-reference onto the corresponding Urbelief, and making this our own in the form of an "assuming," then "something speaks for this." Not the belief itself, simply, is characterized as rational, although it has part in reason. We see, there are here further reason-theoretical distinctions and on them related researches necessary. Essence-connections lift themselves off between the different qualities with the to them peculiar reason-characters, and indeed mutual ones; and finally all lines run back to the Urbelief and its Urreason, or to the "truth." This announces the back-running. A rational conjecture points back to the Urbelief: "something speaks for" the corresponding belief (though the belief is not itself rational). So all reason-lines run back to the Urbelief and its Urreason — to truth. So truth is the ultimate correlate, grounding all reason.

He defines truth as the correlate of the perfect reason-character of the Urdoxa. Truth is obviously the correlate of the perfect reason-character of the Urdoxa, the belief-certainty. The expressions: "An urdoxic proposition, say an assertion-proposition, is true" and: "To the corresponding belief, judging, accrues the perfect reason-character" — are equivalent correlates. Naturally there is no talk of the fact of an experience and of a judging one, although it is eidetically self-evident that the truth actually given can only be in an actual evidence-consciousness, and so also the truth of this self-evidence itself. This defines truth. Truth is the correlate of the perfect reason-character of the Urdoxa (belief-certainty): "the proposition is true" ≡ "the corresponding belief has the perfect reason-character." So truth is the noematic correlate of perfect evidence. (Not a fact about an experience, but an eidetic correlation — though truth is actually given only in an actual evidence-consciousness.) This is Husserl's evidence-theory of truth: truth is what is given in perfect evidence.

He relates modal evidence to urdoxic evidence of changed sense. Lacking us the urdoxic evidence, that of belief-certainty, then can, we say, for its sense-content "S is p" a doxic modality be evident, say the conjecture "S might be p." This modal evidence is obviously equivalent and necessarily connected with an urdoxic evidence of changed sense, namely with the evidence, or with the truth: "That S is p, is conjecturable (probable)"; on the other hand also with the truth: "For that S is p, something speaks"; and again: "For that S is p true, something speaks," and so on. This relates modal to urdoxic evidence. The evidence of a conjecture ("S might be p") is equivalent to an urdoxic evidence of changed sense ("That S is p is probable" — now a certainty about the probability). So modal evidence converts into urdoxic evidence (the modality objectified into a being, §105). So all evidence reduces to urdoxic evidence (truth).

He extends evidence to all thetic spheres and the relations between them. Evidence is, however, by no means a mere title for such reason-occurrences in the belief-sphere (and even only in that of the predicative judgment), but for all thetic spheres and in particular also for the significant reason-relations running between them. That concerns thus the highly difficult and wide-encompassing problem-groups of reason in the sphere of the emotional and will-theses, as well as the interweavings of the same with the "theoretical," that is, doxic reason. The "theoretical" or "doxological" truth, or evidence, has its parallel in the "axiological and practical truth, or evidence," whereby the "truths" of the latter title come to expression and cognition in doxological truths, namely in specifically logical (apophantic) ones. This extends evidence universally. Evidence is in all thetic spheres (doxic, emotional, volitional) and in the relations between them. So there is theoretical (doxological) truth/evidence, and parallel **axiological** truth/evidence (of values) and **practical** truth/evidence (of the will/ought). These value- and will-truths come to expression in doxological (logical, apophantic) truths (by the doxic universality, §117). So there are three parallel kinds of truth — theoretical, axiological, practical — all expressed in doxic (logical) truths. (The footnote credits Brentano's On the Origin of Moral Cognition (1889) as the first thrust in this direction.)

He states the foundational role of the doxic researches and the priority of the doxic. It needs not to be said that for the treatment of these problems investigations of the kind must be foundational, as we above attempted to take in hand: concerning the essence-relations that connect the doxic theses with all other positing-kinds, those of the emotional and will, and again those that lead all doxic modalities back to the Urdoxa. Precisely thereby it is also from last grounds to be made understandable why the belief-certainty and correspondingly the truth plays such a very predominant role in all reason; a role that, by the way, at the same time makes it self-evident that the problems of reason in the doxic sphere must, with respect to the solution, precede those of the axiological and practical reason. This states the priority. The doxic researches (the relation of doxic to other theses, and of the modalities to the Urdoxa) are foundational. They explain why belief-certainty and truth predominate in all reason. And the problems of doxic reason must precede those of axiological and practical reason. So the theory of doxic reason (truth, evidence) is the foundation of the theory of value- and will-reason. This sets the order of inquiry.

### § 140\. Confirmation. Justification without Evidence. Equivalence of the Positional and Neutral Insight

Husserl takes up the coincidence (Deckung) between acts of the same sense but different reason-value. Further studies are required with respect to the problems that the connections of the "coincidence" (Deckung) offer us, which (to name only one distinguished case) between acts of the same sense and proposition but of different reason-value are essence-wise to be established. There can, for example, an evident act and a non-evident one come to coincidence, whereby in the transition from the latter to the former this takes on the character of the verifying, that of the self-verifying. The insightful positing of the one functions as "confirming" for the non-insightful of the other. The "proposition" "verifies" or also "confirms" itself, the imperfect givenness-mode transforms itself into the perfect. This introduces confirmation. An evident and a non-evident act of the same sense can coincide; in the transition, the non-evident becomes self-verifying, the evident confirming. So the proposition confirms itself (the imperfect givenness becoming perfect). This is confirmation (Bewährung): the verification of a positing by bringing it to evidence.

He states that for each genus the forms of confirmation must be clarified, and that not every imperfect evidence terminates in originary evidence. How this process looks, can look, is prefigured through the essence of the relevant positing-kinds, or through the essence of the respective propositions in their perfect fulfillment. For every genus of propositions the forms of in-principle-possible confirmation must be phenomenologically laid clear. If the positing is not unreasonable, then from its essence motivated possibilities are to be taken for that and how it can be transferred into a confirming actual reason-positing. It is to be seen that not every imperfect evidence prescribes thereby a fulfillment-course that terminates in a corresponding originary evidence, in one of the same sense; on the contrary, through certain evidence-kinds such a so-to-speak originary confirmation is in principle excluded. This holds, for example, for the back-memory and in a certain way for every memory in general and likewise essence-wise for the empathy, to which we in the next book order a fundamental kind of evidence. This is an important qualification. For each genus, the forms of confirmation must be clarified. And not every imperfect evidence can terminate in originary evidence of the same sense: for memory and empathy, originary confirmation is in principle excluded (one cannot make a memory into a perception of the same past, nor an empathy into an originary having of the other's experience). So some evidence-kinds (memory, empathy) are essentially non-originary, incapable of originary confirmation. (Empathy and its evidence are deferred to Book Two.)

He distinguishes the motivated possibility from the empty possibility (the writing-desk example). It is still to be noted that the motivated possibility, of which above was the talk, sharply distinguishes itself from the empty possibility: it is determinately motivated through what the proposition, so filled as it is given, encloses in itself. An empty possibility it is that this writing-desk here, on the now-invisible under-side, has ten feet, instead of as in reality four. A motivated possibility is this four-number on the other hand for the determinate perception that I just perform. Motivated is it for every perception in general, that the perception-"circumstances" can change in certain ways, that "in consequence of that" the perception can pass over in corresponding ways into perception-series, into determinately-constituted ones, which are prefigured through the sense of my perception, and which fulfill it, confirm its positing. This distinguishes the possibilities. The motivated possibility is determinately prefigured by the proposition as given (the four feet are motivated by my actual perception of the desk; the further perceptions that would confirm it are prefigured by its sense). The empty possibility is unmotivated (the ten feet — a bare logical possibility, not motivated by the perception). So the motivated possibility is the prefigured confirmation-course; the empty possibility is mere logical conceivability. (This is one of the most essential equivocations of "possibility.")

He distinguishes two cases of the bare possibility of verification, and states the equivalence of positional and neutral insight for essences. With respect to the "empty" or "mere" possibility of the verification, two cases are further to be distinguished: Either the possibility coincides with the actuality, namely so that the insightful-seeing of the possibility eo ipso carries with itself the originary givenness-consciousness and reason-consciousness; or this is not the case. The latter holds in the just-used example. Actual experience and not merely a running-through of "possible" perceptions in the re-presentation delivers a real verification of positings that go onto the Real, say of existence-positings of nature-processes. On the contrary, in every case of an essence-positing, or essence-proposition, the intuitive re-presentation of its perfect fulfillment is equivalent with the fulfillment itself, just as a priori the intuitive re-presentation, indeed the mere phantasy of an essence-connection and the insight into the same are "equivalent," that is, the one passes into the other through mere attitude-change, and the possibility of this mutual transference is not an accidental, but an essence-necessary one. This states the key equivalence. For Real (existence) positings, only actual experience verifies (running through possible perceptions in phantasy does not verify the existence). But for essence-positings, the intuitive re-presentation (even the mere phantasy) of the fulfillment is equivalent to the fulfillment itself: the phantasy of an essence-connection and the insight into it are equivalent (one passes into the other by attitude-change). So essence-insight can be won from phantasy (§70: fiction is the vital element of phenomenology), but existence-cognition requires actual experience. This is the deep ground of why eidetic phenomenology works from phantasy while empirical science needs experience.

### § 141\. Immediate and Mediate Rational Positing. Mediate Evidence

Husserl states that all mediate grounding leads back to immediate, the Ursource of all right being originary evidence. As is known, all mediate grounding leads back to immediate. The Ursource of all right lies, with respect to all object-domains and on them related positings, in the immediate, and more narrowly bounded, in the originary evidence, or in the it motivating originary givenness. From this source, however, can in different ways indirectly be drawn, from it the reason-value of a positing that in itself has no evidence be derived or, if it is immediate, strengthened and confirmed. This states the principle. All mediate grounding leads back to immediate evidence; the Ursource of all right is the originary evidence (the originary givenness). From it, the reason-value of a non-evident positing can be derived (mediate grounding) or an immediate positing strengthened. So originary evidence is the ultimate source of all justification.

He examines the memory-case: memory has a relative, derived right, leading back to perception. Consider the latter case. Let us indicate in an example the difficult problems that concern the relation of the non-evident immediate reason-positings to the originary evidence. In a certain way original, immediate right has every clear memory: in and for itself considered, it "weighs" something, whether much or little, it has a "weight." It has, however, only a relative and imperfect right. With respect to what it re-presents, say a past, there lies in it a relation to the actual present. It posits the past and posits necessarily a horizon with, even if in vague, dark, indeterminate way; brought to clarity and thetic distinctness, this would have to let itself explicate in a connection of thetically-performed memories, which would terminate in actual perceptions, in the actual hic et nunc. The same holds for every kind of memory in our widest, on all time-modes related sense. This examines memory. Memory has a relative, imperfect right (a "weight"). It posits the past with a horizon that, made distinct, would explicate into a chain of memories terminating in actual perception (the present hic et nunc). So memory's right leads back to perception: the memory-chain terminates in the perceptual present.

He states that something of the light of perception radiates back on the memory-chain. With every progress from memory to memory into the clarifying memory-connection, whose last end reaches into the perception-present, the memory strengthens itself. The strengthening is in a certain way a mutual one, the memory-weights are functionally dependent on one another, every memory in the connection has a growing power with the widening of the same, a greater one than it would have in the narrower connection or singled-out. But if the explication is carried through to the actual now, then something radiates from the light of perception and its evidence back onto the whole series. One could even say: In the hidden springs the reasonableness, the right-character of the memory, from the through all confusion and darkness effective power of perception, be it also "out of performance." This is a striking image. The memory-chain, terminating in actual perception, receives a back-radiation of the light of perception's evidence. So the right of memory springs (hiddenly) from the power of perception (even when perception is "out of performance"). So perception is the ultimate ground of memory's right.

He notes memory's own kind of inadequacy (the memory-image "explodes"). In any case, however, such confirmation is needed, so that clearly emerge what there actually bears the mediate reflection of the perception-right. The memory has its own kind of inadequacy in that with "really remembered" non-remembered can mix, or that different memories can interpenetrate and give themselves out as the unity of one memory, while at the actualizing unfolding of its horizon the belonging memory-series separate, and indeed so that the unitary memory-image "explodes," goes apart into a plurality of mutually-incompatible memory-intuitions: whereby similar occurrences would be to describe, as which we (in obviously very generalization-capable way) for perceptions occasionally indicated (back-reference to §138). This notes memory's inadequacy. Memory has its own inadequacy: false memories can mix with true, different memories interpenetrate, and on unfolding the horizon the memory "explodes" into incompatible memory-intuitions (paralleling the perceptual "explosion," §138). So memory is inadequate, defeasible, like perception, and needs confirmation.

He states the general result: all mediate reason leads back to evidence, but only originary evidence is the Ursource. This all serve for the exemplary indication of great and important problem-groups of the "strengthening" and "confirmation" of immediate reason-positings (as also for the illustration of the separation of the reason-positings into pure and impure, unmixed and mixed); above all, however, one grasps here a sense in which the proposition holds, that all mediate reason-positing, and in further consequence all predicative and conceptual reason-cognition, leads back to evidence. Well-understood, only the originary evidence is "original" right-source, and is for example the reason-positing of the memory and so of all reproductive acts, among them also of the empathy, not original and in certain kinds "derived." This states the result. All mediate reason-positing (all predicative cognition) leads back to evidence. But only originary evidence is the original right-source; the reason-positing of memory, reproduction, empathy is derived (not original). So there is a hierarchy: originary evidence (original) grounds the derived evidence of memory, etc.

He introduces mediate evidence proper (the evident synthetic connection). It can, however, also in quite other forms be drawn from the source of the originary givenness. One such form came occasionally already to indication: the weakening of the reason-values in the steady transition from the living evidence to the non-evidence. But let now be pointed to an essentially other group of cases, where a proposition is mediately, in an in all steps evident synthetic connection, related to immediately-evident grounds. There grows up thereby a new general type of reason-positings, phenomenologically of other reason-character than the immediate evidence. So we have also here a kind of derived, "mediate evidence" — that on which usually with the expression exclusively is aimed. This introduces mediate evidence. Besides the weakening of evidence (the transition to non-evidence), there is mediate evidence proper: a proposition related to immediately-evident grounds through an evident synthetic connection (a chain of evident steps). This is what "mediate evidence" usually means (the evidence of a conclusion derived through evident inference). So mediate evidence is the evidence of the conclusion of an evident proof.

He states the structure and the task of the phenomenological reason-theory of mediate grounding. According to its essence, this derived evidence-character can only appear in the end-member of a from immediate evidences proceeding, in different forms running, and in all further steps by evidences borne positing-connection; whereby these evidences are partly immediate, partly already derived; partly insightful, partly non-insightful, originary or non-originary. With this a new field of the phenomenological reason-theory is designated. It is here the task in noetic and noematic respect to study the general as the special essence-occurrences of the reason in the mediate grounding, verifying of every kind and form and in all thetic spheres, the different "principles" of such verification, which are for example essentially other-kind, according as it is a matter of immanent or transcendent, adequately or inadequately to be given objectivities, to lead back to their phenomenological origins and from these, under regard to all participating phenomenological layers, to make "understandable." This states the task. Mediate evidence (the conclusion of a proof) rests on a chain of evidences (immediate or derived, insightful or not). The phenomenological reason-theory must study the essence-occurrences of mediate grounding (verification) in all spheres, the different "principles" of verification (different for immanent/transcendent, adequate/inadequate objectivities), tracing them to their phenomenological origins. So the theory of mediate reason (inference, proof) is a field of phenomenological reason-theory, to be grounded in the immediate evidences. §142 draws the great equivalence between truly-being and the idea of a possible perfect originary givenness, leading to the constitution of the regions and the thing as a Kantian Idea.

---

# Commentary on Husserl, *Ideas I* — File 42 (§§142–144)

### § 142\. Rational Thesis and Being

Husserl states that with the general essence-understanding of reason comes the clarification of the correlation of being, truth, reason, consciousness. With the general essence-understanding of reason, which is the goal of the indicated investigation-groups — of reason in the very widest, onto all positing-kinds, also onto the axiological and practical, extended sense — must eo ipso the general clarification of the essence-correlations connecting the Idea of the truly-being with the Ideas truth, reason, consciousness be won. This states the goal. Understanding reason (in the widest sense, including value and will) yields the clarification of the essence-correlation between truly-being and truth, reason, consciousness. So the theory of reason culminates in the correlation of being and consciousness.

He states the great equivalence: truly-being and being-positable in a perfect rational thesis. A general insight results hereby very soon, namely, that not merely "truly-being object" and "rationally to be posited" are equivalent correlates, but also "truly-being" and in an original perfect reason-thesis to be posited object. To this reason-thesis the object would be not incompletely, not merely "one-sidedly" given. The sense underlying it as matter would for the determinable X according to no apprehension-wise prefigured side leave anything "open": no determinability that were not yet firm determinateness, no sense that were not fully-determined, closed. Since the reason-thesis shall be an original one, it must have its reason-ground in the originary givenness of the in the full sense determinate: The X is not only in full determinateness meant, but in precisely this originarily given. This states the great equivalence. "Truly-being" is equivalent not just to "rationally positable" but to "positable in an original, perfect reason-thesis" — a positing in which the object is given completely, with nothing left open (the X fully determinate, originarily given). So to be truly is to be perfectly, originarily, adequately givable. (This is the equivalence of being and perfect evidence.)

He states the equivalence as a principle: to every truly-being object corresponds the Idea of a possible consciousness giving it perfectly adequately. The indicated equivalence says now: In principle corresponds (in the a priori of the unconditioned essence-generality) to every "truly-being" object the Idea of a possible consciousness, in which the object itself originarily and thereby perfectly adequately is graspable. Conversely, when this possibility is guaranteed, the object is eo ipso truly-being. This is the master-principle of the phenomenology of reason. To every truly-being object corresponds, by essential law, the Idea of a possible consciousness in which the object is originarily and perfectly adequately given. And conversely: if such a possible consciousness is guaranteed, the object truly is. So being and the ideal possibility of perfect adequate givenness are strict correlates. This is the deepest formulation of Husserl's transcendental idealism: to be is to be the correlate of a possible perfect evidence. (Not that the object is reducible to consciousness, but that being and perfect givability are essentially equivalent.)

He states the regional prefiguration: every apprehension-category prefigures the forms of givenness. Of special significance is here still the following: In the essence of every apprehension-category (which is the correlate of every object-category) is determinately prefigured, which formations of concrete, perfect or imperfect apprehensions of objects of such category are possible. Again is essence-wise prefigured for every imperfect apprehension, how it is to be perfected, how its sense is to be completed, through intuition to be filled, and how the intuition further is to be enriched. This states the regional prefiguration. Every object-category has a correlative apprehension-category, which prefigures the possible apprehensions (perfect or imperfect) of objects of that category, and how an imperfect apprehension is to be completed (filled, enriched). So each region prefigures the ways its objects can be given and the ways apprehension of them is completed. (Each region has its own "rules" of givenness.)

He states that every category is itself adequately givable and prescribes a generic rule. Every object-category (or every region and every category in our narrower, pregnant sense) is a general essence, that itself in principle is to be brought to adequate givenness. In its adequate givenness it prescribes an insightful general rule for every particular, in manifolds of concrete experiences conscious-becoming object (which experiences here naturally are not as individual singularities, but as essences, as lowest concreta to be taken). It prescribes the rule for the kind how an under-it-standing object according to sense and givenness-mode is to be brought to full determinateness, to adequate originary givenness; through which singled-out or continuously-running consciousness-connections and through which concrete essence-equipment of these connections. This states the regional rule. Every category (region) is itself adequately givable (the essence of "thing in general" can be intuited), and prescribes an insightful general rule for how any object of that region is to be brought to adequate originary givenness. So the region prescribes the constitution-rule for its objects (the way they are to be given). (This is the regional a priori, §149.)

He gives the thing-example: the unseen determinations are spatial (geometry), temporal, material. How much in these short propositions lies will in the closer expositions in the closing chapter (from §149 on) become understandable. Here suffice a short exemplary indication: The unseen determinations of a thing are, that we know in apodictic evidence, like thing-determinations in general, necessarily spatial: that gives a lawful rule for possible spatial completion-ways of the unseen sides of the appearing thing; a rule that, fully unfolded, is called pure geometry. Further thingly determinations are temporal, are material: to them belong new rules for possible (hence not freely-arbitrary) sense-completions and in further consequence for possible thetic intuitions, or appearances. Of what essence-content these can be, under which norms their stuffs, their possible noematic (or noetic) apprehension-characters stand, that too is a priori prefigured. This gives the thing-example. The unseen determinations of a thing are necessarily spatial (the rule: pure geometry prescribes the possible completions of the unseen sides), temporal, material (with their own rules). So the thing-region prescribes a priori the rules (geometry, etc.) for completing the perception of a thing. (The unseen back of the thing must be spatial; geometry rules its possible shapes.)

### § 143\. Adequate Thing-Givenness as Idea in the Kantian Sense

Husserl removes the apparent contradiction with §138 (the thing only inadequately givable). But before we link up to that, an addition is needed, to remove the appearance of contradiction with our earlier presentation (§138). There are, we said, in principle only inadequately-appearing (hence also only inadequately-perceivable) objects. However, the restricting addition is not to be overlooked that we made. We said, inadequately-perceivable in closed appearance. There are objects — and all transcendent objects, all "realities" that the title nature or world spans, belong here — that in no closed consciousness in complete determinateness and in equally-complete intuitiveness can be given. This recalls §138 with its restriction. The thing is inadequately perceivable in any closed (finite) appearance: no single, finite perception gives the thing completely. So a thing can never be adequately given in a closed appearance. (This seemed to conflict with §142's equivalence, which requires adequate givability.)

He states the resolution: the perfect givenness is prefigured as an Idea in the Kantian sense. But as "Idea" (in the Kantian sense) the perfect givenness is nevertheless prefigured — as a in its essence-type absolutely determinate system of endless processes of continuous appearing, or as field of these processes an a priori determinate continuum of appearances with different but determinate dimensions, thoroughly governed by firm essence-lawfulness. This is the resolution and the famous thesis. The perfect givenness of the thing is prefigured as an Idea in the Kantian sense: not as an actual closed perception (impossible) but as an absolutely determinate system of endless processes of continuous appearing — an a priori determinate continuum of appearances, governed by firm essence-lawfulness. So the thing's adequate givenness is the ideal limit of an endless continuum of appearances (the thing as the Idea regulating the infinite perceptual process). This is the "thing as a Kantian Idea": the thing is the ideal pole of an infinite, lawfully-ordered manifold of possible appearances.

He describes the continuum: all-sided infinite, every line a unanimous appearance-connection of the same X. This continuum determines itself more closely as all-sidedly infinite, in all its phases consisting of appearances of the same determinable X, so connectedly ordered and according to essence-content determined, that every arbitrary line of it in the steady traversal yields a unanimous appearance-connection (which itself is to be designated as a unity of movable appearance), in which the one and same constantly given X continuously-unanimously "more closely" and never "otherwise" determines itself. This describes the continuum. The appearance-continuum is all-sidedly infinite (the thing can be approached from infinitely many sides), all its phases being appearances of the same X. It is so ordered that every line through it (every possible perceptual course) yields a unanimous appearance-connection in which the X determines itself "more closely" (never "otherwise" — no conflict). So the Idea of the thing is the infinitely-extended, lawfully-ordered continuum of harmonious appearances of the one X.

He states the Idea is insightfully given though the infinity is not. Is now a closed unity of the traversal, hence a finite, only movable act, by virtue of the all-sided infinity of the continuum not thinkable (that would yield a countersensical finite infinity): so lies yet the Idea of this continuum and the Idea of the through it prefigured perfect givenness insightfully before — insightful as precisely an "Idea" can be insightful, through its essence designating an own insight-type. The Idea of an essence-wise motivated infinity is not itself an infinity; the insight that this infinity in principle cannot be given, excludes not, but rather demands the insightful givenness of the Idea of this infinity. This is the key epistemological point. The infinite continuum cannot be given in a finite act (that would be a countersensical "finite infinity"). But the Idea of the continuum (and of the perfect givenness it prefigures) is insightfully given — with its own insight-type (the insight into a Kantian Idea, §74, §83). So one has genuine insight into the Idea of the infinite continuum, even though the infinity itself is never given. The insight that the infinity cannot be given demands (does not exclude) the insightful givenness of the Idea of it. So the thing as a Kantian Idea is insightfully grasped, though never adequately given. (This resolves the apparent contradiction: the thing is inadequately given in any finite perception, but its perfect givenness is insightfully prefigured as an Idea.)

### § 144\. Actuality and Originarily-Giving Consciousness: Concluding Determinations

Husserl restates the equivalence, now with the two cases (immanent/finite and transcendent/Idea). It remains thus thereby, that the Eidos truly-being is correlatively equivalent with the Eidos adequately-given- and evidently-positable-being — that, however, either in the sense of finite givenness or givenness in the form of an Idea. In the one case the being is "immanent" being, being as closed experience or noematic experience-correlate; in the other case transcendent being, that is, being whose "transcendence" precisely lies in the infinity of the noematic correlate that it as being-"matter" demands. This restates the equivalence with two cases. Truly-being \= adequately-givable. But adequate givability has two forms: (i) finite givenness (immanent being — an experience or its noematic correlate, adequately given in a closed act); (ii) givenness as an Idea (transcendent being — the thing, whose transcendence is the infinity of its noematic correlate, given only as a Kantian Idea). So immanent being is finitely adequately given; transcendent being is given only as an Idea (the infinite continuum). This is the deep difference between immanent and transcendent being (recalling §44, §46): the immanent is adequately given, the transcendent only inadequately (as an Idea).

He states that for adequate immanent givenness, originarily-filled sense and object coincide. Where a giving intuition is adequate and immanent, there coincide not indeed sense and object, but originarily-filled sense and object. The object is precisely that which in the adequate intuition as originary self is grasped, posited, by virtue of the originarity insightful, by virtue of the sense-completeness and complete originary sense-fulfillment absolutely insightful. This states the immanent case. For adequate immanent intuition (e.g. the perception of an experience, §44), the originarily-filled sense and the object coincide: the object is grasped as originary self, completely, absolutely insightfully. So immanent being is given absolutely (the experience itself, with no remainder). (The sense and object do not coincide in general — the sense is the object-in-the-how — but the originarily-filled sense and object coincide in adequate immanent givenness.)

He states the transcendent case: only the Idea of the object is given, an a priori rule for the infinite inadequate experiences. Where the giving intuition is a transcending one, there the objective cannot come to adequate givenness; given can only be the Idea of such an objective, or of its sense and its "epistemic essence," and with it an a priori rule for the precisely lawful infinities of inadequate experiences. This states the transcendent case. For transcendent intuition (thing-perception), the object cannot be adequately given; only the Idea of the object (and an a priori rule for the lawful infinity of inadequate experiences) is given. So transcendent being is given only as an Idea, with a rule prescribing the infinite course of possible experiences. (The thing is the Idea regulating the infinite inadequate perceptions.)

He notes the rule does not determine the actual course, leaving infinitely many possibilities open. On the ground of the respectively-performed experiences and this rule (or the manifold rule-system that it encloses) cannot, of course, unambiguously be taken, how the further experience-course must play off. On the contrary, infinitely many possibilities remain open, which are, however, through the very content-rich a priori regulation according to the type prefigured. The rule-system of geometry determines absolutely-firmly all possible movement-shapes that the piece of here-and-now observed movement could complete, but no single actual movement-course of the really-moving one does it distinguish. This is an important qualification. The a priori rule (geometry, etc.) does not determine the actual course of experience; infinitely many possibilities remain open (prefigured only as to type). Geometry determines all possible completions of an observed movement, but not which actual one occurs. So the a priori rule prescribes the possibilities (the type), not the actuality; the actual course is settled only by further actual experience.

He states the move to empirical-scientific reason (the determination of the actual). How the on experience grounding empirical thinking helps further there; how something like scientific determination of thingnesses as experientially-posited unities, which yet enclose infinite ambiguities, becomes possible; how within the thesis of nature the goal of unambiguous determination according to the Idea of the nature-object, the nature-process, and so on (which as Idea of an individually-unique is fully-determinate), can be reached: that belongs in a new research-layer. It belongs in the phenomenology of the specifically experiencing, and in particular of the physical, psychological, in general natural-scientific reason, which the ontological and noetic rules that belong to the experience-science as such, leads back to their phenomenological sources. That says, however, that it seeks the phenomenological layers, the noetic and noematic, into which the content of these rules embeds itself, and eidetically researches them. This states the move to scientific reason. The a priori rule leaves the actual course open; empirical-scientific thinking determines the actual (the nature-object, fully determinate as the Idea of an individual unique). This belongs to the phenomenology of natural-scientific reason (physical, psychological), which traces the rules of experience-science back to their phenomenological sources. So there is a further layer: the phenomenology of empirical-scientific reason, which grounds the determination of actual nature. (This is the phenomenological grounding of natural science, developed in Book Two and the Krisis.) With §144, the concluding determinations on actuality and originary givenness are reached: truly-being \= adequately givable (finitely, for the immanent; as an Idea, for the transcendent). §145 turns to critical remarks on the phenomenology of evidence, and the closing chapter (§§149ff.) to the constitution of the regions.

---

# Commentary on Husserl, *Ideas I* — File 43 (§§145–147)

### § 145\. Critical Remarks on the Phenomenology of Evidence

Husserl states that the phenomenology of reason presupposes the general phenomenology. From the carried-out considerations it is clear that the phenomenology of reason, the noetics in a pregnant sense, which wants to subject not consciousness in general, but the reason-consciousness to an intuitive research, throughout presupposes the general phenomenology. That — in the realm of positionality — thetic consciousness of every genus stands under norms, is itself a phenomenological fact; the norms are nothing other than essence-laws that relate to certain, according to their kind and form strictly to be analyzed and described noetic-noematic connections. This states the presupposition. The phenomenology of reason (the noetics, the study of reason-consciousness) presupposes the general phenomenology (of consciousness in general). That thetic consciousness stands under norms is a phenomenological fact; the norms are essence-laws about noetic-noematic connections. (The footnote notes that in phantasy and neutrality all thetic occurrences are mirrored "powerlessly" — neutral theses are not confirmable but "quasi"-confirmable, not evident but "as if" evident.)

He notes unreason and absurdity are included, and the doctrine of evidence is a small but fundamental part. Naturally thereby also the "unreason" as negative counterpart of reason is everywhere to be considered, just as the phenomenology of evidence comprises in itself that of its counterpart, the absurdity. The general essence-doctrine of evidence with its on the most general essence-distinctions related analyses forms a relatively small, though fundamental piece of the phenomenology of reason. This places the doctrine. The phenomenology of reason includes unreason and absurdity (the counterparts of reason and evidence). The general doctrine of evidence is a small but fundamental piece of the whole phenomenology of reason.

He states the central polemical thesis: evidence is not a feeling or index but a positing-mode. Evidence is in fact not some consciousness-index that, attached to a judgment (and usually one speaks only at such of evidence), calls to us like a mystical voice from a better world: Here is the truth\! — as if such a voice had to us free spirits something to say and its right-title not to verify. We need no more to set ourselves apart with skepticisms and to weigh doubts of the old type, which no index- and feeling-theory of evidence can overcome: whether not a lying-spirit (the Cartesian fiction) or a fatal change of the factual world-course could bring it about that precisely every false judgment were equipped with this index, this feeling of thought-necessity, of the transcendent ought, and the like. This is the central polemical thesis. Evidence is not a "consciousness-index" or a "feeling" attached to a judgment (a mystical voice saying "here is truth"). The index/feeling-theory cannot answer the skeptic: a lying-spirit (Descartes's evil genius) could attach the feeling to every false judgment. So evidence cannot be a feeling. (This refutes the feeling-theory of evidence, which Husserl held in his early work and now rejects.)

He states the positive account: evidence is a peculiar positing-mode belonging to the essence-constitution of the noema. Going to the study of the phenomena belonging here themselves and in the frame of phenomenological reduction, one recognizes in fullest clarity that it is here a matter of a peculiar positing-mode (hence nothing less than a content somehow attached to the act, an added of whatever kind), which belongs to eidetically-determined essence-constitutions of the noema (for example the mode original insightfulness to the noematic constitution "originarily"-giving essence-intuition). One recognizes then further that again essence-laws regulate the relation of those positional acts that do not have this distinguished constitution to such that have it; that there is, for example, something like consciousness of the "fulfillment of the intention," of the specifically on the thetic characters related justification and confirmation, just as the corresponding counter-characters of the disrighting, disconfirming. This states the positive account. Evidence is a peculiar positing-mode (not an added content/feeling) belonging to the essence-constitution of the noema (e.g. the originary insightfulness of essence-intuition). And essence-laws regulate the relation of non-evident positings to evident ones (the consciousness of fulfillment, confirmation, and the counter-characters of disconfirmation). So evidence is the rational positing-mode motivated by originary givenness (§136), grounded in essence-laws, not a feeling.
