This is the decisive refutation of the feeling-theory: evidence is the originary self-givenness of the object, a structural mode, immune to the lying-spirit (since it is the object's giving-itself, not a contingent feeling).

He states the logical principles require phenomenological clarification, with the law of contradiction. One recognizes in further consequence that the logical principles demand a deep phenomenological clarification, and that for example the law of contradiction leads us back to essence-connections of possible confirmation and possible disconfirmation (or rational crossing-out). In general one wins the insight that it is here everywhere not a matter of accidental facts, but of eidetic occurrences that stand in their eidetic connection, and that thus, what holds in the Eidos, functions for the fact as an absolutely insuperable norm. This states the grounding of logic. The logical principles (the law of contradiction) require phenomenological clarification: the law of contradiction leads back to essence-connections of possible confirmation/disconfirmation (one cannot confirm a contradiction). So logic is grounded in the phenomenology of evidence (the eidetic connections of confirmation), and the Eidos functions as an insuperable norm for the fact. (The footnote answers Wundt's dismissal of phenomenology as "scholasticism" and "A \= A" word-repetition — Husserl notes the analyses do not go beyond the sphere of purely intuitive givenness.)

He notes not every positing can become immediately evident, and all evidence roots in the regional connections. One makes clear to oneself in this phenomenological chapter also that not every positional experience (for example every arbitrary judgment-experience) in the same way, and specially, that not every one immediately can become evident; further that all ways of the reason-positing, all types of immediate or mediate evidence root in phenomenological connections, in which the fundamentally-different object-regions noetic-noematically lay themselves apart. This states two points. Not every positing can become immediately evident (some require mediate grounding). And all evidence-types root in the phenomenological connections in which the object-regions are constituted. So evidence is regionally differentiated: each region has its own evidence-type rooted in its constitution.

He states the central task: the systematic study of the identity-unifications and identifications. It comes in particular to study systematically the continuous identity-unifications and the synthetic identifications in all domains according to their phenomenological constitution. Having first, what is a first that is needed, learned the inner construction of the intentional experiences according to all general structures, the parallelism of these structures, the layerings in the noema, like sense, sense-subject, thetic characters, fullness: so it holds at all synthetic unifications to make fully clear, how with them not merely in general act-connections take place, but connection to the unity of one act. In particular, how identifying unifications are possible, how here and there the determinable X comes to coincidence, how thereby the sense-determinations and their empty-places, that is here their indeterminacy-moments, behave; likewise how the fullnesses, how with this the forms of the confirmation, of the verification, of the progressing cognition in lower and higher consciousness-step come to clarity and analytic insight. This states the task. The central task is to study the identity-unifications (the continuous and synthetic identifications) — how acts unite into the unity of one act, how the determinable X comes to coincidence (the identity-synthesis, §131), how the sense-determinations and fullnesses behave, and how the forms of confirmation and progressing cognition arise. So the constitution of the identical object through the coincidence of X's, and the forms of confirmation, are the central theme. (This is the program of constitutive phenomenology.)

He stresses all this is in the transcendental attitude, and that "object" is a title for essence-connections of consciousness. These and all parallel reason-studies are, however, carried out in the "transcendental," the phenomenological attitude. No judgment that is there passed is a natural judgment that presupposed the thesis of natural actuality as background, and even there not, where phenomenology of the actuality-consciousness, of nature-cognition, of nature-related value-intuition and value-insight is pursued. Everywhere we go after the formations of the noeses and noemata, we design a systematic and eidetic morphology, lift everywhere essence-necessities and essence-possibilities out: the latter as necessary possibilities, that is, unification-forms of the compatibility that are prescribed in the essences and bounded by essence-laws. "Object" is for us everywhere a title for essence-connections of consciousness; it appears first as noematic X, as sense-subject of different essence-types of senses and propositions. It appears further as title "actual object" and is then title for certain eidetically-considered reason-connections, in which the in them sense-wise unitary X receives its rational positing. This is the transcendental result. All reason-study is in the transcendental attitude (no natural judgment, even when studying nature-cognition). "Object" is a title for essence-connections of consciousness: first the noematic X (the sense-subject), then the "actual object" (the title for the reason-connections in which the X receives its rational positing). So the actual object is the index of a system of rational consciousness-connections. (This is the transcendental-idealist thesis: the object is the correlate of a system of constituting consciousness.)

He extends to the modalities and concludes that phenomenology spans the whole world as "world-sense." Just such titles for determinate, eidetically-bounded and in essence-research to be fixed groups of "teleologically" belonging-together consciousness-formations are the expressions "possible object," "probable," "doubtful," and so on. Always again are there the connections others, in their otherness strictly to be described: so for example it is easily to be seen that possibility of the so-and-so determined X verifies itself not merely through originary givenness of this X in its sense-stock, hence through demonstration of the actuality, but that also mere reproductively-founded intimations in the unanimous closing-together can mutually confirm themselves; likewise that doubtfulness verifies itself in conflict-phenomena between modalized intuitions of certain descriptive kind. With this connect the reason-theoretical investigations that relate to the distinction of things, values, practical objectivities, and that go after the for them constituting consciousness-formations. So phenomenology really spans the whole natural world and all the ideal worlds that it switches off: it spans them as "world-sense" through the essence-lawfulnesses that connect object-sense and noema in general with the closed system of the noeses, and specially through the reason-lawful essence-connections whose correlate is "actual object," which thus on its side in each case represents an index for quite determinate systems of teleologically-unitary consciousness-formations. This is the culminating result. The modalities (possible, probable, doubtful) are titles for groups of teleological consciousness-formations. And phenomenology spans the whole world (natural and ideal) as "world-sense": the world is the correlate of the closed system of noeses, and the "actual object" is an index for determinate systems of teleologically-unitary consciousness-formations. So the world is the universal correlate of constituting consciousness, and phenomenology, by switching off the world, recovers it as world-sense (the noematic correlate of the system of reason). This is the transcendental-phenomenological recovery of the world (anticipating the Krisis's life-world). With §145, the Second Chapter (Phenomenology of Reason) closes.

## Third Chapter: Generality-Levels of the Reason-Theoretical Problematic

Husserl announces the descent from the highest generality to the branchings and their connection with the ontologies. Our meditations on the problematic of a phenomenology of reason moved so far in a height of generality that did not let the essential branchings of the problems and their connections with the formal and regional ontologies come forward. In this respect we must try to come closer; only thereby will the full sense of the phenomenological eidetics of reason and the whole richness of its problems disclose itself to us. This announces the Third Chapter. The previous analyses were at the highest generality. Now Husserl descends to the branchings of the reason-problems and their connection with the formal and regional ontologies — disclosing the full richness of the phenomenology of reason. So the closing chapter systematizes the reason-problems by region.

### § 146\. The Most General Problems

Husserl goes back to the sources of the reason-problematic, beginning with intentionality. Going back to the sources of the reason-problematic and following it as systematically as possible into its branchings. The problem-title that spans the whole phenomenology is called intentionality. It expresses precisely the fundamental property of consciousness, all phenomenological problems, even the hyletic, order themselves into it. Thus phenomenology begins with problems of intentionality; but first in generality and without drawing the questions of the actual-(truly-)being of the in consciousness conscious into their circle. This states the starting point. The whole phenomenology is spanned by intentionality (the fundamental property of consciousness, into which all problems, even the hyletic, order themselves). Phenomenology begins with the general problems of intentionality (the structures of consciousness), before drawing in the questions of actuality (the reason-problems). So the order is: first the general structures of intentionality (the Third Part), then the reason-problems (the Fourth Part).

He recalls the order of the inquiry from higher to lower generality. That positional consciousness with its thetic characters in the most general sense can be designated as a "meaning" and as such necessarily stands under the reason-opposition of validity and invalidity, remains out of consideration \[at first\]. To these problems we could now in the last chapters, with regard to the meanwhile-understandable main structures of consciousness, step. Since it is a matter of eidetic beginnings, we performed the analyses naturally in greatest-possible generality. In all eidetic spheres the systematic way goes from higher to lower generality, even if the tracking analysis links up to particulars. We spoke of reason and reason-thesis in general, of originary and derived, of adequate and inadequate evidence, of essence-insight and individual evidence, and the like. This recalls the order. The reason-problems were treated at the highest generality (reason in general, evidence in general). The systematic way goes from higher to lower generality. So now the chapter descends to the particular (regional) reason-problems.

He notes the descriptions presupposed the general structures (sense, proposition, filled proposition). The descriptions that we designed presupposed already a great phenomenological basis, a whole series of difficult distinctions that we had worked out in the chapters on the most general consciousness-structures. Without the concepts sense, proposition, filled proposition (epistemic essence in the talk of the Logical Investigations) one cannot at all come to the radical formulation of any reason-theoretical problem. These concepts presupposed again others and the to them corresponding essence-distinctions: the differences of positionality and neutrality, those of the thetic characters and their matters, the setting-off of the peculiar essence-modifications that do not enter into the Eidos proposition, like for example the attentional modifications, and so on. This states the presuppositions. The reason-descriptions presuppose the general structures: sense, proposition, filled proposition, and behind them positionality/neutrality, thetic characters/matters, the modifications (attention) outside the Eidos proposition. So the phenomenology of reason rests on the whole analysis of the structures of consciousness (the Third Part). And the descriptions of the last chapter are "mere beginnings," meant to secure firm ground for each new layer and formulate its problems.

### § 147\. Problem-Branchings. The Formal Logic, Axiology, and Practics

Husserl states the general phenomenology of reason branches by the structural differences of the theses. The general phenomenology of reason branches, when we take regard to further structural differences that are determining for the reason-characters: to the diversities according to fundamental kinds of the theses, to the differences of simple and founded theses, and to the with these crossing differences of one-membered theses and syntheses. Main groups of reason-problems (evidence-problems) relate to the main genera of theses and the by them essence-wise demanded positing-matters. At first place stand naturally the Urdoxa, the doxic modalities with the to them corresponding being-modalities. This states the branching. The phenomenology of reason branches by the structural differences of theses: the fundamental kinds (doxic, emotional, volitional), simple vs. founded, one-membered vs. synthetic. The main reason-problems relate to the main genera of theses; at first place the Urdoxa and the doxic modalities (with their being-modalities). So the reason-problems are organized by thesis-kind, the doxic being primary.

He states the inquiry leads to the reason-theoretical clarification of formal logic, axiology, and practics. In the pursuit of such reason-theoretical goals one necessarily arrives at the problems of the reason-theoretical clarification of the formal logic and the to it parallel disciplines that I have called formal axiology and practics. This states the goal. The reason-inquiry leads to the reason-theoretical clarification of the three formal disciplines: formal logic, formal axiology, formal practics (§117). So the phenomenology of reason grounds the three formal disciplines.

He recalls the form-doctrines and distinguishes them from the validity-doctrines. Let first be referred to the earlier expositions (back-reference to §§133–134) about the pure form-doctrines of the propositions and specially of the synthetic propositions, related to the predicative doxic synthesis, as well as to the synthetic forms belonging to the doxic modalities, further to the emotional and will-acts. (So for example the forms of the preferring, those of the valuing and willing "for the sake of another," the forms of the axiological "and" and "or.") In these form-doctrines is noematically of synthetic propositions according to their pure form the talk, without that reason-validity or invalidity were in question. They belong thus not yet to the layer of the reason-doctrine. This distinguishes form-doctrine from validity-doctrine. The form-doctrines (§§133–134) treat the pure forms of propositions, without the question of validity. So they are not yet reason-doctrine. The form-doctrine is the under-step; the validity-doctrine (logic, axiology, practics) is built on it.

He states that raising the validity-question puts us in formal logic and its parallels, where the synthetic forms contain a priori conditions of possible validity. But as soon as we raise this question, namely for propositions in general so far as they are thought as exclusively determined through the pure forms, we stand in the formal logic and in the above-named formal parallel disciplines, which according to their essence are built on the corresponding form-doctrines, as their under-steps. In the synthetic forms — which as such of theses, or propositions, of the relevant proposition-category obviously presuppose much but leave it in its particularity undetermined — lie a priori conditions of possible validity, which come to expression in the essence-laws of the disciplines in question. This is the key thesis. Raising the validity-question (for propositions determined by their pure forms) puts us in formal logic (and the parallels), built on the form-doctrines. The synthetic forms contain a priori conditions of possible validity, expressed in the essence-laws of the disciplines. So the formal disciplines (logic, axiology, practics) state the a priori validity-conditions lying in the pure forms.

He specifies: the predicative forms contain conditions of possible doxic reason-certainty (truth), made explicit by formal apophantics. Specially lie in the pure forms of the predicative (analytic) synthesis a priori conditions of the possibility of doxic reason-certainty, noematically spoken of possible truth. Their objective setting-out performs the formal logic in the narrowest sense: the formal apophantics (the formal logic of the "judgments"), which thus has its foundation in the form-doctrine of these "judgments." This specifies. The predicative forms contain a priori conditions of possible doxic reason-certainty (truth). Formal apophantics (formal logic of judgments) sets these out, built on the form-doctrine of judgments. So formal logic states the formal conditions of truth.

He extends to the emotional/volitional sphere (formal axiology and practics), with the objectification into doxic truth. Similar holds for the to the emotional and will-sphere belonging syntheses and their noematic correlates, hence for their kinds of synthetic "propositions," whose systematic form-doctrine again must give the under-ground for the construction of the formal validity-doctrines. There lie really in the pure synthetic forms of these spheres (as for example in the connections of ends and means) conditions of the possibility of axiological and practical "truth." Thereby turns, by virtue of the "objectification" that performs itself for example also in the emotional acts, all axiological and practical reasonableness in the to us understandable way around into doxic reasonableness and noematically into truth, objectively into actuality: we speak of true or actual ends, means, preferabilities, and so on. This extends the doctrine. The emotional/volitional synthetic forms contain conditions of axiological and practical "truth" (e.g. the end-means connection). And by the objectification (§117), all axiological/practical reasonableness turns into doxic reasonableness, into truth (noematically) and actuality (objectively): we speak of "true" or "actual" ends, means, preferabilities. So the value- and will-truths convert into doxic truths, and formal axiology/practics parallel formal logic.

He states phenomenology clarifies what the "dogmatic" logician isolates. On all these connections relate self-evidently own and highly-important phenomenological investigations. Already the kind of the just-given characteristic of the formal disciplines is phenomenological and presupposes much from our analyses. The researcher in the "dogmatically" treated pure logic grasps abstractively the apophantic forms ("proposition in general" or "judgment," categorical, hypothetical, conjunctive, disjunctive judgment, and so on) and fixes for them the axioms of formal truth. Of analytic synthesis, of noetic-noematic essence-relations, of the ordering of the by him grasped-out and conceptually-fixed essences into the essence-complexes of the pure consciousness, he knows nothing; he researches isolated what only in this full essence-connection can find its full understanding. Only the phenomenology makes us, through return to the sources of the intuition in the transcendentally-purified consciousness, clear what therein actually lies, when we speak now of formal conditions of the truth and now of such of the cognition. This states phenomenology's role. The dogmatic logician grasps the apophantic forms and fixes the axioms of formal truth, but knows nothing of the analytic synthesis, the noetic-noematic relations, or the constitutive context. Phenomenology, by returning to the intuitive sources, clarifies what lies in the talk of formal conditions of truth vs. of cognition. So phenomenology grounds and clarifies formal logic, which the logician treats in isolation.

He states the apophantic truths as essence-connections between the possibility of intuitive fulfillment and the pure form. In general it clarifies us about essences and essence-relations that belong to the concepts cognition, evidence, truth, being (object, state-of-affairs, and so on); it teaches us to understand the construction of the judging and of the judgment, the way how the structure of the noema is cognition-determining, how the "proposition" thereby plays its special role and again the different possibility of its epistemic "fullness." It shows which fulfillment-ways are essence-conditions for the reason-character of the evidence, which kinds of evidence are in question, and so on. In particular it lets us understand that it is at the a priori truths of logic a matter of essence-connections between the possibility of intuitive fulfillment of the proposition (whereby the corresponding state-of-affairs comes to synthetic intuition) and the pure synthetic form of the proposition (the pure-logical form), and that at the same time that possibility is condition of the possible validity. This is the deep result. The a priori truths of logic are essence-connections between the possibility of intuitive fulfillment of the proposition (the state-of-affairs coming to synthetic/categorial intuition) and the pure-logical form. And that possibility (of fulfillment) is the condition of possible validity. So a logical law holds because propositions of its form are intuitively fulfillable (can be brought to evidence). This grounds logic in the phenomenology of fulfillment/evidence: the formal-logical laws express the conditions under which propositions can be fulfilled.

He distinguishes the noematic (apophantic) and noetic (noetics) sides of the formal disciplines. It shows also that, exactly seen, here a double is to be distinguished, corresponding to the correlation of noesis and noema. In the formal apophantics (for example in the syllogistic) is the talk of judgments, as noematic propositions, and their "formal truth." The attitude is throughout noematic. On the other side in the formal apophantic noetics the attitude is noetic, it is the talk of the reasonableness, correctness of the judging, there are norms of this correctness pronounced, namely with relation to the forms of the propositions. For example a contradiction one cannot hold for true; whoever judges according to the premise-forms of the valid syllogistic modes, "must" draw conclusions of the corresponding forms, and so on. In the phenomenological connection these parallels become without further understandable. The occurrences that concern the judging, the noesis, likewise the essence-wise corresponding in the noema, the apophansis, are precisely in their necessary mutual-relation and in the full consciousness-interweaving researched. The same holds naturally of the remaining formal disciplines with respect to the parallelism of noetic and noematic regulations. This distinguishes the two sides. The formal disciplines have a noematic side (formal apophantics — judgments as propositions, formal truth) and a noetic side (formal noetics — the reasonableness/correctness of judging, the norms of correct judging). These are parallel (noesis-noema), understandable in the phenomenological connection. So formal logic has an apophantic (noematic) and a noetic part, and the same holds for axiology and practics. §148 turns to the reason-theoretical problems of the formal ontology (the turn from the apophantic to the ontological), leading to the regional ontologies and the constitution of the regions.

---

# Commentary on Husserl, *Ideas I* — File 44 (§§148–150)

### § 148\. Reason-Theoretical Problems of the Formal Ontology

Husserl makes the turn from the formal logical disciplines to the formal ontologies. From these disciplines a turn leads us to the corresponding ontologies. The connection is phenomenologically given already through the generally-possible glance-turnings that can be performed within every act, whereby the stocks that they bring into view are mutually interwoven through various essence-laws. The primary attitude is that onto the objective, the noematic reflection leads to the noematic, the noetic to the noetic stocks. Out of these stocks the disciplines interesting us here grasp abstractively pure forms, namely the formal apophantics noematic, the parallel noetics noetic forms. As these forms with one another, so are both with ontic forms essence-lawfully connected, which are graspable through back-turning of the glance onto ontic stocks. This makes the turn. Within every act, glance-turnings yield: the objective (ontic) stock, the noematic stock, the noetic stock. Formal apophantics grasps the noematic forms; formal noetics the noetic; and both are connected with the **ontic forms** (graspable by turning to the objective). So formal ontology grasps the ontic forms (the forms of objects), correlative to the apophantic (noematic) and noetic forms.

He states the equivalent transformation of every formal-logical law into a formal-ontological one. Every formal-logical law is equivalently to be transformed into a formal-ontological. Instead of about judgments is now about states-of-affairs, instead of about judgment-members (for example nominal significations) about objects, instead of about predicate-significations about marks judged, and so on. The talk is also no more of the truth, validity of the judgment-propositions, but of the subsistence of the states-of-affairs, of the being of the objects, and so on. This states the apophantic-ontological equivalence. Every formal-logical law transforms into a formal-ontological one: instead of judgments → states-of-affairs, instead of nominal significations → objects, instead of predicate-significations → marks, instead of truth/validity → being/subsistence. So formal logic (apophantic, of judgments) and formal ontology (of objects, states-of-affairs) are equivalent transformations of one another. (This is the central thesis of the relation of apophantics and ontology, developed in Formal and Transcendental Logic.) The phenomenological content of this transformation must itself be clarified (by returning to the concepts).

He extends formal ontology far beyond the mere transformations, via the nominalizations (set-theory, mathematics). The formal ontology goes, by the way, very far out beyond the sphere of such mere transformations of the formal apophantic truths. Great disciplines grow to it through those "nominalizations" of which we earlier spoke (back-reference to §119). In the plural judging the plural appears as plural thesis. Through the nominalizing turn it becomes the object set, and so grows the fundamental concept of set-theory. In this is judged about sets as objects, that have their peculiar kinds of properties, relations, and so on. The same holds for the concepts relation, number, and so on, as fundamental concepts of mathematical disciplines. This extends formal ontology. Through nominalization (§119), the syntactic forms become objects: the plural becomes the object "set" (the fundamental concept of set-theory); likewise relation, number become the fundamental concepts of the mathematical disciplines. So formal ontology includes all of formal mathematics (set-theory, arithmetic), grown by nominalization from the syntactic forms. (This is the unity of formal ontology and formal mathematics, the mathesis universalis.)

He states phenomenology studies only the axioms (as titles for analysis), not the disciplines themselves. Again we have, as at the mere form-doctrines of the propositions, to say that it is not the task of phenomenology to develop these disciplines, hence to pursue mathematics, syllogistic, and so on. Only the axioms interest it and their conceptual stock, as titles for phenomenological analyses. This states phenomenology's role. Phenomenology does not develop the formal disciplines (mathematics, syllogistic); it studies only their axioms and fundamental concepts, as titles for phenomenological analyses (clarifying set, number, relation phenomenologically). So phenomenology grounds the formal disciplines without developing them.

He extends to formal axiology and practics and their material ontologies of values and goods. What is said transfers of itself onto the formal axiology and practics, as well as onto the as theoretical desiderata to them to be ordered formal ontologies of the values (in a very-extended sense), of the goods — in short of the whole ontic spheres that are correlates of the emotional and will-consciousness. This extends to the value-spheres. The formal axiology and practics have corresponding formal ontologies of values and goods (the ontic correlates of the emotional/volitional consciousness). So there are formal ontologies of values and goods, parallel to the formal ontology of objects.

He notes the concept of "formal ontology" has broadened, and the values are materially-determined objects. One notices that the concept of the "formal ontology" in these considerations has broadened. The values, the practical objectivities, order themselves under the formal title "object," "something in general." They are thus, from the standpoint of the universal analytic ontology, materially-determined objects, the to them belonging "formal" ontologies of the values and practical objectivities material disciplines. This is an important clarification. The concept of "formal ontology" has broadened: values and goods fall under the formal title "object in general." So from the standpoint of the universal (analytic) formal ontology, values are materially-determined objects, and their "formal" ontologies are really material disciplines. So there is one universal formal ontology (of "object in general"), under which the ontologies of values/goods are material.

He warns against confusing the analogy with identity (the Kant critique). On the other hand, the analogies that ground in the parallelism of the thetic genera (belief, or belief-modality, valuing, willing) and the to them specifically ordered syntheses and syntactic formations have their force, and a so effective one, that Kant outright designates the relation of the willing of the end and the willing of the means as "analytic" and thereby of course confuses analogy with identity. The properly analytic, that belonging to the predicative synthesis of the Doxa, may not be mixed with its formal analogon, that is related to the syntheses of the emotional and will-theses. Deep-lying and important problems of the phenomenology of reason attach to the radical clarification of these analogies and parallels. This is the Kant critique. The analogy between the doxic and the value/will syntheses is strong, but Kant confused analogy with identity in calling the will of the end and means "analytic" (Groundwork). The properly analytic (the doxic predicative synthesis) must not be confused with its formal analogon (the value/will syntheses). So the parallelism between the spheres is an analogy, not an identity; clarifying it is a deep problem of the phenomenology of reason. (Kant's "analytic" connection of end and means is really only analogous to the doxic analytic.)

### § 149\. The Reason-Theoretical Problems of the Regional Ontologies. The Problem of Phenomenological Constitution

Husserl makes the transition from the formal to the material (regional) ontologies, with the master-thesis: every region constitutes itself in consciousness. After we have discussed the reason-theoretical problems that the formal disciplines pose us, the transition to the material ones would be to perform, and first to the regional ontologies. Every objective region constitutes itself consciousness-wise. An object determined through the regional genus has as such, so far as it is actual, its a priori prefigured ways to be perceivable, in general clearly or darkly presentable, thinkable, verifiable. We come thus again, with respect to the reasonableness-founding, back to the senses, propositions, epistemic essences; but now not to the mere forms, but, since we have the material generality of the regional and categorial essence in view, to propositions whose determination-content is taken in its regional determinateness. Every region offers here the leitfaden for an own closed investigation-group. This is the master-thesis of constitutive phenomenology. **Every objective region constitutes itself in consciousness.** An actual object of a region has a priori prefigured ways to be perceivable, presentable, thinkable, verifiable. So the reason-problems descend from the formal to the material: each region has its propositions (taken in their regional determinateness), and **each region is the leitfaden (guiding-thread) for a closed investigation-group** (the constitution of that region). So constitutive phenomenology proceeds region by region.

He takes the thing-region as leitfaden, stating the central problem of phenomenological constitution. We take, say, the region material thing as leitfaden. Understanding rightly what this leading says, we grasp at the same time a general problem that is determining for a great and relatively-closed phenomenological discipline: the problem of the general "constitution" of the objectivities of the region thing in the transcendental consciousness, or shorter expressed, "of the phenomenological constitution of the thing in general." In one with it we learn also the to this leit-problem ordered investigation-method. Just the same holds then for every region and every on its phenomenological constitution related discipline. This states the central problem. Taking the thing-region as leitfaden, the central problem is the **phenomenological constitution of the thing** — the constitution of the thing-objectivities in transcendental consciousness. And the method learned here holds for every region. So the constitution of the thing is the model for all constitutive analysis.

He describes the noema and its ideally-closed group of possible noemata, with the originary-giving ones. It is a matter of the following. The Idea of the thing, to remain at this region, is, when we now speak of it, consciousness-wise represented through the conceptual thought "thing" with a certain noematic stock. To every noema corresponds essence-wise an ideally-closed group of possible noemata, that have their unity therein, that they are capable of a synthetic unification through coincidence. Is the noema, as here, a unanimous one, then there find themselves in the group also intuitive and in particular originarily-giving noemata, in which all other-kind of the group fulfill themselves in the identifying coincidence, drawing from them in the case of positionality confirmation, fullness of reason. This describes the constitution. The thing-Idea is represented by the noema "thing." To every noema corresponds an ideally-closed group of possible noemata, unified by coincidence (the synthetic unification of the X). Among them are the originarily-giving noemata (the perceptions), in which the others (memories, expectations) fulfill themselves, drawing confirmation and reason-fullness. So the thing is constituted by the closed group of coinciding noemata, with the originarily-giving (perceptual) ones as the source of confirmation. (The thing is the unity of its possible appearances, the perceptions being the fulfilling ones.)

He describes the method: starting from the dark word-presentation, generating phantasy-intuitions, ideating the essence "thing." We start thus from the verbal, perhaps quite dark presentation thing, as we just have it. In freedom we generate intuitive presentations of the same "thing"-in-general and make clear to ourselves the vague sense of the word. Since it is a matter of a "general presentation," we must proceed exemplarily. We generate arbitrary phantasy-intuitions of things, say free intuitions of winged horses, white ravens, gold-mountains, and the like; also those would be things, and presentations of them serve thus for exemplification just as well as presentations of things of actual experience. Thereon we grasp, performing ideation, in intuitive clarity the essence "thing" as subject of generally-bounded noematic determinations. This describes the method (§70, the eidetic method). Starting from the dark word "thing," one generates phantasy-intuitions of things (even winged horses, gold-mountains — fictions serve as well as actual things, §70) and ideates the essence "thing" (the subject of generally-bounded noematic determinations). So the essence "thing" is won by eidetic intuition from arbitrary examples (including fictions).

He states the essence "thing" is given originarily but never adequately (recalling §143). Now it is to be noted (in memory of earlier-established, §143) that hereby indeed the essence "thing" is originarily given, but that this givenness in principle can be no adequate one. To adequate givenness we can bring the noema or the thing-sense; but the manifold thing-senses, also taken in their fullness, contain not as an immanent originary-intuitive stock the regional essence "thing," just as little as the manifold on one and the same individual thing related senses contain the individual-essence of this thing. In other words, whether it is a matter of the essence of a thing-individual or of the regional essence thing in general, in no case does a single thing-intuition or a finitely-closed continuity or collection of thing-intuitions suffice to win in adequate way the wished essence in the whole fullness of its essence-determinatenesses. To an inadequate essence-intuition, however, every one suffices; over against an empty essence-grasping, as such is to be established on the exemplary under-ground of a dark presentation, it has at any rate the great advantage of having the essence originarily given. This recalls §143. The essence "thing" is originarily given but never adequately (no single or finite collection of thing-intuitions gives the essence in its full determinateness). Only an inadequate essence-intuition is reached, but it has the advantage (over an empty grasping) of having the essence originarily given. So the essence "thing" is inadequately but originarily intuited. This holds for all generality-levels (from the individual-essence to the region thing).

He states the general essence-insight: every imperfect givenness harbors a rule for its perfecting, with the centaur example. Now it is, however, a general essence-insight, that every imperfect givenness (every inadequately-giving noema) harbors in itself a rule for the ideal possibility of its perfecting. To the essence of the centaur-appearance that I now have — an appearance giving the essence of the centaur merely "one-sidedly" — it belongs that I can go after the different sides of the thing, the at first indeterminate and open-remained freely phantasying determine and make intuitive. In the progress of this ever-more-perfectly intuitivizing and more-closely-determining phantasy-process we are in wide measure free; we can to the phantasied centaur at will ascribe more-closely-determining properties and property-changes intuitively; but completely free we are not, so far as we shall progress in the sense of a unanimous intuition-course, in which the determinable subject is identically the same and constantly can remain unanimously determinable. We are for example bound through a lawful space as a frame that the Idea of a possible thing in general firmly prescribes us. However arbitrarily we may deform the phantasied, space-shapes pass over again into space-shapes. This is the key insight, with the centaur example. Every imperfect givenness harbors a rule for its perfecting. The centaur-appearance (giving the centaur one-sidedly) can be perfected by freely phantasying the other sides. We are largely free (we can ascribe properties at will), but **not completely free**: if we progress in a unanimous intuition-course (the X remaining identical and unanimously determinable), we are **bound** by a lawful **space** (the Idea of a possible thing prescribes it). However we deform the phantasied, space-shapes pass into space-shapes. So the constitution of the thing is bound by the a priori frame of space: the thing must be spatial, and its possible appearances are ruled by the essence of space (geometry). This is the regional a priori: the Idea of the region prescribes the rule for the possible appearances.

He states what the constitution-rule means: the region prescribes the "limitlessness in the progress" of unanimous intuitions. What says now phenomenologically this talk of rule or law? What lies therein, that the inadequately-given region "thing" prescribes rules for the course of possible intuitions — and that says obviously as much as possible perceptions? To this the answer reads: To the essence of such a thing-noema belong, and absolutely insightfully, ideal possibilities of the "limitlessness in the progress" of unanimous intuitions, namely according to typically-determined prefigured directions (hence also parallel limitlessnesses in the continuous concatenations of corresponding noeses). This states the constitution-rule. The region "thing" prescribes rules for the course of possible intuitions (perceptions): to the thing-noema belong, insightfully, ideal possibilities of the **limitlessness in the progress** of unanimous intuitions, in typically-prefigured directions. So the thing-noema prescribes an infinite, typically-ordered continuum of possible appearances (recalling §143, the thing as a Kantian Idea). So constitution means: the region prescribes the lawful, infinite manifold of appearances in which its objects are given.

He unfolds the components of the thing-Idea as Ideas: res temporalis, res extensa, res materialis. The thing is, according to its Idea, necessarily enduring, in principle with respect to its duration endlessly extendible. We grasp in "pure intuition" (for this ideation is the phenomenologically-clarified concept of Kant's pure intuition) the "Idea of temporality" and all in it enclosed essence-moments. The thing is, according to its Idea, further res extensa, it is for example in spatial respect of infinitely-manifold form-transformations and, at identically-held shape and shape-change, of infinitely-manifold changes of place capable, it is in infinitum "movable." We grasp the "Idea of space" and the to it ordered Ideas. The thing is finally res materialis, it is substantial unity, as such unity of causalities and possibly of infinitely-manifold ones. Also with these specifically-real properties we strike on Ideas. So are all components of the thing-Idea themselves Ideas, every one implies the "and so further" of "infinite" possibilities. This unfolds the thing-Idea. The thing-Idea has three components, each itself an Idea: **res temporalis** (the thing endures, endlessly extendible in time — the Idea of temporality, grasped in "pure intuition," which is Husserl's clarification of Kant's pure intuition); **res extensa** (the thing is spatial, infinitely movable and transformable — the Idea of space); **res materialis** (the thing is a substantial unity of causalities — the Idea of materiality/causality). Each component is an Idea implying an infinite "and so further." So the thing-Idea is a system of Ideas (temporality, space, materiality), each infinite. (This is the constitution of the thing-region as the unity of these Ideas — the program of Book Two's analysis of material nature.)

He insists this is not theory or metaphysics but eidetic description. What we there carry out is not "theory," "metaphysics." It is a matter of essence-necessities, in the thing-noema and correlatively in the thing-giving consciousness unremovably enclosed, throughout insightfully to be grasped and systematically to be researched. This insists on the method. The unfolding of the thing-Idea (temporality, space, materiality) is not theory or metaphysics but **eidetic description**: the essence-necessities enclosed in the thing-noema (and in the thing-giving consciousness), insightfully grasped. So the constitution of the thing is a descriptive eidetic task, not a metaphysical construction. (This guards against reading the doctrine as a metaphysics of nature; it is the description of the essence "thing" and its constitution.)

### § 150\. Continuation. The Region Thing as Transcendental Leitfaden

Husserl explains how the region thing serves as transcendental leitfaden. After we have made clear to ourselves in the most-general the infinities that the thing-intuition as such (according to noesis and noema) harbors in itself — or as we can also say: the Idea of the thing and what it harbors in itself of dimensions of infinity — we will also soon be able to understand in how far **the region thing can serve as leitfaden** of phenomenological investigations. This sets up the leitfaden. The thing-Idea harbors dimensions of infinity (temporality, space, materiality). Now Husserl explains how the region thing guides phenomenological investigation. So the region thing is the transcendental leitfaden (guiding-thread) for the constitutive analysis.

He describes the straight glance at the X (in perception, free phantasy, ideation). Intuiting an individual thing, going after its movements, its approachings and distancings, its turnings and rotations, its form- and quality-changes, its causal behavior in the intuition, we **perform continua of intuiting**, coinciding so and so, closing together to the unity-consciousness: the glance is thereby directed at the identical, at the X of the sense (or of the positional or neutralized proposition), at the one and same that changes, turns, and so on. So too, when we in free intuition pursue the endlessly-possible modifications according to the different fundamental directions, in the consciousness of the limitlessness in the progress of this intuition-process. And again likewise, when we pass over to the attitude of ideation and bring, say, the regional Idea of the thing to clarity: thereby thus proceeding like the geometer in the freedom and purity of his geometrical intuition. This describes the straight glance. In all three cases (perceiving an individual thing, freely phantasying its modifications, ideating the regional Idea), the glance is directed straight at the **X** (the identical that changes), performing continua of intuiting that coincide into unity. So the natural (straight) glance is at the object (the X), like the geometer in his geometrical intuition.

He states that in the straight glance we do not "see" the intuiting itself, so reflection is needed. With all that, however, we know nothing of the processes of the intuiting itself and the to it belonging essences and essence-infinities, nothing of its stuffs and noetic moments, nothing of its noematic stocks, of the on both sides distinguishable and eidetically-graspable layers. What we actually live (or in the phantasy-modification unreflectedly conscious have), we do not **see**. There needs thus the change of the attitude, there needs the different hyletic, noetic, noematic "reflections" (all rightly so called, because they are deflections from the original, "straight" glance-direction onto the X). These reflections are it that now open us a great, in itself connected research-field, or a mighty, under the Idea thing-region standing problematic. This is the key methodological turn. In the straight glance (at the X), we do not "see" the intuiting itself (the hyletic, noetic, noematic stocks). To see them, we need the **reflections** (hyletic, noetic, noematic — all deflections from the straight glance onto the X). These reflections open the great research-field of the constitution of the thing. So the constitutive analysis requires the reflective turn from the object (the X) to the constituting consciousness (the appearances, the noeses).

He states the guiding questions of the constitution of the thing (noetic and noematic). There arises namely the question: **How are the to the unity of the intuitively-presenting thing-consciousness belonging noeses and noemata systematically to be described?** Holding to the noematic sphere, the question is: How look the manifold positing intuitions, the "intuition-propositions," in which an "actual thing" comes to givenness and intuition-wise, in original "experience," verifies its "actuality"? How look, to abstract from the doxic thesis, the mere — noematically-understood — **appearances**, that in themselves, purely eidetically considered, one and the same thing, the respectively quite-determinate thing "bring to appearance," that to this intuition- or appearance-manifold belongs as **necessary correlate**? This states the guiding questions. The constitution of the thing asks: (noetically) how are the noeses and noemata of thing-consciousness systematically described? (noematically) how look the intuition-propositions in which an actual thing is given and verifies its actuality? and (abstracting from the thesis) how look the mere **appearances** in which the same thing comes to appearance, as the necessary correlate of the appearance-manifold? So the constitutive analysis describes the appearances and the noeses in which the thing is given. (This is the program of Husserl's analyses of thing-constitution in Thing and Space and Book Two.)

He insists phenomenology demands determinate, completing work, not vague generalities. Phenomenology remains in principle at no vague talk, at no dark generalities, it demands systematically-determinate, into the essence-connections and up to the last-attainable particularizations of the same penetrating clarification, analysis, and description: it demands completing work. This insists on rigor. Phenomenology demands not vague generalities but determinate, penetrating clarification, analysis, and description — "completing work" (the actual carrying-out of the analysis). So the constitution of the thing is a rigorous, detailed eidetic task.

He states the constitution-thesis: the regional Idea prescribes the manifolds of appearances rules. The regional Idea of the thing, its identical X with the determining sense-content, posited as being — prescribes manifolds of appearances rules. That says: they are not in general manifolds, accidentally coming-together, as already comes out from this, that they in themselves, purely essence-wise, have relation to the thing, the determinate thing. The Idea of the region prescribes quite determinate, determinately-ordered, in infinitum progressing, as ideal totality taken firmly-closed appearance-series, a determinate inner organization of their courses, that essence-wise and researchably hangs together with the partial-Ideas that in the regional thing-Idea as its components are generally drawn. This states the constitution-thesis. The regional Idea (the thing as X with its sense-content, posited as being) prescribes **rules** for the manifolds of appearances: not accidental manifolds, but determinate, ordered, infinitely-progressing, firmly-closed appearance-series, with an inner organization hanging together with the partial-Ideas (temporality, space, materiality). So the thing-Idea rules the infinite manifold of appearances, organized by its component Ideas.

He introduces the thing-schema (the res extensa as the lowest stratum). It shows itself for example — as a piece of this organization — that the unity of a mere res extensa is thinkable without the unity that the Idea of the res materialis norms: although no res materialis is thinkable that were not res extensa. It turns out namely (always in eidetic-phenomenological intuition), that every thing-appearance necessarily harbors in itself a layer that we call the **thing-schema**: it is the merely with "sensuous" qualities filled space-shape without any determinateness of "substantiality" and "causality" (in quotation marks, noematically-modified understood). Already the belonging Idea of a mere res extensa is title for a fullness of phenomenological problems. This introduces the thing-schema. Within the thing-appearance there is a stratum, the **thing-schema**: the space-shape filled with sensuous qualities, without substantiality or causality (the mere res extensa). A mere res extensa is thinkable without res materialis (but not conversely). So the thing-schema (the sensuously-filled spatial shape) is the lowest stratum of the thing, on which the material (substantial-causal) determinations are founded. (This is the stratified constitution of the thing: schema → material thing, developed in Book Two.)

He states the great thesis: even God can intuit a spatial thing only through perspectival appearances. What we phenomenologically-naive take for mere facta: that "to us humans" a space-thing always appears in a certain "orientation," for example in the visual field oriented up and down, right and left, near and far; that we can see a thing only in a certain "depth," "distance"; that all changing distances in which it is to be seen are related to an invisible but as ideal limit-point well-familiar center of all depth-orientations, by us "localized in the head" — all these alleged facticities, hence contingencies of the space-intuition, that are foreign to the "true," "objective" space, prove themselves up to slight empirical particularizations as **essence-necessities**. It shows itself thus that something like space-thingly not merely for us humans, but also **for God** — as the ideal representative of the absolute cognition — is intuitable only through appearances, in which it "perspectivally" in manifold but determinate ways changing and thereby in changing "orientations" is given and must be given. This is the famous and decisive thesis. What seem mere human facts (a thing appears in an orientation, at a distance, related to a center "in the head") are **essence-necessities**, not human contingencies. So a spatial thing is intuitable **even for God** (the ideal representative of absolute cognition) **only through perspectival appearances**, in changing orientations. So the inadequacy of thing-perception (its perspectival, one-sided character) is not a human limitation but an essential necessity of any consciousness of a spatial thing whatever — even an absolute (divine) intellect must intuit a spatial thing through perspectival adumbrations. This is the deepest statement of the adumbration-doctrine (§§41–44): perspectival givenness belongs to the essence of the spatial thing, not to the human knower. (God is here, as throughout, the epistemological limit-concept, §51, §79.)

He states the constitution-problem and the origin of the space-presentation. It holds now to ground this not only as general thesis, but to pursue according to all single-formations. The problem of the "origin of the space-presentation," whose deepest, phenomenological sense has never been grasped, reduces itself to the phenomenological essence-analysis of all the noematic (or noetic) phenomena, in which space intuitively presents itself and as unity of the appearances, of the descriptive presentation-ways spatial "constitutes itself." This states the origin-problem. The problem of the "origin of the space-presentation" (a classical problem of psychology) reduces, in its deepest sense, to the phenomenological essence-analysis of the appearances in which space constitutes itself. So the origin of space is a constitutive problem (how space is constituted as the unity of its appearances), not a psychological-genetic one.

He states the meaning of "the problem of constitution." The problem of the constitution says thereby clearly nothing other than that the regulated and to the unity of an appearing **necessarily** belonging-together appearance-series can be intuitively surveyed and theoretically grasped despite their (in the determinate "and so further" precisely unambiguously masterable) infinities — that they in their **eidetic ownness** are analyzable and describable, and that the **lawful accomplishment of the correlation between the determinate appearing as unity and the determinate infinite manifolds of the appearances** can be fully insighted and so undressed of all riddles. This is the definitive statement of what "constitution" means. The problem of constitution means: the regulated appearance-series (belonging necessarily to the unity of an appearing thing) can be intuitively surveyed and theoretically grasped despite their infinities; they are eidetically analyzable and describable; and the **lawful correlation between the appearing thing (as unity) and the infinite manifold of appearances** can be fully insighted, stripped of all riddles. So **constitution is the lawful correlation between the object (unity) and its infinite manifold of appearances** — and the constitutive analysis makes this correlation insightful. This is the definitive sense of constitution (the master-correlation of §86, now fully stated). It holds for the res extensa (and res temporalis) and for the higher, founded unities (the material — substantial-causal — thing). With §150, the constitution of the thing-region is laid out as the transcendental leitfaden. §151 turns to the strata of the thing-constitution and the task of a transcendental constitution-theory; §152 extends to the other regions; §153 states the full extension of the transcendental problem, ending the book.

---

# Commentary on Husserl, *Ideas I* — File 45 (§§151–153)

### § 151\. Strata of the Transcendental Constitution of the Thing. Supplements

Husserl states that the constitution-investigations are determined by the stages and strata of thing-constitution. These investigations are essentially determined through the different stages and strata of the thing-constitution in the frame of the originarily-experiencing consciousness. Every stage and every stratum in the stage is characterized thereby, that it constitutes an own unity, which on its side is necessary middle-member for the full constitution of the thing. This states the stratification. The thing-constitution has stages and strata, each constituting its own unity, which is a necessary middle-member for the full constitution of the thing. So the thing is constituted in a layered, graded way (each stratum a partial unity contributing to the whole).

He describes the first stage: simple perceptual thing-constitution (the sense-thing, the sensual schemata, the substantial-causal thing) in one stream. Take, say, the stage of the simple perceptive thing-constitution, whose correlate is the with the sensuous qualities equipped sense-thing, so we relate to a single consciousness-stream, to the possible perceptions of a single perceiving ego-subject. We find here various unity-strata, the sensuous schemata, the "seeing-things" (Sehdinge) of higher and lower order, that in this order must be perfectly set out and according to their noetic-noematic constitution, both singly and in connection, studied. Uppermost stands in the strata of this stage the substantial-causal thing, already a reality in the specific sense, but still constitutively bound to one experiencing subject and its ideal perception-manifolds. This describes the first stage. Within a single consciousness-stream (one ego's perceptions), the thing is constituted in strata: the sensuous schemata, the "seeing-things" (Sehdinge — the thing as merely visually given, §150's thing-schema), up to the substantial-causal thing (a reality, but still bound to one experiencing subject and its perceptions). So the lowest stage constitutes the thing for one ego, culminating in the substantial-causal thing (still subject-relative).

He describes the next stage: the intersubjectively identical thing, constituted through empathy. The next-higher stage is then the intersubjectively-identical thing, a constitutive unity of higher order. Its constitution is related to an open plurality of subjects standing in the relation of "mutual understanding" (Einverständnis). The intersubjective world is the correlate of the intersubjective, that is, of the through "empathy" (Einfühlung) mediated experience. So we are referred to the manifold, by the many subjects already individually-constituted sense-thing-unities; in further consequence to the corresponding, hence to different ego-subjects and consciousness-streams belonging perception-manifolds; above all, however, to the new of the empathy and the question, how it in the "objective" experience plays a constituting role and gives those separated manifolds unity. This describes the higher stage. The intersubjectively-identical thing (the "objective" thing) is a higher-order constitutive unity, constituted through **empathy** (Einfühlung) — the experience of other subjects. The intersubjective world is the correlate of intersubjective (empathy-mediated) experience. So the objective thing is constituted across a plurality of egos through empathy, which unifies their separate perception-manifolds. (Empathy and intersubjectivity are deferred to Book Two and developed in the Cartesian Meditations.) So the objective thing requires intersubjective constitution.

He notes the analyses must be complete, recalling the basic system (unanimous appearance) treated so far. Thereby all investigations must be conducted in the through the essence of the matters demanded completeness and all-sidedness. So we have above, according to the purpose of the introduction, merely a first, a fundamental system of constituting appearance-manifolds taken into view, namely that in which one and the same thing constantly appears unanimously. The perceptions come in the limitless progress according to all systematic lines to pure coincidence, the theses experience constantly confirmation. There is here only closer-determination, never other-determination. This notes a restriction. The analyses so far treated only the **basic system** — the case where the thing appears constantly unanimously (the perceptions coinciding, the theses confirmed, only closer-determination, never other-determination). So the analysis so far assumed harmonious experience (no conflict).

He states the counter-cases must also be reckoned: the phenomenology of "null appearance" (Schein). No thing-determination come to positing through the preceding experience-course (within this ideally-closed system) experiences "crossing-out" and "replacement" through other determinations of the same constitution-category. There are no disturbances of the unanimity and no occurrences of the re-balancing of the disturbance, let alone that "exploding" of the unanimity, with which the posited thing wholly comes to crossing-out. Now, however, these counter-cases are phenomenologically not less to be reckoned, since also they in the connection of possible constitution of an experience-actuality play their role or can play it. The way of the factual, as ideally-possible cognition leads through errors, so already on the lowest cognition-stage, that of the intuiting actuality-grasping. So the perception-courses in which partial breaks of the unanimity occur and the unanimity is only through "corrections" to be obtained, are systematically according to the noetic and noematic essence-stocks to be characterized: the apprehension-changes, the peculiar thetic occurrences, the revaluations and devaluations of the earlier-apprehended for example as "appearance," "illusion," the passing-over into a stretch-wise unbalanced "conflict," and so on. Over against the continuous synthesis of the unanimity must the syntheses of the conflict, of the reinterpretation and other-determination, and however they may otherwise be called, come to their right: for a phenomenology of the "true actuality" is also the **phenomenology of the null appearance** (des nichtigen Scheins) quite indispensable. This states the necessity of the negative cases. The counter-cases (the disturbances of unanimity, the "explosion," the crossing-out, §138) must also be analyzed: the apprehension-changes, the revaluations (as "illusion"), the conflict-syntheses. So a phenomenology of "true actuality" requires also a **phenomenology of null appearance** (illusion, error) — the negative counterpart. The way of cognition leads through error, even at the lowest level. So the constitution of actuality includes the constitution of illusion and its correction.

### § 152\. Transference of the Problem of Transcendental Constitution to Other Regions

Husserl states the thing-analysis transfers to all regions, by problem and method. One sees without further that what here exemplarily for the constitution of the material thing was said — namely with respect to the constitution in the system of the manifolds of the before all "thinking" lying experience — must transfer to all object-regions, according to problem and according to method. For "sensuous perceptions" enter now naturally the to the relevant regions essence-wise ordered kinds of originarily-giving acts, that beforehand the phenomenological analysis must set out and research. This states the transference. The thing-analysis (the constitution of the material thing through perception) transfers to all regions, by problem and method. For each region, the "sensuous perceptions" are replaced by the originarily-giving acts proper to that region (e.g. for values, the value-intuitions). So every region has its own originarily-giving acts and its own constitution. So constitutive phenomenology is universal across the regions.

He notes the difficult problems of the interweaving of the regions, with the thing not isolated from the subject. Very difficult problems attach to the **interweaving of the different regions**. They condition interweavings in the constituting consciousness-formations. The thing is nothing isolated over against the experiencing subject, as already from the above indications about the intersubjective constitution of the "objective" thing-world became noticeable. Now, however, this experiencing subject is itself in the experience as real, as **human** or **animal** constituted, just as the **intersubjective communities** as animalic communities. These communities, although essentially founded in psychic realities, which on their side are founded in physical, prove themselves as new-kind objectivities of higher order. This notes the interweaving. The regions are interwoven: the thing is not isolated from the experiencing subject, which is itself constituted (as human or animal) in experience, and the intersubjective communities as animalic communities. These (founded in psychic, which are founded in physical realities) are new-kind higher-order objectivities. So the regions (physical thing, psyche, community) are founded on one another in a hierarchy, and their interweaving is a difficult problem. (The constitution of the human/animal subject and of communities is the theme of Book Two.)

He extends to the value- and practical objects and the cultural formations, which resist all naturalistic reduction. In general it shows itself that there are many-kind objectivities that defy all psychologistic and naturalistic reinterpretations. So all kinds of **value-objects** and **practical objects**, all concrete **cultural formations** that determine our actual life as hard actualities, like for example **state, right, custom, church**, and so on. All these objectities must, so as they come to givenness, according to fundamental kinds and in their stage-orders be described and for them the **problems of the constitution** posed and solved. Their constitution leads also quite self-evidently back to that of the space-thingnesses and the psychic subjects: they are precisely founded in such realities. This extends to culture. The value-objects, practical objects, and cultural formations (state, right, custom, church) are real, hard actualities that resist naturalistic/psychologistic reduction. Each must be described and its constitution-problem posed. And their constitution leads back to that of the spatial things and psychic subjects (in which they are founded). So the cultural and spiritual objectivities are founded, higher-order constitutive unities, irreducible to nature. (This is the constitution of the spiritual world, the theme of Book Two and the Krisis's Geisteswissenschaften.)

He states the privileged place of the phenomenology of material nature, but the irreducibility of the founded unities. As lowest stage lies finally the material reality all other realities at the ground, and thus comes certainly to the **phenomenology of material nature a distinguished position**. But prejudice-free regarded and phenomenologically to its sources led back, the founded unities are precisely founded and new-kind; the new that with them constitutes itself can, as the essence-intuition teaches, never and nevermore be reduced to mere sums of other realities. This is a key statement. Material nature is the lowest stage (founding all other realities), so the phenomenology of material nature has a distinguished position. But the founded unities (psyche, community, culture) are new-kind: the new that constitutes itself in them can **never be reduced to sums of other realities** (essence-intuition teaches this). So nature is foundational but the higher realities are irreducible to it (against naturalism/reductionism). So each level is founded on the lower yet genuinely new.

He states each type of actuality has its own constitutive phenomenology and concrete reason-doctrine, with the universal task. So leads in fact every peculiar type of such actualities its own constitutive phenomenology, and with it a new concrete reason-doctrine with itself. Everywhere is the task according to the principle the same: it holds, the complete system of the the originary givenness of all such objectities constituting consciousness-formations according to all stages and strata to bring to cognition, and thereby the consciousness-equivalent of the relevant kind "actuality" to make understandable. Also all that here truth-wise is to be said, in order to exclude the many and near-lying misunderstandings that concern the correlation of being and consciousness (as for example that all actuality "dissolves into psychic"), can only be said on the ground of the in phenomenological attitude and in the light of the intuition grasped essence-connections of the constitutive groups. This states the universal task and a key clarification. Each type of actuality has its own constitutive phenomenology and concrete reason-doctrine. The universal task: to bring to cognition the complete system of consciousness-formations constituting the originary givenness of each kind of objectity, and so make understandable the "consciousness-equivalent" of each kind of actuality. And the misunderstandings about the correlation of being and consciousness (e.g. that all actuality "dissolves into the psychic" — the idealist misreading, §55) can be cleared only on the ground of the constitutive essence-connections. So the correlation of being and consciousness is not a dissolution of being into the psychic; it is the lawful constitutive correlation (the thing is the correlate of, not a piece of, consciousness).

### § 153\. The Full Extension of the Transcendental Problem. Articulation of the Investigations

Husserl notes the vast extension of the research and defers the worked examples to Book Two. Of the enormous extension of the just as possible recognized and demanded researches, a so generally-held discussion as was so far only possible cannot awaken any sufficient representation. For that it would need at least for the main types of actualities pieces of executing investigations; it would need thus a proceeding as we followed with respect to the problematic of the general consciousness-structures. Meanwhile the next book will give us, with the discussion of the present-day so much occupying disputes about the mutual relation of the great science-groups that the titles **natural science, psychology, and humanistic science** designate, and especially about their relation to phenomenology, occasion to bring at the same time the constitution-problems into a more graspable nearness. This defers to Book Two. The full extension of the research cannot be conveyed in this general discussion; it would need worked examples for the main types of actualities. Book Two will provide this, with the disputes about natural science, psychology, and the humanistic sciences (Geisteswissenschaften) and their relation to phenomenology. So the worked constitutive analyses are deferred to Book Two (Ideen II).

He states the constitution-problems concern the principial of all material sciences. So much will, however, already here have become clear, that it is at them a matter of serious problems, and that research-domains open themselves that concern **all in the genuine sense principial of all material (sachhaltig) sciences**. The "principial" is indeed nothing other than what groups itself around the regional Ideas according to fundamental concepts and fundamental cognitions, and what in corresponding regional ontologies finds, or should find, its systematic unfolding. This states the reach. The constitution-problems concern the **principial** (the foundations) of all material sciences — what groups around the regional Ideas (the fundamental concepts and cognitions, unfolded in the regional ontologies). So phenomenology grounds the principial (foundations) of all the material sciences (each grounded in its regional ontology, which is grounded in its constitution).

He extends to the formal sphere, so that constitutive research finally spans the whole of phenomenology. What is said transfers from the material to the **formal sphere** and to the to it appropriated ontological disciplines, hence to all principles and principle-sciences in general, provided we extend the Idea of the constitution suitably. Thereby the frame of constitutive researches extends of course so that it finally is able to encompass the whole phenomenology. This is the great extension. The constitution-problems extend from the material to the formal sphere (formal ontology, the formal principle-sciences). So, suitably extended, the Idea of constitution finally **encompasses the whole of phenomenology**. So all of phenomenology is constitutive phenomenology (the constitution of all objectivities, material and formal). (This anticipates the closing identity below.)

He describes the supplementary considerations: from the originarily-giving consciousness through the reproductive and dark to the whole sphere of "presentation." This will of itself impose itself when we make the following supplementary considerations: In first line the problems of the object-constitution are related to the manifolds of possible **originarily-giving** consciousness. Hence for example for the things to the totality of possible **experiences**, indeed perceptions of one and the same thing. To this attaches the supplementary consideration of the reproductive positional consciousness-kinds and the research of their constitutive reason-accomplishment, or what comes to the same, their accomplishment for the simple-intuiting cognition; likewise the consideration of the darkly-presenting (but simple) consciousness and the on it related reason- and actuality-problems. In short, we move at first in the **mere sphere of "presentation."** This describes the first range. The constitution-problems concern first the originarily-giving consciousness (perception, experience), then the reproductive (memory) and dark (empty) consciousness — the whole sphere of "presentation" (Vorstellung, the simple, pre-categorial acts). So the first range is the constitution of objects in the sphere of simple presentation.

He extends to the higher (understanding/reason) sphere, with the synthetic operations. With this connect, however, the corresponding researches, related to the accomplishments of the higher, in the narrower sense so-called "understanding-" or "reason-sphere," with their explicating, relating, and other "logical" (then also axiological and practical) syntheses, with their "conceptual" operations, their assertions, their new, mediate grounding-forms. Objectivities that at first in **monothetic acts**, say in mere experiences, were given (or in the Idea as given thought), can one thus subject to the play of the **synthetic operations** and through them synthetic objectivities of ever-higher stage constitute, that in the unity of the total-thesis multiple theses and in the unity of their total-matter multiple, articulating matters contain. One can colligate, collectiva (sets) of different stage-order (sets of sets) "form," one can "parts" out of the "whole," properties, predicates at their subject "lift out," objects to objects "set in relation," at will this one to the referent, that one to the relatum "make," and so on. This extends to the higher sphere. The constitution extends to the understanding/reason sphere — the logical, axiological, practical syntheses, the conceptual operations, the assertions and inferences. Objectivities given monothetically (in experience) can be subjected to the synthetic operations (colligating, forming sets, lifting out parts and predicates, relating), constituting synthetic objectivities of ever-higher stage (sets of sets, etc.). So the higher (categorial) objectivities are constituted by the synthetic operations on the simple ones.

He notes these syntheses can be performed originarily, in phantasy, or in modification (assumption, hypothesis), in infinitum. Such syntheses can one "actually," "properly," that is in the **synthetic originarity** perform; then has the synthetic objectivity according to its synthetic form the character of the originarily-given (for example of the really-given collection, subjection, relation, and so on), and it has the full character of the originarity, when the theses have it, when thus the thetic act-characters are originarily as rational motivated. One can also free phantasies draw in, originarily-given and quasi-given set in relation, or the syntheses throughout in the modification perform, the so-conscious into an "assumption" transform, hypotheses "form," from it "consequences derive"; or also comparisons and distinctions perform, the in them given equalities or differences themselves again synthetic operations subject, with all that ideations, essence-positings or \-assumings connect, and so in infinitum. This describes the modes. The syntheses can be performed in synthetic originarity (the collection really given), or with phantasies (relating originary and quasi-given), or in modification (assumption, hypothesis — forming hypotheses, deriving consequences), or in comparison/distinction, combined with ideations, in infinitum. So the synthetic constitution has the same modes (originary, neutral, assuming) as the simple, iterable without end.
