He states the constitution-problems for the synthetic objectivities (clarity, possibility, actuality, mediate proof). The operations lie thereby partly intuitive, partly non-intuitive, possibly quite confused acts of lower or higher stage of the objectivation at the ground. In the case of the darkness or confusion one can go out to clarify the synthetic "formations," the question of their possibility, their redemption through "synthetic intuition" raise; or also that of their "actuality," that of their redeemability through explicit and originarily-giving synthetic acts, possibly on the ways of mediate "inferences" or "proofs." Phenomenologically are all these types of syntheses in correlation to the in them "constituted" synthetic objectivities to subject to an investigation, that the different givenness-modes and their significance for "actual being" of such objectivities or for true possible-being, for actual probable-being of the same, to clarify, and so according to all reason- and truth-, or actuality-questions. We have thus also here "**constitution-problems**." This states the synthetic constitution-problems. The synthetic objectivities (sets, relations, states-of-affairs) raise constitution-problems: their clarification, their possibility (redemption through synthetic intuition), their actuality (redemption through originarily-giving synthetic acts, or mediate proofs), and all the reason/truth/actuality-questions. So there are constitution-problems for the categorial/synthetic objectivities too (the constitution of mathematical and logical objects). So constitution covers the formal/categorial sphere as well.

He distinguishes the formal-logical from the regional constitution-investigations. Now the logical syntheses are indeed on lowest theses with simple matters (senses) grounded, but in a way that the essence-lawfulnesses of the synthetic stage, and specially the reason-laws — in a very wide, determinately-bounded "formal" sphere — are **independent of the particular matters of the synthetic members**. Precisely thereby is a general and formal logic possible, that abstracts from the "matter" of the logical cognition and it in indeterminate freely-variable generality (as "something") thinks. Accordingly divide themselves also the on constitution related investigations into such that attach to the **formal** fundamental concepts and take only them as "leitfäden" of the reason-problems, and on the other side into the before-described, that attach to the **regional** fundamental concepts and first to the concept of the **region itself**, namely with the question, how an individual of such region comes to givenness. This distinguishes the two. The formal-logical constitution-investigations take the **formal** fundamental concepts as leitfäden (the reason-laws being independent of the particular matters — hence a formal logic, abstracting from matter, is possible). The regional constitution-investigations take the **regional** fundamental concepts and the region itself as leitfaden (how an individual of the region is given). So there are formal and regional (material) constitution-investigations, paralleling the formal and regional ontologies.

He states the regional categories bring the determination of the synthetic form by the regional matter. With the **regional categories** and the through them prefigured investigations comes the special determination, **which the synthetic form experiences through the regional matter**, to its right, and likewise the influence that the special bindings (as such in the regional axioms find their expression) exert on the regional actuality. The executed transfers obviously to all act- and object-spheres, hence also to the objectivities for whose constitution **emotional acts with their specific theses and matters a priori have to come up**, and in a way that again according to form and material particularity to clarify is the great, scarcely suspected, let alone attacked task of the corresponding constitutive phenomenology. This completes the program. The regional categories bring the determination of the synthetic form by the regional matter (the regional axioms binding the regional actuality). This transfers to all spheres, including the value-objectivities (whose constitution requires emotional acts). So the full program is the constitutive phenomenology of all regions and all act-spheres (doxic, emotional, volitional). The constitution of the value-spheres is "the great, scarcely suspected task."

He states the relation of constitutive phenomenology to the ontologies: the ontologies give the leitfäden, but phenomenology is not grounded by them. Thereby becomes also the intimate relation of the constitutive phenomenologies to the a priori ontologies and finally to all eidetic disciplines evident (phenomenology itself we here except). The **stage-sequence of the formal and material essence-doctrines** prefigures in a certain way the **stage-sequence of the constitutive phenomenologies**, determines their generality-stages and gives them in the ontological and material-eidetic fundamental concepts and fundamental propositions the "**leitfäden**." For example are fundamental concepts of the ontology of nature, like **time, space, matter** and their next derivations, indices for strata of the constituting consciousness of material thingness, as the belonging fundamental propositions indices for essence-connections in and between the strata. The phenomenological clarification of the pure-logical makes it then understandable, that and why also all mediate propositions of the pure time-doctrine, of the geometry, and so of all ontological disciplines, are indices for essence-lawfulnesses of the transcendental consciousness and its constituting manifolds. This states the relation. The ontologies (formal and material) give the **leitfäden** for the constitutive phenomenologies: each ontological fundamental concept (time, space, matter) is an **index** for a stratum of constituting consciousness, each ontological proposition an index for an essence-connection in consciousness. So the ontologies guide phenomenology (every ontological concept points to a constitutive structure). And the mediate propositions (geometry, time-doctrine) are also indices for the transcendental consciousness.

He insists phenomenology is not grounded by the ontologies; the phenomenologist does not judge ontologically. Expressly must, however, be remarked, that in these connections between constitutive phenomenologies and the corresponding formal and material ontologies **nothing of a grounding of the former through the latter lies**. The phenomenologist judges not ontologically, when he recognizes an ontological concept or proposition as index for constitutive essence-connections, when he sees in it a leitfaden for intuitive demonstrations that bear their right and their validity purely in themselves. This general establishment will confirm itself to us still later in more thorough expositions, which by virtue of the importance of this situation are indeed demanded. This is a crucial methodological clarification. Phenomenology is **not grounded by the ontologies**: the ontological concept is only a **leitfaden** (a guide), not a ground. The phenomenologist does not judge ontologically; he uses the ontological concept as a clue to find the constitutive connections, whose right lies purely in the intuitive demonstrations themselves. So phenomenology is autonomous (grounded in its own intuitions), using the ontologies only as guides. (This guards the autonomy of transcendental phenomenology against the misreading that it rests on the ontologies.)

He states the great closing identity: a complete solution of the constitution-problems would coincide with a complete phenomenology of reason, and this with phenomenology in general. An all-sided, in equal way the noetic and noematic consciousness-strata considering **solution of the constitution-problems** would obviously be equivalent with a **complete phenomenology of reason** according to all its formal and material formations and at the same time according to its anomalous (negative-rational) as well as its normal (the positive-rational). Further, however, it imposes itself, that a so complete **phenomenology of reason would come to coincidence with phenomenology in general**, that a systematic execution of all consciousness-descriptions that through the total-title object-constitution are demanded, would have to comprise in itself **all consciousness-descriptions in general**. This is the great closing thesis of the book. A complete solution of the constitution-problems (noetic and noematic) would be equivalent to a **complete phenomenology of reason** (in all its formal and material formations, normal and anomalous). And this complete phenomenology of reason would **coincide with phenomenology in general**: a systematic execution of all the consciousness-descriptions demanded by the total-title "object-constitution" would comprise **all consciousness-descriptions whatever**. So **constitutive phenomenology \= the phenomenology of reason \= phenomenology as a whole**. The constitution of objectivity (how all objectivity is constituted in and for consciousness) is the comprehensive task that contains all of phenomenology. This is the culminating self-understanding of *Ideas I*: phenomenology, fully developed, is the science of the constitution of all objectivity in transcendental consciousness — the phenomenology of reason in its full extension. With this great identity, the book closes.

---

## Closing note on the commentary

This completes the paragraph-by-paragraph commentary on the main text of Husserl's *Ideen I* (Husserliana III/1), from the Introduction through §153, the final section. The whole has been carried through in forty-five files (\~257,000 words), each paragraph treated in its own words at equal depth, with the original headings translated, the meaning-bearing footnotes folded in, and the forward- and backward-connections traced throughout.

The book's architecture, in retrospect: the **First Part** secured the eidetic method and the absolute givenness of essences, against naturalism. The **Second Part** carried out the phenomenological reductions, winning pure consciousness as the absolute being-region and the "transcendental residuum," with the great theses on intentionality, the absolute being of consciousness over against the merely-presumptive being of the world, and the pure ego. The **Third Part** developed the systematic doctrine of method and the central structures: the *hyle/morphe* distinction, the **noesis–noema** correlation, the noematic sense and its characters, the **doxic modalities** and the **Urdoxa**, the **neutrality-modification**, the syntheses and nominalization, and the *Logos*\-layer of expression (*Sinn* and *Bedeutung*). The **Fourth Part**, "Reason and Actuality," brought the whole to its goal: the noematic sense and the **determinable X**, the theory of **reason** and **evidence** (originary givenness as the ground of right; adequate vs. inadequate; assertoric vs. apodictic), the equivalence of **truly-being** with perfect adequate givability, the **thing as a Kantian Idea**, and finally the doctrine of **constitution** — region by region, culminating in the identity of constitutive phenomenology, the phenomenology of reason, and phenomenology as such.

Throughout, the guiding correlation has held: object and consciousness, noema and noesis, being and the possible evidence in which it gives itself — the a priori correlation that is the master-theme of transcendental phenomenology, and that Husserl would continue to unfold in *Ideen II*, the *Cartesian Meditations*, *Formal and Transcendental Logic*, and the *Krisis*.
